Psychiatric Disorders, Chapter 3

Final Textbook 3, Psychiatric Disorders, full-length edition: chapter 3 of 11. The book goes up chapter by chapter; the A4 colour and black and white PDFs come once all 11 chapters are in.

Chapter 3 Neurodevelopmental Disorders

In this chapter
  1. 3.1 Disorders of a Developing Brain: Defining and Classifying the Neurodevelopmental Disorders: say what makes a disorder neurodevelopmental, trace the childhood categories from "idiocy" to DSM-5-TR, place each disorder in DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11, and explain why children are assessed through several informants and how Indian law certifies these disabilities.
  2. 3.2 Intellectual Developmental Disorder: Definition, Criteria and Severity: apply the three criteria for intellectual developmental disorder, grade its severity in the old IQ bands and by adaptive functioning, and tell it apart from global developmental delay, borderline intellectual functioning and its other look-alikes.
  3. 3.3 The Causes of Intellectual Disability: Genes, Pregnancy, Birth and Childhood: describe the chromosomal, genetic, metabolic, prenatal, perinatal and postnatal causes of intellectual disability, from Down syndrome and fragile X to phenylketonuria and fetal alcohol syndrome, and explain how many of them can be prevented.
  4. 3.4 Communication Disorders: Language, Speech, Fluency and Social Communication: distinguish speech, language and communication, diagnose the four communication disorders, and work out why a child who does not speak is silent.
  5. 3.5 Autism Spectrum Disorder I: From Kanner to the Spectrum, and the Diagnosis: trace autism from Bleuler's word to DSM-5's single spectrum, apply the two DSM-5-TR criteria with their severity levels and specifiers, and tell autism apart from its look-alikes, including Rett syndrome.
  6. 3.6 Autism Spectrum Disorder II: Who, Why and What Helps: describe how common autism is and what co-occurs with it, evaluate the genetic, environmental, brain and cognitive explanations (including theory of mind and the discredited vaccine claim), and judge the treatments, including the drugs used.
  7. 3.7 Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder I: History, Symptoms and Diagnosis: trace ADHD from Crichton and Still to DSM-5-TR and ICD-10's hyperkinetic disorder, sort its eighteen symptoms into their two lists, apply the age, duration and setting rules, and tell ADHD from the conditions that mimic it.
  8. 3.8 Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder II: Course, Causes and Drug Treatment: describe who has ADHD and how it changes from childhood to adulthood, evaluate its genetic, brain, environmental and psychological explanations, and compare the stimulant and non-stimulant drugs with the psychological treatments.
  9. 3.9 Specific Learning Disorder: Dyslexia, Dysgraphia and Dyscalculia: apply the DSM-5-TR criteria and specifiers for specific learning disorder, explain the phonological theory of dyslexia and why the IQ-discrepancy rule was dropped, and tell a learning disorder from ADHD, intellectual disability and poor teaching.
  10. 3.10 Motor Disorders: Developmental Coordination, Stereotypies, Tics and Tourette's Disorder: diagnose developmental coordination disorder and stereotypic movement disorder, define a tic and tell it from its look-alikes, and diagnose, explain and treat Tourette's disorder and the other tic disorders.

Weightage. 29 of the 314 questions mapped in this book (9.2%) test this chapter, 23 of them from real past papers. 1 of its 10 sections is High yield.

For two or three years before 1800, woodcutters and peasants in the hills of the Tarn, in the south of France, told stories of a naked boy who lived alone in the forests around the small town of Lacaune. He was glimpsed grubbing for acorns and roots, and when people came near he ran. He was caught at least once, kept for about a week in Lacaune, and escaped back into the woods. Then, in the first days of January 1800 (8 January is the date usually given), he came out of the forest near the village of Saint-Sernin-sur-Rance, in the department of the Aveyron, and was taken in at the house of a dyer. He looked about twelve years old. He did not speak. His body was covered with scars, 23 of them by one count, and one long scar ran across his throat, as if someone had once tried to cut it. The naturalist Pierre-Joseph Bonnaterre, who studied him at Rodez over the following months, found a boy who seemed hardly to feel the cold: when Bonnaterre undressed him and led him out into the snow, he frolicked in it with obvious delight. He rocked back and forth for long periods. Later, in Paris, his teacher noticed something stranger still. A pistol fired close behind his head drew hardly any response, yet the soft crack of a walnut, his favourite food, made him turn at once.

News of the "savage of the Aveyron" reached Paris, where scholars were arguing about what a human being would be like if he grew up outside society, and in August 1800 the boy was brought to the capital and placed in the National Institution for Deaf-Mutes, directed by the Abbé Roch-Ambroise Cucurron Sicard. A boy who did not speak, people assumed, must be deaf. Crowds came to stare at him. The most famous physician in France, Philippe Pinel (1745 to 1826), the reformer of the Paris asylums (Book 2, Section 1.3), examined him and compared him with the patients he knew at the hospital of Bicêtre. In a report to the Society of Observers of Man late in 1800 he concluded that the boy was no noble savage whom the forest had made wild. He was an "idiot" from birth, like the children in the Paris hospitals, and he could not profit from instruction. A young physician at the institution, Jean Marc Gaspard Itard (1774 to 1838), took the opposite view. The boy, he argued, had lived in almost complete solitude since early childhood; what looked like idiocy was the result of that isolation, and education could undo it. At the end of 1800 Sicard put Itard in charge of the boy. With the help of his housekeeper, Madame Guérin, Itard took him on, and named him Victor, because of all the sounds the boy heard, the vowel "o" was the one he responded to most.

For five years Itard worked with Victor almost every day and wrote down everything he saw. He began with the senses: hot baths of two or three hours a day, after which Victor began, for the first time, to show that he felt the cold; games in which a chestnut was hidden under one of three cups, to hold his attention and train his memory; then matching shapes, colours and, at last, letters cut out of metal. Victor learned to recognise some written words and to set out the metal letters of LAIT to ask for milk, and he came to understand some simple spoken words. He grew attached to Madame Guérin; when her husband died and she wept at the table, Victor quietly cleared away the dead man's place. Itard even tested whether the boy had a sense of justice. After a lesson that Victor had done well, Itard pretended to punish him, dragging him towards the closet where he had been shut up before. Victor resisted furiously and bit him, and Itard took the bite as proof of a moral sense. But Victor never learned to speak. A handful of sounds and words, "lait" (milk) and "Oh Dieu" (oh God) the best known, were almost all the speech he ever produced. Itard described his work in a memoir of 1801, De l'éducation d'un homme sauvage (On the education of a wild man), and in a report of 1806 to the Minister of the Interior, and he judged the experiment a failure. Victor lived on with Madame Guérin in a house in the impasse des Feuillantines in Paris and died there in 1828, when he would have been about forty. Itard's methods outlived him: his pupil Édouard Seguin (1812 to 1880) turned them into the first systematic method for educating children with intellectual disability, and Maria Montessori (1870 to 1952) later built on both (Book 2, Section 10.5.8). In 1970 the director François Truffaut retold the story in his film L'Enfant sauvage (The Wild Child), playing Itard himself.

More than two centuries later, nobody knows for certain what was wrong with Victor, and the explanations still on the table are the questions of this chapter. Pinel thought he had been born with an intellectual disability. Itard thought he was a normal child stunted by isolation, a boy in whom the window for learning language had closed before anyone reached him (Book 1, Section 8.8.4 tells the parallel story of Genie). A third answer only became possible after 1943, when autism was first described. Victor rocked, paid little attention to people, showed almost no interest in communicating, ignored a pistol shot and turned to a walnut. The psychologist Uta Frith (born 1941), in her book Autism: Explaining the Enigma (1989), suggested that he may have been autistic, and perhaps abandoned because of it; the French writer Serge Aroles, who went back to the archives, argued that the records point to a moderate degree of autism and that Victor was not a true wild child at all, but an abused boy whose scars came from people rather than from the forest. So: how do we tell a disorder of development from the effects of neglect? How do we tell intellectual disability from autism? And what can education and treatment do for a brain that developed differently from the start? Remember Victor; you will return to him at the end of the chapter.

3.1 Disorders of a Developing Brain: Defining and Classifying the Neurodevelopmental DisordersMedium yield · 2 Qs

Spend a morning in the child guidance clinic of a large Indian teaching hospital and watch who comes through the door. A grandmother carries in a three-year-old boy who has never said a word and who spins the wheels of a toy car for as long as anyone lets him. A father brings a ten-year-old whose school has written, for the third year running, that he "cannot sit in his seat". A mother sits with a quiet girl in Class 4 who speaks two languages fluently but still cannot read a simple sentence in either. A teenager is brought because, for years, he has blinked, jerked his head and cleared his throat many times an hour. These children look very different, and yet they belong to one family of conditions. None of them has lost a skill he once had, and none of them fell ill after a period of good health. In each, the brain has developed along an unusual path from the beginning, and the difficulty became visible as the child grew and the world began to ask more of him. That shared idea, a difference in how the developing brain is built, is what the word "neurodevelopmental" means, and this section sets up everything the rest of the chapter needs: what the term includes and excludes, how the classification of childhood disorders reached its present form, where each disorder sits in the two great manuals, why a child can never be assessed from a single source, and how Indian law turns a diagnosis into a certificate.

What makes a disorder neurodevelopmental

DSM-5-TR opens its first diagnostic chapter with a definition. The neurodevelopmental disorders are a group of conditions with onset in the developmental period, the years from conception through childhood and adolescence in which the brain and its skills are still being built. They typically show themselves early, often before the child starts school, and they are characterised by developmental deficits or differences in brain processes that produce impairments of personal, social, academic or occupational functioning. Each part of that sentence does work. "Onset in the developmental period" separates these disorders from conditions that strike a mature brain. "Deficits or differences in brain processes" says where the trouble lies: not in the child's circumstances, his upbringing or his character, but in the way his brain has come to process language, attention, movement, social information or learning. And "impairments of functioning" says why anyone should care: the difference matters because it makes ordinary life harder at home, at school, with friends and later at work.

The family is broad. DSM-5-TR notes that the range of difficulty runs from very specific limitations, such as a problem with learning to read or with the control of executive functions (the processes that plan, organise and hold back behaviour), to global impairments of social skills or of intellectual ability. A child with a specific learning disorder may be bright, sociable and well coordinated and still be unable to decode the words on a page. A child with profound intellectual disability may need help with every activity of the day. Both have a neurodevelopmental disorder, because both differences arose in the developing brain.

The next sentence of DSM-5-TR is easy to skim past, but it explains a great deal. These disorders, it says, were once thought to be categorically defined, present or absent like a broken bone. More recent dimensional measurement shows a range of severity, often without a very clear boundary with typical development. Think about attention. Every child is inattentive sometimes; some children are inattentive much of the time; a few are so inattentive that they cannot learn in a classroom. Reading, social understanding, motor coordination and intelligence are the same: each is a continuum on which every child has a place. Where, then, does the disorder begin? DSM-5-TR's answer is that a diagnosis requires the presence of both symptoms and impaired function. A boy who is restless and fidgety but learns well, has friends and is managing at home has a temperament, not a disorder. The same symptoms in a boy who is failing every subject and has been excluded from every game cross the line. You will meet this rule in every criteria set of the chapter, usually as a criterion that the symptoms "interfere with" or "limit" functioning.

DSM-5-TR adds two more observations that shape how the disorders are diagnosed. First, some neurodevelopmental disorders are defined by excess and some by deficit, and some by both. In ADHD the overactivity and impulsivity are behaviours that are more frequent or more intense than in other children of the same developmental age and sex; in intellectual disability the core is a failure to reach expected milestones; and autism needs both, since DSM-5-TR diagnoses it only when deficits of social communication are accompanied by excessively repetitive behaviours, restricted interests and insistence on sameness. Second, the disorders show a great deal of co-occurrence: they appear together far more often than chance would predict. Many children with autism spectrum disorder also have intellectual developmental disorder; many children with ADHD also have a specific learning disorder. They also travel with other childhood disorders outside the family: communication disorders and autism with anxiety disorders, ADHD with oppositional defiant disorder, tics with obsessive-compulsive disorder. Why should this be? Partly because the disorders share genes and risk factors such as extreme prematurity, and partly because the developing brain is a connected system, so a difference that starts in one set of circuits seldom stays confined to it. The practical lesson is simple: when you find one neurodevelopmental disorder, look for the others (Figure 3.3 in Section 3.1.6 maps the commonest overlaps).

A useful teaching idea helps here, although it is a way of thinking rather than a DSM rule. Development can go wrong in two broad ways. In developmental delay the child travels the usual road but more slowly: he sits, walks, talks, dresses himself and learns his letters in the usual order, only later than his age-mates, and at any moment he resembles a typical younger child. Much intellectual disability looks like this. In developmental deviance the road itself is different: some skills arrive on time or even early, others lag far behind, and some behaviours appear that are not part of typical development at any age. Autism is the clearest example. Consider two four-year-olds. Aarav, born very prematurely, walks steadily, feeds himself, speaks in two- and three-word phrases, plays simple pretend games with his cousins and loves to be chased; in every domain he is where a typical child of about two and a half would be. Myra can finish a fifty-piece jigsaw puzzle faster than her father and has known her numbers to a hundred since she was three, yet she does not answer to her name, rarely looks at faces, repeats whole advertisements word for word and screams if her shoes are not lined up by the door. Aarav is behind; Myra is different. Figure 3.1 draws the contrast. Neither picture is a diagnosis, but the distinction tells a clinician what to look for: an even lag points first towards intellectual disability, an uneven profile with unusual behaviours points towards autism or a specific disorder of one skill.

With that idea in hand, here is the family as DSM-5-TR arranges it. Each member gets its own section later in the chapter.

  • Intellectual developmental disorders: intellectual developmental disorder (intellectual disability), global developmental delay and unspecified intellectual developmental disorder, in which reasoning, learning and everyday adaptive skills are all below age expectations (Sections 3.2 and 3.3).
  • Communication disorders: language disorder, speech sound disorder, childhood-onset fluency disorder (stuttering) and social (pragmatic) communication disorder, in which the development of language, of speech sounds, of the flow of speech or of the social use of language is impaired (Section 3.4).
  • Autism spectrum disorder: persistent deficits in social communication and social interaction together with restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour, interests or activities (Sections 3.5 and 3.6).
  • Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD): impairing levels of inattention and disorganisation, hyperactivity and impulsivity, or both (Sections 3.7 and 3.8).
  • Specific learning disorder: persistent difficulty in learning to read, write or calculate, out of keeping with the child's general ability and schooling (Section 3.9).
  • Motor disorders: developmental coordination disorder, stereotypic movement disorder and the tic disorders, of which Tourette's disorder is the best known (Section 3.10).
  • Other specified and unspecified neurodevelopmental disorder: categories for impairing presentations that do not meet the full criteria for any specific disorder. In other specified neurodevelopmental disorder the clinician records the reason, and DSM-5-TR's own example is "neurodevelopmental disorder associated with prenatal alcohol exposure", a range of developmental disabilities following exposure to alcohol in the womb (Section 1.8.6 describes the related condition for further study, and Section 3.3.5 teaches fetal alcohol syndrome as a cause). In unspecified neurodevelopmental disorder the clinician does not give the reason, often because there is not yet enough information, as in an emergency department.

Three confusions trip up students, and each needs clearing away before you go further. The first is a matter of words. The neurodevelopmental disorders are not the same thing as the neurodevelopmental model of schizophrenia, the theory proposed in 1987 by Daniel Weinberger and, separately, by Robin Murray and Shôn Lewis, that schizophrenia begins with a disturbance of early brain development that stays silent until adolescence (Sections 1.5.4 and 4.7.6). The model is a theory about one adult disorder; the neurodevelopmental disorders are a chapter of the manual.

The second confusion is with the neurocognitive disorders, such as delirium and the dementias, which DSM-5-TR places at the opposite end of the manual (Chapter 10 of this book). The difference is the difference between never having built a skill and losing one. A neurocognitive disorder is a decline from a level of functioning the person once had; a neurodevelopmental disorder is a failure or difference in development itself. DSM-5-TR is explicit that intellectual developmental disorder is distinct from the neurocognitive disorders, which are characterised by a loss of cognitive functioning. The two can occur together, and then both are diagnosed: a man with Down syndrome who develops Alzheimer's disease in middle age has both, and so does a child whose intellectual disability follows a severe head injury once his abilities have stabilised. This old distinction between a mind that never developed and a mind that was lost goes back to the nineteenth century, and Section 3.2.1 tells its history.

The third confusion is the commonest in examinations. Not every disorder that begins in childhood is a neurodevelopmental disorder. DSM-5-TR scatters the disorders of childhood across its chapters according to their nature, not their age of onset. Separation anxiety disorder and selective mutism are anxiety disorders (Sections 6.4.7 and 6.4.8). Reactive attachment disorder and disinhibited social engagement disorder, which follow grossly inadequate care, are trauma- and stressor-related disorders (Section 7.4.5). Enuresis and encopresis are elimination disorders, and pica and avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder are feeding and eating disorders (Chapter 8). Oppositional defiant disorder and conduct disorder are disruptive, impulse-control and conduct disorders (Chapter 11), and disruptive mood dysregulation disorder is a depressive disorder (Section 5.3.2). Section 3.1.2 and its Table 3.1 show how each of them was moved out of the old childhood section. ADHD itself was a close call: the DSM-5 work groups found evidence that could have placed it among the disruptive behaviour disorders, but the preponderance of evidence from its symptoms, its comorbidity and its risk factors put it here (Section 1.8.2 tells the reasoning).

One more frame sits behind the whole chapter. Developmental psychopathology, the study of how disorders arise and change as a person develops, insists that a behaviour can only be judged against what is expected at a particular age: a tantrum at three is ordinary and at thirteen is not, and the same disorder looks different at two, at eight and at twenty-five (Book 2, Section 10.1.2, and the Developmental book). Every criteria set in this chapter is written with that principle in mind, which is why so many of them say "inappropriate for age" or "below age expectations".

Exam trap

"Which disorder is classified as a neurodevelopmental disorder?" The answer is ADHD. The usual distractors each live in another chapter of DSM-5-TR: obsessive-compulsive disorder in the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders (Section 6.5), generalised anxiety disorder in the anxiety disorders (Section 6.4) and borderline personality disorder in the personality disorders (Chapter 11). The full neurodevelopmental list is intellectual developmental disorders, communication disorders, autism spectrum disorder, ADHD, specific learning disorder and the motor disorders, including tics. Two more traps: separation anxiety disorder and selective mutism begin in childhood but are anxiety disorders, and the neurodevelopmental model of schizophrenia is a theory, not a DSM category.

From "disorders of childhood" to a neurodevelopmental chapter

Psychiatry came to children late. For most of its history the only childhood condition with a long medical literature was what the nineteenth century called idiocy, the condition Pinel saw in Victor (Section 3.2.1 follows its names from Esquirol to DSM-5-TR). The other members of the family were described one at a time, mostly by physicians and educators who worked with children rather than by the authors of classifications. John Langdon Down (1828 to 1896) described the syndrome that bears his name in 1866 (Section 3.3.2). George Frederic Still (1868 to 1941) lectured in 1902 on restless, inattentive children with what he called a "defect of moral control" (Section 3.7). Alfred Binet (1857 to 1911) and Théodore Simon (1873 to 1961) published their intelligence scale in 1905, giving clinicians the first practical way to measure the intellectual side of development (Book 1, Section 9.2.3). Theodor Heller, a special educator in Vienna, described in 1908 children who developed normally and then lost their skills (Section 3.5.2). Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939) coined the word "autism" in 1911, but for a symptom of schizophrenia, and it was Leo Kanner (1894 to 1981) in 1943 and Hans Asperger (1906 to 1980) in 1944 who described autism in children (Section 3.5.1). Kanner had already written Child Psychiatry (1935), the first English-language textbook of the subject, and the child guidance clinics of the 1920s and 1930s had begun to treat children as patients in their own right (Book 2, Sections 10.5.1 and 10.1.6). The official manuals took much longer to make room for them, and the story of how they did so explains the shape of the present chapter. Chapter 1 describes each edition of the DSM in general; here we follow only what each did to the disorders of childhood.

The first Diagnostic and Statistical Manual, DSM-I (1952), had almost nothing to say about children. It recognised mental deficiency, in familial or hereditary and idiopathic forms graded by IQ into mild, moderate and severe (its "mild" level reached as high as an IQ of 85), with a separate "chronic brain syndrome with mental deficiency" when a brain disease was known. Most other childhood problems were filed as transient "adjustment reactions" of infancy, childhood or adolescence, or as "special symptom reactions" such as a learning disturbance, a speech disturbance, enuresis or sleepwalking. A child of the kind Kanner had described, if diagnosed at all, received "schizophrenic reaction, childhood type".

DSM-II (1968) replaced mental deficiency with mental retardation, graded by IQ into five levels from borderline to profound, and created the first group of behaviour disorders meant specifically for the young: "behavior disorders of childhood and adolescence". Its members were the hyperkinetic reaction of childhood (or adolescence), the forerunner of ADHD (Section 3.7), the withdrawing reaction, the overanxious reaction, the runaway reaction, the unsocialized aggressive reaction and the group delinquent reaction. The word "reaction", which DSM-II dropped from most adult diagnoses, survived here, and it reflected a view of the time: a disturbed child was mainly a child reacting to his circumstances. A separate group of "special symptoms" collected speech disturbance, specific learning disturbance, tic, other psychomotor disorders, disorders of sleep, feeding disturbance, enuresis, encopresis and headache. And autism was still not a diagnosis in its own right. DSM-II's "schizophrenia, childhood type" was for schizophrenic symptoms appearing before puberty, which might show themselves, it said, in autistic, atypical and withdrawn behaviour, a failure to develop an identity separate from the mother's, and a general unevenness, gross immaturity and inadequacy of development.

The decisive change came with DSM-III (1980), which gave childhood its first full section, "disorders usually first evident in infancy, childhood, or adolescence". The section brought together mental retardation; attention deficit disorder, with or without hyperactivity; conduct disorder; the anxiety disorders of childhood (separation anxiety, avoidant and overanxious disorders); the eating disorders, anorexia nervosa and bulimia included, since they usually began in adolescence; the stereotyped movement disorders, which were then the tic disorders, Tourette's disorder among them; disorders with physical manifestations such as stuttering, enuresis and encopresis; and a new group of pervasive developmental disorders, headed by infantile autism. For the first time the official American manual separated autism from schizophrenia. The specific developmental disorders, of reading, arithmetic, language and articulation, were recorded on Axis II, beside the personality disorders, so that a child's lifelong learning problem would not be forgotten behind his presenting complaint (Section 1.7.5 explains the axes).

DSM-III-R (1987) took a step towards the modern idea. It gathered mental retardation, the pervasive developmental disorders (now autistic disorder and pervasive developmental disorder not otherwise specified, PDD-NOS) and the specific developmental disorders under one heading, "developmental disorders", and coded all three on Axis II. Attention deficit disorder became attention-deficit hyperactivity disorder. DSM-IV (1994) and its text revision DSM-IV-TR (2000) renamed the section disorders usually first diagnosed in infancy, childhood, or adolescence and divided it into ten groups: mental retardation; learning disorders; motor skills disorder; communication disorders; pervasive developmental disorders; attention-deficit and disruptive behavior disorders; feeding and eating disorders of infancy or early childhood; tic disorders; elimination disorders; and "other disorders of infancy, childhood, or adolescence", a mixed group that held separation anxiety disorder, selective mutism, reactive attachment disorder of infancy or early childhood and stereotypic movement disorder. The pervasive developmental disorders grew to five, since Rett's disorder, childhood disintegrative disorder and Asperger's disorder joined autistic disorder and PDD-NOS. Only mental retardation was now coded on Axis II, beside the personality disorders; the learning, motor and communication disorders moved to Axis I. DSM-IV itself warned that the section existed for convenience: adults could receive these diagnoses, and children could receive diagnoses from any other part of the manual.

That warning points to the weakness of the arrangement. The section grouped disorders by the age at which they were usually first diagnosed, not by what they were. It put autism and intellectual disability, lifelong differences in how the brain is built, beside bedwetting, a fear of leaving mother, a refusal to eat and a habit of stealing cars. DSM-5 (2013) dissolved it. The disorders whose essence is a difference in early brain development were kept together in a new chapter, "Neurodevelopmental Disorders", which became the first chapter of the manual because DSM-5 arranges its chapters along the life span, beginning with the disorders that show themselves earliest (Section 1.8.2). The rest were sent to the chapters that match their nature, as Table 3.1 shows. Inside the new chapter several things changed at once: the four separate pervasive developmental disorders became one autism spectrum disorder, the three learning disorders became one specific learning disorder with specifiers, social (pragmatic) communication disorder was added, transient tic disorder was renamed provisional tic disorder, and mental retardation became intellectual disability, with severity judged by adaptive functioning rather than IQ (Section 3.2.3). DSM-5-TR (2022) made one more change of name. DSM-5 had called the condition "intellectual disability (intellectual developmental disorder)"; DSM-5-TR reversed the order to intellectual developmental disorder (intellectual disability), to show its relationship with ICD-11's "disorders of intellectual development", and kept "intellectual disability" in brackets because educators, advocacy groups and the public use it. In the United States, Rosa's Law (Public Law 111-256) had already replaced "mental retardation" with "intellectual disability" throughout federal law in 2010. This chapter uses the two terms interchangeably.

Figure 3.2 puts the story on one page. Read its upper line as the history of ideas, in which individual clinicians described the conditions, and its lower line as the history of the manuals, which slowly caught up with them. Notice how late the manuals arrive: idiocy, hyperactivity and autism, three of the central ideas of this chapter, had all been described long before any official classification gave children a section of their own.

Exam trap

"Disorders usually first diagnosed in infancy, childhood, or adolescence" is the DSM-IV and DSM-IV-TR heading (DSM-III said "first evident"), and "pervasive developmental disorders" was their name for the autism group (autistic disorder, Rett's disorder, childhood disintegrative disorder, Asperger's disorder and PDD-NOS). ICD-10 keeps both ideas alive in its own blocks (Section 3.1.3). In DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR the heading is neurodevelopmental disorders, and it is not a home for every childhood disorder: separation anxiety disorder, selective mutism, reactive attachment disorder, the elimination disorders, conduct and oppositional defiant disorders and disruptive mood dysregulation disorder all live elsewhere. In DSM-IV-TR only mental retardation was coded on Axis II; in DSM-III it was the specific developmental disorders.

Where each disorder lives: DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11

An Indian clinician has to move between three systems. Research papers and most entrance examinations speak the language of DSM-5-TR; hospital records, insurance forms and the law still largely speak WHO's ICD-10; and ICD-11, in force internationally since 2022, is gradually replacing it. Each system places the neurodevelopmental disorders a little differently, and each gives them different codes. Chapter 1 has already laid out the large blocks: ICD-10's Chapter V gives F70 to F79 to mental retardation, F80 to F89 to the disorders of psychological development and F90 to F98 to the behavioural and emotional disorders with onset usually occurring in childhood and adolescence (Section 1.9.4), and ICD-11's Chapter 6 opens with a single grouping of neurodevelopmental disorders, 6A00 to 6A0Z (Section 1.9.5). This subsection goes down to the individual codes, which Chapter 1 does not give.

Start with the code numbers printed in DSM-5-TR. The APA does not have codes of its own: DSM-5-TR prints the codes of ICD-10-CM, the clinical modification of ICD-10 used in the United States (Section 1.8.3). They look like WHO ICD-10 codes, and most of them mean the same thing, but not all. Two examples matter for this chapter. F88 does double duty in DSM-5-TR, standing for both global developmental delay and other specified neurodevelopmental disorder, because ICD-10-CM files both under its "other disorders of psychological development"; this is the same borrowing that gives some other conditions in DSM-5-TR their R and Z codes (Section 1.8.7). And F90.0 and F90.1 change meaning between the two systems. In DSM-5-TR they are ADHD, predominantly inattentive presentation, and ADHD, predominantly hyperactive/impulsive presentation; in the WHO's ICD-10 they are "disturbance of activity and attention" and "hyperkinetic conduct disorder".

ICD-10 itself spreads the family across three blocks. The first, F70 to F79, holds mild (F70), moderate (F71), severe (F72) and profound (F73) mental retardation, with F78 for "other" and F79 for "unspecified". A fourth character records how much the person's behaviour is affected: .0 for no or minimal impairment of behaviour, .1 for significant impairment requiring attention or treatment, .8 for other impairments and .9 when none is mentioned. A young man with moderate mental retardation who has begun hitting his carers would therefore be coded F71.1. The second block, F80 to F89, the disorders of psychological development, holds two kinds of condition. The "specific developmental disorders" are those in which one skill lags while development is otherwise normal: of speech and language (F80, from specific speech articulation disorder, F80.0, through expressive and receptive language disorders, F80.1 and F80.2, to acquired aphasia with epilepsy, the Landau-Kleffner syndrome of Section 3.4.2, F80.3), of scholastic skills (F81: specific reading disorder, specific spelling disorder, specific disorder of arithmetical skills and a mixed disorder, with developmental expressive writing disorder among the "other" disorders at F81.8), of motor function (F82), and mixed (F83). Beside them sit the pervasive developmental disorders of F84: childhood autism (F84.0), atypical autism (F84.1), Rett syndrome (F84.2), other childhood disintegrative disorder (F84.3), an ICD oddity with no DSM equivalent called overactive disorder associated with mental retardation and stereotyped movements (F84.4), and Asperger syndrome (F84.5). The third block, F90 to F98, mixes disorders that DSM-5-TR would call neurodevelopmental with others that it would not. It contains the hyperkinetic disorders (F90), ICD-10's narrower cousin of ADHD (Section 3.7 explains the difference), next to the conduct disorders, the emotional disorders of childhood, elective mutism and the attachment disorders; the tic disorders (F95: transient, chronic motor or vocal, and "combined vocal and multiple motor tic disorder [de la Tourette]", F95.2); and, under F98, nonorganic enuresis and encopresis, pica, stereotyped movement disorders (F98.4), stuttering [stammering] (F98.5) and cluttering (F98.6). Attention deficit disorder without hyperactivity has no place in F90 at all: ICD-10 lists it among the "other specified" disorders at F98.8.

ICD-11 tidied all of this into one grouping, as DSM-5 did (Section 1.9.6 summarises the change). Its neurodevelopmental disorders are the disorders of intellectual development (6A00, with mild, moderate, severe and profound levels, a "provisional" category for a child under four or a person who cannot be validly assessed, and an unspecified one); developmental speech or language disorders (6A01, divided into developmental speech sound disorder, developmental speech fluency disorder and developmental language disorder, the last with sub-codes for receptive and expressive, mainly expressive, mainly pragmatic and other impairments); autism spectrum disorder (6A02, sub-coded by two questions, whether there is a disorder of intellectual development and how far functional language is impaired, into five codes, 6A02.0 to 6A02.3 and 6A02.5, since the current release has no 6A02.4); developmental learning disorder (6A03, with impairment in reading, in written expression, in mathematics, or of another kind); developmental motor coordination disorder (6A04); attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (6A05, in predominantly inattentive, predominantly hyperactive-impulsive and combined presentations); and stereotyped movement disorder (6A06, with or without self-injury), with 6A0Y and 6A0Z for other specified and unspecified presentations. Three things lie just outside the grouping. Tourette syndrome (8A05.00) and the other primary tic disorders are classified in the chapter on diseases of the nervous system; Tourette syndrome and the two chronic tic disorders (8A05.01 and 8A05.02) are only cross-listed among the neurodevelopmental disorders, and Tourette syndrome, as Section 6.6 notes, among the obsessive-compulsive or related disorders as well. Rett syndrome is no longer a mental disorder at all but a developmental anomaly (LD90.4). And a secondary neurodevelopmental syndrome (6E60) is recorded when neurodevelopmental features that do not meet any specific disorder are the direct result of another health condition, ICD-11's examples being the autistic-like features of Rett syndrome, the aggression and self-mutilation of Lesch-Nyhan syndrome and the abnormal language development of Williams syndrome (Section 3.3.3).

Last, DSM-5-TR uses a few kinds of specifier across the whole chapter, so it helps to know them before you meet them disorder by disorder. Severity specifiers exist for intellectual developmental disorder, autism spectrum disorder, ADHD, specific learning disorder and stereotypic movement disorder. Specifiers describing the current symptoms exist for ADHD (its presentation), specific learning disorder (the academic domains and subskills affected) and persistent motor or vocal tic disorder (motor tics only or vocal tics only). And autism spectrum disorder and stereotypic movement disorder can both be specified as "associated with a known genetic or other medical condition or environmental factor", which lets the clinician record a cause, such as fragile X syndrome or exposure to valproate in the womb, that may matter for the course and for the family. Table 3.2 brings the codes together.

Exam trap

Four code traps. (1) The same code can mean different things: in DSM-5-TR, F90.0 is ADHD, predominantly inattentive presentation, but in WHO ICD-10 it is "disturbance of activity and attention", and F90.1 is predominantly hyperactive/impulsive ADHD in DSM-5-TR but hyperkinetic conduct disorder in ICD-10. (2) ICD-10 puts autism (F84) among the disorders of psychological development (F80 to F89) but the hyperkinetic disorders (F90) and tics (F95) among the behavioural and emotional disorders with onset usually occurring in childhood and adolescence (F90 to F98); stuttering is F98.5, not an F80 code. (3) In ICD-11, Tourette syndrome is 8A05.00 in the nervous system chapter, only cross-listed with the neurodevelopmental disorders, and Rett syndrome (LD90.4) has left the mental disorders altogether. (4) ICD-11's "provisional" disorder of intellectual development is for a child under four; DSM-5-TR's global developmental delay is for a child under five (Section 3.2.4).

Why several informants: assessing a developing child

An adult patient can usually tell you what is wrong. A child often cannot, and even when he can, his behaviour depends heavily on where he is and who is with him. That is why child assessment collects information from several informants, by several methods, about several settings, an approach called multi-informant assessment. Book 2, Section 10.1.4 tells the research behind it: when investigators compared different people's ratings of the same children, agreement between a parent and a teacher turned out to be low, and most of the disagreement was not error but a true picture of a child who behaves differently in different places. Consider Siddharth, aged eight, who sits in a Class 3 section of fifty-two children in a crowded government school in Kanpur. His class teacher writes that he is never in his seat, calls out answers, pushes in the line and loses his books every week. His mother, who works long shifts in a garment unit, sees him mostly when he is tired and asleep. His grandmother, who sits beside him at the kitchen table for his homework, one adult to one child, says he is "a little naughty but sits fine with me". In the clinic, on a quiet morning with one friendly adult and a box of new puzzles, he works steadily for forty minutes. Who is right? All of them. Each has seen a true slice of the boy's life, and only together do the slices show where and when his difficulty appears and what the different settings demand of him.

DSM-5-TR itself explains why the clinic is often the worst place to judge. In its text on ADHD it notes that the signs may be minimal or absent when the child is receiving frequent rewards for good behaviour, is under close supervision, is in a new setting, is doing something especially interesting, has constant outside stimulation such as a screen, or is in a one-to-one situation, and it gives the clinician's office as the example. Siddharth in the clinic was all of these at once. A clinician who relied on observation alone would have sent him home with a clean bill of health.

The criteria do not merely recommend several sources; for most of the disorders in this chapter they cannot be applied without them. Go through them one by one.

  • ADHD. Several inattentive or hyperactive-impulsive symptoms must be present in two or more settings, such as home, school or work, with friends or relatives, or in other activities. DSM-5-TR adds that confirming substantial symptoms across settings typically cannot be done accurately without consulting informants who have seen the person in those settings, and that symptoms typically vary with the context even within one setting. When an adult is assessed, his own memory of his childhood is unreliable, so DSM-5-TR advises getting information from others as well. ICD-11 says the same in its own words: the symptoms must be evident across multiple situations or settings, but are likely to vary with the structure and demands of each one.
  • Autism spectrum disorder. The social communication deficits must be present "across multiple contexts", and both groups of criteria may be met "currently or by history". DSM-5-TR explains why history is allowed: symptoms change with development and may be masked by strategies the person has learned, so the parents' account of the toddler years is part of the evidence, although the present picture must still cause significant impairment.
  • Intellectual developmental disorder. The adaptive deficits must limit functioning in one or more activities of daily life "across multiple environments, such as home, school, work, and community". Adaptive functioning is measured with standardised scales completed with knowledgeable informants (a parent or other family member, a teacher, a counsellor or care provider) and with the person himself as far as possible, together with educational, developmental, medical and mental health records. An intelligence test in a clinic room can measure reasoning; only the people who live and work with a child can say whether he can buy vegetables, take a bus or keep a friend.
  • Specific learning disorder. DSM-5-TR states that its four criteria are to be met on a clinical synthesis of the person's developmental, medical, family and educational history, school reports and psychoeducational assessment. No single test score can show that a difficulty has lasted despite targeted help, or that the child was taught adequately in the first place.
  • Communication disorders. Assessment must take account of the child's cultural and language background, especially in bilingual homes, and tests standardised on one group may not give appropriate norms for another. A true language disorder in a bilingual child affects both languages, so both should be assessed. A child who speaks Bhojpuri at home and is taught in English cannot be judged on an English test alone.

Behind all of these sources stands one that no rating scale replaces: the developmental history, the clinician's step-by-step account of the child's life from conception to the present. It begins with the pregnancy (illnesses, infections, bleeding, medicines, alcohol and tobacco), moves to the birth (at term or early, the birth weight, whether the baby cried at once or needed oxygen or a stay in the neonatal unit, jaundice, seizures) and then follows the milestones in each domain: when the child first smiled, sat, crawled, walked, babbled, pointed, said words and then phrases, and when he became toilet trained. It asks about illnesses, head injuries and seizures, about hearing and vision, about schooling, and about relatives with similar difficulties or a marriage between relatives. Above all it asks one question that can change the whole diagnosis: has the child ever lost a skill he once had? Such a loss, called regression, is never part of ordinary development, and it sends the clinician looking for autism with regression, for the rare neurological and genetic conditions of Sections 3.3 and 3.5.2, and for epilepsy. Each part of the history maps onto a later section of this chapter: the pregnancy and birth onto the causes of intellectual disability (Section 3.3), the milestones onto the distinction between delay and deviance (Section 3.1.1), and the course over time onto the question of whether a child is catching up or falling further behind. The normal sequence of language milestones is taught in Book 1, Section 8.8.5.

Some findings in the history are serious enough that the clinician should not wait and see. Nisha, aged two years and two months, is brought by her parents because she says only "mama" and "dudu". Her grandmother is not worried: "Her father also talked late; she will talk when she is ready." Many late talkers do catch up, but Nisha has no two-word phrases at 24 months, one of the warning signs in Table 3.3, and the right response is a hearing test and a developmental assessment now, not a review in six months. The table collects the best-known warning signs. The first five come from the practice parameter on screening for autism published in 2000 by the American Academy of Neurology and the Child Neurology Society, written by a group led by Pauline Filipek, which called them absolute indications for immediate further evaluation; they flag any developmental disorder, not only autism.

Table 3.3 Warning signs that call for developmental assessment without delay.
AgeWarning signWhy it mattersSource
By 12 monthsNo babblingCheck hearing; early sign of language or wider developmental disorderFilipek and colleagues (2000)
By 12 monthsNo gestures, such as pointing or waving bye-byeGestures are the first social communication; their absence is an early autism signFilipek and colleagues (2000)
By 16 monthsNo single wordsLanguage delay; check hearing and overall developmentFilipek and colleagues (2000)
By 24 monthsNo spontaneous two-word phrases (not just echoing)Language disorder, intellectual disability or autism possibleFilipek and colleagues (2000)
Any ageAny loss of language or social skillsRegression is never normal: needs medical and neurological assessmentFilipek and colleagues (2000)
By about 18 monthsNot walkingMotor delay; may be part of global developmental delay or a neuromuscular disorderUK National Health Service child-development guidance
Clinical link

Taking a developmental history in an Indian clinic has its own craft. Parents rarely remember dates, so anchor each milestone to an event they do remember ("Was she walking at her first birthday? At Diwali that year? When her brother was born?"), and use the immunisation card, birthday photographs and, increasingly, the videos on the family's mobile phone, which can show a toddler's babble, pointing or hand-flapping more reliably than memory. Ask who looks after the child during the day, since that person, often a grandmother, may be the best informant of all, and ask which languages are spoken to the child. Hear the family's reassurances ("boys talk late", "he is just naughty", "she will grow out of it") respectfully, but do not let them override a warning sign.

Exam trap

"When diagnosing developmental disorders, multi-informant assessment is recommended because..." The answer is that symptoms may vary across contexts: a child's behaviour at home, at school and in the clinic genuinely differs, so each informant sees a valid but partial picture. Reject the other options. "Parents tend to over-report problems" blames one informant, and parents may equally under-report. "Teachers provide no relevant information" is false, since teachers are often the best judges of attention and learning. "Only clinical observation is not sufficient" is true, but it is a consequence of the real reason (behaviour in the clinic is one more context, and often an unrepresentative one), not the reason itself. Remember the criterion that makes the point in a single line: ADHD needs several symptoms in two or more settings.

The tools you will meet, one line each

The later sections of this chapter mention many tests and scales by name, and examiners like to ask which tool belongs to which disorder. The tests themselves, how they are built, scored and interpreted, belong to the Assessment book, and the intelligence tests are introduced in Book 1 (Sections 9.3.2 to 9.3.6 and 9.8.2). Table 3.4 is therefore a map, not a lesson: one line per tool, grouped by what it is for, with the section of this chapter where it matters. Four groups are worth keeping apart. Developmental schedules for infants and preschool children compare a child's skills with the ages at which typical children achieve them and yield a developmental age and a developmental quotient (DQ, developmental age divided by chronological age, times 100). Intelligence tests yield a mental age or, in their modern form, a deviation IQ. Adaptive behaviour scales, completed with an informant, describe what the person actually does in daily life; the Vineland Social Maturity Scale gives a social age and a social quotient. And the disorder-specific instruments screen for, diagnose or rate the severity of one condition. Two Indian entries deserve a word. The Developmental Screening Test of J. Bharat Raj, published in Mysore (Indian sources give 1977 or 1983), is a quick Indian screen of mental development from birth to fifteen years that gives a developmental age and DQ. The Seguin Form Board, a performance test descended from the form boards Édouard Seguin designed for teaching, asks the child to fit ten shaped blocks into their recesses as fast as possible over three timed trials; the best time is converted into a mental age from Indian norms, and because it needs no language it is still part of many Indian test batteries for young children.

Exam trap

Match the quotient to the test. Intelligence tests give a mental age and an IQ; the Vineland Social Maturity Scale gives a social age and a social quotient; developmental schedules such as Gesell's, Bayley's and the Developmental Screening Test give a developmental age and a developmental quotient (Book 1, Section 9.8.2). Screening tools (M-CHAT-R/F) are not diagnostic tools (ADOS-2, ADI-R, INDT-ASD), and neither is a certification tool: in India, disability in autism is certified with ISAA, while CARS is an American rating scale (the IHBAS twist is in Section 3.1.6).

Neurodevelopmental disorders in India: numbers, law and certification

How many Indian children have a neurodevelopmental disorder? For a long time nobody could say, because most Indian figures came from single clinics, single schools or single districts, each using its own methods. The first large answer, covering several regions with one method, came from a multicentre study published in 2018, and it is worth knowing in detail.

Key study: How common are neurodevelopmental disorders in Indian children? (Arora and colleagues, 2018)

A national team coordinated by the INCLEN Trust (International Clinical Epidemiology Network) and led by Narendra K. Arora set out to measure the burden in children aged 2 to 9. Using cluster sampling, they assessed 3,964 children, with almost equal numbers of boys and girls, from five deliberately different populations: Palwal in Haryana (north-central, all rural), Kangra in Himachal Pradesh (north), Dhenkanal in Odisha (east), Hyderabad in Telangana (south, all urban) and North Goa (west). Every child was examined for seven conditions (vision impairment, epilepsy, neuromotor impairment including cerebral palsy, hearing impairment, speech and language disorders, autism spectrum disorder and intellectual disability), and the 6- to 9-year-olds were also assessed for ADHD and learning disorders.

Pooled across the sites, 9.2 percent of children aged 2 to under 6 and 13.6 percent of those aged 6 to 9 had at least one of these conditions, roughly one child in eleven and one in seven. The sites differed widely (from 2.9 to 18.7 percent in the younger group), but there was no significant difference by sex, by rural or urban residence or by religion, and almost one fifth of the affected children had more than one condition. Hearing impairment, intellectual disability, speech and language disorders, epilepsy and learning disorders were the common conditions across the sites, and autism was about as common as in studies from other countries. The risk factors were telling: birth outside a health institution, asphyxia at birth, illness in the newborn period, infections of the brain after birth, stunting, and low birth weight or prematurity. The authors concluded, in their paper in PLOS Medicine, that most of these risks could be modified by public health measures.

Two cautions. The sample contained fewer stunted and low-birth-weight children than the national population and 15.6 percent of families refused, so the true burden is probably higher. And the study's list of conditions is wider than the DSM-5-TR chapter, since vision and hearing impairment, epilepsy and cerebral palsy are not mental disorders; its figures measure developmental disability in the broad sense, not the neurodevelopmental disorders of this chapter alone.

The finding that so many children had more than one condition is the Indian face of a pattern you met in Section 3.1.1: the neurodevelopmental disorders overlap. Figure 3.3 draws the family as overlapping ovals and numbers the overlaps that matter most clinically. Autism and intellectual disability often occur together; so do ADHD and specific learning disorder; tics are common in children with ADHD; and tics are closely linked with obsessive-compulsive disorder, which sits outside the family altogether. The communication disorders and the motor disorders shade into their neighbours too. The figure is a schematic of which conditions travel together, not a chart of how often; the figures belong to the sections on each disorder.

What do Indian clinicians actually see? No survey measures it, but the pattern is familiar in every child clinic. Children arrive late, because relatives reassure the parents that "he will talk when he is ready", that "boys are naughty" or that a dreamy girl is "just quiet". The first person consulted is often a paediatrician, a teacher, an elder in the family or a religious healer, and a referral to a psychologist frequently comes from the school only when marks begin to fall, or when a child cannot keep up in an English-medium classroom. Some families fear that a label will spoil a child's marriage prospects, or a sibling's, and some would rather hear "weakness" or "slow" than a diagnosis. And the causes that weigh more heavily in India than in richer countries (iodine deficiency, asphyxia at birth, infections of the brain, undernutrition and low birth weight) are among the most preventable (Section 3.3 teaches them). The public health system's main tool for finding these children early is the Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakram (RBSK) of 2013, which screens children for the "four Ds", defects at birth, diseases, deficiencies and developmental delays including disability, and refers them to District Early Intervention Centres (Book 2, Section 10.1.6).

Once a disorder is diagnosed, the law decides what the child is entitled to. Book 2 teaches the laws (Section 1.10.9) and the certification procedures (Section 9.9.8) in full; here is what you need for this chapter. The Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016 recognises 21 specified disabilities, and under its heading of intellectual disability it lists specific learning disabilities and autism spectrum disorder; its older predecessor, the Persons with Disabilities Act 1995, had recognised mental retardation but not autism. Most of the Act's entitlements, such as reserved posts, reserved college seats and free education from 6 to 18, go to people with benchmark disability, certified as not less than 40 percent of a specified disability. The National Trust Act 1999 covers exactly four conditions (autism, cerebral palsy, mental retardation and multiple disabilities) and lets a family obtain a legal guardian for an adult who cannot manage his own affairs through a local level committee. The Rehabilitation Council of India, a statutory body since the RCI Act of 1992, registers the professionals, clinical psychologists among them, who carry out the assessments. Certification follows official guidelines notified in 2024, which replaced those of 2018, and applications are now made through the national Unique Disability ID (UDID) portal. Each of the three disabilities of this chapter has its own method, set out in Table 3.5.

Table 3.5 How the neurodevelopmental disabilities are certified in India under the 2024 guidelines (from Book 2, Section 9.9.8).
DisabilityHow it is assessedPoints examiners ask
Autism spectrum disorderDiagnosis with the AIIMS-modified INDT-ASD; disability rated with ISAA (40 items, six domains, each rated 1 to 5)From age 6: mild 40 to 59, moderate 60 to 79, severe 80 percent or more; a child under 6 gets 60 to 79 percent on a temporary certificate and is reassessed between 6 and 7
Intellectual disabilityStandardised tests of intelligence and adaptive behaviour by a clinical psychologist on the medical boardThe VSMS social quotient fixes the percentage; a child under 5 is certified as having global developmental delay
Specific learning disabilitiesTeacher screening at 8 years or in Class III, whichever comes first, then educational and psychological assessmentNot certified before age 8; needs IQ 85 or more and attainment at least 3 SD below the current class on the NIMHANS battery, or below 40 percent on the GLAD
All threeCertificate states the disability and its percentage, permanent or temporaryBenchmark disability is 40 percent or more; IDEAS is for mental illness only, not for these disabilities
Exam trap

The Indian tool to assess and certify disability in autism is ISAA, the Indian Scale for Assessment of Autism. IHBAS, however, keyed CARS (the American Childhood Autism Rating Scale of Schopler and colleagues) both times it asked, in 2020 and 2024, so in an IHBAS paper expect CARS to be the marked answer, and in any other paper choose ISAA (Book 2, Section 9.9.8 gives both questions). Keep the other numbers straight: benchmark disability 40 percent; specific learning disability not before age 8; autism percentage from ISAA from age 6; intellectual disability under 5 certified as global developmental delay; the National Trust covers autism, cerebral palsy, mental retardation and multiple disabilities, but not specific learning disabilities or mental illness.

Scan Your Brain

  3. A child who passes through the usual sequence of development, only more slowly, shows developmental ; a child with an uneven profile and unusual behaviours shows developmental deviance.
  4. DSM-II (1968) called the forerunner of ADHD the hyperkinetic of childhood.
  5. In the WHO's ICD-10, the code F90.1 stands for hyperkinetic disorder.
  6. In ICD-11, Tourette syndrome (8A05.00) is classified in the chapter on diseases of the system.
  7. For ADHD, several symptoms must be present in two or more .
