Answers: (1) developmental, (2) function (functioning), (3) delay, (4) reaction, (5) conduct, (6) nervous, (7) settings, (8) quotient, (9) 40, (10) Indian Scale for Assessment of Autism (ISAA).

3.2 Intellectual Developmental Disorder: Definition, Criteria and SeverityMedium yield · 5 Qs

Of all the disorders in this chapter, intellectual disability is the one that people have recognised for longest, and the one whose boundary has moved most often. Every village has always known a child who learned to walk late, spoke little, could not keep up with the others at school and needed looking after long after his brothers and sisters had grown up and left. What has changed, again and again, is what that child was called, how he was measured, where the line between him and everyone else was drawn, and what was done for him. This section follows those changes and then sets out the modern definition: the three criteria of DSM-5-TR, the reason each is there, the levels of severity, the labels used when a child is too young or too hard to test, and the conditions that can be mistaken for intellectual disability. The causes have a section of their own (Section 3.3).

A condition that kept changing its name

For most of history the words were blunt. A person who never developed ordinary understanding was an idiot, from the Greek idiōtēs, a private person cut off from public life; a less severely affected person was an imbecile, from the Latin for weak; and in the English-speaking world of the late nineteenth century the mildest group, people who could talk and work but could not manage school or their own affairs, were feeble-minded. These were not insults when they were coined. They were technical terms for three grades, from the most to the least severe, and doctors, judges and teachers used them as calmly as a modern clinician says "moderate" or "mild". The trouble is that every technical term for people with low intelligence has, sooner or later, been taken up as a playground taunt, and once that happens it can no longer be used in a clinic. You will see this cycle repeat itself four or five times in what follows.

The first person to define the condition carefully was Jean-Étienne Dominique Esquirol (1772 to 1840), the favourite pupil of Philippe Pinel at the Salpêtrière in Paris. In his treatise Des maladies mentales of 1838, Esquirol complained that earlier writers had confused idiots with demented patients, and he drew the line that this chapter still uses. "Idiocy is not a disease," he wrote, "but a condition in which the intellectual faculties are never manifested; or have never been developed sufficiently to enable the idiot to acquire such an amount of knowledge, as persons of his own age, and placed in similar circumstances with himself, are capable of receiving." By contrast, dementia was the loss of a mind that had once been whole, and he put the difference in an image that examiners still like: "A man in a state of dementia is deprived of advantages which he formerly enjoyed; he was a rich man, who has become poor. The idiot, on the contrary, has always been in a state of want and misery." (The quotations are from the English translation of 1845.) Older nosologists had called the condition amentia, being without a mind, as opposed to dementia, being out of one's mind. Esquirol also split it into two grades, imbecility and idiocy proper, the first attempt at what we now call levels of severity.

Read Esquirol's definition again and two things stand out. First, it is developmental: the faculties never developed, so the condition begins with life or in early childhood. That is exactly the difference between the neurodevelopmental disorders of this chapter and the neurocognitive disorders of Chapter 10, in which abilities that were once acquired are lost. Second, it measures the person against "persons of his own age, and placed in similar circumstances", which is the germ of the modern rule that a person's functioning must be compared with that of age-mates from the same culture. What Esquirol lacked was any hope. "We can conceive of no possibility of changing this state," he wrote. His teacher Pinel had reached the same verdict about the wild boy of Aveyron, whom he judged an incurable "idiot" (see the opening of this chapter), and it took Itard's pupil Seguin to show that such children could be taught (Book 2, Section 10.5.8).

From the late nineteenth century the vocabulary moved into the law. In Britain the Idiots Act of 1886 separated idiots and imbeciles from lunatics, so that they could be cared for and trained apart from the insane, and the Mental Deficiency Act of 1913 recognised four classes (idiots, imbeciles, feeble-minded persons and "moral imbeciles") and allowed people certified as "mentally defective" to be detained indefinitely in special colonies. The general term on both sides of the Atlantic became mental deficiency, and it was the term of the first DSM in 1952. In the United States, Henry Goddard added the word "moron" in 1910 for the mildest grade and used it in the cause of eugenics (Book 1, Section 9.2.4).

From DSM-II (1968) to DSM-IV-TR (2000) the official term was mental retardation, and its definition was written largely by the American Association on Mental Deficiency (AAMD). Two of its revisions show how much the boundary is a decision rather than a discovery. The definition prepared by Rick Heber (1959, revised 1961) required three things for the first time: subaverage general intellectual functioning, impairment in adaptive behaviour (the practical and social skills of everyday life), and onset during the developmental period. Adaptive behaviour was put into the definition precisely so that an IQ score would not decide the matter on its own. But Heber set the intellectual line at one standard deviation below the mean, about IQ 85, with a "borderline" level between one and two standard deviations, so that in principle about one person in six fell below it. In 1973 the revision edited by Herbert Grossman required "significantly subaverage" functioning, two standard deviations below the mean, about IQ 70. Overnight, people with IQs between about 70 and 85 who had been "retarded" under the old definition were no longer retarded at all, although nothing about them had changed. The descendant of Heber's borderline level survives today, outside the disorder, as borderline intellectual functioning (Section 3.2.4).

The association's own name records the same history. It was founded in 1876, with Édouard Seguin among its founders, as the Association of Medical Officers of American Institutions for Idiotic and Feeble-minded Persons; it became the American Association for the Study of the Feebleminded in 1906, the AAMD in 1933, the American Association on Mental Retardation in 1987, and the American Association on Intellectual and Developmental Disabilities (AAIDD) in 2007. In 2010 the United States passed Rosa's Law (Public Law 111-256), named after Rosa Marcellino, a schoolgirl with Down syndrome in Maryland, which replaced "mental retardation" with "intellectual disability" throughout federal law. DSM-5 (2013) followed with the name intellectual disability (intellectual developmental disorder), and DSM-5-TR (2022) turned the two round to give intellectual developmental disorder (intellectual disability), so that its name matches ICD-11's disorders of intellectual development. ICD-11 describes these disorders as a "metasyndrome" of the developmental period analogous to dementia in later life, a phrase Esquirol would have understood at once. DSM-5-TR notes that the medical and research literature use both names, while "intellectual disability" is the term of teachers, advocacy groups and the public. This chapter uses the two interchangeably.

India travelled the same road. The Persons with Disabilities Act 1995 spoke of "mental retardation"; the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016 speaks of "intellectual disability" (Book 2, Section 1.10.9). The Mental Healthcare Act 2017, curiously, still uses the old phrase, but only to exclude it: its definition of mental illness "does not include mental retardation which is a condition of arrested or incomplete development of mind of a person, specially characterised by sub normality of intelligence". Alongside the change of name came a change of grammar. Clinicians now use person-first language, "a person with intellectual disability" rather than "a retarded person" or "a mental retard", because the person is not the condition.

Exam trap

Esquirol (1838) separated idiocy (the faculties never developed) from dementia (faculties once present, then lost): the rich man become poor is the demented person, not the idiot. Do not confuse Esquirol with Goddard, who coined "moron" (1910), or with Seguin, who taught such children. Heber's 1959 AAMD definition put the line at one SD below the mean (about 85); Grossman's 1973 revision moved it to two SD (about 70). Mind the British usage: in Britain "learning disability" means intellectual disability, whereas "learning difficulty" or "specific learning difficulty" usually means a problem such as dyslexia (Section 3.9). And under the Mental Healthcare Act 2017, intellectual disability is not a mental illness.

The three criteria, and why each is there

DSM-5-TR opens with a one-sentence summary: intellectual developmental disorder is a disorder with onset during the developmental period that includes both intellectual and adaptive functioning deficits in the conceptual, social and practical domains. Then it gives three criteria, and all three must be met. Each closes a door that would otherwise let the wrong people in, or keep the right people out, so it is worth taking them one at a time and asking what each is for.

Criterion A: a deficit in intellectual functions. The functions meant are the ones that intelligence tests sample: reasoning, problem solving, planning, abstract thinking, judgement, academic learning and learning from experience. The criterion insists that the deficit be confirmed by both clinical assessment and individualised, standardised intelligence testing. Neither is enough alone. A clinician's impression can be fooled by a shy, frightened or unfamiliar child, and a test score can be fooled by a bad day, a strange language or a poorly chosen test. Requiring both is a cross-check.

How low is low enough? DSM-5-TR answers in its text rather than in the criterion itself: people with the disorder have scores "approximately two standard deviations or more below the population mean, including a margin for measurement error (generally ± 5 points)". On the tests clinicians use, which are built to have a mean of 100 and a standard deviation (SD) of 15, two SD below the mean is 70, so the zone is 65 to 75 (70 plus or minus 5). Figure 3.4 shows what that means. Because intelligence test scores in the population follow the bell-shaped normal curve, only about 2.3 percent of people score below 70. Why a margin of 5 points? Because no test measures perfectly. Every score is an estimate of a person's true ability, and the standard error of measurement tells you how far the estimate is likely to stray. On a well-built test such as the Wechsler scales it is only 2 or 3 points, but a clinician wants to be fairly sure, so the band within which the true score lies with about 95 percent confidence runs roughly 5 points either side: a score of 70 means that the true score probably lies somewhere between 65 and 75. A child who scores 73 on Monday might score 68 on Friday without any change in his brain. Drawing a hard line at 70 would therefore label one child and not another when the test cannot tell them apart; the margin hands the decision back to criterion B and to clinical judgement.

Not just any test will do. DSM-5-TR asks for tests that are individually administered, psychometrically valid, comprehensive and culturally appropriate, and it spells out what can go wrong. Brief screening tests and group tests can give invalid scores. When a person's subtest scores are wildly uneven (very good at puzzles and very poor at vocabulary, say), the overall IQ averages things that do not belong together and may itself be invalid; a profile of strengths and weaknesses then tells you more than one number. Scores are inflated by practice effects, when a child is retested too soon on the same test and remembers it, and by the Flynn effect, the steady rise in average test performance over the decades, which means that a test with old norms compares today's child with a weaker generation and flatters him. The effect is not trivial at the line: in one study by Kanaya, Scullin and Ceci (2003), the number of people scoring just below 70 tripled when they were given a newly renormed version of the test. Above all, the test must be normed for the person's sociocultural background and native language. A child from a Kannada-medium village school tested in English on a test standardised in America is being tested on his English and his schooling, not on his intelligence, which is why Indian clinicians use Indian adaptations (Book 1, Section 9.3.6; the tests are listed in Table 3.4 of Section 3.1.5). Disorders that affect hearing, vision, movement or language can also pull scores down for reasons that have nothing to do with intellect.

And even a valid score is not the whole story. IQ tests sample conceptual reasoning well but sample poorly how a person reasons in real situations and copes with practical tasks. DSM-5-TR gives the example of a person whose IQ is somewhat above the 65 to 75 zone but whose adaptive problems in social judgement are so severe that his everyday functioning is like that of people with much lower scores; it concludes that using IQ tests "as the sole criteria for the diagnosis of an intellectual developmental disorder is insufficient". That sentence is the reason criterion B exists.

Criterion B: a deficit in adaptive functioning. By adaptive functioning DSM-5-TR means how well a person meets the community's standards of personal independence and social responsibility, compared with others of the same age and sociocultural background. DSM-5-TR groups it into three domains. The conceptual domain covers memory, language, reading, writing, mathematical reasoning, practical knowledge, problem solving and judgement in new situations. The social domain covers awareness of other people's thoughts, feelings and experiences, empathy, communication, the ability to make friends, and social judgement. The practical domain covers looking after oneself and managing life: personal care, job responsibilities, money, recreation, controlling one's own behaviour, and organising school and work tasks. The deficit must mean a failure to meet developmental and sociocultural standards, and without ongoing support it must limit functioning in one or more activities of daily life, such as communication, social participation and independent living, across several environments: home, school, work and community. The text adds the threshold: criterion B is met when at least one of the three domains is impaired badly enough that ongoing support is needed for the person to perform adequately. It does not require all three.

Why insist on adaptive deficits? Because the diagnosis exists to identify people who need help to live, and some people who score below 70 do not. A man who scores 66 because the test was in his second language, or because he never went to school, may run a tea stall, keep accounts in his head, raise a family and be respected in his village; calling him disabled would be a mistake of measurement, not a discovery. Criterion B is also the reason the diagnosis has to be made with the help of people who know the person. Adaptive behaviour is assessed by clinical evaluation together with individualised, culturally appropriate, standardised measures completed with knowledgeable informants (a parent or other family member, a teacher, a carer) and with the person himself as far as possible; in India the Vineland Social Maturity Scale is the usual instrument and the Vineland Adaptive Behavior Scales its modern successor (Book 1, Section 9.8.2; Table 3.4). DSM-5-TR warns that adaptive functioning is hard to judge in controlled settings such as prisons, where daily life is organised for you, so information about life outside should be sought.

Criterion C: onset during the developmental period. Both the intellectual and the adaptive deficits must be present during childhood or adolescence. DSM-IV-TR put a number on it, before age 18; DSM-5-TR does not, and the AAIDD extended its own limit to before age 22 in 2021 (Book 1, Section 9.8.1). The criterion is what keeps the disorder developmental. A 40-year-old engineer whose intellect is devastated by a stroke or by encephalitis has a neurocognitive disorder (Chapter 10), not intellectual disability, however low his scores. The criterion can be met by history: a woman first assessed at 30 can receive the diagnosis if school reports and her family's account show that the difficulties were there in childhood.

Push a case through all three. Ishaan is nine and in Class 4 of a Kannada-medium government school near Mandya. His teacher sends him to the district hospital because he "cannot read even simple words and does not follow anything". His mother says he walked at about 18 months, spoke his first words at two and a half, and was dry at night only at five. He can bathe and dress himself, though he needs reminding; he cannot tell the time; he cannot be sent to the shop because he cannot count the change; he plays happily with children of six; and last month he handed over his new bicycle to an older boy who asked for it. On an individually administered Indian adaptation of the Wechsler scale, given in Kannada, his full-scale IQ is 62, with no wild scatter between subtests, and the psychologist's observations agree. On the Vineland Social Maturity Scale, completed with his mother and his teacher, his social quotient is 58. Criterion A is met: a valid, culturally appropriate score in the right zone, confirmed clinically. Criterion B is met: deficits in the conceptual domain (reading, numbers, money, time), the social domain (gullibility, play with younger children) and the practical domain (shopping, needing reminders), at home and at school, needing ongoing support. Criterion C is met: the delays go back to infancy. The diagnosis is intellectual developmental disorder; Section 3.2.3 explains how its severity is graded.

Exam trap

"The cardinal DSM-IV-TR criterion for mental retardation was an IQ how far below the mean?" Two standard deviations, about 70 on a test with mean 100 and SD 15 (RML 2021). One SD (about 85) was Heber's old 1959 line and now marks the upper edge of borderline intellectual functioning; four SD (about 40) is the boundary between moderate and severe disability in ICD-11; seven SD would be a score of minus 5, which no test can produce. DSM-5-TR keeps the two-SD idea but adds a margin of about 5 points, giving 65 to 75, and it moves the number out of the criterion into the text.

Now take a different young man. Joel is 19 and lives in Kochi. He scraped through school with poor marks and failed his Class 12 examinations twice. On the WAIS-IV, given in Malayalam and English by a careful psychologist, his full-scale IQ is 70. But Joel works in his uncle's bakery, handles the cash counter without mistakes, takes two buses to get there, uses his phone for payments, plays football with his friends and helps look after his grandmother. Does he have intellectual disability? No. His score falls inside the 65 to 75 zone, so criterion A on its own could be satisfied, but criterion B is not: he meets the standards of independence and responsibility expected of a young man of his age and community without ongoing support. A score is not a diagnosis.

Exam trap

"On the WAIS-IV, an FSIQ of 70 typically corresponds to which classification?" The keyed answer is "borderline intellectual functioning; intellectual disability requires deficits in adaptive functioning" (MET Mock 2), and its point is right: an FSIQ near 70 without adaptive deficits is not intellectual disability. But keep three things apart. (1) On the WAIS-IV, 70 falls in the publisher's band labelled "borderline", 70 to 79 (Book 1, Section 9.3.4). (2) DSM-IV-TR's borderline intellectual functioning was IQ 71 to 84, so 70 is technically just below it. (3) A score of 70 lies inside DSM-5-TR's 65 to 75 zone, so it does not rule out intellectual disability; what decides is criterion B. Reject "average" because the WAIS-IV average band is 90 to 109, two SD higher; "mild intellectual disability solely on score" because no score diagnoses alone; and "profound" because profound lies below 20.

How severe: from IQ bands to adaptive functioning

Once the diagnosis is made, the next question a parent asks is "how bad is it?", and the honest answer has changed with the manuals. Book 1 (Section 9.8.1, Table 9.13) lists the four traditional levels, mild, moderate, severe and profound, with the IQ ranges that defined them in DSM-IV-TR and ICD-10 and the share of cases at each. Here the task is to go further: to see what each level meant in a person's life, why DSM-5 stopped defining the levels by IQ, and what it put in their place.

Under the old system the level was read off the IQ. ICD-10, which is still the classification behind most Indian hospital records, gives a band for each and adds a mental age: the age at which an average child performs as the adult does on the tests. An adult with mild disability, in ICD-10's words, has an IQ of about 50 to 69 and a mental age "from 9 to under 12 years"; moderate disability means an IQ of 35 to 49 and an adult mental age of 6 to under 9; severe, 20 to 34 and 3 to under 6; profound, below 20 and below 3. The mental age is a description of test performance, not of the person: a 30-year-old woman with moderate disability is not a child of seven, and she has thirty years of experience, interests, relationships and a body that a seven-year-old does not. Special education attached its own labels to the levels. Children with mild disability were called educable, because they could be expected to learn basic academic skills, and children with moderate disability trainable, because the expectation was self-care and simple work skills rather than academics; older texts called the severe and profound levels "custodial" or "dependent". All of these labels are now obsolete, but they survive in exam questions.

What did the levels mean for an adult? DSM-IV-TR's descriptions, summarised in Table 3.6, are the source of most textbook answers. People with mild disability, about 85 percent of all cases, usually look and talk much like other children in the preschool years and are often identified only when school begins to demand reading and arithmetic; by their late teens they can learn academic skills up to roughly the sixth-grade level, and as adults most achieve the social and vocational skills needed to support themselves, with some guidance at times of stress. People with moderate disability, about 10 percent, show obvious delays in the preschool years; they rarely progress beyond the second-grade level in academic subjects, but with teaching they learn to look after their own personal care, travel familiar routes and do unskilled or semi-skilled work under supervision, and most live in the community in supervised or family settings. That is what "partial independence in activities of daily living" means. People with severe disability, about 3 to 4 percent, acquire little or no communicative speech in early childhood; during the school years they may learn to talk, to look after themselves in elementary ways and to recognise a few "survival" words such as their name or a sign on a door, and as adults they can do simple tasks under close supervision. People with profound disability, about 1 to 2 percent, usually have an identifiable neurological or genetic condition, often with sensory and motor impairments, and they need help and supervision in every part of daily life.

Table 3.6 The four levels in the old, IQ-based system: ICD-10 ranges, adult mental age, share of cases and typical outcome.
LevelICD-10 IQAdult mental age (ICD-10)Share of casesOld school label; typical adult outcome
Mild50 to 699 to under 12 yearsAbout 85%"Educable"; often noticed only at school; reaches about Class 6 level by the late teens; usually self-supporting with training and some guidance
Moderate35 to 496 to under 9 yearsAbout 10%"Trainable"; delays plain before school; learns self-care with teaching; partial independence in daily living; supervised or supported work
Severe20 to 343 to under 6 yearsAbout 3 to 4%Older "custodial" or "dependent"; little speech in early childhood; basic self-care learned slowly; needs supervision at all times
ProfoundBelow 20Below 3 yearsAbout 1 to 2%Older "custodial"; usually a neurological or genetic cause with sensory and motor impairments; depends on others for all care

DSM-5 kept the four names but changed what they are based on. Its severity levels "are defined on the basis of adaptive functioning, and not IQ scores, because it is adaptive functioning that determines the level of supports required. Moreover, IQ measures are less valid in the lower end of the IQ range." Both reasons repay a moment's thought. The first is practical: a family, a school or a disability board wants to know what help a person needs, and two people with the same IQ can need very different amounts. Imagine two young men who both score 45. One travels to a sheltered workshop by bus, earns a little money packing paper bags and goes to the temple with his friends on Sundays; the other has frequent seizures, does not sleep and strikes out when frustrated, and cannot be left alone. By IQ they are identical; by adaptive functioning they are not, and it is the second description that tells you what to do. The second reason is technical. The commonly used intelligence scales have a floor: their lowest possible scores are around 40, and below that they cannot discriminate at all, so the distinction between severe and profound disability cannot honestly be made with a test score. ICD-11 says so explicitly: severe and profound disorders of intellectual development "are differentiated exclusively on the basis of adaptive behaviour", because standardised tests cannot reliably or validly distinguish among people functioning below about the 0.003rd percentile.

DSM-5-TR's severity table describes each level in each of the three domains, and Table 3.7 condenses it. Notice how the descriptions are written. There are no numbers; instead there are pictures of everyday life, from the mildly affected adult who works in a job that does not demand conceptual skills but needs help with banking, health decisions and raising a family, to the profoundly affected person who understands simple gestures, enjoys music and the company of familiar people, and depends on others for every aspect of physical care.

Table 3.7 DSM-5-TR's severity levels in the three domains of adaptive functioning (summarised in our own words).
LevelConceptualSocialPractical
MildFew obvious differences before school; then trouble learning reading, writing, arithmetic, time and money; adults are concrete thinkers and weak in planning and short-term memoryImmature with peers; misreads social cues; language more concrete than expected; poor judgement of social risk, so easily manipulated (gullible)Personal care age-appropriate; needs support with complex tasks (shopping, transport, cooking, banking, child care); often employed in jobs that do not stress conceptual skills
ModerateSkills lag markedly throughout; adults reach about elementary-school level and need support for all use of academic skills; others may take over daily conceptual tasksSpoken language much simpler than peers'; real friendships and sometimes romantic relationships; misreads cues; carers must help with life decisionsCan learn eating, dressing, toileting and hygiene, and all household tasks, but only after long teaching; employment possible with considerable support; maladaptive behaviour in a significant minority
SevereLittle understanding of written language, number, quantity, time or money; carers provide extensive support for problem solving throughout lifeVocabulary and grammar very limited; speech in single words or phrases, perhaps supplemented by augmentative means; family relationships a source of pleasureNeeds support for all daily activities and supervision at all times; cannot make responsible decisions about safety; self-injury in a significant minority
ProfoundThinks about the physical world rather than symbols; may match and sort objects; motor and sensory impairments may prevent use of objectsVery limited understanding of speech or gesture; expresses wishes through non-verbal, non-symbolic means; enjoys familiar peopleDependent on others for all physical care, health and safety; may help with simple tasks such as carrying dishes; enjoys music, walks and water with support

Return to Ishaan. At nine he needs support with reading, numbers, money and time, he is gullible with older children, and he needs reminders and help with tasks such as shopping, but he looks after his own body and joins in family life. That is the picture of the mild level in every domain, so his diagnosis is intellectual developmental disorder, mild. Notice that the level was chosen from his adaptive functioning; his IQ of 62 happens to agree, but it was not the deciding fact.

Two other systems grade severity differently, and both are examined. The AAIDD, as DSM-5-TR puts it, uses a classification that is "multidimensional rather than categorical". Instead of a severity label it describes a profile of the supports a person needs, and it grades their intensity as intermittent, limited, extensive or pervasive (Book 2, Section 10.5.8, explains each). So where DSM-5-TR might say "moderate intellectual disability", the AAIDD would describe "a person with intellectual disability who needs limited supports in home living, health and safety, and academic skills". ICD-11, for its part, keeps the four levels and defines them in standard deviations and percentiles: mild disorder of intellectual development lies about two to three SD below the mean (roughly the 0.1st to 2.3rd percentile), moderate about three to four SD below (roughly the 0.003rd to 0.1st percentile), and severe and profound four or more SD below, with severe and profound separated, as you have seen, by adaptive behaviour alone. Because standardised tests are simply not available in much of the world, ICD-11's diagnostic guidelines also supply tables of behavioural indicators, descriptions of what people at each level typically can and cannot do at different ages, which clinicians use when testing is impossible.

Exam trap

"Individuals who are moderately retarded..." The answer is that they can achieve partial independence in activities of daily living (Amity 2023). "Can become self-supporting adults with appropriate training" describes the mild level ("educable"). "Are generally institutionalised" is wrong for every level today: most people with intellectual disability, including most with severe disability, live with their families or in community settings. "Are usually diagnosed in infancy" fits the severe and profound levels, whose motor, language and social delays show in the first two years (DSM-5-TR); moderate disability usually becomes obvious in the preschool years, and mild disability often only at school. Remember too that in DSM-5 severity is set by adaptive functioning, while the IQ bands are ICD-10's and DSM-IV-TR's.

Too young, too hard to test, or not quite: global developmental delay, unspecified intellectual developmental disorder and borderline intellectual functioning

The three criteria assume that you can measure intelligence and adaptive behaviour. Sometimes you cannot, either because the child is too young for the tests to mean much, or because a disability gets in the way of testing. And sometimes the measurements come out just above the line. DSM-5-TR has a label for each situation, and examiners love to ask which is which.

Aditi is three and lives in Bhopal. She sat alone at 11 months and walked at 26 months; she says "ma" and "pa" but no other words; she does not point at things she wants; she cannot stack three blocks or feed herself with a spoon; and she plays with toys mostly by mouthing and banging them. Her hearing and thyroid tests are normal. On a developmental schedule her developmental quotient (DQ) is about 50 in every area. Does she have intellectual disability? Perhaps, but nobody can yet say so honestly, or say how severe it is. Intelligence tests for children of her age measure mainly sensory, motor and early language skills, and scores in the first years of life predict later IQ only loosely; children who are delayed at three because of prematurity, illness, deprivation or a hearing problem may catch up, while others will turn out to have autism, a language disorder or cerebral palsy rather than, or as well as, intellectual disability. So the clinician gives a provisional label: global developmental delay.

The label global developmental delay (F88) is reserved, in DSM-5-TR's words, for individuals under the age of 5 years "when the clinical severity level cannot be reliably assessed during early childhood". It is diagnosed when a child fails to meet expected developmental milestones in several areas of intellectual functioning and cannot undergo systematic assessment of intellectual functioning, including children who are simply too young for standardised testing, and it "requires reassessment after a period of time". The label is a promise to look again, not a verdict. DSM-5-TR adds, carefully, that some children under 5 whose presentation will eventually meet the criteria for intellectual developmental disorder have deficits that meet the criteria for global developmental delay; it does not say all, or even most. It also notes that because early and continuing intervention can improve functioning, it is common practice to delay the diagnosis of intellectual developmental disorder in infants and young children until a course of intervention has been tried. Aditi will be investigated for a cause (Section 3.3), given early stimulation through the district early intervention centre with her mother as her first teacher, and reassessed when she is old enough for standardised testing, at about six. In India the same logic runs through disability certification: a child under five is certified as having global developmental delay rather than intellectual disability (Book 2, Section 9.9.8).

The second label is for the opposite problem: a child old enough to test whom no available test can reach. The category of unspecified intellectual developmental disorder (F79) is reserved for individuals over the age of 5 years when assessing the degree of disability "by means of locally available procedures" is difficult or impossible because of associated sensory or physical impairments (blindness, prelingual deafness), locomotor disability, severe problem behaviour or a co-occurring mental disorder. Think of a twelve-year-old with severe cerebral palsy, no speech and poor vision, who cannot hold a pencil or point reliably to a picture. A clinician can see from his history and his everyday functioning that his understanding is very limited, but no Wechsler scale, Binet scale or performance test can be given to him in a way that would mean anything. DSM-5-TR says the category should be used "only in exceptional circumstances", because the first duty is to find a way of assessing (non-verbal tests, tests in sign language, eye-gaze responses, adaptive measures from informants), and it, too, requires reassessment. Notice the phrase "locally available": a child in a district hospital without a psychologist trained in non-verbal testing may need this label when the same child in a national institute would not.

ICD-11 merges the two situations into a single code, "disorder of intellectual development, provisional" (6A00.4), used when there is evidence of the disorder but the person is an infant or a child under the age of four, or when a valid assessment is not possible because of sensory or physical impairments, motor or communication impairments, severe problem behaviour or a co-occurring disorder. So DSM-5-TR's line falls at five and ICD-11's at four.

The third label is for people who are not quite inside the disorder, and borderline intellectual functioning is not a mental disorder at all. Section 1.8.7 explains its status: it is one of DSM-5-TR's "other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention", coded R41.83, used when a person's borderline intellectual level is itself the focus of attention or affects treatment or prognosis. DSM-IV-TR defined it by an IQ of 71 to 84; DSM-5 dropped the number and asks instead for a careful assessment of intellectual and adaptive functioning and the gap between them. In clinical practice in India the person with borderline intellectual functioning is very often the child whom schools call the slow learner: a boy or girl with an IQ in the 70s or low 80s who is a little behind in every subject, not dramatically behind in one, who learns if taught slowly and repeatedly, and who falls further behind each year as the syllabus speeds up. Such a child does not have intellectual disability, because his adaptive functioning is broadly adequate, and he does not have a specific learning disorder either, because his difficulty is general rather than confined to reading, writing or arithmetic (Section 3.9.6 makes the comparison from the other side; Indian certification of a specific learning disability requires an IQ of 85 or more, Book 2, Section 9.9.8). He falls between the categories, and he needs what neither label supplies: patient teaching at his own pace, a realistic choice of subjects and courses, and protection from being called lazy. Table 3.8 sets the four labels side by side.

Table 3.8 Global developmental delay, intellectual developmental disorder, unspecified intellectual developmental disorder and borderline intellectual functioning compared.
Label (code)AgeWhat is knownWhy the label is usedReassess?
Global developmental delay (F88)Under 5Missed milestones in several areas; severity cannot be reliably assessedToo young (or unable) for systematic testing; outcome uncertainYes, required
Intellectual developmental disorder, mild to profound (F70 to F73)Any age; onset in developmentCriteria A, B and C met; level set by adaptive functioningTestable person who meets all three criteriaLevel may change; review as needed
Unspecified intellectual developmental disorder (F79)Over 5Disability evident, degree unmeasurable locallyBlindness, prelingual deafness, locomotor disability, severe problem behaviour or another disorder block testing; exceptional use onlyYes, required

Exam trap

"A key differentiation between global developmental delay and intellectual disability is..." the age of assessment: under 5 years for global developmental delay, 5 and over for intellectual disability (JEMAS 2025). The age matters because it stands for testability: below five, severity cannot be reliably measured. The distractors name things both conditions can have: social deficits, language delay and motor impairment all occur in global developmental delay and in intellectual disability, so none of them separates the two. Do not confuse DSM-5-TR's under 5 with ICD-11's under 4 for the provisional category.

Exam trap

"In which situation would a diagnosis of global developmental delay be inappropriate?" Answer: the 7-year-old boy with a full-scale IQ of 65 and severe impairment in adaptive functioning (RML 2022). He is over five, he has been tested validly, his score lies in the 65 to 75 zone and his adaptive deficits are established: he meets criteria A, B and C, so the diagnosis is intellectual developmental disorder, and a provisional label would be wrong. Each of the other options describes a situation in which a young child's picture is incomplete, which is exactly what global developmental delay is for: a child too young to show the full picture or to complete the assessments (the textbook case); a child whose test scores point to intellectual disability but whose adaptive skills have not yet been established; and a child whose adaptive functioning points to it but whose cognitive level has not been measured with standardised instruments. In each, the clinician holds back the full diagnosis until reassessment.

Who has intellectual disability, and what becomes of them

How common is intellectual disability? DSM-5-TR gives an overall figure of about 10 per 1,000, that is, about 1 percent of the population, but it varies with a country's level of development: about 16 per 1,000 in middle-income countries and about 9 per 1,000 in high-income ones. That difference is itself a clue to the causes. Many causes of intellectual disability are preventable (iodine deficiency, birth asphyxia, infections, malnutrition, lead), and they weigh most heavily where health care is weakest, which is why Section 3.3 spends so long on prevention. DSM-5-TR makes the same point with a striking comparison from Western Australia, where the prevalence among Aboriginal children was 39 per 1,000 against 16 per 1,000 among the more affluent non-Aboriginal children, and it attributes such differences to environmental risks, such as perinatal injury and chronic social deprivation, that travel with poverty and poor access to care. Within the United States, by contrast, prevalence does not vary significantly between ethnoracial groups.

Prevalence is also higher in young people than in adults. Part of the reason is that the severe forms shorten life, but a larger part is that mild disability is often most visible at school. A girl who cannot keep up with the syllabus stands out in Class 5; at 25, cooking, caring for children or working on a family farm, she may need some help with money and paperwork but never come near a clinic again. Indian counts show the other side of the problem, which is under-recognition. The Census of 2011 recorded only about 15 lakh people with "mental retardation" among the roughly 2.68 crore people it counted with any disability, far fewer than a prevalence of 1 percent would predict in a country of more than a hundred crore. Census figures depend on families reporting a disability to an enumerator, and DSM-5-TR notes that where intellectual disability is explained as a punishment for the parents' wrongdoing or as a supernatural affliction, shame leads to under-reporting. The best Indian survey of children, the INCLEN study, is described in Section 3.1.6.

More males than females are diagnosed, at both the mild level (on average about 1.6 to 1) and the severe level (about 1.2 to 1), although the ratios vary widely between studies. DSM-5-TR offers three possible reasons: genes on the X chromosome (a boy has only one X, so a faulty X-linked gene such as the one behind fragile X syndrome is not masked by a second copy, Section 3.3.3), sex differences in other genetic factors such as particular copy number variants, and a general male vulnerability to brain injury.

What is the course? Onset is in the developmental period, but the age and the features at onset depend on the cause and the severity. In the more severe forms, delayed motor, language and social milestones can usually be seen in the first two years; mild disability may not be recognised until school age, when academic learning becomes difficult. When the cause is a genetic syndrome there may be a characteristic appearance, as in Down syndrome, and sometimes a characteristic pattern of behaviour (Section 3.3.1). When the cause is acquired, as after meningitis, encephalitis or a head injury in childhood, the onset may be abrupt, and if the child has lost skills he once had, both intellectual developmental disorder and a neurocognitive disorder may be diagnosed. The disorder is generally non-progressive: the brain does not keep deteriorating. There are exceptions among the genetic conditions. In Rett syndrome (Section 3.5.2) there are periods of worsening followed by stabilisation; in others, such as Sanfilippo syndrome (Section 3.3.4) and Down syndrome, intellectual functioning worsens progressively in varying degrees. In Down syndrome the decline often represents a neurocognitive disorder due to Alzheimer's disease developing in adult life (Section 3.3.2), and then both diagnoses are given.

The disorder is generally lifelong, although its severity level may change over time, and the course is shaped by medical and genetic conditions and by co-occurring problems such as hearing or visual impairment and epilepsy. Here DSM-5-TR strikes a hopeful note. Early and continuing intervention can improve adaptive functioning through childhood and adulthood, and in some cases it improves intellectual functioning so much that the diagnosis no longer fits, which is one more reason for delaying the diagnosis in very young children. For older children and adults the manual draws a careful distinction. If a person's improved adaptive skills reflect stable, generalised new learning, the diagnosis may no longer be appropriate; if the improvement depends on supports and interventions continuing, the diagnosis still stands. A young man who manages his job only because a job coach visits every morning still has intellectual disability; the coach is part of the reason he is doing well.

Several features of adult life deserve a clinician's attention. People with intellectual disability have more physical health problems than the general population, obesity among them, and many cannot put into words what they feel, so illnesses go undiagnosed and untreated. Because they are less aware of risk and danger, their rates of accidental injury are raised. A naive trust in others and a tendency to be easily led, called gullibility, is common, and together with a poor sense of risk it exposes them to exploitation, fraud, physical and sexual abuse, unintended involvement in crime and false confessions. These features matter in courts; DSM-5-TR mentions the American "Atkins-type hearings" that follow the Supreme Court's ruling in Atkins v. Virginia (2002) that people with intellectual disability may not be executed. People with intellectual disability can also be distressed by their own limitations, and they are at risk of suicide. The risk is associated with a co-occurring mental disorder, with higher intellectual and adaptive functioning (probably because such people understand their situation and its losses more fully) and with recent stressful events, and because a co-occurring mental disorder may show itself in unusual ways, any change in a person's behaviour should prompt a question about suicidal thoughts.

Remember

Prevalence about 1 percent (10 per 1,000; 16 in middle-income and 9 in high-income countries); more males (1.6 to 1 mild, 1.2 to 1 severe); severe forms show in the first two years, mild forms at school; generally non-progressive and lifelong, with Rett syndrome, Sanfilippo syndrome and Down syndrome as the exceptions that can worsen; suicide risk is linked to co-occurring mental disorder, recent stressors and, perhaps surprisingly, higher intellectual and adaptive functioning.

Co-occurring disorders and the differential diagnosis

People with intellectual disability have, on average, more of almost everything else. DSM-5-TR estimates that some co-occurring conditions, mental disorders, cerebral palsy and epilepsy among them, are three to four times commoner than in the general population. The most frequent co-occurring mental and neurodevelopmental disorders it lists are attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder; depressive and bipolar disorders; anxiety disorders; autism spectrum disorder; stereotypic movement disorder, with or without self-injury (Section 3.10.2); impulse-control disorders; and major neurocognitive disorder. Major depressive disorder can occur at every level of severity, even in people who cannot say that they are sad; it then shows itself in withdrawal, loss of interest in favourite activities, tearfulness, poor sleep, weight loss or new aggression. In DSM-5-TR's words, self-injurious behaviour, such as head banging, biting the hands or picking at the skin, "requires prompt diagnostic attention" and may warrant a separate diagnosis of stereotypic movement disorder. People with more severe disability may also show aggression and disruptive behaviour, including harm to others and to property, and it is often the behaviour, rather than the disability, that brings them to a psychiatrist.

Recognising these disorders is hard for two reasons. The first is communication: a person with limited speech cannot describe a low mood, a voice, a fear or a toothache, so DSM-5-TR insists that knowledgeable informants are essential for identifying irritability, mood swings, aggression, eating problems and sleep problems. The second is a habit of mind in the people around them. In 1982 Steven Reiss and his colleagues called it diagnostic overshadowing: once a person carries the label of intellectual disability, every new symptom tends to be put down to the disability itself (Book 2, Section 10.5.8). Saanvi is 24, has moderate intellectual disability and lives with her parents in Pune. Over a fortnight she begins to scream at night, to slap the side of her face and to refuse her food. Her family, and at first the doctor at the local clinic, say that "it is her condition; she gets like this". Only when her sister notices a swelling of her cheek is she taken to a dentist, who finds an abscess at the root of a molar. Once the tooth is treated, the screaming and slapping stop. A sudden change in behaviour in a person with intellectual disability is a message, and the clinician's job is to work out what it says: pain, physical illness, side effects of a medicine, abuse, a change of carer or routine, depression, or psychosis.

The differential diagnosis begins with a rule that sounds obvious but is often broken: intellectual developmental disorder should be diagnosed whenever criteria A, B and C are met, and it "should not be assumed because of a particular genetic or medical condition". A child with Down syndrome or cerebral palsy must still be assessed; many children with cerebral palsy, for instance, have normal intelligence, and some children with Down syndrome, especially the mosaic form (Section 3.3.2), score well above the usual range. When a genetic syndrome is present it is recorded as a separate, concurrent diagnosis. The main look-alikes then follow, and Table 3.9 sums them up.

  • Major and mild neurocognitive disorders are losses of cognitive functioning; intellectual disability is a failure to develop it (Esquirol's rich man made poor, versus the man who was always poor). The two can coexist: a man with Down syndrome who develops Alzheimer's disease, or a woman with intellectual disability who loses further capacity after a head injury, receives both diagnoses. When a brain injury in childhood leaves a child with stable, non-progressive deficits that meet the criteria, both diagnoses can again be used.
  • Communication disorders and specific learning disorder are confined to one domain (language, speech, reading, writing or mathematics), with no general intellectual and adaptive deficit. A child who cannot read but reasons well, plays cricket strategically and runs errands competently is not intellectually disabled. If someone meets the full criteria for both, both are diagnosed (Sections 3.4 and 3.9).
  • Autism spectrum disorder commonly co-occurs with intellectual disability, and testing a child with autism is difficult: his social and communication difficulties may stop him understanding or complying with test procedures, so a low score may underestimate what he can do. IQ in autism can be unstable in early childhood, and DSM-5-TR asks for reassessment across development (Sections 3.5 and 3.6).
  • Borderline intellectual functioning lies above the intellectual criterion and lacks the degree of adaptive deficit the disorder requires (Section 3.2.4).
  • Sensory impairment: a child with an undetected hearing loss or poor vision may seem slow, inattentive and unresponsive. Hearing and vision should be tested before any judgement about intelligence, and tests chosen to bypass the impaired sense.
  • Severe deprivation and lack of schooling: a child who has been neglected, never taught, or kept out of school may perform at a low level that improves, sometimes dramatically, with care and teaching. The wild boy of Aveyron (the opening of this chapter) is the oldest case on record of the argument over whether a child's state was disability or deprivation; Section 3.3.6 describes deprivation as a cause in its own right.
  • The wrong test: a child tested in a language he does not speak, or against norms from another culture, may score low for reasons that have nothing to do with ability (Section 3.2.2).
Table 3.9 Intellectual developmental disorder and its look-alikes.
Look-alikeThe feature that separates itCan both be diagnosed?Where taught
Major or mild neurocognitive disorderLoss of abilities once acquired, versus failure to develop themYes (Down syndrome with Alzheimer's disease; head injury)Chapter 10
Communication disorderDeficit confined to language or speech; non-verbal reasoning and adaptive skills adequateYes, if both sets of criteria are metSection 3.4
Specific learning disorderDeficit confined to reading, writing or mathematics; general intelligence usually averageYes, if both are metSection 3.9
Autism spectrum disorderSocial communication and restricted, repetitive behaviour are central; IQ may be normal or underestimatedYes, oftenSections 3.5 and 3.6
