Exam trap

A genetic syndrome does not diagnose intellectual disability: the criteria must be met in each person, and the syndrome is coded alongside. Intellectual disability and a neurocognitive disorder can both be given (Down syndrome with Alzheimer's disease is the classic example). Intellectual disability, communication disorders and specific learning disorder are not mutually exclusive either. And "behaviour problems are part of her condition" is the error called diagnostic overshadowing (Reiss, 1982), not a diagnosis.

Care, support and medicines

There is no cure for intellectual disability, but there is a great deal of help, and almost all of it is educational and social rather than medical. Book 2 (Section 10.5.8) teaches the methods in full, so here they are only named, with what the evidence shows. The guiding idea since the 1960s has been normalisation, formulated by Bengt Nirje (1969) and spread by Wolf Wolfensberger (1972): people with intellectual disability should live lives as close as possible to everyone else's, in ordinary homes, schools and workplaces rather than institutions. The core clinical method is adaptive skills training: a skill such as brushing teeth or buying milk is broken down by task analysis, taught step by step by chaining, prompting and fading, and reinforced (Book 2, Sections 4.5.2 to 4.5.4), and challenging behaviour is reduced by first working out what the behaviour achieves for the person, through functional analysis, and then teaching a better way of achieving it, which is the basis of positive behaviour support (Book 2, Sections 2.8.2 and 4.6). Education has moved from special schools through integrated education to inclusive education, international policy since UNESCO's Salamanca Statement of 1994 and Indian law under the RPwD Act 2016, with an individualised education programme for each child. The evidence for this whole approach is consistent on three points. Even people with severe and profound disability can learn useful skills when teaching is systematic. Skills learned in a clinic often do not transfer by themselves, so they must be taught and practised in the real settings where they are needed. And early, sustained, family-centred intervention improves adaptive functioning, which is exactly what DSM-5-TR's account of the course would lead you to expect.

Where do medicines fit? The first point is negative and must be made clearly, because families in India are often sold "brain tonics": no medicine improves intellectual functioning itself. The claim has been tested. Piracetam, a supposed cognitive enhancer that was widely given to children with Down syndrome, was tested by Lobaugh and colleagues in Toronto in a randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled crossover trial published in 2001: four months of piracetam did not improve cognition or behaviour in children aged about 6 to 13, and seven children had stimulant side effects, among them aggressiveness, agitation, sexual arousal and poor sleep. The only "medical" treatments that prevent intellectual disability work on particular causes before the damage is done: a low-phenylalanine diet for phenylketonuria and thyroxine for congenital hypothyroidism, which Section 3.3.4 describes.

The second point is that co-occurring mental and neurological disorders should be treated, and treated with the usual medicines: antidepressants for depression and anxiety (Section 5.7.2), stimulants for ADHD (Section 3.8), antiepileptic drugs for epilepsy, antipsychotics for psychosis (Section 4.10). Three cautions apply. Doses usually start lower and rise more slowly, because people with brain damage are often more sensitive to side effects. Side effects must be watched for actively, because the person may not be able to report a dry mouth, constipation, stiffness, dizziness or a feeling of restlessness, and may show them only as a change in behaviour. And the diagnosis must be real: a medicine given for "depression" that is really toothache, or for "aggression" that is really fear of a new carer, treats nothing.

The third point is the controversial one. Antipsychotic drugs, especially risperidone, are widely prescribed to people with intellectual disability who have no psychotic illness at all, to calm aggression, self-injury and other challenging behaviour. Risperidone has good evidence for reducing severe irritability in children with autism (Section 3.6.8), and that evidence is often stretched to cover adults with intellectual disability. The harms are well known: weight gain and metabolic changes leading towards diabetes, sedation, raised prolactin, and extrapyramidal side effects such as stiffness and restlessness (Section 4.10.5). The question is whether the benefits are real.

Key study: Tyrer and colleagues' trial of antipsychotics for aggressive challenging behaviour (2008)

Question. Do antipsychotics reduce aggression in adults with intellectual disability who are not psychotic? Method. Peter Tyrer and his colleagues randomly assigned 86 such adults, recruited from ten centres in England and Wales and one in Queensland, Australia, to flexible doses of haloperidol (a first-generation antipsychotic, 28 people), risperidone (a second-generation antipsychotic, 29) or placebo (29), and measured aggression, other problem behaviour, quality of life, side effects, carers' burden and costs at 4, 12 and 26 weeks. Result. Aggression fell substantially in all three groups by four weeks, and it fell most in the placebo group: a median fall of 79 percent from baseline with placebo, against 58 percent with risperidone and 65 percent with haloperidol. At no point did the placebo group do worse than either drug group. Conclusion. The authors wrote, in The Lancet, that antipsychotic drugs "should no longer be regarded as an acceptable routine treatment for aggressive challenging behaviour in people with intellectual disability". Criticism. The trial was small, so it could have missed a modest benefit of the drugs, and the large fall in every group shows how much of the improvement in such studies comes from attention, monitoring and the natural ups and downs of behaviour rather than from any drug. That is precisely the lesson: without a placebo group, every one of those falls would have been credited to the medicine.

The same worry led to action. In 2015 Public Health England estimated that up to 35,000 adults with a learning disability (the British term for intellectual disability) were being prescribed an antipsychotic, an antidepressant or both without an appropriate clinical reason, and in 2016 NHS England launched STOMP, "stopping over-medication of people with a learning disability, autism or both", with a pledge signed by the professional bodies of psychiatrists, nurses, general practitioners, pharmacists and psychologists. The sensible practice that follows from all this is simple to state. Look first for a cause of the behaviour (pain, illness, a side effect, abuse, a change in routine, a mental disorder) and for what the behaviour achieves; use positive behaviour support and communication training; and if a drug is used for behaviour, use it for a limited time, at the lowest dose, with a clear target, and review it regularly with a plan to withdraw it.

Clinical link

What does a clinical psychologist actually do for a child like Ishaan in an Indian district early intervention centre or a NIEPID clinic? Assessment comes first: a developmental history, a culturally appropriate intelligence test and an adaptive behaviour scale with the parents and teacher, which together give the diagnosis, the level and, where the family wants it, the disability certificate (Book 2, Section 9.9.8). Then the parents need someone to explain, plainly and kindly, what the diagnosis means and what it does not mean, what is known about the cause (Section 3.3) and what the future may hold, and to listen to their grief, which often returns at each stage of life. Then comes the practical work: a home programme of early stimulation or skills training, advice to the school on placement and on an individualised education programme, screening for co-occurring problems such as epilepsy, hearing loss, ADHD and depression, and information about rights and schemes under the RPwD Act and the National Trust Act (Book 2, Sections 1.10.9 and 10.5.8). Families are also warned, gently, against costly "cures".

3.3 The Causes of Intellectual Disability: Genes, Pregnancy, Birth and ChildhoodMedium yield · 2 Qs

"Why did this happen to our child?" is usually the parents' first question and sometimes their only one. It deserves a careful answer, for three reasons. A cause may be treatable or preventable in the next child; it may predict medical problems that should be looked for, such as heart disease in Down syndrome or obesity in Prader-Willi syndrome; and it may lift a burden of blame that parents, grandparents and neighbours have placed on the mother. Barlow and Durand remark that there are literally hundreds of known causes. This section first shows how they are sorted and how often one is found, then describes the genetic syndromes that every examiner asks about, the inborn errors of metabolism with phenylketonuria as the great success story, the damage done in the womb by alcohol, infections and iodine deficiency, and finally the injuries of birth and childhood, ending with what can be prevented.

How causes are sorted

The simplest way to sort causes is by when they act, and DSM-5-TR does exactly this. The prenatal causes act before birth: genetic syndromes, whether from a change in the sequence of one or more genes, a copy number variant (a stretch of chromosome deleted or duplicated) or a disorder of a whole chromosome; inborn errors of metabolism; malformations of the brain; maternal disease, including disease of the placenta; and environmental influences such as alcohol, other drugs, toxins and teratogens. The perinatal causes are events around labour and delivery that lead to neonatal encephalopathy, a damaged and malfunctioning brain in the newborn. The postnatal causes act after birth: injury from lack of oxygen and blood supply, traumatic brain injury, infections, demyelinating disorders, seizure disorders such as infantile spasms, severe and chronic social deprivation, and toxic and metabolic syndromes and poisonings such as lead and mercury. Figure 3.5 lays these out along the course of development, with the examples taught in the rest of this section.

A second way of sorting causes goes back to Edward Zigler (1930 to 2015), the Yale psychologist who also helped to found the American Head Start programme. In a 1967 paper in Science, "Familial mental retardation: a continuing dilemma", Zigler argued that clinicians would bring order to a confused field if, instead of treating everyone below an IQ cut-off as one group, they distinguished two (Book 1, Section 9.8.3, gives the idea in a sentence). The first, the organic group, has an identifiable physical cause, a chromosome, a gene, an infection, an injury, and is usually more severely affected, often with physical signs. The second and larger group, which he called the familial group and later writers the cultural-familial group, has no identifiable cause, is usually mildly affected, often has parents and brothers and sisters who also score low, and is concentrated in poor families. Zigler's bold claim was that the familial person is not abnormal at all: intelligence, like height, is shaped by many genes of small effect and by the environment, so it is normally distributed, and somebody must occupy its lower tail. The familial person, he argued, is less intelligent than average but not abnormal, as much a part of the normal distribution as the people at its upper end, developing more slowly and less far than the average person. This is the two-group approach, which Zigler and Robert Hodapp developed in their book Understanding Mental Retardation (1986).

The two-group idea explains a fact about the shape of the IQ distribution. If intelligence were perfectly normal, only about 4 people in 10,000 would score below 50, more than three SD below the mean. But with intellectual disability affecting about 1 percent of the population and roughly 15 percent of cases lying at the moderate level or below (Section 3.2.3), the real figure is nearer 15 in 10,000, several times what the curve predicts. The excess is the organic group: a small second distribution of people whose intelligence has been pulled down by an accident of biology, sitting under the low tail of the main curve. It also explains a pattern you will meet in every clinic. When the cause is a de novo accident, such as an extra chromosome or a new mutation, the family is usually of average ability and can come from any walk of life; when there is no identifiable cause, the disability is usually mild and often runs, in a lesser degree, through the family.

Key study: is severe intellectual disability a different thing? (Reichenberg and colleagues, 2016)

Question. Zigler's two-group idea was a hypothesis that had been argued over for almost a century. Is mild intellectual disability really just the low end of the normal range, and severe disability something else? Method. Abraham Reichenberg, Robert Plomin, Paul Lichtenstein and their colleagues used very large data sets to study more than 1,000,000 pairs of brothers and sisters and about 9,000 pairs of twins whose IQ and intellectual disability had been recorded. The logic is a family one: if mild disability is simply the low tail of the normal curve, the factors that make people differ in IQ in the normal range, shared genes and shared family environment, should also make a person's siblings score low; if severe disability is a separate accident, they should not. Result. The factors influencing mild intellectual disability (the lowest 3 percent of the IQ distribution) were similar to those influencing IQ in the normal range, whereas the factors influencing severe disability (the lowest 0.5 percent) were different. Conclusion. Most severe intellectual disability is, in the authors' words, "a distinct condition, qualitatively different" from the bulk of intellectual disability, which "represents the low extreme of the normal distribution of intelligence". The study, published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, is a modern confirmation of Zigler's two groups.

How often is a cause actually found? Less often than parents hope. Estimates vary with the people studied and the tests used: Barlow and Durand note that most cases have no identified cause, and even with modern testing a cause is found in perhaps half of all cases. It is found far more often in severe than in mild disability, and in mild disability without physical signs it is usually not found at all. DSM-5-TR describes the full evaluation: a pre- and perinatal history, a three-generation family tree (pedigree), a physical examination for dysmorphic features, genetic testing (a karyotype, or more often now a chromosomal microarray, and tests for particular syndromes), metabolic screening and brain imaging. Genetic technology has changed the yield. A chromosomal microarray, which scans the whole genome for small deletions and duplications too tiny to see under the microscope, finds a cause in about 15 to 20 percent of people with unexplained developmental delay or intellectual disability, against about 3 percent for an ordinary karyotype (David Miller and colleagues' consensus statement, 2010). The newer method of exome sequencing, which reads the protein-coding part of every gene, did better still in a 2019 meta-analysis by Srivastava and colleagues, with a yield of about 36 percent overall. Many of the mutations found are de novo mutations: new changes that arose in the egg, the sperm or the early embryo and are present in neither parent, which is why a severe genetic disability can appear in a family with no history of it. Ishaan, from Section 3.2, illustrates the other pole. His examination showed no unusual features, his hearing, thyroid tests and microarray were normal, his growth and head size were ordinary, and both his parents had left school early and struggled with reading; his elder sister was also behind at school. No organic cause was found, and his picture is the cultural-familial one.

Finally, some genetic syndromes come with a recognisable pattern of behaviour as well as a recognisable face. DSM-5-TR calls this a behavioural phenotype: specific behaviours that are characteristic of a particular genetic disorder. Its own example is Lesch-Nyhan syndrome, whose sufferers bite their own lips and fingers (Section 3.3.3); others are the insatiable hunger of Prader-Willi syndrome, the exceptional sociability of Williams syndrome, the frequent laughter of Angelman syndrome and the gaze aversion and social anxiety of fragile X syndrome. Behavioural phenotypes matter for two reasons. Clinically, a behaviour can be the first clue to the diagnosis. Scientifically, they show that genes influence specific psychological functions, not just general intelligence: two children with the same IQ but different syndromes can be very different people.

Exam trap

Zigler's two-group approach (1967): organic (identifiable cause, usually more severe, any social class) versus cultural-familial (no identified cause, usually mild, low end of the normal curve, often with poverty and with relatives who also score low). The organic group explains the excess of very low IQs that a normal curve does not predict. DSM-5-TR's timing groups are prenatal, perinatal and postnatal; severe and chronic social deprivation is listed as a postnatal cause. DSM-5-TR's own example of a behavioural phenotype is Lesch-Nyhan syndrome.

Down syndrome

In 1866 John Langdon Down (1828 to 1896), the medical superintendent of an asylum for "idiots" at Earlswood in Surrey, published a short paper called "Observations on an ethnic classification of idiots". Following the race theories of his day, he tried to sort his patients by their resemblance to supposed racial types, and he described one group, with flat faces and slanting eyes, as the "Mongolian" type. The racial theory was nonsense, but the clinical description was accurate, and the condition carried the name "mongolism" for a century. In 1961 nineteen scientists wrote publicly that the term had misleading connotations, and in 1965 the World Health Organization dropped it after a request from the delegation of the Mongolian People's Republic. The condition is now called Down syndrome, and it is the commonest chromosomal cause of intellectual disability, occurring in roughly 1 in 1,000 births worldwide.

Its cause was found in Paris less than a century after Down's paper. Human cells were shown to have 46 chromosomes only in 1956. In the laboratory of Raymond Turpin at the Hôpital Trousseau, a young physician, Marthe Gautier (1925 to 2022), grew cells from children with Down syndrome and in 1958 counted 47 chromosomes in them. Her colleague Jérôme Lejeune (1926 to 1994) had the slides photographed, and in 1959 the three reported the extra small chromosome, with Lejeune's name first and Gautier's misspelt "Gauthier". Lejeune received most of the credit for decades; Gautier said late in life that her part had been played down, and in 2014 the French Federation of Human Genetics gave her its Grand Prize. The extra chromosome is a third copy of chromosome 21, which is why the condition is also called trisomy 21.

There are three ways of getting the extra material, and they matter for genetic counselling. In about 95 percent of cases there is a whole extra chromosome in every cell, produced by nondisjunction, a failure of the pair of chromosome 21s to separate when the egg (or, less often, the sperm) is formed, so that it carries two copies instead of one. In about 2 to 4 percent the extra chromosome 21 is attached to another chromosome, a Robertsonian translocation; this is the only form that can be inherited from a healthy parent who carries a balanced version of the translocation, so the parents of a child with translocation Down syndrome should have their own chromosomes checked. In about 1 to 2 percent the error happens after conception, so that only some of the body's cells are trisomic; children with this mosaicism are, on average, less severely affected. The risk of nondisjunction rises with the mother's age. By the usual estimates, a woman of 20 has a risk of about 1 in 1,400 per birth, a woman of 30 about 1 in 1,000, and a woman of 40 about 1 in 85. Yet most babies with Down syndrome are born to mothers aged 35 or younger, simply because younger women have many more babies. Prenatal screening and diagnosis are described in the Developmental book.

The physical features are recognisable at birth: a flat facial profile and a flat bridge to the nose, eyes that slant upwards with folds of skin at their inner corners (epicanthic folds), a small mouth from which the tongue tends to protrude, small ears, short broad hands often with a single crease across the palm (in about half), low muscle tone (in most newborns) and, later, short stature. The medical problems are what the family must be warned about. About 40 percent of babies have a congenital heart defect; thyroid problems, especially hypothyroidism, are common throughout life; most children have some hearing problem, often from fluid in the middle ear; vision problems are common; and leukaemia is many times commoner in childhood than in other children. Each of these, if missed, adds to the disability: an untreated hearing loss or an underactive thyroid makes any child slower.

The intellectual disability is usually mild to moderate, and the profile is uneven. Children with Down syndrome are often relatively good at social understanding, at learning from pictures and at visual memory, and relatively weak in expressive language, grammar and verbal short-term memory, so they usually understand more than they can say. This is why sign and picture supports help them, and why the cheerful, affectionate stereotype, though it fits many children, does not describe a behavioural phenotype that every child shares.

The most important fact about adult life in Down syndrome comes from the chromosome itself. The gene for the amyloid precursor protein, the protein from which the amyloid deposits of Alzheimer's disease are made, lies on chromosome 21, and a person with trisomy 21 has three copies of it. Adults with Down syndrome therefore develop the brain changes of Alzheimer's disease early, and dementia often begins in their forties or fifties, decades earlier than in the general population; about half or more of those who live to 60 are affected. This is the situation DSM-5-TR describes in which progressive worsening of intellectual functioning represents the "overlay" of a neurocognitive disorder, and both diagnoses are given (Section 3.2.5). The observation helped lead to the finding that at least one form of Alzheimer's disease is caused by a gene on chromosome 21. Meanwhile life expectancy has risen dramatically with heart surgery and better care, from about 12 years in 1912 and 25 in the 1980s to about 50 to 60 years in high-income countries today.

Exam trap

Down (1866) described the syndrome; Lejeune, Gautier and Turpin (1959) found the extra chromosome 21. Down syndrome is the commonest chromosomal cause of intellectual disability; fragile X is the commonest inherited cause. About 95 percent of cases are free trisomy from nondisjunction (risk rises with maternal age), and only the translocation form can be inherited from a carrier parent. The link with early Alzheimer's disease runs through the amyloid precursor protein gene on chromosome 21.

Fragile X and the other genetic syndromes

Tanmay is eight and lives in Nagpur. He has moderate intellectual disability, speech that tends to repeat the same phrase over and over, and a striking way of greeting people: he turns his head and body away while saying "hello", glancing back only briefly. He flaps his hands when excited, bites the side of his hand when anxious, cannot sit still in class and becomes distressed in crowds. He has a long face, large prominent ears and very flexible finger joints. His mother, who was always weak at mathematics, mentions that her own brother "never finished school" and still lives with their parents. That family history, a boy affected and a mother's brother affected, through a mother who is only mildly affected, is the signature of an X-linked condition, and a DNA test confirms it: Tanmay has fragile X syndrome.

The disorder called fragile X syndrome is the commonest inherited cause of intellectual disability, affecting about 1 in 4,000 males and 1 in 8,000 females. Its history is a lesson in how a syndrome is assembled. In 1943 the British neurologist James Purdon Martin and the geneticist Julia Bell described a family in which intellectual disability passed down through the female line and affected the males, an early account of X-linked intellectual disability. In 1969 Herbert Lubs noticed an unusual "marker X chromosome" in cultured cells from affected males: near the tip of its long arm the X chromosome looked pinched, as if the end were hanging by a thread. That fragile site, at the band called Xq27.3, gave the syndrome its name. The gene itself, FMR1, was identified in 1991.

The mechanism is unusual and worth understanding. Near the start of the FMR1 gene is a stretch of DNA in which the three-letter sequence CGG is repeated: a trinucleotide repeat. Most people have between about 5 and 44 repeats, most often around 30. People with 55 to 200 repeats carry a premutation: they usually have no intellectual disability, but the repeat is unstable, and when a woman passes a premutation to her children it can expand. Beyond about 200 repeats, a full mutation, the gene is chemically switched off by methylation, and its protein, which is needed for the normal development of synapses, is not made. Because the expansion happens mainly when the gene passes through a mother, the condition can grow worse from one generation to the next. Premutation carriers have their own, later problems: women have a raised risk of early ovarian failure, and some older men develop a tremor and unsteadiness. A boy has only one X chromosome, so a full mutation affects him fully: affected males usually have moderate intellectual disability, with an average IQ below 55. A girl with a full mutation has a second, normal X in about half her cells, so she is usually much more mildly affected, often with IQ in the borderline to low-average range, learning difficulties and shyness.

The physical features (a long face, large or protruding ears, a high-arched palate, very flexible joints, soft skin, flat feet, and large testes after puberty) are subtle in young children, so the behaviour is often the first clue. The behavioural phenotype of fragile X syndrome is one of the best known: gaze aversion and social anxiety (the turned-away greeting), shyness, hyperactivity and short attention, hand-flapping and hand-biting, perseverative speech that circles back to the same words, and sensitivity to noise and crowds. A substantial minority of affected boys meet the criteria for autism spectrum disorder, with estimates ranging from about 15 to 60 percent, and fragile X is the commonest single-gene cause of autism (Section 3.6.4). For Tanmay's family the diagnosis has consequences beyond Tanmay. His mother carries the mutation, so each of her future children has a one-in-two chance of inheriting the affected X, and her sisters may be carriers too; genetic counselling is offered to the whole family.

Down syndrome and fragile X syndrome are the two most common genetic causes, but examiners ask about many others, and each one teaches a different genetic mechanism. Two of them share a single region of chromosome 15, 15q11-q13, and show one of the strangest facts in genetics, genomic imprinting: in this region some genes work only on the copy inherited from the father and others only on the copy inherited from the mother. If the father's copy is lost (usually by a deletion, sometimes because the child has inherited two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father, called maternal uniparental disomy), the result is Prader-Willi syndrome: a floppy baby who feeds poorly, followed from early childhood by an insatiable appetite, obesity that can be life-threatening, short stature, small hands and feet, underdeveloped genitals, skin picking, temper outbursts and obsessive-compulsive features, with mild to moderate intellectual disability. Families must lock the kitchen. If the mother's working copy of a gene in the same region, UBE3A, is lost, the result is Angelman syndrome, first described by the English paediatrician Harry Angelman in 1965: severe intellectual disability, little or no speech, a jerky, unsteady (ataxic) gait with raised arms, seizures, a small head, and frequent laughter and smiling with an easily excited, happy manner. (An old nickname for the syndrome, based on the jerky movements, is obsolete and offensive and is not used.) The same piece of chromosome, missing from different parents, gives two quite different children.

Williams syndrome, described in 1961 by the New Zealand cardiologist John C. P. Williams, is caused by a microdeletion of about 27 genes from one copy of chromosome 7, at 7q11.23, a region that includes the gene for elastin, the protein that gives elasticity to blood vessels and other tissues; the missing elastin gene explains the narrowing of the aorta above the heart valve (supravalvular aortic stenosis) that is the syndrome's typical heart problem. Children with Williams syndrome have a distinctive "elfin" face (a broad forehead, a short upturned nose, full cheeks and lips, a wide mouth and a small chin), are often oversensitive to sound, and are remarkably friendly, talkative and interested in faces, a pattern sometimes called hypersociability. Pinel describes their uneven abilities: an average IQ of about 55, yet relatively rich vocabulary and expressive storytelling, often a love of music and good recognition of faces, alongside severe attentional problems and visuospatial skills even worse than their IQ predicts, so that drawing a simple object is almost impossible. His example is Anne Louise McGarrah, who at 42 could not add 13 and 18 but read biographies and novels with enthusiasm and talked knowledgeably about Mozart, Chopin and Bach. Williams syndrome is often described as autism's mirror image, social where autism is not.

The remaining syndromes in Table 3.10 can be learned row by row. The condition called cri-du-chat syndrome, described by Lejeune's group in 1963, is caused by a deletion from the short arm of chromosome 5 (hence its other name, 5p minus); its name is French for "cry of the cat", after the high-pitched mewing cry of affected babies, and it usually brings severe intellectual disability. Lesch-Nyhan syndrome, described in 1964 by Michael Lesch, then a medical student, and his teacher William Nyhan, is an X-linked recessive deficiency of the enzyme hypoxanthine-guanine phosphoribosyltransferase (HPRT), which leads to overproduction of uric acid (with gout and kidney stones), abnormal movements, intellectual disability and, from about the age of three, compulsive and uncontrollable self-injury, biting of the lips, tongue and fingers, so severe that children may need their teeth removed or their arms restrained; it is DSM-5-TR's example of a behavioural phenotype. Tay-Sachs disease is an autosomal recessive deficiency of the enzyme hexosaminidase A, so that a fatty substance (GM2 ganglioside) accumulates inside nerve cells; the baby develops normally for the first few months and then, from about three to six months, loses skills, goes blind and deaf, has seizures and usually dies in early childhood, and the eye shows a cherry-red spot on the retina, first described by the British ophthalmologist Waren Tay in 1881. In the United States about 1 in 27 to 30 Ashkenazi Jews is a carrier. Two sex-chromosome conditions are favourite distractors because they usually do not cause intellectual disability: Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY), in which a tall male with small testes and infertility often has language and reading difficulties but intelligence in the normal range, and Turner syndrome (45,X), in which a short female with a webbed neck and failure of the ovaries usually has normal intelligence, with weaker visuospatial and mathematical skills. Tuberous sclerosis and Rett syndrome complete the list: the first is a dominant condition with skin lesions, seizures and growths in the brain, with intellectual disability in about half and a strong link to autism (Section 3.6.4); the second is a disorder of girls caused by mutations in the MECP2 gene, with regression and hand-wringing (Section 3.5.2).

Exam trap

Sort the syndromes by mechanism. Wrong number of chromosomes: Down (47,+21), Klinefelter (47,XXY), Turner (45,X). Deletion: cri-du-chat (5p), Williams (7q11.23). Imprinting: Prader-Willi (father's 15q11-q13 lost) and Angelman (mother's copy lost), the same region with the parent of origin deciding which syndrome results. Single gene: fragile X (trinucleotide repeat), PKU, Tay-Sachs, Lesch-Nyhan. Commonest chromosomal cause: Down; commonest inherited cause: fragile X. Turner and Klinefelter usually do not cause intellectual disability. Match the clue: self-biting means Lesch-Nyhan, a cat-like cry cri-du-chat, insatiable hunger Prader-Willi, constant laughter Angelman, hypersociability with poor drawing Williams, a cherry-red spot Tay-Sachs, gaze aversion with large ears fragile X.

Inborn errors of metabolism: phenylketonuria and its relatives

In 1934 a young Norwegian mother, Borgny Egeland, brought her two children to Oslo. Liv and Dag had seemed normal at birth, but both had fallen steadily behind and both now had severe intellectual disability. Their mother had taken them from doctor to doctor without an answer, and she had noticed something that nobody had taken seriously: a strong, musty smell, especially in Dag's urine. The doctor she finally reached was Asbjørn Følling (1888 to 1973), a physician who had trained first as a chemist and who worked as a biochemist at the national hospital in Oslo. Følling took the smell seriously. He tested the children's urine with ferric chloride, a routine test for ketones, and instead of the expected colour the urine turned deep green. By painstaking chemical work he isolated the substance responsible and identified it as phenylpyruvic acid. Then he did what turned a curiosity into a discovery: he collected urine from about 430 people living in institutions for the intellectually disabled and found the same green reaction in a small group of them (eight, by most accounts), so that with Liv and Dag he could describe ten patients. He concluded that these people shared a single, previously unknown metabolic disease, and he published the finding in 1934. The English geneticist Lionel Penrose later gave it the name by which it is known, phenylketonuria (PKU). Følling's work came too late for Liv and Dag, but it has saved many thousands of children since. One of the people later found to have PKU, through screening at the Vineland Training School in New Jersey, was Carol, the daughter of the novelist Pearl Buck, who had written a moving book, The Child Who Never Grew, about her search for an explanation.

The biochemistry, as Pinel explains, turned out to be reasonably straightforward. Phenylalanine is an essential amino acid: we cannot make it, so it comes from the protein in food. Normally a liver enzyme, phenylalanine hydroxylase, converts most of it into another amino acid, tyrosine, which the body in turn uses to make the neurotransmitters dopamine and noradrenaline and the skin pigment melanin. In PKU the enzyme is missing or nearly inactive. Two things follow (Figure 3.6). Phenylalanine builds up in the blood to levels that are toxic to the developing brain, and some of the excess is diverted into abnormal by-products, the phenylketones, of which phenylpyruvic acid is one; these spill into the urine and sweat and give the musty smell that Borgny Egeland noticed. At the same time less tyrosine is made, so dopamine levels are particularly low, and less melanin is made, so untreated children tend to have unusually fair skin and hair. Without treatment the result is severe intellectual disability, together with seizures, hyperactivity, irritability, vomiting, behaviour problems and eczema.

PKU is inherited as an autosomal recessive condition. A child is affected only if he inherits a faulty copy of the phenylalanine hydroxylase gene from both parents; the parents, each with one faulty and one normal copy, are healthy carriers, and each of their children has a one-in-four chance of being affected. According to Pinel, about 1 person in 100 of European descent is a carrier, and about 1 infant in 16,000 in the United States is born with PKU; the rate is lower among African Americans and Asian Americans. Worldwide estimates range from about 1 in 12,000 births to about 1 in 24,000 (the figure of a 2020 global survey by Hillert and colleagues), with very wide variation between countries, from about 1 in 4,500 in Italy to about 1 in 125,000 in Japan in that survey.

The disease became the great success story of the field because of two further discoveries. The first was treatment. In Birmingham in 1951, a mother persuaded the German paediatrician Horst Bickel and his colleagues John Gerrard and Evelyn Hickmans to try to help her daughter Sheila, who had PKU. They devised a diet very low in phenylalanine, and Sheila's condition improved; their report appeared in The Lancet in 1953. The second was early detection. The ferric chloride test does not work in newborn babies, who do not yet excrete enough phenylpyruvic acid, and by the time it turned positive the damage was done. The American microbiologist Robert Guthrie (1916 to 1995), whose own son had an intellectual disability and whose niece had been diagnosed with PKU, devised a simple bacterial test that could measure phenylalanine in a drop of blood from a baby's heel, dried on filter paper. Screening began in 1961, and within two years some 400,000 American newborns had been tested and 39 babies with PKU found; Guthrie and Ada Susi published the method in 1963. The filter-paper "Guthrie card" is still the basis of newborn screening programmes around the world, which now test for many treatable conditions.

Timing is everything. The diet prevents intellectual disability only if it begins within the first few weeks of life, which makes PKU a textbook example of a sensitive period: a window during which an experience, here the brain's exposure to toxic levels of phenylalanine, has its greatest effect. It is also the textbook example of a gene-environment interaction (Section 1.5): the faulty genotype does harm only in an environment that contains phenylalanine. The diet itself is demanding. It severely restricts natural protein (meat, fish, eggs, milk, pulses, nuts), so the child takes most of his protein as a special phenylalanine-free formula, and he must avoid the artificial sweetener aspartame, which is why diet soft drinks carry the warning "Phenylketonurics: contains phenylalanine". For an Indian vegetarian family whose protein comes from dal and milk, the diet is especially hard, and the formula is expensive. Older practice relaxed the diet in adolescence or adulthood; current guidance is to continue it for life, because high phenylalanine levels in adults impair attention, mood and concentration, and even well-treated children may show subtle cognitive weaknesses. Two medicines now help some patients: sapropterin, a synthetic form of the enzyme's natural cofactor, which boosts residual enzyme activity in people whose enzyme still works a little, and pegvaliase, an enzyme that breaks down phenylalanine, approved in the United States in 2018 for adults poorly controlled by other means.

The last twist is maternal PKU. A woman with PKU who was treated in childhood may grow up with normal intelligence and stop her diet. If she becomes pregnant with high phenylalanine levels, the phenylalanine crosses the placenta and damages the baby's brain and heart, even though the baby, who has usually inherited only one faulty copy from her, does not itself have PKU. The result can be a small head and intellectual disability in a child with no metabolic disease at all. When the mother lowers her phenylalanine before conception and keeps it low throughout pregnancy, the risk disappears.

Exam trap

"In PKU the body fails to convert phenylalanine into which substance, owing to a deficiency of phenylalanine hydroxylase?" Tyrosine (Real PYQ 2025). The other options are real molecules with other jobs: urea is the end product of the urea cycle, the body's way of disposing of nitrogen from amino acids; tryptophan is the amino acid from which serotonin (and melatonin) is made; histidine is the amino acid from which histamine is made. Remember the chain phenylalanine, then tyrosine, then L-dopa, dopamine and noradrenaline. Følling (1934) was a Norwegian physician and biochemist; Bickel gave the diet (1950s); Guthrie gave the newborn blood test (1961 onwards). PKU is autosomal recessive.

PKU has relatives, and three groups are worth knowing. The first, galactosaemia, is an inherited inability to break down galactose, one of the two sugars in the lactose of milk. A baby who seems well at birth begins to vomit, becomes jaundiced and fails to thrive once milk feeds begin, and untreated the condition damages the liver, causes cataracts and leads to intellectual disability; a lactose-free, galactose-free diet prevents most of the damage, and Guthrie adapted his method to screen for it. The second, congenital hypothyroidism, a thyroid gland that is missing, too small or unable to make its hormone, affects about 1 baby in 2,000 to 3,000 and is the commonest preventable cause found by newborn screening. Before screening, most affected babies were recognised only after weeks or months, because the early signs are subtle; the babies fed poorly, were sluggish and constipated, and over the following years developed the stunted growth, coarse features and intellectual disability once called sporadic cretinism. A small daily tablet of levothyroxine (thyroxine), crushed into milk and started in the first weeks, allows normal development. (The endemic cretinism caused by a mother's lack of iodine is described in Section 3.3.5.) The third group, the lysosomal storage disorders, in which a missing enzyme lets a substance accumulate inside cells, cause intellectual disability that gets worse rather than staying stable. Tay-Sachs disease (Section 3.3.3) is one; the mucopolysaccharidoses, such as Hurler syndrome and Sanfilippo syndrome, are others, and Sanfilippo syndrome is DSM-5-TR's example of progressive intellectual decline. In India, newborn screening is not yet universal: many babies, especially those born outside large hospitals, are never tested for PKU or congenital hypothyroidism, so these preventable disabilities still occur.

Prenatal causes: alcohol, other teratogens, infections and iodine

A teratogen is anything in the environment of the womb that can disturb the development of the embryo or fetus: a drug, a chemical, an infection, radiation, or a disease or deficiency in the mother. The general principles (that timing matters, that dose matters, that the brain remains vulnerable throughout pregnancy because it keeps developing long after the other organs have formed) belong to the Developmental book. Here the question is narrower: which teratogens cause intellectual disability, and how would you recognise them?

The most important is alcohol. As early as 1968 the French paediatrician Paul Lemoine and his colleagues described 127 children of alcoholic parents in a French medical journal, with a characteristic pattern of growth failure, facial features and intellectual problems, but their report attracted little notice outside France. In June 1973 Kenneth Lyons Jones, David W. Smith and their colleagues in Seattle described eight unrelated children born to mothers who were chronic alcoholics, all with the same pattern of malformations, and in a second paper that November in The Lancet, Jones and Smith gave the pattern a name: fetal alcohol syndrome (FAS). Its features fall into three groups. The first is a characteristic face, and three features form the diagnostic triad: a smooth philtrum (the vertical groove between the nose and the upper lip is flattened or absent), a thin upper lip, and short palpebral fissures (the eye openings are small from corner to corner), often with a low nasal bridge, a short nose, folds at the inner corners of the eyes and a flat midface. The second is growth deficiency before and after birth, including a small head (microcephaly). The third is damage to the central nervous system, which shows itself as intellectual disability or, more often, as problems with learning, memory, attention, planning, impulse control and behaviour in a child whose IQ may be in the normal range.

Alcohol crosses the placenta freely, and the fetus is exposed to the same blood alcohol level as its mother for longer, because its liver cannot yet break alcohol down efficiently. The face takes shape in the first weeks of pregnancy, often before a woman knows she is pregnant, which is why the facial features follow drinking early in pregnancy, while the brain can be harmed at any stage. The full syndrome is the extreme of a range of outcomes, now called fetal alcohol spectrum disorder: fetal alcohol syndrome; partial fetal alcohol syndrome, with some of the facial features; and alcohol-related neurodevelopmental disorder, with the brain effects but without the face. DSM-5-TR includes, among its conditions for further study, neurobehavioural disorder associated with prenatal alcohol exposure (ND-PAE), defined by impaired neurocognitive, self-regulatory and adaptive functioning after more than minimal exposure in the womb, which it defines as more than 13 drinks a month during pregnancy or more than two drinks on any one occasion; it can be diagnosed with or without the facial features (Section 1.8.6 explains the category). DSM-5-TR estimates the mean global prevalence of fetal alcohol spectrum disorder in 2012 at 7.7 per 1,000 people, and far higher among children in care. No safe level of drinking in pregnancy has been established, so the only safe advice is none, and because the cause is entirely known, fetal alcohol spectrum disorder is often described as one of the leading preventable causes of intellectual and developmental disability.

Exam trap

"Maya's baby was born with a smaller than average head, facial malformations and an intellectual disability. It is likely that Maya, during early pregnancy..." drank alcohol (RML 2022). The combination of small head, a characteristic face and intellectual disability is fetal alcohol syndrome. Caffeine causes no malformation syndrome. Cannabis has no characteristic facial syndrome. Mercury is a genuine fetal brain poison and can also cause a small head: in Minamata in Japan, where a chemical factory discharged methylmercury into the bay from the 1930s until 1968 and the poisoning was recognised in 1956, children born to mothers who had eaten contaminated fish had severe brain damage with a picture resembling cerebral palsy, even when their mothers were barely affected, because the placenta concentrates methylmercury in the fetus. But mercury does not produce the FAS face. The facial triad (smooth philtrum, thin upper lip, short palpebral fissures) is the clue to alcohol.

Other teratogens can be listed more briefly. Some anticonvulsant drugs taken in pregnancy harm the fetus: phenytoin causes a recognisable fetal hydantoin syndrome, and valproate raises the risk of spina bifida, lowers the child's IQ by several points and raises the risk of autism (Section 3.6.5), which is why it is avoided in women who may become pregnant. Isotretinoin, a vitamin A derivative taken for severe acne, causes severe malformations of the brain, face and heart. Cocaine, opioids and other drugs contribute mainly through prematurity, poor growth and the chaotic lives that often go with heavy use; their direct effects on intelligence have proved smaller than was once feared. Smoking lowers birth weight and raises the risk of prematurity. High doses of radiation in early pregnancy, as among the children of mothers exposed to the atomic bombs in Japan, produce small heads and intellectual disability.

Several infections caught by the mother during pregnancy can reach the fetus, and they are remembered by the acronym TORCH. T is toxoplasmosis, a parasite caught from cat faeces or undercooked meat, which can cause hydrocephalus, calcium deposits in the brain and inflammation of the retina. O stands for "other" agents: syphilis, chickenpox (varicella), HIV and, most recently, the Zika virus, whose epidemic in Brazil in 2015 and 2016 was recognised when doctors noticed a sudden rise in babies born with severe microcephaly. R is rubella. In 1941 the Australian ophthalmologist Norman Gregg noticed an unusual number of babies with cataracts after a rubella (German measles) epidemic and traced them to their mothers' infection in early pregnancy; the full congenital rubella syndrome combines deafness, cataracts, heart defects and intellectual disability, and it is entirely preventable by vaccination (India began nationwide measles-rubella vaccination with a campaign in 2017). C is cytomegalovirus, the commonest congenital infection, which is usually silent in the mother and is a leading non-genetic cause of deafness in children as well as a cause of intellectual disability. H is herpes simplex, usually caught during birth rather than in the womb.

The largest preventable prenatal cause of all, worldwide, is a lack of one element. The thyroid gland needs iodine to make its hormones, and the developing brain needs thyroid hormone. Where soils are poor in iodine, as in mountains and river valleys from which it has been washed out, mothers become iodine deficient, the fetus is deprived of thyroid hormone at a critical time, and the result in its severest form is endemic cretinism: intellectual disability, often with deafness and mutism, a squint, disturbances of posture and gait, and stunted growth. Esquirol's treatise of 1838 already discussed cretinism and asked whether it and goitre (a swollen thyroid) had the same cause. Milder deficiency does not produce cretins but lowers the intelligence of a whole population by several IQ points, which is why an editorial in the Bulletin of the World Health Organization in 2002 called iodine deficiency "the world's greatest single cause of preventable brain damage in childhood". The remedy is cheap: iodised salt.

Indian context

A "goitre belt" once ran along the Himalayan foothills and the plains of northern and north-eastern India. The Government of India began the National Goitre Control Programme in 1962; in 1984 it took the policy decision to iodise all edible salt, and in August 1992 the programme was renamed the National Iodine Deficiency Disorders Control Programme (NIDDCP), to cover the whole range of iodine deficiency disorders rather than goitre alone. Universal salt iodisation is its central strategy, and according to the Directorate General of Health Services, 94.3 percent of households were using adequately iodised salt in 2019 to 2021 (National Family Health Survey). The programme is one of India's most successful pieces of prevention of intellectual disability.

Finally, the mother's own health shapes the fetal brain. Severe maternal undernutrition and disease of the placenta (as in pre-eclampsia) starve the fetus and lead to growth restriction and prematurity; poorly controlled diabetes in early pregnancy raises the risk of malformations; an untreated mother with PKU poisons her fetus with phenylalanine (Section 3.3.4); and when a mother who is Rh negative carries an Rh-positive baby, her antibodies can destroy the baby's red blood cells and cause the severe newborn jaundice described in Section 3.3.6, a danger now largely prevented by giving her anti-D immunoglobulin.

Around birth and after: asphyxia, prematurity, infection, injury, poisoning and deprivation

Birth is a dangerous journey for the brain. The commonest perinatal cause of intellectual disability is birth asphyxia: a shortage of oxygen and blood flow to the baby's brain during labour or delivery, from a prolonged or obstructed labour, a compressed umbilical cord or a separated placenta. The resulting brain injury is called hypoxic-ischaemic encephalopathy; a baby who does not breathe at birth, is floppy and unresponsive and then has seizures in the first days is showing it, and survivors of the more severe grades may be left with cerebral palsy, epilepsy and intellectual disability. Babies born very early are vulnerable in a different way. In extreme prematurity and very low birth weight (under 1,500 g), the fragile blood vessels beside the brain's ventricles can bleed (intraventricular haemorrhage) and the white matter around them can be damaged, leading to cerebral palsy, intellectual disability and, much more often, milder problems with learning and attention. A third danger is severe newborn jaundice. Bilirubin, the yellow pigment released when red blood cells break down, is normally cleared by the liver; when it rises very high, as in Rh disease, it can enter the brain and stain and damage the basal ganglia and brainstem, a condition called kernicterus, which leaves a child with writhing (athetoid) cerebral palsy, deafness, problems moving the eyes upwards and sometimes intellectual disability. Phototherapy and, when needed, exchange transfusion prevent it. Infection of the newborn, with sepsis or meningitis, completes the list of perinatal causes.

After the newborn period the brain can still be damaged, and the postnatal causes listed by DSM-5-TR are mostly familiar from any paediatric ward. Infections of the brain are the most important in India: bacterial meningitis (from Haemophilus influenzae type b, the pneumococcus, the meningococcus or tuberculosis), viral encephalitis, including Japanese encephalitis, which causes epidemics of encephalitis among children in several Indian states, and cerebral malaria. Survivors may be left with intellectual disability, epilepsy, deafness and behaviour problems, and the onset of the disability is then abrupt rather than gradual (Section 3.2.5). Head injury from falls and road accidents, and abusive head trauma, in which an infant is violently shaken or struck, can cause bleeding over the brain and diffuse damage. Lead poisoning lowers IQ and impairs attention even at blood levels once thought harmless, and no safe level is known; children are exposed through old lead-based paint, the fumes and dust of informal battery recycling and metal smelting, and some traditional remedies and cosmetics. When Robert Saper and his colleagues bought 70 Ayurvedic herbal medicines in South Asian grocery shops in Boston and analysed them, 14 (one in five) contained lead, mercury or arsenic, some at levels that could cause poisoning (JAMA, 2004). Severe and prolonged malnutrition in the first years of life, infantile spasms and other severe epilepsies of early childhood, and a thyroid deficiency left untreated (Section 3.3.4) can all hold back the developing brain.

The last cause on DSM-5-TR's list is the most psychological: severe and chronic social deprivation. The wild boy of Aveyron (the opening of this chapter) and Genie, the Californian girl kept tied and silent in a room until she was thirteen (Book 1, Section 8.8.4), are the extreme cases, but each is a single child about whom we can never be sure what came first, a disability or the neglect. What was needed was a large group of children who had suffered severe deprivation for a known time, followed by a known rescue. History supplied one.

Key study: the English and Romanian Adoptees study (Rutter, Sonuga-Barke and colleagues, 1998 to 2017)

Background. After the fall of Romania's communist government in 1989, the world saw its state orphanages, where infants lay in cots for most of the day with little food, stimulation or human contact. Many were adopted abroad, and between February 1990 and September 1992 more than 300 came to families in the United Kingdom. Method. Michael Rutter (1933 to 2021) of the Institute of Psychiatry in London and his team, later led by Edmund Sonuga-Barke, took a random sample of 165 of these children, who had spent from a few weeks to about 43 months in institutions, compared them with 52 children adopted within the United Kingdom who had not been deprived, and assessed them at 4, 6, 11 and 15 years and again in young adulthood (22 to 25 years). This was a natural experiment: the deprivation was severe and fairly uniform, it ended abruptly at adoption, and the age at adoption varied for reasons that had little to do with the child. Results. On arrival most of the children were severely impaired in growth and in every area of development. Their catch-up was remarkable, and how much they caught up depended above all on how long the deprivation had lasted. Children who had left the institutions before the age of six months did about as well as the British adoptees at almost every age and on almost every measure. Children deprived for longer showed persistent "deprivation-specific" problems: a quasi-autistic pattern, disinhibited social engagement (indiscriminate friendliness with strangers), inattention and overactivity, and cognitive impairment. Strikingly, in the final follow-up the raised rate of cognitive impairment in the longer-deprived group had fallen to normal by young adulthood, whereas the autistic features, the disinhibited social behaviour and the inattention persisted, and emotional problems rose in early adult life. About a fifth of those deprived for more than six months had no problems at any assessment. Conclusion. Severe early deprivation can produce intellectual impairment, but much of it is reversible when the child is moved to a loving family, and the earlier the better; some social and attentional consequences last into adulthood. The study is the strongest human evidence for both the power of the early environment and the plasticity of the developing brain.

Deprivation of a less dramatic kind is part of the explanation of cultural-familial disability (Section 3.3.1). Poverty multiplies risk at every step: poor maternal nutrition and antenatal care, birth without a skilled attendant, infections, lead and malnutrition in infancy, and then a home with few books and little time for talk, followed by an overcrowded school. Each adds a little, and in a child whose genetic endowment already places him towards the lower end of the normal range, the sum can be the difference between a slow learner and a child with mild intellectual disability. The encouraging side of the same fact is that early intervention, nutrition and stimulation can shift the outcome. India's Integrated Child Development Services, launched in 1975, deliver supplementary nutrition, preschool education and health check-ups to young children through local anganwadi centres, and the district early intervention centres of the Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakram screen children for developmental delay (Book 2, Section 10.1.6).

Many of the causes in this section can be prevented, and prevention is usually described at three levels (Book 2, Section 9.6.2): primary prevention stops the condition from occurring at all, secondary prevention detects it early and treats it before disability results, and tertiary prevention reduces the impact of a disability that already exists. Table 3.11 applies them to intellectual disability.

Table 3.11 Preventing intellectual disability, cause by cause.

Congenital rubella syndromeRubella (measles-rubella) vaccination of children, so that girls are immune before pregnancyPrimary
Fetal alcohol spectrum disorderNo alcohol in pregnancy or when a pregnancy is possiblePrimary
Iodine deficiency and endemic cretinismUniversal salt iodisationPrimary
Neural tube defects (spina bifida with hydrocephalus can bring learning difficulties)Folic acid before conception and in early pregnancyPrimary

Clinical link

Indian parents often arrive with an explanation already in place. The grandmother says the child is like this because his mother went out during an eclipse, or was frightened by a snake when she was pregnant; a relative calls it the fruit of past karma; a neighbour blames the mother for something she ate. DSM-5-TR notes that cultural explanations of intellectual disability can include supernatural influences and punishment for the mother's or parents' presumed wrongdoing, and that these bring shame and silence. The clinician's task is neither to mock these beliefs nor to leave them unchallenged. Listen first, and find out who is being blamed, because it is usually the mother. Then explain, in plain words and in the family's language, what the investigations have shown: "His brain has an extra piece of one chromosome; that happens by chance when the egg is formed, and nothing anyone did in the pregnancy caused it," or, when no cause is found, "We have looked carefully, and we have found nothing that anyone did or failed to do." Separate cause from blame, say clearly that the mother is not at fault, and explain the practical implications: whether another child could be affected, whether genetic counselling is needed (for example, where marriage between relatives is customary, recessive conditions such as PKU and Tay-Sachs disease are commoner, a fact that can be explained without blaming anyone), and what can be done now. A family that stops searching for whom to blame has more energy for helping the child.
