3.4 Communication Disorders: Language, Speech, Fluency and Social CommunicationLower yield · 1 Q

Four children in four Indian cities will serve as this section's guides. Each has been brought to a speech and hearing clinic for the same reason, that he or she does not talk the way other children of the same age talk, and each is silent or half-silent in a different way. Vihaan, four and a half, sits on his mother's lap in Chennai and points at the fish tank; he says "fish" and "blue fish", and when the receptionist asks him how old he is, he looks at her, smiles, and says "fish" again. Aadhya, nearly five, chatters non-stop to her father in Jaipur, in long, confident sentences, but almost nobody outside her family can understand a word, because "cup" comes out as "tup", "spoon" as "poon" and "kela" as "tela". Sara, six, in Kochi, knows exactly what she wants to say and has the words and the grammar to say it, but the first sound will not come: "W-w-w-why ... why is the ... the ... sssssun hot?", and she blinks hard as she pushes. Parth, twelve, in Kolkata, talks fluently and precisely, with an adult's vocabulary, yet his class teacher has written in his diary that he "does not understand how to talk to people". When his science teacher asked, "Can you open the window?", he said "Yes," and went on writing.

All four have a communication disorder, and all four have a different one. To see why, and to see why the first thing a clinician asks about any child who does not talk is where the problem lies (in speech, in language or in communication?), you have to begin with the distinction that DSM-5-TR itself begins with.

Speech, language and communication are not the same thing

In everyday talk the three words are used almost interchangeably. In the clinic they mean three different layers of the same activity. Speech, in DSM-5-TR's definition, is the expressive production of sounds: the physical act of turning breath into audible, recognisable syllables. It includes articulation (shaping the sounds with the jaw, tongue and lips), fluency (the smooth, timed flow of one sound into the next), voice and resonance (the quality of the sound the larynx and the nasal passages produce). Language is something more abstract. It is the form, function and use of a conventional system of symbols in a rule-governed way: the code itself. The symbols may be spoken words, but they may equally be signs, written words or pictures, which is why a deaf child fluent in Indian Sign Language has full language without any speech at all. Communication is the widest of the three. It is any behaviour, verbal or nonverbal, intentional or unintentional, that can influence another person's behaviour, ideas or attitudes. A frown, a shrug, a pointed finger, a change of tone and a deliberate silence are all communication; so is a sentence.

Think of a telephone call. Speech is the line: can the signal be heard clearly and without breaks? Language is the message: are there words, put together in a grammar that the listener shares? Communication is the conversation: does the speaker greet the listener, take turns, notice that the listener is confused and explain again, and understand what the listener meant but did not say? A fault can occur in any one of these layers while the others work well, and the four communication disorders of DSM-5-TR are, roughly, the faults of each layer (Figure 3.7). Aadhya's line is crackly; Sara's keeps cutting out; Vihaan's message is thin and poorly built; Parth's line and message are perfect, but he does not know how to hold the conversation.

Language itself has two directions and several levels. Receptive language is the understanding of what others say, sign or write; expressive language is the production of one's own messages. The two can be impaired to different degrees, and DSM-5-TR insists that both be assessed, because a child who talks little may understand a great deal, and, more dangerously, a child who seems to understand may be guessing from the situation. The levels of language are the ones Book 1, Section 8.7 describes in detail and are only listed here: phonology, the sound system of a language and the rules for combining its sounds; morphology, the structure of words (walk, walk-ed, walk-ing; ladka, ladke, ladkon); syntax, the rules for ordering words into sentences; semantics, meaning, both of words and of sentences; and pragmatics, the use of language in social context, which is where language shades into communication. The ages at which children normally reach each milestone, from babbling to first words to two-word phrases and full sentences, are taught in Book 1, Section 8.8.5; this section assumes them.

Two further points from DSM-5-TR's introduction to the group shape every diagnosis that follows. First, assessment must take account of the child's cultural and language background, above all in bilingual homes, and the standardised tests of language and of non-verbal ability must be relevant to the child's own linguistic and cultural group: a test built and normed on one group may give misleading results in another. A Chennai child who speaks Tamil at home and is meeting English for the first time in a preschool classroom will score poorly on an English vocabulary test for reasons that have nothing to do with a disorder. Second, communication disorders are more common in boys than in girls, perhaps because early communication develops slightly differently in the two sexes. They also keep company with each other and with the rest of the neurodevelopmental family: autism, ADHD, specific learning disorder and intellectual developmental disorder all co-occur with them at high rates, as do anxiety disorders and some medical conditions such as seizure disorders.

Every disorder in this section is developmental: it is there from the time the skill should have appeared. That rules out the adult who loses language after a stroke (aphasia, or dysphasia when partial) or whose speech becomes slurred because the muscles or their nerves are damaged (dysarthria). Those are acquired signs, taught in Section 2.4.6, and the distinction matters because the same words are used for both. A child with a "developmental language disorder" was never on the normal path; an adult with aphasia was on it and fell off. The names have also changed. DSM-IV-TR's expressive language disorder and mixed receptive-expressive language disorder became a single language disorder, phonological disorder became speech sound disorder, stuttering became childhood-onset fluency disorder, and social (pragmatic) communication disorder was created new in DSM-5; Table 3.1 in Section 3.1.2 shows where every old category went, and Table 3.2 in Section 3.1.3 gives the codes.

Exam trap

Speech is sound production (articulation, fluency, voice); language is the symbol system (spoken, written or signed); communication is any behaviour that influences another person, verbal or not. A deaf adult who signs fluently has language without speech. A child who stutters has a speech (fluency) disorder, not a language disorder. A child who speaks in perfect sentences but cannot hold a conversation has a communication (pragmatic) problem. The communication disorders are developmental; aphasia and dysarthria after brain injury are not among them.

Language disorder

Go back to Vihaan, the boy who answered "fish" when he was asked his age. His parents speak Tamil at home, and since the age of three he has attended an English-medium preschool. His first words came at about two, roughly a year after his cousins' first words, and he began to put two words together only in the last few months. In Tamil and in English alike he uses a small set of words over and over, and his sentences, when he makes them, are short and broken: "Appa go office." "Me want blue one." He cannot say what he did at his grandmother's house yesterday, and if his teacher gives him a two-step instruction ("put your bag on the shelf and then sit on the mat") he does the first half, or watches what the other children do and copies them. Yet he is not a withdrawn child. He brings his mother the toys he wants her to look at, laughs when his father pretends to be a tiger, points to show her an aeroplane, and plays happily with the other children, as long as the play does not depend on talk. A hearing test is normal. On puzzles, form boards and picture-matching games that need no words, he works at the level of his age. His father's younger brother, the family remembers, "did not talk properly till he was five" and was teased for it at school.

Vihaan has a language disorder. DSM-5-TR defines it with four criteria, and each is easier to remember once you see what it is for. The first describes the problem itself: persistent difficulties in acquiring and using language, in any modality (spoken, written, signed or otherwise), because of deficits in understanding or in producing it. Three examples are named. The child has a reduced vocabulary, knowing and using fewer words than expected; limited sentence structure, meaning difficulty in putting words and word endings together into sentences by the rules of grammar and morphology ("Appa go office" lacks both the verb ending and the small grammatical words); and impaired discourse, the ability to use words and sentences to explain something, describe a series of events or hold a conversation. Notice that the modality is open. A deaf child who signs can have a language disorder in sign, because the disorder is in the code, not in the mouth.

The second criterion sets the threshold: the child's language must be substantially and quantifiably below what is expected for age, and it must matter, limiting communication, social participation, schoolwork or later work. "Quantifiably" is the important word. The diagnosis rests on history, on observation of the child in more than one setting and on standardised language tests that compare the child with others of the same age, so that a quiet child is not mislabelled and a struggling child is not missed. The third criterion requires onset in the early developmental period. The fourth is a set of exclusions, and each exclusion exists because the same picture can have another cause: the difficulties must not be due to hearing loss or another sensory impairment, to a motor problem, or to a medical or neurological condition, and they must not be better explained by intellectual developmental disorder or global developmental delay (Section 3.2), in which language lags because everything lags.

What does language disorder look like in practice? DSM-5-TR's description reads like Vihaan's file. First words and phrases are late. Vocabulary is smaller and less varied than expected, and sentences are shorter and simpler, with grammatical errors, especially in the past tense. Word-finding problems are common: the child knows the word but cannot fetch it ("the thing, the thing you cut with"). Definitions are thin, and synonyms, words with several meanings and age-appropriate word play pass the child by. Memory for language is often weak too, which shows as difficulty following instructions of increasing length, rehearsing a string of verbal information (a phone number, a shopping list) and remembering new sound sequences, the very skill a child needs to learn new words. In older children the clearest sign is often a story that falls apart: asked to tell what happened in a film, the child gives fragments without the key events or the order in which they happened.

Two features catch clinicians out. The first is that problems of comprehension are often underestimated, because children become skilled at using the situation to guess what is meant. When the teacher holds up a bag and points at the shelf, Vihaan seems to understand the instruction; strip away the gesture and the context, and his understanding is much weaker than it looked. This is why DSM-5-TR insists that receptive and expressive skills be assessed separately. The second is that children adapt. Many with a language disorder seem shy or reluctant to talk, or will talk only with their family. These social signs are not part of the diagnosis, but when they are marked and lasting they are a reason to refer the child for a full language assessment rather than to wait for him to "come out of his shell".

How does a clinician know that a two-year-old with a small vocabulary has a disorder rather than a slow start? Often the clinician cannot, and DSM-5-TR is frank about it. Early vocabulary and the age of first word combinations vary enormously between healthy children, and no single early sign predicts much. Many toddlers who are slow to talk, the children often called late talkers, catch up on their own. In a population study the best single predictor of how a child's language would look at age 7 was a late onset of language at 24 months. From about age 4, individual differences in language become stable and can be measured accurately, and a language disorder diagnosed at 4 or later usually persists into adult life, although the particular pattern of strengths and weaknesses changes as the child grows. In judging a late-talking toddler, clinicians therefore look beyond the number of words to the things that predict trouble: whether the child understands what is said, uses gestures to communicate, plays imaginatively and has relatives with language difficulties. Vihaan, at four and a half, with delays in both understanding and expression and an affected uncle, has gone beyond the range of a late talker.

The outlook depends on which side of language is affected. Children whose main difficulty is receptive do worse than those whose difficulty is mainly expressive: receptive problems respond less well to treatment and are often followed by difficulties in reading comprehension. The social costs are real. Children with a language disorder are at risk of being bullied by their peers, and DSM-5-TR notes that women who had a language disorder in childhood may face nearly three times the risk of sexual assault in adult life compared with women who did not, a reminder that a child who cannot explain or report what is happening to her is a child who is easier to exploit.

Why do some children have such difficulty? Language disorders are highly heritable. Family members are more likely than chance to have had language problems themselves, twin studies consistently find a substantial genetic contribution, and molecular studies suggest many genes acting together rather than one gene of large effect. A single gene of large effect does exist in rare families (FOXP2, Section 3.4.3), but it explains very few cases. One cause that parents often fear can be set aside: bilingualism does not cause a language disorder or make one worse. Children learning two languages may show some delays or differences in each, but a true language disorder shows in both languages. That is the practical rule for a child like Vihaan, and the reason his assessment had to be done in Tamil as well as in English.

The differential diagnosis follows the exclusions in the criteria. Normal variation comes first, and before the age of 4 it is hard to rule out; regional dialects and the social varieties of a language must be respected, so that a child who speaks the Tamil of his village, or a Hindi strongly coloured by Bhojpuri, is not judged against the language of a textbook. Hearing loss must always be excluded first, because even a moderate hearing loss starves a child of the language he needs to learn from; language disorder can still be diagnosed in a hard-of-hearing child, but only when the language problems are greater than the hearing loss would explain. Intellectual developmental disorder often first shows itself as delayed language, which is why a non-verbal measure of ability, such as the puzzles and form boards Vihaan did well on, is part of every assessment. (DSM-5-TR adds that language disorder can occur at any level of intellectual ability, and that a gap between verbal and non-verbal scores is not required for the diagnosis.) Autism spectrum disorder also commonly begins with delayed language, but it brings things that language disorder does not: a lack of social interest or odd social approaches (pulling an adult by the hand without looking at her), odd play (carrying toys about without playing with them), unusual communication (knowing the alphabet but not answering to one's own name), and rigid routines and repetitive behaviour (flapping, spinning, echolalia). Vihaan's pointing, showing and shared laughter are the signs that tell against autism.

Finally, language can be lost through a disease of the brain. In Landau-Kleffner syndrome, described in six children by William Landau and Frank Kleffner in 1957 and called acquired aphasia with epilepsy in ICD-10 (F80.3), a child who had been speaking normally gradually loses the ability to understand and then to use speech, often with seizures and with a markedly abnormal electroencephalogram (EEG), especially during sleep. The general rule DSM-5-TR draws is worth learning as a rule: a loss of speech and language at any age needs a full neurological assessment, including a routine and a sleep EEG to look for epilepsy, and a decline in social and communication skills during the first two years of life is a reason to assess for autism (Section 3.5.6).

Language disorder is treated by speech and language therapy, which is often delivered through parents who are taught to follow the child's lead, expand what he says ("Car." "Yes, a red car!") and build talk into daily routines. The evidence from trials is that therapy helps expressive vocabulary and speech sounds more reliably than it helps comprehension, which fits the poorer outlook of receptive problems. The psychotherapies of childhood are taught in Book 2, but speech and language therapy is the work of a separate profession, the speech-language pathologist.

Indian context

Most Indian children grow up with two or three languages: a home language, a regional language and the English of the school. Tests of language built and normed in the United States or Britain, or even in one Indian language, can mislabel such a child, so assessment should be done in the child's strongest language and the history should cover every language the child hears. India's main national centre for training speech-language pathologists and audiologists is the All India Institute of Speech and Hearing (AIISH) in Mysuru, set up in 1965, and many medical colleges and district early intervention centres now offer speech therapy.

Exam trap

DSM-5 merged DSM-IV-TR's expressive language disorder and mixed receptive-expressive language disorder into one language disorder. Receptive impairment carries the poorer prognosis. Bilingualism does not cause a language disorder; a true disorder appears in both languages. A child who loses language he once had needs a neurological work-up (Landau-Kleffner syndrome, epilepsy) and an autism assessment; that is regression, not a developmental language disorder.

Speech sound disorder

Aadhya, the chatterbox from Jaipur, has no shortage of words or grammar. She tells long stories about her school and her cousins in correct Hindi sentences, she understands everything her father says, and she plays and jokes with other children. Her trouble is with the sounds themselves. She says "tup" for cup, "poon" for spoon, "tela" for kela (banana) and "tat" for cat; sounds made at the back of the mouth (k, g) come out at the front (t, d), and pairs of consonants are cut down to one. At nearly five, strangers understand very little of what she says, children at school have started to call her "baby", and she has begun to cry when she is not understood. Her hearing is normal, the roof of her mouth is intact, and the speech therapist finds that she can move her tongue and lips normally when asked to copy movements.

This is speech sound disorder, the DSM-5 successor of DSM-IV-TR's phonological disorder. Its criteria are short. There is a persistent difficulty in producing speech sounds that makes speech hard to understand or prevents the child from getting messages across in words; the difficulty limits effective communication and so gets in the way of social participation, schooling or work; it begins in the early developmental period; and it is not due to a congenital or acquired condition such as cerebral palsy, cleft palate, deafness or hearing loss, traumatic brain injury, or another medical or neurological condition. The exclusion is the heart of the diagnosis: speech sound disorder is the label for a child whose sound errors have no structural, neurological or sensory explanation.

Producing a speech sound needs two kinds of skill, and a child can fail at either. One is phonological knowledge, the child's grasp of the sound system of the language and of which contrasts change meaning (kela and tela are different words because k and t are different sounds). The other is motor: the ability to coordinate the articulators, the jaw, tongue and lips, with breathing and voice. Children who are still learning to talk simplify words in predictable, rule-governed ways, dropping final sounds, reducing clusters, or replacing back sounds with front ones. Aadhya's errors are of this phonological kind, a pattern rather than a set of separate mistakes, and her problem is that she has not outgrown patterns most children drop by four. An articulation error, by contrast, is a difficulty with the motor production of particular sounds; the commonest is the lisp, a mispronunciation of the hissing sounds s and z, in which the airstream escapes over the front or the side of the tongue, sometimes with a habit of pushing the tongue forward when swallowing. When the motor difficulty is one of planning and sequencing speech movements, the terms childhood apraxia of speech and verbal dyspraxia are used; such children may also have had trouble learning to chew, keep their mouth closed or blow their nose, and may be clumsy in other ways (developmental coordination disorder, Section 3.10.1).

When should a child's sounds be clear? DSM-5-TR gives rough norms for English. At age 2 perhaps only half of what a child says is intelligible to a stranger; by 3 most of it should be; and by 5 most sounds should be produced clearly and most words pronounced correctly by the standards of the child's community. A group of English sounds that children master late, the "late eight" (l, r, s, z, th, ch, the j-sound of "judge" and the zh-sound of "measure"), may be misarticulated up to about age 8 without concern if only one is affected. Each language has its own order of acquisition, so English norms cannot simply be applied to a child learning Hindi, Tamil or Bengali. Boys are affected somewhat more often than girls (DSM-5-TR gives ratios of about 1.5 to 1.8 boys for every girl).

Most children with speech sound disorder respond well to therapy and improve with age, so the disorder is often not lifelong. The outlook is poorer when a language disorder is present as well, and that combination raises the risk of a later specific learning disorder in reading (Section 3.9), because the same weak grasp of speech sounds that garbles a child's words also makes it hard to link letters to sounds. Some children carry a genetic condition that affects speech, such as Down syndrome or 22q11 deletion syndrome; and in 2001 a mutation in the gene FOXP2 was found to cause a severe speech and language disorder in a three-generation London family known as the KE family. When such a condition is present it is coded as well.

The differential diagnosis runs down the list of things that can garble speech for other reasons. Normal variation and dialect come first; bilingual children assessed only in English make more errors and seem less intelligible than they are. Hearing loss causes sound errors of its own. Structural problems such as a cleft palate let air escape through the nose. Dysarthria, a motor speech disorder caused by weakness or incoordination of the speech muscles, as in cerebral palsy, is told apart by other neurological signs and by the quality of the voice, although in children under 3 the distinction can be hard. And a child who speaks little may have selective mutism, the anxiety disorder taught in Section 6.4.8, in which a child who speaks normally at home is silent at school; selective mutism sometimes grows out of a speech disorder, when a child is ashamed of how she sounds.

Exam trap

Speech sound disorder (formerly phonological disorder) is a disorder of speech, not of language: vocabulary and grammar can be normal, as in Aadhya. It is excluded, not diagnosed, when the cause is cleft palate, cerebral palsy (dysarthria), deafness or brain injury. By age 3 most of a child's speech should be intelligible; by 5 most sounds should be clear.

Childhood-onset fluency disorder (stuttering)

Sara's parents in Kochi date the start of her trouble precisely. At three and a half, a few months after her brother was born, she began to repeat the first sounds of words, easily and without seeming to notice: "b-b-ball", "m-m-mummy". Her grandmother told everyone not to worry. Two years later the repetitions are joined by other things. Some sounds stretch ("sssssun"); sometimes her mouth is set for a word and nothing comes out at all, and she pushes, blinks hard and jerks her head until it does; and she has learned tricks to get round the words she fears, saying "my name is ... I am the girl in Class 1" instead of "Sara", because the s at the start of her own name is the hardest sound of all. She will not answer the telephone. In the school choir, however, she sings without a single break, and she talks fluently to the family cat. Her father stammered as a boy and still avoids making speeches at office functions.

Sara has childhood-onset fluency disorder, the DSM-5 name for stuttering (stammering in British usage; the two words mean the same thing). The first criterion describes a disturbance in the normal fluency and timing of speech that is inappropriate for the child's age and language skills, persists over time, and shows itself in frequent and marked occurrences of one or more of seven kinds of break:

  1. Sound and syllable repetitions ("b-b-ball", "ba-ba-banana").
  2. Prolongations of sounds, consonants as well as vowels ("sssssun", "aaaaand").
  3. Broken words, with a pause inside a word ("be ... cause").
  4. Audible or silent blocking, a filled or unfilled pause in which the speaker is stuck and no sound, or only a strained sound, comes out.
  5. Circumlocution, substituting words or phrases to avoid a word the speaker expects to stutter on (Sara's "the girl in Class 1").
  6. Words produced with an excess of physical tension.
  7. Monosyllabic whole-word repetitions ("I-I-I-I see him").

The second criterion requires consequences: anxiety about speaking, or limits on effective communication, social participation, schoolwork or work. The third requires onset in the early developmental period; when dysfluency begins in adolescence or adult life it is coded instead as adult-onset fluency disorder (F98.5), which is usually tied to a neurological insult, a medical condition or another mental disorder. The fourth excludes dysfluency due to a speech-motor or sensory deficit, to a neurological insult such as a stroke, tumour or head injury, or to another medical condition, and dysfluency better explained by another mental disorder.

Several features make stuttering unlike any other speech problem. It varies from situation to situation and is usually worst when there is special pressure to communicate: reading a report aloud in class, giving one's name, a job interview. It is often absent during oral reading, singing, or talking to pets or to objects, exactly as in Sara's choir and her conversations with the cat. As the child becomes aware of it, fearful anticipation develops: the speaker scans ahead for dangerous words and avoids them, changes the rate of speech, or avoids whole situations such as the telephone or public speaking. Stuttering is often accompanied by movements, such as eye blinks, tics, tremors of the lips or face, jerks of the head, odd breathing movements and clenched fists, which begin as efforts to force a word out and become habits of their own. Stress and anxiety make all of this worse, but this does not mean that anxiety is the cause, a point that matters for the history of the theories below.

The course is well documented. Developmental stuttering begins by age 6 in 80 to 90 percent of those affected, with onset anywhere from about 2 to 7 years of age; it may come on gradually or quite suddenly. Typically it starts with repetitions of first consonants, of the first words of a phrase, or of long words, while the child is unaware of them; as it progresses the breaks become more frequent, fall on the most meaningful words, and draw the avoidance and emotional reactions that Sara now shows. The encouraging fact is that most children recover: longitudinal studies find that 65 to 85 percent of children grow out of it, and severity at age 8 predicts who will recover and who will carry the stutter into adolescence and beyond. Boys stutter more often than girls, and the risk among first-degree relatives of a person who stutters is more than three times the risk in the general population. Mutations in four genes have been found in some families, and brain-imaging studies show structural and functional differences in children who stutter, so the modern view is that stuttering is a disorder of the timing and control of speech movements with a strong genetic contribution.

It was not always seen this way. The American speech pathologist Wendell Johnson (1906 to 1965), who himself stuttered, argued from the 1930s that stuttering begins not in the child but in the listener. All young children are sometimes dysfluent, he reasoned; when anxious parents label normal repetitions as "stuttering" and correct them, the child becomes self-conscious, struggles to avoid the breaks, and the struggle becomes the disorder. This was the diagnosogenic theory, the theory that the diagnosis itself causes the condition. It shaped advice to parents for decades ("never draw attention to the child's speech") and was tested in one of the most notorious experiments in the history of speech science.

Key study: the "Monster Study" (1939)

In 1939 Johnson's graduate student Mary Tudor ran an experiment at the Iowa Soldiers' Orphans' Home in Davenport. Twenty-two orphans aged 5 to 15, ten of whom already stuttered, were divided into groups. Six children with normal speech were told, over sessions from January to May, that they were beginning to stutter and must correct themselves; stutterers in one group were told their speech was fine. None of the fluent children became a stutterer in the clinical sense, but those who were told they were stuttering became self-conscious and reluctant to speak, their schoolwork fell off, and some said in later life that they had carried speech and emotional difficulties ever since. The study was never published. A newspaper exposed it in 2001, the University of Iowa apologised the same year, and in 2007 the State of Iowa paid $925,000 to seven of the former children. The experiment is now taught as a lesson in research ethics, and its data do not support the diagnosogenic theory.

Two misunderstandings follow from the older view and are still common. Stuttering is not caused by anxiety, although stuttering causes a great deal of anxiety: a child who has been laughed at for years learns to fear speaking, and adults who stutter have high rates of social anxiety, but treating the anxiety alone does not usually remove the stutter. And stuttering has nothing to do with disordered thinking; the person knows exactly what he wants to say, and Section 2.4.6 makes the same point about stammering as a sign in the mental status examination.

The differential diagnosis begins with normal dysfluency. Young children trip over their words all the time, and their slips have a characteristic look: whole-word or phrase repetitions without strain ("I want, I want ice cream"), incomplete phrases, interjections ("um", "er"), unfilled pauses and parenthetical remarks. What marks the beginning of stuttering is a rise in the frequency and complexity of breaks as the child grows older, part-word repetitions, prolongations and blocks, signs of physical tension and the first efforts to avoid. A child whose stutter shows when he reads aloud may be wrongly thought to have a reading disorder (Section 3.9), because timed tests of oral reading measure fluency, not decoding. Bilingual children may be dysfluent in a language they are still learning; a fluency disorder appears in both languages. Stuttering can be a side effect of medication, recognised by its timing. The repetitive vocalisations of Tourette's disorder (Section 3.10) differ in their nature and timing: a vocal tic interrupts speech with a sound that has nothing to do with the word being said. And cluttering, listed separately in ICD-10 (F98.6), is a rapid, erratic, jerky rate of speech with faulty phrasing that makes speech hard to understand, but without the repetitions, blocks and struggle of stuttering; unlike the person who stutters, the person who clutters is often unaware of the problem.

Treatment is the work of the speech-language pathologist. For preschool children the best-studied approach is the Lidcombe Program, developed in Australia, in which a clinician trains a parent to praise stutter-free speech and, now and then, to ask gently for a stuttered word to be said again; a randomised trial published in 2005 found it efficacious for children of preschool age. For older children and adults there are two families of method. Fluency shaping teaches a new way of speaking, slow and prolonged at first with gentle voice onsets and easy breathing, which is then brought gradually up to a normal rate. Stuttering modification accepts that some stuttering will occur and teaches the speaker to stutter more easily and openly, with less tension and less avoidance. The behavioural techniques of regulated breathing, published by Nathan Azrin and R. Gregory Nunn in 1974 as an adaptation of habit reversal, and the older method of massed or negative practice, are taught in Book 2, Sections 4.9.1 and 4.9.3. Devices that alter how speakers hear their own voice help some people. No medicine is approved for stuttering; drugs that block dopamine have been tried, but speech treatment remains the mainstay.

Clinical link: the king who stammered

Prince Albert, Duke of York, later King George VI, stammered from childhood. His halting speech at the closing of the British Empire Exhibition at Wembley in 1925 was painful to hear, and in 1926 he began to work with Lionel Logue (1880 to 1953), an Australian speech therapist practising in London. With breathing and voice exercises and much practice, the king went on to deliver the wartime broadcasts of the Second World War, beginning in September 1939. The story was told in the film The King's Speech (2010). It illustrates three textbook facts: the stammer began in childhood, it was worst under the pressure of public speaking, and it improved with speech practice but never vanished.

Exam trap

Stuttering is childhood-onset fluency disorder; onset in adolescence or adulthood is adult-onset fluency disorder. It begins by age 6 in 80 to 90 percent, and 65 to 85 percent recover. It is often absent in singing, in reading aloud and in talking to pets. Anxiety results from stuttering rather than causing it, and Johnson's diagnosogenic theory (parents' labelling causes it) is discredited. Cluttering is rapid, disorganised speech without blocks or struggle. Stuttering is a speech disorder, not a language disorder or a thought disorder.

Social (pragmatic) communication disorder

Parth, the twelve-year-old from Kolkata, is the hardest of the four to recognise, because on a test of vocabulary or grammar he would score above his age. His difficulty shows only when language has to be used with another person. When his science teacher asked, "Can you open the window?", he answered the question he had been asked ("Yes") rather than the request he had been given. He greets his principal with the same "Hi, what's up?" he uses with his friends, and then explains the plot of a film to him in the same breathless way. When a classmate does not follow one of his stories, which begin in the middle and leave out who the people are, Parth simply says it again, word for word, a little louder. He interrupts, or waits so long to take his turn that the conversation has moved on. When his friends say of a boy who blames the umpire for his own dropped catch, "naach na jaane, aangan tedha" (the one who cannot dance blames the crooked courtyard), Parth asks what dancing has to do with cricket. At four he was slow to talk and had a year of speech therapy. But he has never lined up objects, never been upset by a change of route or routine, has no all-consuming special interest, and is not bothered by noise or textures; he plays cricket in the lane every evening and likes to be with other boys, although they find him "weird".

This is social (pragmatic) communication disorder (SPCD), a diagnosis introduced in DSM-5. Its first criterion describes persistent difficulties in the social use of verbal and nonverbal communication, and, unusually, it requires all four of the following:

  1. Deficits in using communication for social purposes, such as greeting people and sharing information, in a way that suits the social context.
  2. An impaired ability to change one's communication to match the context or the listener: speaking differently in a classroom and on a playground, to a child and to an adult, and avoiding overly formal language where it does not belong.
  3. Difficulty following the rules of conversation and storytelling: taking turns, rephrasing when one is misunderstood, and using words and signals to regulate the interaction.
  4. Difficulty understanding what is not said outright, such as making inferences, and understanding non-literal or ambiguous language: idioms, humour, metaphors and words whose meaning depends on the context.

The second criterion requires that these deficits limit effective communication, social participation, relationships, school or work. The third requires onset in the early developmental period, with a qualification borrowed from the autism criteria: the deficits may not become fully apparent until the social demands on the child exceed his limited capacities. The fourth contains two exclusions that define the disorder. The difficulties must not be due to another medical or neurological condition or to low ability in word structure and grammar (a child who cannot build sentences will of course struggle in conversation, but his problem is a language disorder), and they must not be better explained by autism spectrum disorder, intellectual developmental disorder, global developmental delay or another mental disorder.

Pragmatics, as Section 3.4.1 explained, is the level of language that uses all the others in a social setting, so SPCD cannot be diagnosed until the others have had a chance to develop. DSM-5-TR therefore says it is rarely diagnosable before about age 4; by 4 or 5 most children have enough speech and language for specific social communication deficits to be seen, and milder forms may not become obvious until early adolescence, when conversation and friendships become more complex and more subtle. Parth's difficulties were noticed seriously only in Class 6, when his friends began to tease each other in the quick, ironic way of older boys. The most common associated feature is exactly the history Parth has: a delay in reaching language milestones and earlier, if not current, problems with structural language. ADHD, emotional and behavioural problems and specific learning disorders are more frequent than usual, and a family history of autism, communication disorders or specific learning disorder increases the risk. Outcomes vary. Some children improve a great deal; others carry the difficulties into adult life, and even those who improve may be left with lasting problems in relationships and in skills that depend on pragmatics, such as written expression and reading comprehension.

The decisive differential is autism, and the rule DSM-5-TR gives is the single most important fact about SPCD. Autism spectrum disorder is the primary consideration in anyone with social communication deficits, and the two are separated by the presence, in autism, of restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour, interests or activities (criterion B of autism, Section 3.5.4). The social communication problems in SPCD may be milder, but they are of the same kind. The catch is that repetitive behaviours may be obvious only in early childhood. A person who lined up toys, flapped his hands and screamed at changes of routine at the age of three, but no longer does any of these, still meets criterion B "by history" and receives a diagnosis of autism, not SPCD. Autism therefore supersedes SPCD whenever its criteria are met, and SPCD should be considered only when neither the present picture nor a careful developmental history shows the restricted, repetitive behaviours of autism. That is why Parth's clinician spent as long on his toddler years as on his present behaviour.

The other differentials are briefer. ADHD can cause social communication problems of its own (interrupting, talking too much, missing what was said), and the two can coexist. Social anxiety disorder (Section 6.3) can look similar, and the difference is one of timing: the person with SPCD never had effective social communication, whereas the socially anxious person developed the skills normally but cannot use them because of fear. In intellectual developmental disorder and global developmental delay, social communication is often weak, and SPCD is diagnosed as well only when the social communication deficits clearly go beyond the person's general intellectual level.

The idea behind SPCD is older than its name. In 1983 the Swiss-born American child neurologist Isabelle Rapin (1927 to 2017) and her colleague Doris Allen described a "semantic-pragmatic deficit syndrome" among children with developmental language disorders: talkative children with word-finding problems who could not keep a conversation on its topic. In 1987 the British psychologist Dorothy Bishop (born 1952) and a colleague adopted the term "semantic-pragmatic disorder" in their classification of childhood language disorders. Bishop later argued that such children lie on the borderlands between autism and specific language impairment, and her further work, including a checklist completed by parents and teachers to measure pragmatic skill (the Assessment book), led to the term pragmatic language impairment. Whether these children form a separate disorder or are simply at the mild end of the autism spectrum is still debated. Critics of SPCD point out that it is defined largely by what it lacks, that its boundary with autism rests on one criterion that depends on parents' memories of the toddler years, and that much less research has been done on it than on autism. Defenders reply that a child like Parth, with real and disabling social communication problems but no repetitive behaviour, needs a name, and that before 2013 such children were often labelled with the vague PDD-NOS (Section 3.5.2) or given no fitting diagnosis at all. The treatment, either way, is speech and language therapy aimed at conversational skills, with social skills teaching of the kind Book 2, Section 10.5.5 describes.

Exam trap

SPCD is new in DSM-5. It shares autism's social communication deficits but has no restricted, repetitive behaviour, now or in the past; if such behaviour was ever present, the diagnosis is autism. It needs all four pragmatic deficits, is rarely diagnosed before age 4, and is excluded when the problem is really poor grammar and vocabulary (language disorder). Versus social anxiety: SPCD never had the skills; the anxious child has the skills but fear stops their use.

When a child does not speak: four reasons for silence

Every clinician who works with children meets the same referral again and again: "He does not talk." The four children of this section show that "not talking" can mean very different things, and the same complaint can come from several other conditions as well. The way to sort them out is to ask one question in three parts. Can this person produce speech at all? Is the language system itself, the code of words and grammar, intact? And if the answer to both is yes, what stops the person from using it with others? Put differently, where does the barrier lie: in the channel, in the code, in the social use of the code, or in something that blocks a perfectly good code from being used?

Start with the person for whom the barrier is the spoken-language channel itself. Some people are mute from birth or early childhood because spoken language never became available to them. The clearest example is a child born profoundly deaf who has not been given a sign language: such a child has no access to the sounds from which spoken language is learned and, without sign, may reach school age with almost no language at all. Another is a person with severe cerebral palsy or another neuromotor condition so severe that the speech muscles cannot be controlled (anarthria); here understanding may be complete, but no intelligible speech can be produced. "Inherently mute" is a phrase used in exam questions, not a DSM-5-TR term, but it describes these people well. For them language, at least in its spoken form, really is the barrier to communication, and the solution is to give them another channel: a sign language, writing, or augmentative and alternative communication (AAC), the picture boards, symbol systems and speech-generating devices taught in Book 2, Section 10.5.4.

Contrast three children for whom language is not the barrier. In selective mutism a child who speaks freely and normally at home falls silent at school or with strangers, for at least a month and not just during the first month at a new school; speech and language are usually normal, and the barrier is anxiety. Section 6.4.8 teaches the disorder. In autism spectrum disorder structural language may be absent, delayed or entirely fluent, but its social use is always impaired, and it comes with restricted, repetitive behaviour: the barrier is social communication (Section 3.5). In specific learning disorder spoken communication is usually unaffected; the barrier is the written code of reading, spelling and number (Section 3.9). And mutism can appear as a sign in a child or adult who spoke normally until the illness began, in catatonia, depressive stupor and some dissociative and conversion states (Sections 2.4.6 and 4.5.7). Table 3.12 brings all of these together.

Table 3.12 Why is this person not communicating? The conditions sorted by where the barrier lies.
ConditionCan produce speech?Language itself intact?Where the barrier lies
Inherent mutism: congenital deafness without sign; anarthriaNo, never acquiredDeaf child without sign: language not acquired; anarthria: understanding may be intactThe spoken-language channel itself; needs sign or AAC
Hearing loss, milder (audiology)Yes, often unclearDelayed if not detectedThe input channel
Language disorder (3.4.2)YesNo: vocabulary, grammar, discourse impairedThe code
Speech sound disorder; stuttering (3.4.3, 3.4.4)Yes, but unclear or brokenYesSound production or fluency
Social (pragmatic) communication disorder (3.4.5)Yes, fluentStructure yes; social use noSocial use, without repetitive behaviour
Autism spectrum disorder (3.5)From none to fluentVaries; social use always impairedSocial communication, plus repetitive behaviour
Selective mutism (6.4.8)Yes, in safe settingsUsually yesAnxiety in particular settings
Mutism as a sign: catatonia, stupor, conversion (2.4.6)Yes, before the illnessYesThe current mental state
Intellectual developmental disorder (3.2)Depends on severityDelayed in line with other skillsGeneral intellectual level
Specific learning disorder (3.9)YesSpoken language usually normalReading, writing or number

Three practical rules come out of the table. The first is that hearing is always tested first, because a hearing loss can imitate almost every other row; every one of the children in this section had a hearing test before anything else was done. The second is that a non-verbal measure of ability, observation of play and a history of social interest are what separate the code-level problems (language disorder, intellectual developmental disorder) from the social ones (autism, SPCD). The third is that the setting matters: a child who talks at home but not at school has a problem of use, not of capacity, and that single fact points away from every developmental row and towards selective mutism.

Look back at the four children in these terms. Vihaan's barrier is the code; his speech sounds are clear, but his words and grammar are thin (language disorder). Aadhya's code is rich, but the sounds that carry it are distorted (speech sound disorder). Sara's code and sounds are both intact, but their flow breaks down (childhood-onset fluency disorder). Parth's channel, code and flow are all perfect, but he cannot use them to fit a listener and a situation, and he has no repetitive behaviour (SPCD). Not one of them is "inherently mute", and not one is autistic, which is the subject of the next section. A related question, in which neurodevelopmental disorder language development is most commonly compromised, is answered in Section 3.6.2.

Exam trap: "Language is a barrier to communication for ..."

The keyed answer is the inherently mute person: for someone who has no capacity for spoken language, the spoken-language channel itself is blocked, so language is literally the barrier and another channel (sign, writing, AAC) is needed. In selective mutism language is intact and anxiety blocks its use in some settings. In autism the difficulty is the social use of communication, whatever the level of language. In a learning disorder spoken communication is usually unaffected; the difficulty is with reading, writing or arithmetic. The stem is loosely worded, so learn the reasoning (where does the barrier lie?) rather than the letter. (One printed explanation of this question ends with a stray "C"; that is not the key.)

3.5 Autism Spectrum Disorder I: From Kanner to the Spectrum, and the DiagnosisHigh yield · 5 Qs

Each of the children in Section 3.4 had trouble with one layer of communication, and each of them wanted to communicate. Vihaan pointed to show his mother the aeroplane; Sara pushed and blinked to get her words out because she badly wanted to be heard; even Parth, who misread every hint, liked to be among the other boys. Autism is different in two ways. It always reaches the deepest layer, the social use of communication, whatever the state of speech and language, and it always brings with it something that no communication disorder has: a restricted, repetitive way of behaving, of being interested and of responding to the senses. This section follows the idea from the day it was named to its present form as a single spectrum, then teaches the diagnosis criterion by criterion through one child, and ends with the conditions that can be mistaken for it. Who has autism, why, and what helps are the business of Section 3.6.

Naming autism: Bleuler, Kanner, Asperger and the triad

The word is older than the disorder. In 1911 the Swiss psychiatrist Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939), in his book on the group of schizophrenias, used autism (from the Greek autos, self) for one of its fundamental symptoms: the patient's withdrawal from reality into an inner world of his own, one of the "four As" taught in Section 4.1.4. For three decades the word belonged to adult psychiatry. Then, in 1943, Leo Kanner (1894 to 1981), an Austrian-born child psychiatrist at Johns Hopkins in Baltimore, published "Autistic disturbances of affective contact" in the journal Nervous Child. It described eleven children, eight boys and three girls, who shared two striking features from the start of life: an extreme autistic aloneness, a failure to relate to people in the ordinary way, and an anxiously obsessive desire for the maintenance of sameness. Kanner borrowed Bleuler's word but gave it a new meaning, a disorder of childhood present from the beginning rather than a retreat from a world once shared, and from 1944 he called the condition early infantile autism. In 1944 the Viennese paediatrician Hans Asperger (1906 to 1980), working independently, described boys whose speech was fluent, even pedantic, but who talked at people rather than with them, pursued narrow interests with great intensity and were often clumsy; he called their condition "autistic psychopathy". A Moscow psychiatrist, Grunya Efimovna Sukhareva (1891 to 1981), had described six similar boys as early as 1925, but her paper was little known outside Russia for most of the century. The rest of the story, from Kanner's first patient to Asperger's record in wartime Vienna, is told in Book 2, Section 10.5.1, and Table 3.13 sets out the people and what each contributed.

Table 3.13 The people who shaped the idea of autism.
PersonYearContributionWhy it matters
Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939)1911Coined "autism" for withdrawal into an inner world, a symptom of schizophreniaThe word, not the childhood disorder
Grunya Sukhareva (1891 to 1981)1925Six boys with "schizoid psychopathy" in MoscowAn early description, long overlooked
Leo Kanner (1894 to 1981)1943Eleven children (8 boys, 3 girls): autistic aloneness and insistence on sameness; "early infantile autism" from 1944First described infantile autism
Hans Asperger (1906 to 1980)1944"Autistic psychopathy": fluent speech, one-sided talk, narrow interests, clumsinessThe verbally able form
Bernard Rimland (1928 to 2006)1964Infantile Autism: argued for a biological causeChallenged blaming parents
Bruno Bettelheim (1903 to 1990)1967The Empty Fortress: the "refrigerator mother"Influential, then discredited
Susan Folstein and Michael Rutter1977First twin study of autism (21 pairs)Showed a genetic basis (Section 3.6.4)
Lorna Wing (1928 to 2014) and Judith Gould1979Triad of impairments: social interaction, communication, imaginationThe spectrum idea; DSM-IV's three groups
Lorna Wing1981"Asperger's syndrome: a clinical account"Brought Asperger's work into English

The idea that turned autism into a spectrum came from London. In a survey of children in Camberwell, a district of south London, Lorna Wing (1928 to 2014) and Judith Gould found in 1979 that Kanner's classic cases were only the most obvious members of a much larger group of children who shared, in milder or more varied forms, a triad of impairments: in social interaction, in communication and in imagination, the last shown in a narrow, repetitive pattern of activities in place of pretend play. In 1981 Wing introduced the term "Asperger's syndrome" to English-speaking readers, and the notion of an "autistic spectrum" followed. The triad became the backbone of the diagnostic manuals for the next thirty years, as Section 3.5.2 shows.

Between Kanner and Wing came the darkest chapter in the history of the idea. Kanner had noticed that many of his children's parents were highly educated and, as he saw them, emotionally reserved, and in 1949 he described them in terms of emotional refrigeration. The Austrian-born Bruno Bettelheim (1903 to 1990), director of the University of Chicago's Orthogenic School for disturbed children, built this into a theory in The Empty Fortress (1967): a cold, rejecting mother, the refrigerator mother, left her baby feeling that the world was hostile, and the child withdrew into autism as a defence. The treatment that followed from the theory was to separate children from their parents. The theory collapsed for four reasons. First, the psychologist Bernard Rimland (1928 to 2006), himself the father of an autistic son, argued in Infantile Autism (1964) that the evidence pointed to the brain, noting among other things that autistic children were born to warm parents as well as reserved ones, their brothers and sisters were usually unaffected, and the condition was far commoner in boys. Second, twin and family studies, beginning with Susan Folstein and Michael Rutter's study of 21 twin pairs in 1977, showed that autism is strongly genetic (the numbers are in Section 3.6.4). Third, studies of larger and less selected samples found that the parents of autistic children did not differ in any substantial way from other parents; in a 1975 London study, for example, Rutter's group compared the parents of autistic children with the parents of children who had a severe receptive language disorder and found no evidence that the parents of the autistic children were colder or less responsive; Kanner's educated parents reflected the families who could reach a famous clinic in Baltimore. Fourth, removing children from their families did not cure them, and Bettelheim's claims of success were never substantiated. The theory's lasting legacy was a generation of parents made to feel guilty for their child's disability.

One last echo of the past belongs here. When Victor of Aveyron, whose story opened this chapter, rocked to and fro, ignored the people around him and showed no interest in communicating, he was behaving in ways that later readers have recognised as autistic. Whether he was autistic, intellectually disabled, damaged by years alone in the forest, or some mixture of these can never be settled from records two centuries old, and the opening of this chapter leaves the question open for that reason.

Exam trap: who first described infantile autism?

Leo Kanner, in 1943. Eugen Bleuler only coined the word autism, in 1911, for a symptom of schizophrenia. Hans Asperger described the verbally fluent form a year after Kanner, in 1944. The other names in the option lists belong elsewhere: Kurt Schneider is known for the first-rank symptoms of schizophrenia (Section 4.1.6); Norman Cameron for over-inclusive thinking and Kurt Goldstein for concrete thinking (Section 2.4.5); and "Jasper" stands for Karl Jaspers, the founder of descriptive psychopathology (Section 2.1.3). The refrigerator-mother theory is Bettelheim's, and the triad of impairments is Wing and Gould's.

From infantile autism to one spectrum

The official manuals took a long time to catch up with Kanner. DSM-I (1952) and DSM-II (1968) had no category for autism at all: an autistic child could be diagnosed only with "schizophrenic reaction, childhood type" (DSM-I) or "schizophrenia, childhood type" (DSM-II), a reflection of the belief, dominant until the 1970s, that autism was the earliest form of schizophrenia. Two lines of research ended that belief. Studies of age at onset, above all those published in 1971 by the British child psychiatrist Israel Kolvin (1929 to 2002) and his colleagues, showed two separate peaks, one in the first years of life and one in later childhood and adolescence, with very few cases between; and the children with early onset did not develop hallucinations and delusions, did not have a family history of schizophrenia, and a sizeable minority later developed epilepsy. Autism was something else.

DSM-III (1980) was the first edition to name it. Under the new umbrella of the pervasive developmental disorders (PDDs), a term chosen to say that the disorder affects many areas of development at once, it listed infantile autism with six criteria: onset before 30 months of age; a pervasive lack of responsiveness to other people; gross deficits in language development; if speech was present, peculiar patterns such as echolalia, metaphorical language and pronoun reversal; bizarre responses to the environment, such as resistance to change or peculiar attachments to objects; and the absence of the delusions, hallucinations and incoherence of schizophrenia. Children whose problems began after 30 months were given "childhood onset pervasive developmental disorder". The DSM-III definition was strict and fitted only children who looked like Kanner's.

DSM-III-R (1987) renamed the condition autistic disorder, dropped the 30-month rule in favour of onset in infancy or childhood, and replaced the short list with sixteen items in three groups, of which at least eight were needed, at least two from the social group, one from the group covering communication and imaginative activity, and one from the group covering a restricted repertoire of activities and interests. The three groups were Wing and Gould's triad written into the manual. A residual category, pervasive developmental disorder not otherwise specified (PDD-NOS), took children with some autistic features who did not meet the full criteria.

DSM-IV (1994) and its text revision, DSM-IV-TR (2000), kept the triad but split the PDDs into five disorders. Autistic disorder needed at least six of twelve items, at least two from qualitative impairment in social interaction, one from qualitative impairment in communication and one from restricted, repetitive and stereotyped patterns of behaviour, interests and activities, together with delay or abnormal functioning before age 3 in social interaction, in language as used for social communication, or in symbolic or imaginative play. Asperger's disorder needed the social and the restricted-repetitive criteria but, by definition, no clinically significant delay in language (single words by age 2 and communicative phrases by age 3) and no clinically significant delay in cognitive development. Rett's disorder and childhood disintegrative disorder, both described below, completed the group, and PDD-NOS, which included "atypical autism" (autism with late onset, atypical symptoms or symptoms below the threshold), caught the rest. ICD-10 (1992) had a parallel set of categories under F84, set out in the first two columns of Table 3.14.

DSM-5 (2013) swept the subtypes away. A single autism spectrum disorder replaced autistic disorder, Asperger's disorder, childhood disintegrative disorder and PDD-NOS, and a note in the criteria directs that anyone with a well-established DSM-IV diagnosis of autistic disorder, Asperger's disorder or PDD-NOS should now be given the diagnosis of autism spectrum disorder. There were three main reasons for the change. The first was that clinicians could not agree on the subtypes. In a study of 2,102 children aged 4 to 18 at twelve university-based autism centres in North America, published in 2012, Catherine Lord and her colleagues found that children with very similar scores on standardised measures were given different subtype diagnoses at different centres, depending on how each centre weighed verbal IQ and language level: the line between autistic disorder, Asperger's disorder and PDD-NOS was drawn differently from clinic to clinic, even in expert hands. The second was that the social and the communication problems proved to be one thing, not two. The communication group of DSM-IV largely described the social use of language, and studies of how symptoms cluster found that social and communication symptoms formed a single dimension, so they were merged into one domain, criterion A. Language delay itself, which is not specific to autism, was taken out of the criteria and turned into a specifier (Section 3.5.5). The third was that something important had been missing: unusual responses to sensory input, long described by parents and autistic adults, were added for the first time as part of the second domain, criterion B. Figure 3.8 shows how the triad became two domains.

Those with social communication deficits but no restricted, repetitive behaviour, who might once have received PDD-NOS, were pointed to the new social (pragmatic) communication disorder (Section 3.4.5). ADHD, which DSM-IV did not allow to be diagnosed alongside a PDD, could now be diagnosed together with autism, an important change given how often the two co-occur (Section 3.6.2).

The change was fiercely debated. Parents feared that children would lose the services attached to a diagnosis, and many adults diagnosed with Asperger's disorder had built an identity and a community around the name and did not want to lose it. Early studies gave alarming and reassuring answers. Applying a draft of the DSM-5 criteria to checklists from the DSM-IV field trial, James McPartland, Brian Reichow and Fred Volkmar of Yale reported in 2012 that only about 61 percent of people with a clinical PDD diagnosis met the new criteria, and only about a quarter of those with Asperger's disorder or PDD-NOS. Using richer data from parent interviews on more than 4,000 children with DSM-IV diagnoses, Marisela Huerta, Catherine Lord and colleagues reported the same year that the DSM-5 criteria identified 91 percent of children with clinical DSM-IV diagnoses. The "grandfather" note quoted above was the manual's answer to the fear: nobody with an established diagnosis was to lose it.

DSM-5-TR (2022) kept the structure and made one clarifying change that matters for exams. DSM-5 had said that criterion A was "manifested by the following" three deficits, which some readers took to mean any one of them. DSM-5-TR changed the wording to "manifested by all of the following", to keep the threshold where it was meant to be. ICD-11, in force since 2022, followed the same path: a single autism spectrum disorder (6A02) with no Asperger syndrome, coded further by whether a disorder of intellectual development is present and by how far functional language is impaired.

Table 3.14 The autism categories in four classifications, read across each row.
