Two of the old PDDs deserve a fuller account, because they are still examined and because they show why the spectrum was drawn where it was. Childhood disintegrative disorder was first described in 1908 by Theodor Heller (1869 to 1938), a Viennese special educator, who called it dementia infantilis; it was long known as Heller's syndrome. Its defining feature is a late and dramatic loss of skills. In DSM-IV-TR the child had to develop apparently normally for at least the first two years, in communication, relationships, play and adaptive behaviour, and then, before age 10, lose previously acquired skills in at least two areas: expressive or receptive language, social skills or adaptive behaviour, bowel or bladder control, play, or motor skills. After the loss the child showed autistic abnormalities in at least two of the three areas of the triad. DSM-5-TR no longer has a separate disorder. It records such a child as having autism spectrum disorder with regression after at least two years of normal development, notes that this is rare and much more unusual than the early losses seen in many autistic toddlers (Section 3.5.6), and warns that it calls for extensive medical investigation, because conditions such as Landau-Kleffner syndrome (Section 3.4.2) and the syndrome of continuous spike and waves during slow-wave sleep can cause the same picture. Losses of self-care, toileting and motor skills point the same way.

Rett syndrome takes its name from Andreas Rett (1924 to 1997), a German-born paediatrician working in Vienna, who in 1966 described 22 girls with the same unusual history; he is said to have noticed the condition when two girls sitting in his waiting room made the same hand-washing movements. His German paper went largely unread until 1983, when the Swedish paediatric neurologist Bengt Hagberg (1923 to 2015) and colleagues published a report of 35 girls from three countries in English and named the syndrome after him. The course is distinctive. After apparently normal early development, usually up to somewhere between 6 and 18 months, development stalls and then goes backwards. The child loses purposeful use of her hands and develops repetitive, stereotyped hand movements, wringing, washing, clapping or mouthing, at the midline of the body; she loses whatever speech she had; head growth slows, so that a head of normal size at birth becomes small (acquired microcephaly); walking becomes unsteady or is never achieved; breathing becomes irregular, with episodes of over-breathing and breath-holding while awake; and seizures are common. After the regression the condition stabilises, and many women live into adult life with severe intellectual and physical disability. In DSM-IV-TR the criteria spelled out this timetable: normal development through the first five months, then slowing of head growth between 5 and 48 months, loss of hand skills between 5 and 30 months with stereotyped hand movements, early loss of social engagement, poorly coordinated gait or trunk movements, and severely impaired language with severe psychomotor retardation.

Why did DSM-5 take Rett syndrome out of the autism group? Because in 1999 Ruthie Amir, Huda Zoghbi and their colleagues at Baylor College of Medicine in Houston showed that most cases are caused by mutations in a single gene, MECP2, on the X chromosome, which encodes a protein (methyl-CpG-binding protein 2) that regulates the activity of many other genes in the developing brain. The mutations usually arise anew rather than being inherited. A girl has a second X chromosome carrying a working copy of the gene, so in many of her cells the normal copy is active; a boy with the mutation has no such back-up, and affected boys usually have a severe brain disorder from birth and die in infancy, often within the first two years. That is why Rett syndrome is seen almost only in girls, at a rate of roughly 1 in 10,000 to 15,000 female births. Once a condition has a known genetic cause, it is better classified by that cause than by the behaviour it produces for a time. And the autistic picture is temporary: social withdrawal is marked during the regressive phase, typically between ages 1 and 4, but afterwards most girls become more socially interested, and autistic features cease to be a major concern. DSM-5-TR therefore warns that autism should be diagnosed in Rett syndrome only when all its criteria are met, and when they are, the diagnosis is recorded as "autism spectrum disorder associated with Rett syndrome" using the specifier taught in Section 3.5.5.

Exam trap

DSM-5 merged autistic disorder, Asperger's disorder, childhood disintegrative disorder and PDD-NOS into one autism spectrum disorder. Rett syndrome was not merged: it is a genetic condition (MECP2, X chromosome, almost only girls, loss of purposeful hand use with hand-wringing, loss of speech, slowing head growth, regression after early normal development). Childhood disintegrative disorder (Heller, 1908) is regression after at least two years of normal development. Wing and Gould's triad (three groups) became DSM-5's two domains, and sensory reactivity was added. DSM-5-TR clarified that criterion A needs all three deficits.

Criterion A: social communication and social interaction

Ayaan is 3 years and 4 months old, the only child of Laila, a 35-year-old primary-school teacher, and Daniyal, a 38-year-old accountant. The family lives in a flat in Hyderabad with Daniyal's mother. Their paediatrician has referred Ayaan to the child guidance clinic of a government teaching hospital, and the referral letter gives the parents' complaint in their own words: "He does not talk and does not listen."

The clinic psychologist begins, as every autism assessment should, not with his speech but with the way he relates to people. The picture his parents give, and that the psychologist sees for herself in the playroom, is consistent. Ayaan rarely looks at faces. When he wants juice he does not ask, point or look at his mother; he takes her by the wrist, leads her to the fridge and puts her hand on the handle, as if her hand were a tool for opening doors. He does not point to show her things that interest him, a passing bus or a bird on the balcony, and when she points to something and says "Look, Ayaan!", he does not follow her finger. He does not turn when his name is called, so persistently that his parents feared he was deaf; but a hearing test, using otoacoustic emissions and brainstem evoked response audiometry, was normal. He does not wave bye-bye. At family gatherings he ignores his cousins entirely and plays alone, and nobody has ever seen him feed a doll, pretend that a block is a car or a cup holds tea.

These are the signs that criterion A describes. It requires persistent deficits in social communication and social interaction across multiple contexts, shown in all three of the following areas, either now or by history. Each is described in DSM-5-TR as a range, from subtle to severe, and the examples are meant as illustrations, not a checklist.

  1. Deficits in social-emotional reciprocity (A1). Social-emotional reciprocity is the back-and-forth of social life: noticing another person, sharing thoughts, interests and feelings with them, and responding to what they offer. At the severe end the child neither starts nor responds to social interaction; in the middle there is reduced sharing of interests, emotions or feelings; at the subtle end there is an odd social approach and a failure of ordinary back-and-forth conversation. In young children it shows as little or no initiation of interaction, no sharing of emotion and little imitation of others. What language there is tends to be one-sided, used to request or to label rather than to comment, share or chat.
  2. Deficits in nonverbal communicative behaviours used for social interaction (A2). This covers eye contact, gesture, facial expression, body orientation and the tune of the voice, and above all how they are fitted together with speech. At one end there is a total lack of facial expression and nonverbal communication; in the middle, abnormal eye contact and body language and difficulty understanding and using gestures; at the subtle end, verbal and nonverbal communication that are poorly integrated, so that a fluent adult's "body language" looks wooden, exaggerated or oddly timed. Eye contact is judged against the norms of the person's culture.
  3. Deficits in developing, maintaining and understanding relationships (A3). At one end there is no interest in other children at all; in the middle, difficulty sharing imaginative play or making friends; at the subtle end, difficulty adjusting behaviour to suit the social context. These are judged against norms for age, gender and culture.

Two phrases in the criterion carry a lot of weight. "Across multiple contexts" means that the deficits are not confined to one setting or one relationship: a child who is withdrawn only at school, or only with a particular teacher, has some other problem. "Currently or by history" means that the clinician may count deficits that were clearly present earlier in life, which matters for older children and adults who have learned to compensate. And "all three" means what it says: since DSM-5-TR clarified the wording, a person with deficits in only one or two of these areas does not meet criterion A.

What criterion A looks like changes enormously with age and ability, which is one reason the condition is called a spectrum. At two, the clearest sign is often a failure of joint attention, the shared focus of two people on a third thing that a typically developing toddler creates by pointing to show, bringing objects to an adult, and following an adult's point or gaze, then looking back at the adult's face to share the moment. DSM-5-TR names impaired joint attention as an early feature of autism, and Ayaan has every part of it: he points neither to show nor to follow, and he uses his mother's hand without looking at her face. A toddler who does not respond to his name although he hears perfectly well, and who does not wave or smile back, is showing the same deficit.

At eight, in a child of average intelligence who talks fluently, the picture is different. Picture a Class 3 boy in Pune who knows every bus route in the city by number and lectures anyone who will stand still about which bus goes where, without noticing that his listener is bored, has asked a question, or has walked away. He stands too close, looks past the listener's shoulder, speaks in a flat, adult-sounding voice, and cannot understand why the other boys will not let him join their game when he insists on changing its rules. His vocabulary is excellent; what fails is the social use of it. At twenty-five, an engineer in a Bengaluru software firm, very good at his job, may be undone by everything around the job: he cannot tell when a colleague is joking, takes a manager's "we should catch up sometime" as an appointment and turns up, does not know when or how to join a conversation at the coffee machine, and finds social events so exhausting that he leaves early and sleeps for hours afterwards. Older children and adults like these often learn to compensate, consciously calculating what most people do by intuition, and the compensation can hide the deficit in short or structured meetings, which is why DSM-5-TR advises longer assessments, observation in natural settings and questions about the cost of social effort.

Ayaan meets criterion A on all three counts, and in every setting his parents describe: his reciprocity is limited (he initiates contact only to get things, and shares neither interests nor feelings), his nonverbal communication is very limited (little eye contact, no pointing, no gestures such as waving), and he shows no interest in other children and no pretend play. That alone does not make the diagnosis. The second domain, the subject of Section 3.5.4, must also be present.

Clinical link

In many Indian homes children are taught not to stare at elders and to lower their eyes when scolded, and a shy child may hide behind a parent with strangers. Reduced eye contact alone is therefore weak evidence. What counts is the whole pattern of social communication, judged against what other children in the same family and community do: whether the child looks at his mother's face to share a moment, brings things to show her, follows her point, smiles back, and enjoys simple games such as peekaboo with familiar people.

Exam trap

What DSM-5 requires for autism is persistent deficits in social communication and social interaction across multiple contexts (criterion A, all three of reciprocity, nonverbal communication and relationships) plus restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour (criterion B). Delayed speech alone is not required (many autistic people speak fluently) and is not sufficient (it is common in language disorder). Intellectual disability is not required; it is a specifier. Hyperactivity and impulsivity belong to ADHD.

Criterion B: restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour, interests or activities

Ask Laila and Daniyal what Ayaan does with his day, and a second set of features appears. He lines up his toy cars along the edge of the sofa, nose to tail, and screams if anyone moves one. He will stand for long periods watching the ceiling fan turn, or sit in front of the washing machine watching the drum go round. When he is excited his hands flap rapidly at his sides. The road to his maternal grandmother's house must be the same every time; if the auto-rickshaw driver takes a different turning, Ayaan cries until they arrive. He covers his ears and becomes distressed at the whistle of the pressure cooker and the noise of the mixer-grinder. He eats only plain rice with curd and one brand of biscuit. His speech, such as it is, has its own repetitive quality. He uses about fifteen single words, mostly the names of things he likes ("fan", "bus", and the numbers one to ten, which he recites), and no two-word phrases at all. He repeats advertisement jingles and lines from a cartoon word for word, sometimes hours after hearing them; and when his mother asks, "Do you want juice?", he answers, "You want juice."

Criterion B requires restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour, interests or activities, shown in at least two of four forms, now or by history. As with criterion A, the examples are illustrative.

  1. Stereotyped or repetitive motor movements, use of objects, or speech (B1). This includes simple motor stereotypies (hand-flapping, finger-flicking, rocking, spinning), repetitive use of objects (lining up toys, spinning coins, flipping objects over and over), and repetitive speech: echolalia, the stereotyped use of words, phrases or patterns of intonation, and idiosyncratic phrases whose meaning only the family understands.
  2. Insistence on sameness, inflexible adherence to routines, or ritualised patterns of verbal or nonverbal behaviour (B2). Small changes cause extreme distress (a different route to school, furniture moved, a new brand of soap); transitions from one activity to another are very hard; thinking is rigid and rule-bound; there may be greeting rituals, repetitive questioning or pacing the edges of a room; the child needs to take the same route or eat the same food every day.
  3. Highly restricted, fixated interests that are abnormal in intensity or focus (B3). The interest may be in an unusual object (a toddler who carries a particular piece of string or a steel lid everywhere) or an unusually narrow topic pursued with unusual intensity (washing machines, ceiling fans, railway timetables, the batting averages of every Test cricketer since 1932). The topic itself may be ordinary; what marks it out is the intensity, the narrowness and the way it crowds out everything else.
  4. Hyper- or hyporeactivity to sensory input, or unusual interest in sensory aspects of the environment (B4). This is the item DSM-5 added. Hyperreactivity shows as distress at particular sounds (pressure cookers, mixer-grinders, hand-dryers, crowds) or textures (labels in clothing, sand, certain foods); hyporeactivity as apparent indifference to pain, heat or cold; sensory seeking as excessive smelling or touching of objects, or visual fascination with lights, spinning objects and movement. Extreme reactions to the taste, smell, texture or look of food, and severe food restriction, are common and can be the first thing that brings a child to a doctor.

Ayaan meets all four, although two would be enough. His hand-flapping, the lined-up cars and his echoed phrases are B1; his distress when a car is moved and his insistence on one route are B2; his absorption in fans and the washing-machine drum is B3, and arguably also the visual fascination with movement of B4; and his ears covered against the pressure cooker and the mixer-grinder, with his narrow diet, are B4.

His speech deserves a closer look, because it shows two classic features of autistic language that were already in Kanner's 1943 paper. Echolalia is the repetition of words or phrases heard from others. In immediate echolalia the child repeats what has just been said, as Ayaan does with "You want juice"; in delayed echolalia he reproduces something heard earlier, sometimes much earlier and with the original intonation, as with his jingles and cartoon lines. Echolalia is normal in toddlers for a short period while they are learning to talk; in autism it is more frequent and lasts much longer. It is not always meaningless. Studies since the early 1980s have shown that echoed phrases often serve a purpose, such as requesting, protesting or taking a turn in the only way the child can: Ayaan's "You want juice" is very probably his way of saying "yes, I want juice", built from the words he has heard used in that situation. That is also why autistic children so often show pronoun reversal, referring to themselves as "you" (or by name) and to others as "I": they have learned the phrase as a whole, in the form in which it was said to them, rather than learning that "I" and "you" shift depending on who is speaking. Echolalia as a sign, which also occurs in catatonia and in some dementias, is described in Section 2.9.4.

Not every repetitive behaviour in a young child is autistic. Most toddlers have strong preferences, love repetition and like to watch the same video again and again. DSM-5-TR says that the line is drawn by the type, frequency and intensity of the behaviour: a child who lines up objects for hours every day and is very distressed if one is moved is showing something different from a child who likes his cars in a row. Nor are the repetitive behaviours always harmful. Many verbally able autistic people learn to suppress them in public, and for some, rocking or finger-flicking is calming, a way of managing anxiety or sensory overload. Special interests can be a source of pleasure and motivation and, later, of a career.

Three other kinds of repetitive behaviour need to be distinguished. Compulsions in obsessive-compulsive disorder are performed in response to an intrusive, unwanted thought, to reduce anxiety or prevent a feared event, and the person usually experiences them as unwelcome; the repetitive behaviours of autism are typically experienced as pleasurable or soothing and are not driven by an obsessional fear (Section 6.5.8 sets out the comparison). Tics are sudden, rapid, non-rhythmic movements or sounds, often preceded by an urge, whereas autistic stereotypies are rhythmic, often prolonged and tend to appear with excitement or absorption (Section 3.10.3). And stereotypic movement disorder (Section 3.10.2) is diagnosed when stereotypies occur without the social communication deficits of criterion A; when they are part of autism, the second diagnosis is not given unless the stereotypies cause self-injury and become a focus of treatment in their own right.

Exam trap

A child with no eye contact, delayed language and repetitive movements has the profile of autism spectrum disorder: a social communication deficit plus restricted, repetitive behaviour. ADHD is inattention and hyperactivity-impulsivity; conduct disorder is a repetitive pattern of violating others' rights and major social rules; specific learning disorder is difficulty with reading, writing or mathematics. None of them requires both a social communication deficit and repetitive behaviour. Criterion B needs two of four; criterion A needs all three.

Onset, impairment, severity and specifiers

Criteria A and B describe what autism is. The remaining three criteria say when it must have started, how much it must matter, and what it must not be confused with, and each closes a door that would otherwise let the wrong people into the diagnosis.

Criterion C requires that the symptoms be present in the early developmental period. This is what makes autism a neurodevelopmental disorder: it is not something that happens to a person who was once socially typical, as a depression or a psychosis can. The criterion contains two qualifications that came from experience with verbally able children and adults. The symptoms may not become fully apparent until social demands exceed the person's limited capacities, which is why a bright, talkative child may get through primary school with only a reputation for being "odd" and then fall apart in the far more complicated social world of secondary school. And they may be masked by learned strategies in later life, which is why an adult diagnosed at forty can still meet criterion C even though his difficulties were never named in childhood. Criterion D requires clinically significant impairment in social, occupational or other important areas of current functioning; autistic traits that cause no impairment are not a disorder. Criterion E requires that the disturbance not be better explained by intellectual developmental disorder or global developmental delay. Intellectual disability and autism frequently occur together, but a child with a severe intellectual disability will have limited social communication simply because all his skills are limited. The rule DSM-5-TR gives is that to diagnose both, social communication must be below the level expected for the person's general developmental level. Without this rule, almost every child with a severe intellectual disability would be called autistic, and the label would lose its meaning.

Severity is rated, and rated separately for each of the two domains, because a person may need much more help with one than with the other. DSM-5-TR describes three levels in terms of the support a person needs: level 1, "requiring support"; level 2, "requiring substantial support"; and level 3, "requiring very substantial support". Table 3.15 paraphrases what each level looks like. DSM-5-TR adds several cautions. Severity can vary with context and change over time, and a person's current symptoms may even fall below level 1. The levels are a description, not a passport: they should not be used on their own to decide whether a person is eligible for services, because people with relatively better overall skills may face different and sometimes greater psychosocial difficulties, and service needs can only be worked out individually.

Table 3.15 The severity levels of autism spectrum disorder, rated separately for each domain (paraphrased from DSM-5-TR).
LevelSocial communicationRestricted, repetitive behaviours
Level 3: requiring very substantial supportSevere deficits in verbal and nonverbal social communication cause severe impairment; the person very seldom starts an interaction and responds very little to others' approaches. Example: a person with few intelligible words who rarely initiates contact, does so in unusual ways only to get needs met, and responds only to very direct approaches.Inflexibility, extreme difficulty coping with change, or other repetitive behaviours markedly interfere with functioning in every sphere; great distress or difficulty when having to change focus or action.
Level 2: requiring substantial supportMarked deficits; social impairment is obvious even with supports in place; limited initiation of interaction and reduced or unusual responses to others. Example: a person who speaks in simple sentences, whose interaction is limited to narrow special interests, and whose nonverbal communication is markedly odd.Inflexibility, difficulty coping with change, or other repetitive behaviours occur often enough to be obvious to a casual observer and interfere with functioning in a variety of settings; distress or difficulty changing focus or action.
Level 1: requiring supportWithout supports, the deficits cause noticeable impairment; the person has difficulty starting interactions and responds to others in clearly atypical or unsuccessful ways, and may seem uninterested. Example: a person who speaks in full sentences and takes part in communication, but whose to-and-fro conversation fails and whose attempts at friendship are odd and usually unsuccessful.Inflexibility causes significant interference in one or more settings; difficulty switching between activities; problems of organisation and planning limit independence.

Next come the specifiers, which turn a single broad label into a description of the individual. Each answers a question a teacher, parent or therapist will want answered.

  • With or without accompanying intellectual impairment. Autistic people's abilities are often uneven, and a single IQ can mislead, so DSM-5-TR asks for separate estimates of verbal and non-verbal ability, using untimed non-verbal tests where language is limited. Even those of average or high intelligence usually have an uneven profile, and the gap between intellectual ability and everyday adaptive skills is often large.
  • With or without accompanying language impairment. The current level of language is assessed and recorded. If there is language impairment, the description might be "no intelligible speech" (nonverbal), "single words only" or "phrase speech"; without language impairment, "speaks in full sentences" or "has fluent speech". Because comprehension may lag behind expression in autism, receptive and expressive language are considered separately. This is where the language delay of the old criteria went (Figure 3.8).
  • Associated with a known genetic or other medical condition or environmental factor. Examples are a genetic condition such as Rett syndrome, fragile X syndrome or Down syndrome; a medical condition such as epilepsy; or exposure in the womb to a known teratogen or infection, as in fetal valproate syndrome, fetal alcohol syndrome or congenital rubella. The associated condition is also coded. DSM-5-TR stresses that listing a condition here does not claim that it caused the autism, only that it is relevant to the person's care.
  • Associated with a neurodevelopmental, mental or behavioural problem. This notes problems such as irritability, sleep problems, self-injury or developmental regression that are a focus of treatment. Where another problem meets the criteria for a disorder in its own right (ADHD, developmental coordination disorder, an anxiety, depressive or bipolar disorder, a tic disorder, a feeding, elimination or sleep disorder), it is diagnosed separately as well.
  • With catatonia. Some autistic people, most often in adolescence, develop catatonia: posturing, negativism, mutism, stupor, or a worsening of stereotypies and self-injury. It is recorded separately as catatonia associated with autism spectrum disorder (Section 4.5.7 teaches catatonia across the disorders).

DSM-5-TR gives a fixed order for recording all this: first the level of support for each domain, then the intellectual specifier, then the language specifier with the current verbal level, then any associated condition, and finally catatonia if present. Work through it for Ayaan. For social communication the team judges that he needs substantial support (level 2): his deficits are marked and obvious even with support, he approaches his mother mainly to get things, and he shows almost no interest in other children; the line between levels 2 and 3 in a three-year-old is a matter of clinical judgement, and the levels are expected to change as he grows. For restricted, repetitive behaviour he is also at level 2: his rituals, his distress at changes and his sensory reactions are obvious to any visitor and disrupt meals, travel, play and home life, but they do not yet dominate every sphere of his day. His language is clearly impaired, so the language specifier applies, with his verbal level recorded as single words. Whether he has an accompanying intellectual impairment cannot be decided from his speech; it depends on his non-verbal ability, which Section 3.5.6 describes. And whether a known genetic or medical condition is associated depends on the tests the team has ordered. Section 3.5.7 puts the pieces together.

One consequence of the specifiers is worth stating plainly, because it explains what happened to Asperger's disorder. DSM-5-TR notes that many people who would once have been diagnosed with Asperger's disorder now receive a diagnosis of autism spectrum disorder without accompanying language or intellectual impairment. The old subtype lives on as a combination of specifiers.

Exam trap

An IQ below 70 is not a core symptom of autism: intellectual impairment is a specifier ("with or without accompanying intellectual impairment"), and many autistic people have average or high intelligence. The core symptoms are social communication and social reciprocity (criterion A) and unusual repetitive behaviour (criterion B). Severity is rated separately for the two domains: level 1 "requiring support", level 2 "requiring substantial support", level 3 "requiring very substantial support". To diagnose autism alongside intellectual disability, social communication must be below the person's general developmental level.

Recognising autism: the first signs, girls and adults

When did Ayaan's difficulties begin? His parents' account of his early development is typical of many autistic children, and it is worth hearing in full. His motor milestones were normal: he sat at 6 months and walked at 13. He babbled at the usual age, and at about 14 months he said his first words, "amma" and "ball". Then, at about 18 months, he stopped saying "amma" and for several months added no new words; the vocabulary he has now, about fifteen single words, came slowly afterwards. His parents remember that his words stopped soon after a vaccination at around 16 months, and they ask whether the vaccine caused his autism. It is the question that thousands of parents have asked since the late 1990s, and Section 3.6.5 answers it with the evidence.

DSM-5-TR's account of onset matches this history closely. The behavioural features of autism become evident in early childhood, and in some children a lack of interest in social interaction is visible in the first year. Most often the symptoms are recognised in the second year of life, between 12 and 24 months; earlier than 12 months if developmental delays are severe, later than 24 months if the symptoms are subtle. In some children skills are lost. Parents describe a gradual or a fairly rapid deterioration in social behaviour or language, typically between 12 and 24 months, exactly the window in which Ayaan lost his words. Prospective studies, which follow the younger brothers and sisters of autistic children from birth because their risk is raised, have shown that in most cases the onset of autism involves a decline in key social and communication behaviours over the first two years, such as looking at faces, smiling back and babbling to others, even when parents do not notice a clear loss. Such declines are rare in other neurodevelopmental disorders, so a loss of social or language skills in a toddler is an especially useful warning sign. Regression after at least two years of normal development, the old childhood disintegrative disorder, is much rarer and calls for neurological investigation (Section 3.5.2).

The first symptoms that bring a child to a clinic are often the ones Ayaan showed: delayed language together with a lack of social interest or unusual social behaviour (pulling an adult by the hand without looking at her), odd play (carrying toys about without playing with them, lining them up, spinning their wheels), and unusual patterns of communication (knowing the alphabet or the numbers but not answering to one's own name). Deafness is often suspected and then ruled out, as it was for Ayaan. During the second year repetitive behaviours and the absence of ordinary play become more obvious. Autism is not a degenerative disorder; learning and compensation continue throughout life, and the course and outcome are described in Section 3.6.3.

How is the diagnosis made? DSM-5-TR is clear that diagnoses are most valid and reliable when they combine several sources of information: the clinician's own observation, the caregivers' history and, where possible, the person's own account (the case for multiple informants is made in Section 3.1.4). Standardised instruments improve reliability, but autistic symptoms are dimensions without universally agreed cut-off scores, so the diagnosis remains a clinical judgement that no single questionnaire or observation score can make. The instruments themselves are described in the Assessment book and listed in Table 3.4 (Section 3.1.5); in one line each, the Modified Checklist for Autism in Toddlers, Revised with Follow-up (M-CHAT-R/F), is a parent screening questionnaire for toddlers; the Autism Diagnostic Observation Schedule, second edition (ADOS-2), is a structured play-based observation; the Autism Diagnostic Interview-Revised (ADI-R) is a detailed interview with a parent; the INCLEN Diagnostic Tool for Autism Spectrum Disorder (INDT-ASD) is an Indian diagnostic tool; the Indian Scale for Assessment of Autism (ISAA) is the tool used for disability certification in India (Section 3.1.6); and the Childhood Autism Rating Scale (CARS) is a clinician's rating scale. For Ayaan, the team combines an ADOS-2 observation with a detailed parent interview. On a developmental scale his non-verbal problem solving, such as fitting shapes, completing form boards and finding hidden toys, is close to the level expected for his age, while his language is far below it. Because fragile X syndrome is one of the commonest known genetic conditions associated with autism, and because a paternal cousin "did not talk till five", the team also orders a test for fragile X.

Not every autistic person looks like Ayaan, and two groups in particular are diagnosed late or missed. The first is girls and women. Autism is diagnosed three to four times more often in males than in females (the true sex ratio is discussed in Section 3.6.1), and on average girls are diagnosed later. In clinic samples, autistic girls are more likely than autistic boys to have an intellectual disability and epilepsy, a pattern suggesting that girls without these more visible problems are being overlooked. DSM-5-TR describes why. Compared with autistic boys, autistic girls may hold a better reciprocal conversation, share interests more, combine words and gestures more naturally and adjust their behaviour to the situation, while having similar underlying difficulties in social understanding. Many work hard at camouflaging (also called masking): watching and copying the dress, voice and manner of socially successful girls and women, rehearsing conversations and suppressing their own repetitive behaviours in public. Their repetitive behaviours may be less obvious, and their special interests may be more social or more ordinary in topic (a singer, an actor, horses, a television series) while remaining unusual in their intensity. Picture a quiet, well-behaved Class 9 girl in Lucknow, top of her class, who has one close friend and copies her in everything, who knows every lyric of one singer's songs and can talk of nothing else, and who comes home from school every day so exhausted that she shuts herself in her room. Her teachers see a shy, studious child; nobody thinks of autism until she becomes depressed at sixteen. DSM-5-TR also notes that rates of gender variance are higher among autistic people than in the general population.

The second group is adults. Some people come for a first diagnosis in adult life, often after an autistic child in the family has been diagnosed and a parent recognises himself in the description, or after a breakdown in relationships at work or at home. A detailed developmental history may be hard to obtain when the person's parents are old or dead, so DSM-5-TR asks clinicians to give weight to the person's own account of his difficulties. Autism may be diagnosed when the criteria are met now, particularly if there is a history of poor social and communication skills in childhood. A convincing report that the person had ordinary, lasting, reciprocal friendships and good nonverbal communication all through childhood makes autism much less likely; but ambiguous or missing developmental information is not, by itself, a reason to rule it out. Many autistic adults describe using strategies to mask their difficulties in public and the exhaustion of keeping up a socially acceptable front.

In India the age at diagnosis tends to be later than in high-income countries, for reasons that are easy to understand. Family members, and sometimes doctors, reassure parents that "boys talk late" or that a quiet child will "pick up when he goes to school"; a relative who was also late to talk is held up as proof that there is nothing to worry about; specialist services are concentrated in cities; and assessments built in English for Western children must be used with care in a child who hears Telugu, Urdu and Hindi at home. A diagnostic interview conducted in the family's own language, with questions about the behaviours that matter (pointing, showing, response to name, pretend play), and attention to how the child behaves with familiar family members as well as in the clinic, makes the biggest difference.

Exam trap

Autism is usually recognised in the second year (12 to 24 months). Loss of words or social skills between 12 and 24 months is common in autism and rare in other neurodevelopmental disorders; regression after two or more years of normal development (old childhood disintegrative disorder) is rare and needs neurological investigation. The diagnosis is clinical: no test score makes or excludes it. Girls are diagnosed later and less often partly because of camouflaging, and an adult can be diagnosed for the first time even when childhood records are incomplete.

What else could it be?

Almost every feature of autism, taken alone, occurs in some other condition. Poor eye contact can be shyness, delayed speech a language disorder, silence at school selective mutism, rocking the result of neglect, a narrow interest a passion, repetitive checking an obsession. The diagnosis therefore rests on the combination: deficits in all three areas of social communication, at least two kinds of restricted, repetitive behaviour, a developmental onset, and a pattern that the alternatives do not explain. DSM-5-TR lists the conditions to consider, and Table 3.16 gathers them with a few that its list leaves implicit.

ADHD is the commonest source of confusion in school-age children. Problems of attention, either too narrowly focused or too easily distracted, are common in autism, and so is hyperactivity; and some children with ADHD show social communication problems of their own, interrupting, speaking too loudly and ignoring personal space. The developmental course and, above all, the absence in ADHD of restricted, repetitive behaviours and unusual interests separate the two. Since DSM-5 the two may be diagnosed together, and ADHD should be added when a child's inattention or hyperactivity goes beyond what is usual for children of the same mental age; it is one of the commonest conditions co-occurring with autism (Sections 3.6.2 and 3.7).

Intellectual developmental disorder without autism can be very hard to tell apart in very young children, and in people who have not developed language or symbolic skills, because repetitive behaviour is common in both. The rule is the one criterion E builds in. Autism is diagnosed in a person with intellectual disability when social communication and interaction are clearly more impaired than the person's non-verbal skills, such as fine motor skills and non-verbal problem solving, would predict; when social communication is at the same level as everything else, the diagnosis is intellectual developmental disorder alone. Language disorder and SPCD were set apart in Section 3.4: a child with a language disorder may have secondary social difficulties, but normal nonverbal communication and no restricted, repetitive behaviour; a child with social communication deficits but no restricted, repetitive behaviour, now or in the past, has SPCD, and autism supersedes SPCD whenever its criteria are met. Selective mutism is set apart by its history: early development is usually normal, the child communicates normally in some settings, and even where the child is silent, social reciprocity is intact and there is no restricted, repetitive behaviour (Section 6.4.8).

Stereotypic movement disorder (Section 3.10.2) is not diagnosed separately when the stereotypies are part of autism; both diagnoses are given only when the stereotypies cause self-injury and become a focus of treatment. Rett syndrome, as Section 3.5.2 explained, may meet the criteria for autism during its regressive phase, typically between ages 1 and 4, but most girls then become more socially interested, so autism is diagnosed only if all its criteria are met. Reactive attachment disorder (Section 7.4.5) can look strikingly like autism in a young child: muted positive emotion, delays in cognition and language, and poor social reciprocity. Three things separate them. The child with reactive attachment disorder has a history of severe social neglect, which autistic children only rarely have; he lacks the restricted interests, rituals and unusual sensory reactions of autism, although both groups may rock or flap; and, perhaps surprisingly, it is the autistic child who shows attachment behaviour typical for his developmental level, seeking his parents for comfort, while the child with reactive attachment disorder does so rarely or inconsistently, if at all. Closely related are the autistic-like patterns, sometimes called "quasi-autism", seen in some children raised in severely depriving institutions, many of whom improved markedly after adoption (Section 3.3.6 tells the story of the Romanian adoptees).

Anxiety disorders overlap with autism in complicated ways, because social withdrawal and repetitive behaviours are core features of autism but can also be expressions of anxiety, and anxiety disorders are common in autistic people (Section 3.6.2). In social anxiety disorder (Section 6.3) the person understands social situations well and avoids them for fear of embarrassment and scrutiny; the autistic person's difficulty is in the understanding itself. Obsessive-compulsive disorder (Section 6.5.8) shares repetitive behaviour with autism, but in OCD the compulsions answer intrusive thoughts, about contamination, order, sex or religion, and are performed to relieve anxiety, whereas the repetitive behaviours of autism, hand-flapping, finger-shaking, insistence on routines and lining up objects, may be experienced as pleasurable and reinforcing. Schizophrenia beginning in childhood (Chapter 4) is rare and usually follows a period of normal or near-normal development; its prodrome may bring social impairment and odd interests and beliefs that look like autism, but hallucinations and delusions are not features of autism. The clinician must also allow for the autistic person's literal reading of questions: asked "Do you hear voices when no one is there?", an autistic young man may say "Yes", meaning the radio. The two can co-occur, and then both are diagnosed. In adults of average ability, autistic behaviour may be read as a personality disorder, especially schizoid or schizotypal personality disorder, which share odd speech, constricted affect, few close friends and unusual preoccupations (Chapter 11); the most useful guide is the early developmental history, with its lack of imaginative play, its repetitive behaviours and its sensory sensitivities. Finally, hearing impairment must be excluded in every young child who does not respond to his name or talk, as it was for Ayaan.

Table 3.16 The differential diagnosis of autism spectrum disorder.
ConditionWhat it shares with autismWhat tells it apartWhere taught
Rett syndromeSocial withdrawal and hand stereotypies during regressionGirls; loss of purposeful hand use; slowing head growth; MECP2; social interest returns3.5.2
Language disorderDelayed languageNormal nonverbal communication and social interest; no repetitive behaviour3.4.2
Social (pragmatic) communication disorderSocial communication deficitsNo restricted, repetitive behaviour, now or in the past3.4.5

Selective mutismSilence with othersNormal early development; speaks in safe settings; reciprocity intact6.4.8

Now return to Ayaan for the last time in this section. His hearing is normal, so deafness is excluded. His non-verbal problem solving is close to his age level while his social communication is far below it, so his difficulties are not simply those of a global delay or an intellectual disability. He has no history of neglect; he was born at term after an uncomplicated pregnancy, with no alcohol, smoking or medicines taken during it, and he has had no seizures. He does not have the hand-wringing, loss of hand use or slowing head growth of Rett syndrome, and he is a boy. He has deficits in all three areas of criterion A and in all four areas of criterion B, beginning in his second year, impairing his life at home, on outings and at every family gathering. The team's diagnosis is autism spectrum disorder, requiring substantial support (level 2) for deficits in social communication and requiring substantial support (level 2) for restricted, repetitive behaviours; without accompanying intellectual impairment on present evidence, to be reassessed at school age; with accompanying language impairment (single words, no phrase speech); not associated with a known genetic or other medical condition. "On present evidence" matters: estimates of intellectual ability in a three-year-old with little language are uncertain, and the team will test him again when he is older. His sleep, which takes him up to two hours to fall into most nights, his parents' question about the vaccine and the result of the fragile X test are taken up in Section 3.6, which also describes what happens next.

Exam trap

The repetitive behaviours of autism are usually pleasurable or soothing; OCD compulsions are unwanted and driven by obsessional anxiety. Autistic children do form attachments to their parents; weak or absent attachment behaviour after severe neglect points to reactive attachment disorder. ADHD and autism can be diagnosed together since DSM-5 (DSM-IV did not allow it). A child with an intellectual disability receives autism as well only if social communication is below his general developmental level. Hallucinations and delusions point to schizophrenia, not autism.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. In DSM-5-TR, is the expressive production of sounds, including articulation, fluency, voice and resonance, while language is a rule-governed system of symbols.

  3. Children whose language impairment is mainly have a poorer prognosis than those whose impairment is mainly expressive.
  4. Developmental stuttering begins by age 6 in percent of affected children, and most of them recover.
  5. Wendell Johnson's theory, that parents' labelling of normal dysfluency causes stuttering, was tested in the 1939 "Monster Study" and is now discredited.
  6. Social (pragmatic) communication disorder is told apart from autism by the absence, now and in the past, of patterns of behaviour, interests or activities.
  7. Infantile autism was first described in 1943 by , whereas Eugen Bleuler coined the word in 1911 for a symptom of schizophrenia.
  8. Lorna Wing and Judith Gould's 1979 triad of impairments covered social interaction, communication and .
