Answers: (1) speech, (2) both (all), (3) receptive, (4) 80 to 90, (5) diagnosogenic, (6) restricted, repetitive, (7) Leo Kanner, (8) imagination, (9) MECP2, (10) substantial.

3.6 Autism Spectrum Disorder II: Who, Why and What HelpsMedium yield · 3 Qs

Section 3.5 ended at the point where a family leaves the clinic with a diagnosis. For Ayaan's parents in Hyderabad, as for every family in that position, the diagnosis is the beginning of a second set of questions, and they are the questions this section answers. How many other children are like him, and why does it seem that every second family now knows an autistic child? What else is likely to come with autism: sleeplessness, epilepsy, anxiety, a learning difficulty? What will he be like at fifteen, and at forty? Why did it happen: was it something in his genes, something in the pregnancy, the vaccine he was given just before his words stopped, or something his parents did? What is different about his brain and about the way he thinks? And, most urgently, what will help him, and which of the treatments that relatives and the internet are already recommending will not? Each question has a research literature behind it, and on several of them that literature has overturned what clinicians confidently believed a generation ago.

How common is autism, and why have the numbers risen?

When Leo Kanner described his eleven children in 1943 (Book 2, Section 10.5.1), he thought he had found a rare condition, and for thirty years the numbers agreed with him. The first population surveys, in England and Denmark in the 1960s and 1970s, counted only about 2 to 4 autistic children in every 10,000. Those surveys looked for Kanner's syndrome in its classic form: a child who was aloof from early life, with little or no useful language and an intense insistence on sameness. Today's figures are of a different order altogether. DSM-5-TR reports that in the United States between 1 and 2 percent of the population meets the criteria for autism spectrum disorder, with similar estimates in samples of children and of adults. Outside the United States the figure has approached 1 percent, with a median global prevalence (the middle value across all the countries studied) of 0.62 percent, and without substantial differences between geographical regions or ethnic groups. A condition once rarer than 1 in 2,500 is now found in about 1 in 100.

The best-known series of numbers comes from the United States Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), whose Autism and Developmental Disabilities Monitoring (ADDM) Network has, every two years since 2000, combed the health and special-education records of all eight-year-olds living in a set of monitoring areas and counted those whose records document autism. For the year 2000, across six sites, the figure was 6.7 per 1,000 eight-year-olds, about 1 in 150. For 2020, across eleven sites, it was 27.6 per 1,000, or 1 in 36. For 2022, across sixteen sites, it was 32.2 per 1,000, about 1 in 31, and boys were identified 3.4 times as often as girls. (Each survey counts children who turned eight in that year, so these three figures describe children born in 1992, 2012 and 2014.) In twenty years the recorded rate had quadrupled. Within the 2022 data the rate also varied more than fivefold from one site to another, from 9.7 per 1,000 in Laredo, Texas, to 53.1 per 1,000 in California, and the CDC itself suggests that such differences may reflect the services available for early detection and evaluation in each community. Those two facts, a steep rise and a large spread between neighbouring communities, are the first clue to what the numbers mean.

DSM-5-TR adds two further observations. Within the United States, recorded prevalence has been lower among African American children (1.1 percent) and Latinx children (0.8 percent) than among White children (1.3 percent), even after differences in family income and resources are allowed for; the manual attributes part of the gap to misdiagnosis, late diagnosis and under-diagnosis in some ethnic and racial groups, not to a real difference in how often autism occurs. And the sex ratio in well-ascertained epidemiological samples (samples in which every child in a population has been screened, rather than only the children who happened to be referred) is about 3 males to 1 female. DSM-5 in 2013 had said that autism is diagnosed four times more often in males, and clinical series give even higher ratios. The difference is itself informative: the more thoroughly a population is searched, the more autistic girls are found, which is why DSM-5-TR voices concern about the under-recognition of autism in girls and women (Section 3.5.6 explains why girls are missed).

Why, then, have the numbers risen so steeply? Is there an epidemic of autism? Five explanations, all supported by evidence, account for most of the rise.

  • The definition widened. Kanner's syndrome was the narrow, severe core. Official criteria first appeared in DSM-III (1980) and were broadened in DSM-III-R (1987); DSM-IV (1994) added Asperger's disorder alongside autistic disorder and pervasive developmental disorder not otherwise specified (a category already present in DSM-III-R), and DSM-5 (2013) folded all of them into one spectrum that includes fluent, intellectually able people with subtle social difficulties (Section 3.5.2). A survey that counts the whole spectrum is counting a different and much larger group than a survey of 1970 that counted Kanner's children.
  • Awareness and screening improved. Parents, teachers and paediatricians now know what to look for, toddlers are screened, and children are identified younger. In the 2022 ADDM data, children born in 2018 had been identified by their fourth birthday 1.7 times as often as children born in 2014.
  • Children moved between labels. Many children who would once have been recorded as having an intellectual disability or a learning disability are now recorded as autistic, a process called diagnostic substitution. Paul Shattuck showed it in 2006 in the records of American special education: between 1994 and 2003 the proportion of children aged 6 to 11 classified as autistic rose from 0.6 to 3.1 per 1,000, while the proportions classified as having mental retardation and learning disabilities fell by 2.8 and 8.3 per 1,000, and the states where autism rose most were the states where the other labels fell most.
  • Recording practices changed. In Denmark, which registers every diagnosis made in its hospitals and clinics, Stefan N. Hansen and colleagues followed all 677,915 children born from 1980 to 1991 and calculated, in 2015, that two changes in how cases were recorded (the adoption of new diagnostic criteria in 1994 and the inclusion of outpatient diagnoses from 1995) accounted for about 60 percent of the increase in recorded prevalence.
  • New groups are being diagnosed. Adults who were missed in childhood, girls who masked their difficulties, and intellectually able children whose problems surface only in secondary school are now diagnosed in large numbers, and in many countries a diagnosis is the key that unlocks school support, therapy and disability benefits, which gives families and professionals a reason to seek one.

Whether any true increase in the number of autistic people lies underneath these changes is not known. If there is one, it is likely to be modest, and two established risk factors that have themselves become more common, older parents and the survival of more extremely premature babies (Section 3.6.5), are plausible contributors. What the rise is not is evidence of a new environmental poison spreading through the population, still less of a vaccine causing autism, a claim taken apart in Section 3.6.5. The Indian survey figures for autism and the other neurodevelopmental disorders in children aged 2 to 9, from the multicentre INCLEN study, are given with the other prevalence data in Section 3.1.

Exam trap

A rise in diagnosed cases is not proof of a rise in cases. The widening of the criteria, better screening, diagnostic substitution and changes in recording explain most of it; it is not an "epidemic" and it is not evidence that vaccines cause autism. For the sex ratio, DSM-5-TR gives about 3 to 1 in well-ascertained samples (the older figure of 4 to 1, found in DSM-5 and in many textbooks and keys, describes who gets diagnosed). And beware the claim, made in some textbooks, that autism is "the most prevalent neurodevelopmental disorder": by DSM-5-TR's own figures ADHD (about 7 percent of children worldwide) and specific learning disorder (5 to 15 percent of school-age children) are both far more common than autism (about 1 percent).

What comes with autism

Autism rarely travels alone. DSM-5-TR's account of comorbidity (the co-occurrence of two or more disorders in the same person; the concept is defined in Chapter 1) reads like a list of everything else in this chapter. Autism is frequently associated with intellectual developmental disorder and with language disorder; specific learning difficulties in reading, writing and number are common, and so is developmental coordination disorder, the motor clumsiness described in Section 3.10.1. Beyond the neurodevelopmental disorders, about 70 percent of autistic people may have at least one co-occurring mental disorder and about 40 percent two or more, with anxiety disorders, depression and ADHD particularly common. The medical conditions most commonly associated with autism are epilepsy and constipation.

Two of these companions deserve a closer look, because examiners ask about them more than about any others. The first is intellectual disability. Kanner thought his children were of normal intelligence trapped behind their aloneness; for most of the later twentieth century the opposite view prevailed, and textbooks routinely stated that half or more of autistic children had an intellectual disability. As the spectrum widened to include fluent and able people, the proportion fell. In the CDC's 2020 data, 37.9 percent of the eight-year-olds with autism whose records contained an IQ score had an IQ of 70 or below; in the 2022 data the figure was 39.6 percent. The proportion was higher among Black, Asian and Hispanic autistic children than among White autistic children (in 2022, 52.8 percent against 32.7 percent for Black and White children). So the modern figure is roughly four in ten, and the old figure of "half or more" belongs to the era of narrower criteria. When autism and intellectual disability occur together, both are diagnosed (with the specifier "with accompanying intellectual impairment", Section 3.5.5), and the outlook depends heavily on the intellectual level.

The second is ADHD. Restlessness, distractibility and impulsivity are common in autistic children, yet until 2013 the two diagnoses could not officially be given together: DSM-IV's ADHD criteria excluded the diagnosis when the symptoms occurred exclusively during the course of a pervasive developmental disorder. DSM-5 removed the exclusion, so a child who meets the criteria for both now receives both, and clinicians can treat the ADHD symptoms directly (Section 3.6.8). How common is the combination? The largest synthesis, a meta-analysis of 96 studies by Meng-Chuan Lai and colleagues in 2019, estimated that 28 percent of autistic people have ADHD, making it the commonest co-occurring psychiatric diagnosis. Table 3.17 sets out the rest of their figures beside DSM-5-TR's statements.

Table 3.17 The conditions that most often accompany autism spectrum disorder.
ConditionHow common in autismWhat to remember
Intellectual disabilityAbout 4 in 10 eight-year-olds (CDC, 2020 and 2022)Older texts say "half or more"; diagnose both; the main prognostic factor
Language disorderFrequent (DSM-5-TR)Has its own specifier; delayed language is the commonest first concern
ADHDAbout 28 percent (Lai and colleagues, 2019)Commonest co-occurring psychiatric diagnosis; allowed alongside autism only since DSM-5
Anxiety disordersAbout 20 percent"Particularly common" in DSM-5-TR; in non-verbal children may show only as changed sleep, eating or behaviour
Sleep-wake disordersAbout 13 percentAbove all trouble falling asleep
Depressive disordersAbout 11 percentAble autistic adults are "prone to anxiety and depression" (DSM-5-TR)
Obsessive-compulsive disorderAbout 9 percentMust be told apart from autistic routines (Section 6.5.8)
Bipolar and schizophrenia spectrum disordersAbout 5 and 4 percentUncommon; not the answer to "most common comorbidity"
Epilepsy, constipationThe commonest medical conditions (DSM-5-TR)Epilepsy goes with greater intellectual disability and lower verbal ability
Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder"Fairly frequent presenting feature"Extreme, narrow food preferences may persist (Chapter 8)

Several of these companions change how a clinician should think about behaviour. Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder (Chapter 8) is a fairly frequent presenting feature, because sensory sensitivity and insistence on sameness narrow the diet to a few foods of one colour, texture or brand; Ayaan, who eats only plain rice with curd and one brand of biscuit, and who takes up to two hours to fall asleep most nights, shows the two commonest everyday companions of autism in one small boy. In a child or adult who has little or no speech, DSM-5-TR warns, a change in sleep or eating or a rise in challenging behaviour such as head-banging should prompt a search for anxiety or depression, and for pain or discomfort from an undiagnosed medical or dental problem: an aching tooth or a constipated bowel cannot be reported in words, and may be reported with fists. Epilepsy, which may begin in early childhood or in adolescence, is commoner the greater the intellectual disability and the lower the verbal ability, and it is one reason why the outlook is poorer in that group. Catatonia (Section 4.5.7) is a rarer but serious companion: some autistic people show catatonic-like slowing and "freezing" mid-action, and a few deteriorate into a full catatonic episode with mutism, posturing, grimacing and waxy flexibility, the risk being greatest in adolescence.

Two final facts are grim but clinically essential. Autistic people are at greater risk of death by suicide than other people; autistic children with impaired social communication have a higher risk of self-harm with suicidal intent and of suicidal thoughts and plans by the age of 16; and autistic adolescents and young adults have more suicide attempts than matched controls even when other psychiatric disorders are taken into account. And co-occurring intellectual disability, epilepsy, mental disorders and chronic medical conditions are associated with a higher risk of premature death. Deaths from injury and poisoning are more frequent than in the general population, and drowning is the leading cause of accidental death in autistic children, which is why water safety and swimming lessons are part of good care for a child who wanders and does not sense danger.

Exam trap

"The most common comorbidity with autism in DSM-5 is ..." The answer is intellectual disability and/or ADHD (practice mock), with anxiety, depression and epilepsy close behind. Conduct disorder, schizophrenia and bipolar I disorder are the distractors: schizophrenia and bipolar disorder each affect only about 4 to 5 percent of autistic people, and disruptive and conduct problems about 12 percent. Two traps hide inside the right answer. First, DSM-IV did not allow autism and ADHD to be diagnosed together; DSM-5 does. Second, the explanation that often accompanies this question says intellectual disability is present in "roughly half" of autistic children; the modern CDC figure is about 4 in 10.

One more companion is so universal that it is easily forgotten: language. Every autistic person has difficulty with the social use of language, because that difficulty is part of criterion A itself (Section 3.5.3). On top of that, a large proportion have a delay or disorder in the structure of language: vocabulary, grammar and comprehension. DSM-5-TR notes that the first symptoms of autism frequently involve delayed language development, often together with a lack of social interest or unusual social interactions; the manual gives language impairment its own specifier, "with or without accompanying language impairment", recorded with the current verbal level (no intelligible speech, single words, phrase speech or fluent speech); and it lists language disorder, defined as an inability to comprehend and construct sentences with proper grammar, as one of autism's most frequent co-occurring diagnoses. Ayaan, at three years and four months, with about fifteen single words, no phrases and echolalia, is the typical picture rather than the exception.

Compare the other options in the question below. In ADHD, delays in language, motor and social development "often co-occur", DSM-5-TR says, but they are "not specific" to the disorder; the core problem is the regulation of attention and activity, and many children with ADHD talk early and talk a great deal (Section 3.7). In specific learning disorder the difficulty lies in learning to read, spell, write or calculate (Section 3.9). A child with dyslexia usually has a phonological weakness that can be traced back to subtle early speech and language difficulties, but the development of spoken language is not what defines the disorder, and many dyslexic children speak fluently and well. "Childhood onset dissociation" is not a diagnostic category at all; the dissociative disorders are taught in Chapter 7, and none of them is defined by a failure of language development.

Exam trap

"Language development is compromised most commonly in" ADHD, autism spectrum disorder, specific learning disability or childhood onset dissociation (JEMAS 2025). The key is autism: delayed or absent language is the commonest first concern, language impairment is common enough to have its own specifier, and language disorder is one of its most frequent co-occurring diagnoses. The site notes that some keys mark specific learning disability. That is the weaker answer: specific learning disorder impairs written language (reading, spelling, written expression) and number, not the development of spoken language, and it is diagnosed only once a child is at school. If a question asks which disorder is defined by a language problem, the answer is the communication disorder called language disorder (Section 3.4); among the four options given here, autism wins.

How autism changes across life

Parents of a newly diagnosed three-year-old almost always ask the same thing: will he grow out of it? The honest answer has two parts. Autism is a lifelong way of being, not a phase; DSM-5-TR is explicit that the social and communication differences and the restricted, repetitive behaviours that define it are clear in the developmental period and remain sufficient to cause impairment later on, even when intervention, compensation and support mask them in some settings. But autism is also, in the manual's words, "not a degenerative disorder". It does not steadily worsen, as the dementias do, and it is typical for learning and compensation to continue throughout life. The loss of words or social skills that some toddlers show between 12 and 24 months (Section 3.5.6) is a one-time regression at the onset, not the start of a downhill course; that is one of the features that separates autism from Rett syndrome and from the rare childhood disintegrative pattern described in Section 3.5.2.

The usual course has a recognisable shape. Symptoms are most marked in early childhood and the early school years. In later childhood most children make developmental gains in at least some areas, and the change parents most often notice is an increased interest in other people: the child who once played alone in the corner begins to seek out a favourite cousin, or to join a game on his own terms. Adolescence is the period of greatest divergence. A small proportion of young people deteriorate behaviourally in their teens, sometimes with the onset of epilepsy, sometimes with the anxiety and depression that come with a dawning awareness of being different, sometimes with catatonia; most others continue to improve.

Who does well? DSM-5-TR names the best-established prognostic factors. The first is the presence or absence of intellectual disability. The second is language: functional language by the age of 5 years (speech used to communicate, not only to echo) is a good prognostic sign. The third is the presence of additional mental health problems, which worsen the outlook at every level of ability. Epilepsy, as noted above, travels with greater intellectual disability and lower verbal ability. Two further observations from the manual explain why even able autistic people often struggle as adults. Adaptive skills, the practical skills of daily living, are typically below measured IQ, so an autistic young man with an IQ of 115 may still be unable to cook a meal, catch the right bus or manage a bank account without help. And extreme difficulty with planning, organisation and coping with change harms academic achievement and independence even in students of above-average intelligence.

Adult outcomes have changed with the diagnosis itself. It was once the case, DSM-5-TR notes, that only a minority of autistic people lived and worked independently as adults. As the diagnosis has come to be made more often in people with good language and intellectual abilities, more have found a niche that matches their special interests and skills and are productively employed, and access to vocational rehabilitation services significantly improves competitive employment for young people in the transition from school to adult life. Even so, the manual warns, autistic adults with lower levels of impairment may remain socially naive and vulnerable, may have difficulty organising practical demands without aid, and are prone to anxiety and depression. Many report using compensation strategies to mask their difficulties in public and pay for it in stress and exhaustion. Little is known about old age in autism, although higher rates of co-occurring medical conditions have been documented, and social isolation and reluctance to seek help are likely to harm health in later life.

Hrishikesh, 27, is a good example of the modern adult outcome. He was diagnosed at eight, with fluent speech and an IQ in the average range, after his teachers noticed that he lectured classmates about railway timetables and could not join their games. He finished a degree in statistics and now works as a data-quality analyst for an insurance company in Pune, where his manager values the fact that he finds errors nobody else sees. He lives with his parents, travels by the same train every day and eats the same lunch. At work he has learned to look at people's foreheads, because looking at their eyes is uncomfortable, and to laugh when others laugh, and by the evening he is so drained that he sits alone in his room and listens to the same playlist for two hours. Two years ago, when a reorganisation changed his team and his desk, he became anxious and depressed and stopped sleeping, and it was the depression, not the autism, that brought him back to a clinic. He is independent at work and dependent at home; he is employed, but at a cost that does not show in his appraisal.

The most famous adult outcome of all belongs to the first child ever described. Donald Gray Triplett (1933 to 2023) was "Case 1, Donald T." in Kanner's 1943 paper; Book 2, Section 10.5.1 tells how his parents brought him from Forest, Mississippi, to Johns Hopkins in 1938. What became of him was pieced together by the journalists John Donvan and Caren Zucker in their 2010 article "Autism's First Child" in The Atlantic and their 2016 book In a Different Key. As a boy of about nine he was sent to live with a childless farming couple some miles outside Forest, who gave his energies practical work on the farm. He returned to his home town, went through the local high school, where teachers and classmates accepted him, and graduated from Millsaps College in 1958. He then worked for decades in the local bank in Forest, in which his family had a stake, learned to drive in his late twenties, played golf almost every day and travelled to dozens of countries. He lived alone in the house he grew up in and died at home in June 2023, aged 89. The lesson Donvan and Zucker drew from his story is the one his town had lived out: a small community that knew him, accepted him and found a place for his abilities mattered as much to the way his life turned out as anything a clinic did for him.

Stories like Donald's and Hrishikesh's feed a debate that every clinician now meets. The word neurodiversity appeared in the late 1990s, in the writing of the Australian sociologist Judy Singer and the American journalist Harvey Blume, and expresses the view that autism, ADHD, dyslexia and similar conditions are natural variations in how human brains are built, analogous to biodiversity, rather than diseases to be cured. Autistic self-advocates argue that much of the disability of autism is created by a world designed for non-autistic people, that the goal of intervention should be to reduce distress and build skills and supports rather than to make an autistic child indistinguishable from his peers, and that "cure" is the wrong frame. Many prefer identity-first language ("an autistic person", as one would say "a left-handed person") to person-first language ("a person with autism"), which treats the condition as something separable from the self. The autistic researcher Damian Milton proposed in 2012 the double empathy problem: that misunderstanding between autistic and non-autistic people runs both ways, since non-autistic people are as poor at reading autistic minds as the reverse, so that the "social deficit" is partly a mismatch between two styles. On the other side, many parents of autistic children with intellectual disability, no speech and self-injury, and some autistic adults, argue that the language of difference does not describe a life that needs round-the-clock care. The two positions agree on more than they disagree: that autistic people should be listened to, that intervention should aim at the person's own goals and well-being, and that support is a right. This book uses both "autistic person" and "person with autism", as much of the current research literature does.

Exam trap

Autism is not a degenerative disorder: regression in the second year is a one-time loss at onset, and most children then make gains. The best-established prognostic factors are intellectual level, language (functional language by age 5 is a good sign) and additional mental health problems. Adaptive skills are typically below measured IQ, not above it. "Neurodiversity" is associated with Judy Singer (and Harvey Blume) in the late 1990s; the "double empathy problem" with Damian Milton (2012), not with Baron-Cohen, whose "mindblindness" is the theory Milton was answering (Section 3.6.7).

Genes

The refrigerator-mother theory (Book 2, Section 10.5.1) was finally abandoned for many reasons, but the decisive one was genetic, and it came from a single study of twins. In the mid-1970s the psychiatrists Susan Folstein and Michael Rutter (1933 to 2021) of the Institute of Psychiatry in London set out to find every same-sex twin pair in Britain in which at least one twin was autistic. The logic of the twin method (Chapter 1, Sections 1.4.2 to 1.4.4) is that identical, or monozygotic (MZ), twins share all their genes while fraternal, or dizygotic (DZ), twins share on average half; if identical pairs are much more often concordant (both affected) than fraternal pairs, genes are at work.

Key study: Folstein and Rutter's twins (1977)

Question. Autism almost never ran visibly in families: autistic people rarely married, and only about 2 percent of their siblings were autistic. Did that mean genes did not matter? Folstein and Rutter pointed out that the reasoning was wrong: autistic adults rarely had children, and a condition then thought to affect only about 2 to 4 children in 10,000 would give few affected relatives even if it were highly heritable. Only twins could settle the question.

Method. Through a national search they found 21 same-sex twin pairs, 11 identical and 10 fraternal, in which at least one twin had autism, assessed the twins themselves, and also looked for milder cognitive and language problems and for complications around birth.

Result. Four of the 11 identical pairs (36 percent) were concordant for autism, against none of the 10 fraternal pairs. When the outcome was widened to any cognitive disorder, usually a language delay, with autism counted as one form of it, concordance rose to 82 percent in identical pairs and 10 percent in fraternal pairs. In six of the pairs that were discordant for autism, only one twin had suffered a biological hazard around birth, and in all six it was the autistic twin.

Conclusion. Genes play a major part, and what is inherited is a broader liability to cognitive and language abnormality, of which autism is the most severe expression; brain injury around birth might add to that liability and help explain why one twin and not the other became autistic.

Criticism. The sample was tiny, the confidence intervals wide and the diagnosis Kanner's narrow one. But later twin studies with larger samples and broader definitions found much higher concordance in identical twins, and the finding about a broader inherited phenotype has stood.

The heritability estimates that followed range widely. DSM-5-TR summarises them as running from 37 percent to more than 90 percent, depending on the twin sample and the definition used. The most authoritative single figure comes from a study by Dan Bai and colleagues, published in JAMA Psychiatry in 2019, which linked the national registers of five countries (Denmark, Finland, Sweden, Israel and Western Australia) covering about two million children followed to the age of 16, of whom 22,156 were diagnosed with autism. The median heritability across the five countries was about 80 percent, and the effect of the shared environment created by the mother during pregnancy was tiny, between 0.4 and 1.6 percent. Heritability (Chapter 1, Section 1.4.5) is a statement about the sources of variation in a population, not about an individual child: "80 percent heritable" does not mean that 80 percent of Ayaan's autism came from his genes, nor that the environment cannot change his outcome.

Families experience genetics as recurrence: if one child is autistic, what is the chance that the next will be? Until 2011 the usual answer was 3 to 10 percent, drawn from studies of families who came forward. The Baby Siblings Research Consortium, an international network led in this report by Sally Ozonoff, followed 664 infants who had an autistic older brother or sister from early in life to 36 months and diagnosed them with a standard observation schedule and an expert clinician. The answer, published in Pediatrics in 2011, was 18.7 percent, nearly one in five. Boys had almost three times the risk of girls, and an infant with more than one autistic older sibling had about twice the risk again. That figure, roughly twenty times the rate in the general population at the time (about 1 percent), is now the basis of genetic counselling, and it is why younger siblings of autistic children are screened and watched closely.

What are the genes? Two very different kinds of genetic risk have been found. The first is rare and large. DSM-5-TR estimates that as many as 15 percent of cases of autism are associated with a known genetic mutation, and that different families carry different ones: copy number variants (deletions or duplications of a stretch of chromosome; Chapter 1) or mutations in single genes. Many of these are de novo mutations, new changes that arose in the egg or sperm that made the child, or very early in the embryo, and are not present in either parent. A de novo mutation is genetic but not inherited, which is why a child can have a strongly genetic autism with no family history at all; and the risk of such mutations, in sperm especially, rises with the father's age, which is one reason older fathers are a risk factor (Section 3.6.5). Even these known mutations are not fully penetrant: not everyone who carries one becomes autistic. The second kind of risk is common and small. For most cases, DSM-5-TR concludes, risk appears to be polygenic: spread across perhaps hundreds of genetic loci, each common in the population and each adding only a little, and detectable only in genome-wide association studies of tens of thousands of people (Chapter 1, Sections 1.4.6 and 1.4.7). The manual adds a caution that applies to all psychiatric genetics: communities of colour have been so little included in genetic research that it is unclear whether these findings apply equally to all populations.

When autism occurs as part of a known genetic syndrome it is sometimes called syndromic autism, and DSM-5-TR records it with the specifier "associated with a known genetic or other medical condition" (Section 3.5.5). Five syndromes are worth knowing by name. Fragile X syndrome, the commonest inherited cause of intellectual disability (Section 3.3.3), produces autistic features in many affected boys. Tuberous sclerosis complex, a genetic disorder that causes benign growths (tubers) in the brain and other organs together with epilepsy, is strongly associated with autism. Rett syndrome, in girls, may pass through an autistic-like phase during its regression (Section 3.5.2). Deletions and duplications of a small region of chromosome 16, 16p11.2, are among the commonest copy number variants found in autistic people. And autism is more common in Down syndrome than in the general population, though often recognised late because social difficulties are put down to the intellectual disability. A child newly diagnosed with autism is therefore often offered genetic testing, above all when there is intellectual disability or unusual physical features. Ayaan's fragile X test, ordered at his assessment, was negative.

Folstein and Rutter's finding that many non-autistic co-twins had language and cognitive problems pointed to something wider than autism itself. Relatives of autistic people, parents and siblings especially, more often than other people show mild, non-disabling versions of autistic traits: social reticence, a preference for routine, intense and narrow interests, pragmatic language oddities or a history of late speech. Clinicians call this the broader autism phenotype. Even in the 1970s, a family history of speech delay was found in about a quarter of autistic children, far more often than a family history of autism. Ayaan's paternal cousin who "did not talk till five" is exactly the kind of relative the concept describes, although one late-talking cousin proves nothing on its own.

Exam trap

The first twin study of autism was Folstein and Rutter, 1977: concordance 36 percent in identical against 0 percent in fraternal pairs, rising to 82 against 10 percent for a broader cognitive disorder. DSM-5-TR's heritability range is 37 to over 90 percent, with about 80 percent in the five-country cohort. The sibling recurrence risk is about one in five (18.7 percent, 2011), not 2 percent (the 1970s estimate) and not 50 percent (what a single dominant gene would give). "Genetic" is not the same as "inherited": de novo mutations are genetic but arise new in the child. Up to 15 percent of cases have a known mutation; most risk is polygenic.

The environment, and the vaccine that did not cause autism

A heritability of 80 percent leaves room for the environment, and "environment" in genetics means everything that is not inherited DNA, including events in the womb. DSM-5-TR lists three environmental risks that "may broadly contribute" to autism, as they contribute to the other neurodevelopmental disorders: advanced parental age, extreme prematurity, and exposure in the womb to certain drugs or teratogens (agents that disrupt the development of the embryo or fetus), with valproic acid as its example. Each has a study behind it.

Parental age. In a large Israeli study reported in 2006, Abraham Reichenberg and colleagues found that men who became fathers at 40 or older were more than five times as likely to have an autistic child as fathers under 30. Older mothers carry some extra risk too. The likeliest mechanism is the one met in Section 3.6.4: the sperm of older men carry more de novo mutations, because sperm-producing cells go on dividing throughout a man's life and each division is a chance for a copying error. Prematurity. Babies born extremely early, many more of whom now survive than a generation ago, are at increased risk of autism, as they are of intellectual disability and ADHD. Valproate. Sodium valproate is an anticonvulsant widely used for epilepsy and bipolar disorder. In a study of all 655,615 children born in Denmark from 1996 to 2006, Jakob Christensen and colleagues found in 2013 that the 508 children exposed to valproate in the womb had an absolute risk of autism spectrum disorder of 4.42 percent by the age of 14, against 1.53 percent in the whole cohort. When the comparison was restricted to children of mothers with epilepsy, the risk of childhood autism (the narrow, classic form) was still about three times higher in the exposed children, which points to the drug rather than the mother's illness. Valproate is therefore avoided in women who may become pregnant, a rule that applies to psychiatrists prescribing it for bipolar disorder as much as to neurologists (Chapter 5).

An older finding and a newer one complete the picture. In the 1960s an epidemic of rubella (German measles) in the United States left many children with congenital rubella, and the child psychiatrist Stella Chess followed 243 of them; in a 1977 follow-up report she described a high rate of autism among them, together with the blindness, deafness and heart defects that rubella in pregnancy causes. Congenital rubella remains the classic example of an infection in pregnancy linked to autism, and rubella immunisation prevents it. In the other direction, a Norwegian cohort of 85,176 children studied by Pål Surén and colleagues in 2013 found that autistic disorder occurred in 0.10 percent of children whose mothers took folic acid supplements around conception, against 0.21 percent of children whose mothers did not. That is an association in an observational study, not proof, but folic acid is already recommended before conception to prevent neural tube defects, so the advice costs nothing. Notice what is absent from this list. Nothing in the evidence implicates the way a mother talks to, holds or feels about her child. The refrigerator-mother theory, told and buried in Book 2, Section 10.5.1, has no modern defender. And nothing implicates vaccination, which brings us to the most damaging false claim in the history of autism research.

On 28 February 1998 The Lancet, one of the most respected medical journals in the world, published a paper by the British doctor Andrew Wakefield (born 1956) and twelve co-authors at the Royal Free Hospital in London. It described twelve children with developmental disorders, most of them autistic, who had been referred to the hospital with bowel symptoms and in whom biopsies were reported to show inflammation of the intestine. The paper said that the parents of eight of the twelve children linked the onset of their child's behavioural symptoms to the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine, and it described the vaccine as the "apparent precipitating event" of a new syndrome that Wakefield would later call "autistic enterocolitis". The paper itself admitted that it had not proved an association between the vaccine and autism. But at a press conference held to launch it, Wakefield said that until the question was resolved he could not support the continued use of the three vaccines in combination, and recommended single vaccines instead.

The effect on public behaviour was immediate. Newspapers ran the story for years; in Britain the MMR controversy became the biggest science story of 2002. Confidence in the vaccine fell, vaccination rates dropped, and the diseases came back. In 1998 there were 56 confirmed cases of measles in the United Kingdom; in the first five months of 2006 there were 449, with the first measles death since 1992. An outbreak in Ireland in 2000 caused about 1,500 cases and three deaths. In 2008, for the first time in fourteen years, measles was declared endemic in the United Kingdom again, meaning that it was once more circulating continuously in the population. Similar falls in uptake followed the spread of the claim to other countries.

The science moved quickly against the claim. Within weeks of publication a panel of experts convened by the British Medical Research Council found no evidence of a link, and large studies soon followed. The paper itself was dismantled by a journalist. In February 2004 Brian Deer, writing in The Sunday Times, revealed that two years before the paper was published Wakefield had been hired, for £150 an hour plus expenses, by a lawyer preparing a lawsuit on behalf of parents against the manufacturers of the vaccine, that public legal-aid money had funded the work, and that some of the twelve children had been recruited through the litigation. None of this had been disclosed to The Lancet. In March 2004 ten of Wakefield's twelve co-authors formally retracted the paper's interpretation, the statement linking the vaccine to the bowel disease and the autism. Later that year Deer reported that Wakefield had also applied for a patent on a single measles vaccine that would have competed with MMR. The General Medical Council, which regulates doctors in Britain, held a hearing that ran from 2007 to 2010. On 2 February 2010 The Lancet fully retracted the paper, its editor describing it as "utterly false" and saying the journal had been deceived, and on 24 May 2010 Wakefield was struck off the UK medical register for serious professional misconduct, so that he could no longer practise as a doctor in Britain. In January 2011 Deer showed in a series of articles in the BMJ that, when the hospital records and the parents' accounts were compared with what the paper reported, diagnoses had been altered or the timing of symptoms changed for all twelve children; the journal's editors called the paper "an elaborate fraud".

Fraud in one paper does not by itself prove that the vaccine is safe; that needed evidence of the opposite kind, from very large populations. The first decisive study was Danish. Kreesten Madsen and colleagues, writing in the New England Journal of Medicine in 2002, followed all 537,303 children born in Denmark from 1991 to 1998, 82 percent of whom had received MMR, and found that vaccinated children were no more likely to be diagnosed with autism than unvaccinated children (relative risk 0.92), with no relation between autism and the age at vaccination, the time since vaccination or the date of vaccination. Seventeen years later the same research group returned to the question with a larger cohort and sharper questions.

Key study: MMR and autism in 657,461 Danish children (Hviid and colleagues, 2019)

Question. Even after the 2002 study, anti-vaccine campaigners argued that MMR might trigger autism in a susceptible minority (for example, children with an autistic older sibling) or cause a cluster of cases in the months after vaccination. Does it?

Method. Anders Hviid and colleagues at the Statens Serum Institut in Copenhagen linked Denmark's national registers for all 657,461 children born from 1999 to the end of 2010, following them from their first birthday until August 2013. They compared the rate of autism diagnoses in MMR-vaccinated and unvaccinated children, adjusting for age, birth year, sex, other childhood vaccines, a sibling history of autism and a score summarising other autism risk factors.

Result. Over more than five million person-years, 6,517 children were diagnosed with autism. The fully adjusted hazard ratio for vaccinated against unvaccinated children was 0.93 (95 percent confidence interval 0.85 to 1.02): no increase. Nor was there any increase in children with autistic siblings, in children with high risk scores, or in any time window after vaccination.

Conclusion. MMR vaccination does not increase the risk of autism, does not trigger autism in susceptible children and is not followed by a clustering of cases.

Criticism. It is an observational study, not a randomised trial (which would be unethical, since it would mean leaving children unprotected against measles), and the authors did not review individual medical charts. But its size gave it the power to detect even a small effect in a subgroup, and its results agree with every other large study in several countries.

A second version of the vaccine theory blamed not the measles virus but mercury. The compound thimerosal (spelled thiomersal in Britain) is a mercury-containing preservative that was once used in several childhood vaccines, though never in MMR, which is a live-virus vaccine. As a precaution, and despite no evidence of harm, American health authorities had thimerosal removed from routine infant vaccines by 2001. If thimerosal had been driving the rise in autism, diagnoses should have levelled off a few years later. They did not; as the ADDM figures in Section 3.6.1 show, they kept rising. Studies comparing children who had and had not received thimerosal-containing vaccines found no difference in autism, and a later American study (Frank DeStefano and colleagues, 2013) found that the total amount of vaccine antigens (the immune-stimulating proteins and sugars in vaccines) a child received in the first two years was not related to the risk of autism either.

Why, then, does the belief survive? Largely because of timing. In many countries MMR is given between 12 and 15 months of age, and in India the measles-containing vaccines of the routine schedule are also given in the first two years. That is exactly the age, between 12 and 24 months, when parents usually first notice autistic features or a loss of words (Section 3.5.6). In any large population, many children will therefore show their first signs of autism in the weeks after a vaccination by coincidence alone, and a parent who saw the two events one after the other will remember them as cause and effect. Prospective studies that follow infants from birth show, in addition, that subtle social differences, such as a decline in looking at other people's eyes from about 2 months of age, are often present long before any vaccine of the second year is given.

Clinical link: answering Ayaan's parents

Laila and Daniyal remember that Ayaan's words stopped soon after a vaccination at about 16 months, and they ask whether the vaccine did it. The question deserves a respectful answer, not a dismissal: they are reporting accurately what they saw. The clinician can explain that a mild loss of words between 12 and 24 months is a well-known way for autism to begin; that routine vaccines are given at the same age, so the two often fall close together by chance; that the study that started the fear was found to be fraudulent and was withdrawn; and that studies of more than a million children have found that vaccinated and unvaccinated children develop autism at the same rate. Then comes the practical point: Ayaan and his future brothers or sisters should complete their vaccinations, because measles is dangerous and autism is not caught from a vaccine. Parents who feel heard are far more likely to accept this than parents who feel corrected.

Exam trap

The MMR-autism claim came from Andrew Wakefield's 1998 Lancet paper on 12 children. Its interpretation was retracted by ten co-authors in 2004, The Lancet fully retracted it in February 2010, Wakefield was struck off in May 2010, and the BMJ called it fraudulent in 2011. Danish cohorts of more than half a million children (2002) and 657,461 children (2019) found no link; thimerosal was removed from American infant vaccines around 2001 and autism kept rising. "Cold" or "refrigerator" parenting does not cause autism either. The environmental risks DSM-5-TR does name are advanced parental age, extreme prematurity and prenatal valproate; congenital rubella is the classic infectious cause.

The autistic brain

If autism is largely genetic and begins before birth, there should be differences in the developing brain, and there are, although none is large enough or consistent enough to diagnose an individual. The neuroanatomy itself belongs to the Physiological book; what follows are the findings the papers mention and what each does and does not show.

Early overgrowth. The most striking finding is about size. In 2003 Eric Courchesne and colleagues in San Diego went back to the medical records of 48 autistic children aged 2 to 5 and extracted the head circumference measurements taken in infancy, a rough but reliable index of brain size. At birth the children's heads were slightly smaller than average. Then, during the first year, they grew abnormally fast, so that by 6 to 14 months the average child was at the 84th percentile; 59 percent of the children with autistic disorder showed this accelerated growth, against 6 percent of typical infants. The faster the early growth, the larger the cerebral cortex at 2 to 5 years. Later studies have confirmed early overgrowth in a subgroup of autistic children, though not in all, and one proposed mechanism, which Pinel and Barnes describe, is reduced synaptic pruning by glial cells, so that the brain fails to cut back the surplus connections that every infant brain first makes. Courchesne had earlier reported, in 1988, that parts of the cerebellum, the vermis especially, were underdeveloped in autistic patients, and the cerebellum, long thought of as a purely motor structure, is now known to take part in attention, language and social cognition.

The amygdala. The amygdala, the structure in the temporal lobe that tags events with emotional, and especially fearful, significance, has been studied because so much of autism concerns the reading of emotion. Studies of young autistic children have reported an enlarged amygdala, while post-mortem studies of autistic adults have found an amygdala of normal size but containing fewer neurons (Cynthia Schumann and David Amaral, 2006). One speculative account, which Barlow and Durand describe, is that early enlargement produces excessive fear and arousal in social situations and that chronic stress later damages the structure; it remains a hypothesis.

The face-processing network. Most people are face experts: they recognise thousands of faces at a glance, and a region on the underside of the temporal lobe, the fusiform face area, responds more strongly to faces than to any other object. In 2000 Robert Schultz and colleagues at Yale scanned 14 intellectually able people with autism or Asperger syndrome while they judged whether two faces, or two objects, were the same. When the control participants compared faces they used the fusiform face area; the autistic participants activated it less and instead used a neighbouring region of the inferior temporal gyrus that controls normally use for objects. The authors' interpretation was that autistic people may process faces feature by feature, as objects, rather than as wholes. Pinel and Barnes add a developmental twist: reduced fusiform activation to faces has been found more consistently in autistic adolescents than in autistic adults, which suggests a delay in the development of the face-processing circuitry rather than a fixed absence. The link to behaviour is the eye-tracking evidence described in Section 3.6.7: a person who does not look at faces, and above all at eyes, gives the face-processing network less practice.

Connectivity. A family of theories proposes that the problem lies less in any one region than in how regions are connected: too much short-range connection within local areas of cortex and too little long-range connection between distant areas, such as the frontal lobes and the rest of the brain (Eric Courchesne and Karen Pierce summed up the idea in 2005 as a frontal cortex "talking only to itself"). Such a pattern would favour detailed local processing over integration, which fits the cognitive theory of weak central coherence (Section 3.6.7). The imaging evidence has been mixed, with both under- and over-connectivity reported, and the idea remains a hypothesis.

Broken mirrors. In the 2000s a popular theory held that autism results from a malfunction of mirror neurons, cells first found in monkeys that fire both when an animal performs an action and when it watches another perform the same action, and that were thought to underlie imitation and empathy. The neuroscientists Vilayanur S. Ramachandran and Lindsay Oberman popularised the "broken mirror" theory in a 2006 article in Scientific American. Later work did not support it. A systematic review by Antonia Hamilton in 2013 found no clear evidence of a general mirror-system deficit in autism: autistic people understand the goals of observed actions well, and several imaging studies found the mirror system working typically. The theory is worth knowing as an example of an attractive idea that outran its evidence.

Neurochemistry. Two chemical findings appear in the papers. The first concerns oxytocin, a hormone and neurotransmitter involved in social bonding, trust and social memory. Some studies found lower blood oxytocin in autistic children, a dose of intranasal oxytocin improved autistic participants' memory for and processing of emotional faces in small laboratory studies, and variations in the oxytocin receptor gene have been associated with autism. These findings raised hopes of a drug for the social difficulties, hopes that a large trial dashed (Section 3.6.8). The second is the oldest biological finding in autism: hyperserotonaemia, raised levels of serotonin in the blood, first reported in 1961. A meta-analysis in 2014 found raised whole-blood serotonin in about 28 percent of autistic people, so roughly a quarter to a third have it. Most of the body's serotonin is in blood platelets and the gut, not the brain, and the finding's meaning is still unclear.

One warning applies to every finding in this subsection. Each is a difference between the average of a group of autistic people and the average of a comparison group; the distributions overlap widely, many autistic people do not show the finding, and some non-autistic people do. None is a diagnostic test, and no brain scan, blood test or EEG can diagnose autism. The diagnosis remains a clinical one, made from development and behaviour (Section 3.5).

Exam trap

Know which finding goes with which name. Early brain (head) overgrowth in the first year: Courchesne, 2003. Reduced fusiform face area activation to faces: Schultz and colleagues, 2000. The "broken mirror" (mirror neuron) theory: Ramachandran and Oberman, 2006, and not supported by later reviews. Hyperserotonaemia (raised blood serotonin) in about a quarter to a third. Oxytocin is a research lead, not a treatment. And no biological marker is used to diagnose autism.

Mindblindness and other psychological theories

Brain findings describe where autism might live; psychological theories try to explain what it is like from the inside, and why one person should have the particular mixture of difficulties and strengths that autism brings: trouble understanding other people, rigid routines, sensory oddities and, often, remarkable attention to detail. Four theories dominate the textbooks and the papers, and each was built around a clever experiment. The first and most famous begins with chimpanzees.

In 1978 the psychologists David Premack and Guy Woodruff published a paper with a memorable title, "Does the chimpanzee have a theory of mind?" By a theory of mind they meant the ability to attribute mental states (beliefs, desires, intentions, knowledge, pretence) to oneself and to others, and to use them to explain and predict behaviour. It is called a "theory" because mental states cannot be seen; we infer them, as a scientist infers an unseen cause from its effects. You use a theory of mind every time you think "she is looking in her bag because she thinks her phone is there". The strongest test of the ability is a false belief: if I can predict that you will act on a belief that I know to be wrong, I must be representing your mind separately from the world and from my own mind. The psychologists Heinz Wimmer and Josef Perner built the first false-belief task for children in 1983, and found that most typical children pass such tasks at about 4 years of age; the normal development of theory of mind is taught in the Developmental book. Two years later, three researchers in London asked whether autistic children, who seemed so puzzled by other people, could pass it.

Key study: Does the autistic child have a "theory of mind"? (Baron-Cohen, Leslie and Frith, 1985)

Question. Is the social impairment of autism due to a specific inability to represent other people's mental states, rather than to general intellectual disability?

Method. Simon Baron-Cohen (born 1958), Alan M. Leslie and Uta Frith (born 1941) tested three groups: 20 autistic children (mean age almost 12, mean IQ 82, a deliberately high-functioning group), 14 children with Down syndrome (mean age about 11, mean IQ 64) and 27 typically developing preschool children (mean age about 4 and a half). The autistic children's average verbal mental age (5 years 5 months) was well above the Down syndrome group's (2 years 11 months). The experimenter acted out a story with two dolls (Figure 3.9). Sally puts a marble in her basket and leaves the room. While she is away, Anne takes the marble out of the basket and puts it in her own box. Sally comes back. The child is asked the Belief Question: "Where will Sally look for her marble?", and two control questions: "Where is the marble really?" and "Where was the marble in the beginning?" The scene was then repeated with the marble moved to a new place, the experimenter's pocket.

Result. Every child in every group answered the control questions correctly, so all of them understood and remembered the story. On the Belief Question, 23 of the 27 typical children (85 percent) and 12 of the 14 children with Down syndrome (86 percent) pointed to the basket. Sixteen of the 20 autistic children (80 percent) pointed to where the marble really was, on both trials; on the second trial they pointed to the pocket, not to the box, which ruled out simple position habits or contrariness.

Conclusion. Autistic children as a group fail to employ a theory of mind: they cannot represent the fact that Sally's belief differs from their own knowledge of the world. Because the Down syndrome children, with a lower mental age, passed, the deficit is specific to autism and is not explained by intellectual disability.

Criticism. Four of the 20 autistic children (a fifth) passed, so the deficit is not universal. Many autistic children pass such tasks later, as their language develops, which led to harder tests. The deficit is not unique to autism. And a theory of mind deficit does not explain the restricted, repetitive behaviour, the sensory differences or the islets of ability that are equally part of autism.

Baron-Cohen developed the idea into a book, Mindblindness (1995), whose title gave the theory its popular name. In this theory mindblindness is the inability, or reduced ability, to "see" other minds: to understand that people have beliefs, desires and intentions of their own that drive what they do. A child who is mindblind lives in a world of behaviour without reasons. People's actions seem arbitrary and unpredictable, which is frightening, and the predictable world of objects, numbers, timetables and machines becomes a refuge. The theory accounts neatly for a cluster of autistic difficulties that are otherwise puzzling: with pretend play (pretending requires representing a representation), with lies and secrets, with jokes, sarcasm and white lies, which all depend on a gap between what is said and what is meant, and with the give-and-take of conversation, which depends on tracking what the listener already knows.

Deception is a particularly telling case, because to deceive you must deliberately plant a false belief in another mind. In a 1992 experiment Beate Sodian and Uta Frith set children the task of keeping a sweet away from a greedy puppet. Autistic children were as good as others at sabotage, simply locking the box so the puppet could not get in, but much poorer at deception, telling the puppet that the box was locked when it was not; their success at sabotage showed that they understood the task, and their success at deceiving was predicted by their success on a false-belief task. Clinicians see the same thing every day. Advait, 10, is an autistic boy in Bengaluru with fluent speech, who can name every station on the city's metro lines in order. When he plays hide-and-seek with his cousins he tells them where he is going to hide, and he cannot understand why they then find him at once. When his class teacher looks at his unfinished worksheet, sighs and says "Wonderful work, Advait", he says "Thank you" and puts it in his bag. When a classmate rolls her eyes as he begins his third explanation of the metro map, he does not notice and carries on.

Notice what is happening in the last two examples. Before Advait can work out what his teacher or his classmate means, he has to take in the cues that carry the meaning: the sigh, the tone of voice, the rolled eyes, the direction of a glance. Psychologists studying how children process social situations, above all Kenneth Dodge and Nicki Crick, whose 1994 social information-processing model is the standard account, divide the process into steps: encoding the social cues (attending to and taking in the relevant information), interpreting them, clarifying one's goals, generating possible responses, choosing one and enacting it. The first step, encoding of social cues, is where research places a large part of the autistic difficulty: the cues are not attended to, or not taken in, in the first place, so the later steps have little to work with.

The most direct evidence comes from eye-tracking. In 2002 Ami Klin and his colleagues at Yale showed film scenes of people interacting to 15 intellectually able autistic males and 15 matched controls and recorded exactly where each viewer looked. The control viewers looked mainly at the actors' eyes, where most of the emotional information in a face lies. The autistic viewers looked far less at the eyes and much more at mouths, bodies and objects; reduced looking at the eyes was the single best predictor of autism, and the more time an autistic viewer spent looking at objects rather than people, the poorer his everyday social functioning. Combined with the face-processing findings of Section 3.6.6 and Pinel and Barnes's observation that autistic people spend less time looking at faces, especially eyes, and remember faces less well, the evidence makes a coherent picture. Social understanding is built from social information, and in autism much of that information is never encoded.

Exam trap

"Research evidence suggests that people with autism show deficits in" anger management, encoding social cues, deception or memory retrieval (RML 2020). The key is encoding social cues: eye-tracking and face-processing studies show reduced attention to faces and eyes, so the first step of social information processing (Crick and Dodge's model) is impaired. Deception is tempting, because autistic children do find it hard to deceive; but that is a consequence of a theory of mind difficulty, not the deficit research names, and the option is worded as if autistic people "lack deception" as a skill to be trained. Anger management problems occur in some autistic people but are not a defining or researched core deficit, and memory retrieval is usually intact: rote memory is often a strength (Donald Triplett recited long passages at two).

The theory of mind account has been tested hard since 1985, and its limits are now clear. First, the fifth of autistic children who passed the original test were not unusual: as language develops, many autistic children pass first-order false-belief tasks (what does Sally think?). So researchers devised harder tests. Second-order tasks ask what one person thinks another person thinks, and in 1991 Sally Ozonoff, Bruce Pennington and Sally Rogers found that second-order deficits were widespread among intellectually able autistic people, while first-order deficits were found in only a subset. In 1994 Francesca Happé (born 1967) introduced the Strange Stories, short stories in which a character says something not literally true (a white lie, a joke, a figure of speech, a piece of sarcasm or a double bluff) and the reader must explain why; able autistic adults who passed standard false-belief tasks still gave less appropriate mental-state explanations. Second, the deficit is not unique to autism. In 1995 Candida Peterson and Michael Siegal found that 65 percent of prelingually deaf Australian children aged 8 to 13, of normal intelligence, failed a simple false-belief task, performing no better than autistic children in earlier studies; Peterson and Siegal explained the result by the children's limited early exposure to conversation about mental states. Third, as the criticism in the Key study box noted, mindblindness says nothing about why autistic people line up cars, flap their hands, cover their ears at the pressure cooker or notice the tiny detail everyone else misses. DSM-5-TR's own summary is measured: theory of mind deficits are common in autism, but "not necessarily present in all cases". The other theories set out to explain what mindblindness leaves out.

Weak central coherence. Most people process information for meaning and gist: they remember the point of a story rather than its words, and see a forest before they see its trees. Uta Frith, in her book Autism: Explaining the Enigma (1989), proposed that autistic people have weak central coherence, a reduced drive to pull details together into a coherent whole, so that they "cannot see the wood for the trees" but see the trees unusually well. The classic evidence comes from two tests of visual detail. In 1983 Frith and Amitta Shah found that autistic children were unusually good at the Embedded Figures Test, in which a simple shape must be found hidden inside a complex drawing, an "islet of ability" in children who were otherwise struggling. In 1993 Shah and Frith used the Block Design test, in which a pattern must be copied with coloured blocks: autistic participants did unusually well when the design was shown whole, and their advantage shrank when the design was shown already divided into its block-sized segments, because, the authors argued, other people are slowed by having to break the whole into parts, whereas autistic people see the parts from the start. The theory explains detail-focused talents, the distress caused by a tiny change in an arrangement, and the tendency to take words literally without using context. It was later softened. Laurent Mottron and others showed that autistic people can process the global picture perfectly well when asked to, and in 2006 Happé and Frith restated the idea as a detail-focused cognitive style, a bias rather than a deficit, which can be an advantage in many tasks.

Executive dysfunction. Executive functions are the control processes, centred on the prefrontal cortex, that allow us to plan, to hold goals in mind, to inhibit an automatic response and to shift flexibly from one rule or task to another. The same 1991 study by Ozonoff, Pennington and Rogers found significant executive deficits in intellectually able autistic people compared with a clinical control group matched on verbal IQ, on tests such as the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test, in which the person must discover a sorting rule (colour, shape or number) and then switch when the rule changes without warning. Autistic participants made many perseverative errors, going on sorting by the old rule after it had stopped working, and also struggled with planning tasks such as the Tower of Hanoi. Executive dysfunction explains rigidity, insistence on sameness, difficulty with transitions and repetitive behaviour better than mindblindness does, and it fits the practical disorganisation that limits so many able autistic adults (Section 3.6.3). Its weakness is that it is not specific: executive deficits are also found in ADHD, obsessive-compulsive disorder, schizophrenia and many other conditions, and on their own they do not explain the social picture. DSM-5-TR again sums it up: executive function deficits are common in autism "but not specific", as are difficulties with central coherence.

Empathising and systemising: the extreme male brain. Baron-Cohen later placed mindblindness inside a broader theory. His empathising-systemising theory (developed from about 2002, and set out for a general audience in his 2003 book The Essential Difference) describes two dimensions on which people differ: empathising, the drive to identify another person's thoughts and feelings and respond with an appropriate emotion, and systemising, the drive to analyse and build rule-based systems (a timetable, a machine, a grammar, a cricket scorecard). On average, Baron-Cohen argued, women score somewhat higher on empathising and men somewhat higher on systemising; autism represents an extreme of the male profile, with very strong systemising and weak empathising, which is the extreme male brain theory of autism (Baron-Cohen, 2002). He linked it to the levels of testosterone a fetus is exposed to in the womb, and his group found that higher testosterone in amniotic fluid predicted more autistic traits in typical children. The theory explains the narrow, rule-bound interests, the talents some autistic people have in mathematics, engineering and computing, and the male excess. It has been sharply criticised, by Cordelia Fine and Gina Rippon among others, for resting on gender stereotypes and on weak evidence about sex differences in the brain, and for making it harder still to recognise autism in girls and women, whose interests are often social in focus (Section 3.5.6).

Enhanced perceptual functioning. Laurent Mottron (born 1952) and colleagues in Montreal turned weak central coherence on its head. Their enhanced perceptual functioning model, updated in 2006 as "eight principles of autistic perception", proposes that low-level perception, for pitch, visual detail and pattern, is not merely spared but superior in autism and more independent of top-down control by expectations and concepts. It explains sensory hypersensitivity, superior visual search and the perceptual basis of some savant skills. Savant skills themselves, the exceptional islands of ability in calendar calculation, music, drawing or memory found in a minority of autistic people, are described in Book 1, Section 9.8.5. Table 3.18 sets the theories side by side.

Table 3.18 The psychological theories of autism compared.
Theory and whoCore claimClassic testExplains wellFails to explain
Theory of mind deficit, "mindblindness" (Baron-Cohen, Leslie and Frith 1985; Baron-Cohen 1995)Difficulty representing other people's beliefs, desires and intentionsSally-Anne false-belief task; second-order tasks; Happé's Strange Stories (1994)Social and communication difficulties; problems with pretence, deception, jokes and sarcasmRepetitive behaviour, sensory differences, islets of ability; not universal (a fifth pass) and not unique (deaf children)
Social cue encoding (Klin and colleagues 2002, within Crick and Dodge's model)Faces, eyes, tone and gesture are not attended to or taken inEye-tracking of film scenes; fusiform face area studiesThe earliest social signs; poor emotion reading; links to the brain findingsRestricted, repetitive behaviour
