Exam trap

Match the theory to its author. Theory of mind as a term: Premack and Woodruff (1978), about chimpanzees. The first false-belief task: Wimmer and Perner (1983). The Sally-Anne study of autism: Baron-Cohen, Leslie and Frith (1985), in Cognition. Mindblindness (1995) and the extreme male brain (2002): Baron-Cohen. Weak central coherence (1989): Frith, not Baron-Cohen. Executive dysfunction: Ozonoff, Pennington and Rogers (1991). Enhanced perceptual functioning: Mottron. In the Sally-Anne study the comparison group that passed despite a lower mental age was the Down syndrome group, which is what shows the deficit is specific to autism rather than to intellectual disability.

Treatment: what helps, what does not, and what drugs can do

Every family of a newly diagnosed child is offered a bewildering menu of treatments, from therapies with decades of trials behind them to injections, diets and devices with none. The first thing to tell them is the general conclusion that Barlow and Durand state plainly: no treatment eliminates autism, and no medicine treats its core social and communication difficulties. What works is teaching. The psychological and educational treatments, which build communication, social, play and daily-living skills and reduce the behaviours that get in the way of learning, are the foundation of care, and they are taught in full in Book 2, Section 10.5, and in the account of applied behaviour analysis and the Lovaas programme in Book 2, Section 4.6.9. They are summarised here, with the evidence for each, in Table 3.19, so that the drugs, which are this book's business, can be seen in their proper, supporting place.

Table 3.19 Treatments for autism: what each is, what the evidence shows, and where Book 2 teaches it.
InterventionWhat it isWhat the evidence showsBook 2
Early intensive behavioural intervention (Lovaas; discrete trial training)Many hours a week of structured one-to-one teaching from the preschool years, built on prompting, shaping and reinforcementHelps many children gain language, adaptive and cognitive skills; Lovaas's 1987 claim of "normal functioning" in 9 of 19 children has not been replicated on that scale; it cures no one4.6.9, 10.5.2
Functional communication trainingFinds what a challenging behaviour achieves and teaches a communicative way of achieving itWell supported for reducing self-injury, aggression and tantrums2.8.2, 10.5.2
TEACCH (structured teaching)Visual schedules, organised workspaces and predictable routines that use visual strengthsPositive but modest gains in some skills and in parents' stress; fewer randomised trials10.5.3
PECS and other augmentative and alternative communicationExchanging pictures to request, then building picture sentences; signs, communication books, speech-generating devicesReliably teaches requesting; no evidence that augmentative communication holds back speech10.5.4
Social stories and social skills groupsShort personalised stories that explain a situation; group teaching of conversation and friendship skills for able childrenInconsistent and generally modest effects10.5.5
Naturalistic developmental behavioural interventions (pivotal response treatment, the Early Start Denver Model, JASPER)Behavioural principles delivered in play and daily routines, following the child's leadRandomised trials show gains, most consistently in expressive language; evidence quality still low to moderate10.5.6
Parent-mediated intervention (PACT)Parents coached, with video feedback, to notice and respond to the child's communicationGreen and colleagues (2010): better parent-child interaction, little change in symptoms at the end of treatment; Pickles and colleagues (2016): modestly lower symptom scores about six years later10.5.6
Speech and language therapyWork on understanding, expression, augmentative communication and the social use of languageA standard part of care, usually combined with the approaches above10.5.4
Occupational therapy; sensory integration therapyDaily-living and motor skills; swings, brushing and weighted vests meant to "organise" sensory processing (Ayres)Occupational therapy for practical skills is useful; sensory integration therapy has weak and inconsistent evidence10.5.7
Facilitated communicationA facilitator supports the person's hand or arm while he typesDiscredited: controlled tests (Wheeler and colleagues, 1993) showed the messages came from the facilitator10.5.7
Unsupported or harmful treatmentsSecretin injections, chelation, gluten-free and casein-free diets, hyperbaric oxygen, stem-cell injectionsNo benefit in controlled trials or no trials at all; chelation has caused deaths; stem-cell therapy for autism is not permitted in routine practice in India10.5.7

Two of the unsupported treatments in the last row have instructive histories. In the late 1990s, after wide publicity about an autistic child whose condition was said to have improved dramatically after a single dose of secretin, a gut hormone that stimulates the pancreas and is used in a diagnostic test of the digestive system, thousands of autistic children may have been given it. The response of science was a randomised, double-blind trial: in 1999 Sandler and colleagues gave a single dose of synthetic human secretin or saline to 60 children aged 3 to 14 and found no benefit; the placebo group, if anything, improved slightly more. Strikingly, after hearing the results, 69 percent of the parents said they were still interested in secretin for their child, a reminder of how powerful hope is and how carefully clinicians must speak to it. The second, chelation, the use of drugs that bind and remove heavy metals from the body, was promoted on the false theory that mercury from vaccines causes autism; it is a treatment for genuine lead poisoning, it has no benefit in autism, and it is dangerous: in 2005, according to the CDC, two children died after chelation treatments, one of them an autistic boy.

Now to the drugs. They are used not for autism but for particular problems that accompany it: severe irritability and aggression, co-occurring ADHD, anxiety or depression, sleeplessness and epilepsy. Table 3.20 summarises them.

Antipsychotics for severe irritability. In the language of the drug trials, irritability means severe tantrums, aggression towards others, self-injury and rapid mood changes, the behaviours that most often break down school placements and exhaust families. The decisive trial was run by the Research Units on Pediatric Psychopharmacology (RUPP) Autism Network, a group of American university centres, and published in the New England Journal of Medicine in 2002 by James McCracken and colleagues. They randomly assigned 101 children aged 5 to 17 (82 boys and 19 girls) with autistic disorder and severe tantrums, aggression or self-injury to eight weeks of risperidone (0.5 to 3.5 mg a day) or placebo. Risperidone reduced the irritability score by 56.9 percent, against 14.1 percent on placebo; 69 percent of the children on risperidone were rated as responders, against 12 percent on placebo; and in two-thirds of the responders the benefit was maintained at six months. The costs were equally clear: an average weight gain of 2.7 kg in eight weeks (against 0.8 kg), and more increased appetite, fatigue, drowsiness, dizziness and drooling. On the strength of this evidence the United States Food and Drug Administration (FDA) approved risperidone in 2006 for irritability associated with autism in children and adolescents (aged 5 to 16), the first drug approved for any aspect of autism; aripiprazole followed in 2009 (aged 6 to 17).

Both are atypical antipsychotics, which block dopamine D2 receptors (aripiprazole as a partial agonist) and serotonin 5-HT2A receptors; their pharmacology and their adverse effects are taught in Sections 4.10.2 to 4.10.5. In children the effects that matter most are weight gain and metabolic change (raised blood sugar and lipids), sedation, extrapyramidal effects such as stiffness and restlessness, and, with risperidone in particular, hyperprolactinaemia, a rise in the hormone prolactin that can cause breast enlargement and milk secretion in boys as well as girls and menstrual disturbance in girls. Weight, waist, blood pressure, blood sugar and lipids are therefore checked before treatment and regularly during it. The drugs reduce the behaviours; they do not improve social communication, and they are not recommended for mild aggression or ordinary tantrums. They are a reason to add, not to abandon, the functional communication training that teaches a child another way to say "stop" or "I want".

Antidepressants for repetitive behaviour. Because repetitive behaviour in autism looks a little like the compulsions of obsessive-compulsive disorder, which respond to selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs; Section 5.7.2), it seemed logical to try them. The STAART trial, led by Bryan King and published in 2009, randomly assigned autistic children with high levels of repetitive behaviour to 12 weeks of citalopram or placebo. The response rates were 32.9 percent on citalopram and 34.2 percent on placebo: no difference at all. Citalopram did produce more adverse effects, especially "activation": increased energy, impulsiveness, poor concentration, hyperactivity, more stereotypy, diarrhoea and insomnia. SSRIs are still used, with care, for clear anxiety and depression in autistic adolescents and adults, as in anyone else, but not to reduce routines in children.

Drugs for co-occurring ADHD. When an autistic child also meets the criteria for ADHD (Section 3.6.2), the ADHD drugs help, but less reliably than in ADHD alone. In the RUPP network's crossover trial of methylphenidate in 2005, 49 percent of 72 autistic children with marked hyperactivity responded, the response was smaller than in typically developing children with ADHD, adverse effects were more frequent, and they led 18 percent of the children to stop the drug. Atomoxetine and guanfacine are used as alternatives. The drugs themselves are taught in Sections 3.8.6 and 3.8.7.

Melatonin for sleep. Melatonin is the hormone the pineal gland releases in darkness to signal night to the body clock. Given an hour or so before bedtime it shortens the time taken to fall asleep, and it is the one drug with good trial evidence for the sleep-onset insomnia so common in autism. In a 13-week randomised trial of a prolonged-release children's preparation published in 2017 by Paul Gringras and colleagues, autistic children's total sleep rose by an average of 57.5 minutes a night on melatonin against 9.1 minutes on placebo, and the time they took to fall asleep fell by 39.6 minutes against 12.5 minutes (adjusted differences of about 32 and 25 minutes in melatonin's favour). It is used after, not instead of, a consistent bedtime routine and good sleep habits.

Antiepileptic drugs treat seizures in autism as in anyone else. Valproate, effective for many seizure types, is avoided in girls and women who may become pregnant, for the reason given in Section 3.6.5.

The drugs that failed. Three biologically plausible drugs were tested in large, careful trials, and all three failed. Oxytocin (Section 3.6.6) was tested by Linmarie Sikich and colleagues in a 24-week placebo-controlled trial of 290 children and adolescents aged 3 to 17, published in the New England Journal of Medicine in 2021; intranasal oxytocin produced no improvement in social functioning over placebo. Bumetanide, a diuretic that alters the balance of chloride inside neurons, looked promising in small trials, but two international phase III trials (one in children aged 7 to 17, one in children aged 2 to 6, with 211 children each) were stopped early in the early 2020s because bumetanide did no better than placebo, as Joaquin Fuentes and colleagues reported in 2023. And secretin, as described above, failed in 1999. The lesson is not that drug research is pointless, but that promising small studies are often wrong, and that only large randomised trials should change practice.

Table 3.20 Drugs used in autism spectrum disorder: what each is for and how good the evidence is.
DrugUsed forKey evidenceMain adverse effects
Risperidone (atypical antipsychotic)Severe irritability: tantrums, aggression, self-injuryRUPP 2002: 69 percent responders against 12 percent on placebo; FDA approval 2006Weight gain, raised prolactin, sedation, metabolic change, extrapyramidal effects
Aripiprazole (atypical antipsychotic, partial agonist)Severe irritabilityFDA approval 2009Weight gain, sedation, restlessness (akathisia)
Citalopram and other SSRIsRepetitive behaviour (children); anxiety and depression (adolescents, adults)STAART 2009: 32.9 against 34.2 percent, no benefit for repetitive behaviourActivation: energy, impulsiveness, hyperactivity, insomnia
Methylphenidate; atomoxetine; guanfacineCo-occurring ADHDRUPP 2005: 49 percent respond to methylphenidate, less than in ADHD aloneSmaller response and more frequent side effects than in ADHD alone (Section 3.8)

Ayaan's plan shows how these pieces fit together for one three-year-old. The team recommends parent-mediated early intervention, in which Laila and Daniyal are coached to build his communication into everyday routines at home, together with speech and language therapy that introduces picture exchange so that he can request without leading an adult by the wrist (Book 2, Sections 10.5.4 and 10.5.6). He is to start at a preschool with a shadow teacher, an aide who sits with him, helps him follow the class routine and prompts him to join in with other children. For his sleep the family will first set up a fixed, calm bedtime routine, and melatonin will be considered if that fails. He is not prescribed an antipsychotic, because he has no severe irritability or self-injury; risperidone is a treatment for those behaviours, not for autism. His parents have been reassured about the vaccine (Section 3.6.5), his fragile X test is negative, and he will be reviewed in six months. Whether he has an accompanying intellectual impairment will be looked at again when he reaches school age.

Indian context

In India autism spectrum disorder is one of the specified disabilities under the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016, with rights to inclusive education; the National Trust (set up under the National Trust Act of 1999) runs schemes for families of people with autism and intellectual disability, and the National Institute for the Empowerment of Persons with Intellectual Disabilities (NIEPID) in Secunderabad trains professionals and develops services (Book 2, Sections 1.10.9 and 10.5.8; certification with ISAA is in Section 3.1). Families are also offered stem-cell injections by private clinics. In December 2022 the National Medical Commission advised that stem-cell therapy is not recommended for autism in clinical practice, and in January 2026 the Supreme Court held that it may not be offered as a clinical service outside approved clinical trials.

Exam trap

No drug treats the core social communication symptoms of autism. Risperidone (FDA 2006) and aripiprazole (FDA 2009) are approved for irritability: severe tantrums, aggression and self-injury; their price is weight gain and, with risperidone, raised prolactin. SSRIs did not reduce repetitive behaviour in children (citalopram, 2009). The treatment of choice is behavioural and educational (Lovaas, TEACCH, PECS and the others in Table 3.19). Secretin, chelation, gluten-free casein-free diets, facilitated communication and oxytocin are the wrong answers to "an effective treatment for autism".

3.7 Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder I: History, Symptoms and DiagnosisMedium yield · 4 Qs

Every school in India has him: the boy in the third row who is out of his seat before the teacher has finished the instruction, who answers questions nobody has asked, who has lost his rubber, his sharpener and his water bottle by the second period, and whose notebook is full of sums that he knew how to do and got wrong anyway. Teachers call him naughty, grandparents say "boys are like this", and his parents wonder whether he is lazy or whether they have failed him. For some such children, the restlessness is simply the ordinary exuberance of childhood. For a minority it is something more persistent and more disabling, a disorder that DSM-5-TR calls attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) and places, as Section 1.8.2 explains, among the neurodevelopmental disorders, because it begins in childhood, reflects differences in the development of the brain and frequently travels with the other disorders of this chapter. This section traces how the condition came to be recognised and named, teaches its eighteen symptoms and the rules for diagnosing it, compares the narrower ICD-10 concept of hyperkinetic disorder, and shows how to tell it from the many conditions that imitate it. Who has ADHD, what causes it, how it changes with age and how it is treated with drugs are the subject of Section 3.8.

Two hundred years of restless children

ADHD is sometimes dismissed as a modern invention, a label for the inevitable restlessness of children forced to sit in classrooms. Its history says otherwise. Physicians were describing the same pattern long before modern schooling, psychiatry or drugs, and the story of how they named it is a story of changing ideas about what the core problem is: bad morals, a damaged brain, too much movement, or too little attention and self-control.

The first clear medical description is usually credited to the Scottish physician Alexander Crichton (1763 to 1856), whose An Inquiry into the Nature and Origin of Mental Derangement appeared in 1798. (A German physician, Melchior Adam Weikard, had described inattentive, shallow and reckless people in a medical textbook of 1775, but his account was far less detailed.) In a chapter on attention and its diseases, Crichton described "the incapacity of attending with a necessary degree of constancy to any one object", in people whose attention was "incessantly withdrawn from one impression to another". Every small event (people walking about the room, a door shutting, a little too much heat or light) distracted them and produced what he called "an unnatural degree of mental restlessness"; such patients, he noted, said that they had "the fidgets". Crichton added three observations that modern research has confirmed: the condition could be present from birth or follow illness; when it was inborn it became evident very early in life and made the child "incapable of attending with constancy to any one object of education"; and it was "generally diminished with age". What Crichton described is closer to the inattentive side of today's disorder than to the hyperactive side.

Half a century later came a portrait that is quoted in almost every history of ADHD, though it was never meant as medicine. In 1845 the Frankfurt physician Heinrich Hoffmann (1809 to 1894), who later ran the city's mental hospital, published a picture book of cautionary verses for children, Der Struwwelpeter ("Shock-headed Peter"). Its second edition, a year later, added "The Story of Fidgety Philip" (Zappel-Philipp), a boy who will not sit still at the dinner table, rocks on his chair until it tips over, and pulls the cloth and the whole meal down with him while his parents look on in fury. Another story in the book, of a boy who walks along looking at the sky until he falls into a river, is sometimes read as a portrait of inattention. These are cultural images, not clinical cases; they show that a restless, heedless child was a recognisable figure in nineteenth-century Germany, nothing more.

The scientific history of ADHD is usually dated to March 1902, when the British paediatrician George Frederic Still (1868 to 1941), later known as the father of British paediatrics, gave the Goulstonian Lectures to the Royal College of Physicians of London, published the same year in The Lancet as "Some abnormal psychical conditions in children". Still was interested in what he called an "abnormal defect of moral control", by which he meant "the control of action in conformity with the idea of the good of all", in children whose failure could not be blamed on their upbringing. Among his cases he described 20 children who showed this defect without any general impairment of intellect and without physical disease: 15 boys and 5 girls, a sex ratio close to the one seen in ADHD clinics today. They were passionate, defiant, spiteful, reckless and unable to learn from punishment, and Still was struck above all by "a quite abnormal incapacity for sustained attention" in many of them. In most of the cases where the onset could be dated it had come before the age of 7, and Still suspected an inborn, biological cause, inherited in some children and due to brain injury before or after birth in others. Modern readers recognise in Still's children a mixture of what would now be diagnosed as ADHD, oppositional defiant disorder and conduct disorder, which is why his "moral control" has not survived as a concept while his description of the attentional problem has.

The next push came from an epidemic. In the years around the end of the First World War (roughly 1917 to the late 1920s), an epidemic of encephalitis lethargica, an inflammation of the brain first described in 1917 by the Viennese neurologist Constantin von Economo, swept around the world. Many children who survived it were left with a post-encephalitic behaviour disorder: they were restless, impulsive, emotionally unstable, sleepless and destructive, quite unlike their former selves, and the phrase "organic driveness" was used for the driven, ceaseless activity that seemed to spring from damage to the brainstem. The lesson clinicians drew was that hyperactivity could be a sign of brain damage, and they then made a much bigger leap: that wherever hyperactivity was seen, brain damage could be presumed, even when none could be demonstrated. In 1937 Charles Bradley, working at a children's home in Rhode Island, made the accidental discovery that the stimulant Benzedrine made many restless children calmer and more attentive (Book 2, Section 10.4.3 tells the story). In 1947 Alfred Strauss and Laura Lehtinen published Psychopathology and Education of the Brain-Injured Child, and the practice of inferring "brain injury" from behaviour alone, in children with hyperactivity, distractibility and learning problems, became standard. Such children were said to have minimal brain damage.

When no damage could be found in most of them, the label changed rather than the idea. In 1957 Maurice Laufer and Eric Denhoff, at the same Rhode Island children's home where Bradley had worked, described a "hyperkinetic impulse disorder", a pattern of hyperactivity that could arise with or without demonstrable brain disease. In 1966 an American national task force, in a report written by Sam Clements, defined minimal brain dysfunction (MBD) as a condition of children of near-average, average or above-average intelligence with learning or behavioural disabilities "associated with deviations of function of the central nervous system". "Dysfunction" replaced "damage" because damage could not be shown. MBD was soon criticised as too vague, lumping together children with hyperactivity, reading problems and clumsiness on the strength of an invisible brain abnormality, and it was gradually replaced by more specific, descriptive labels: hyperactivity, learning disability and dyslexia among them.

The diagnostic manuals now took over the story, and each edition shifted the emphasis.

  • DSM-II (1968) introduced hyperkinetic reaction of childhood (or adolescence), defined in two sentences: the disorder "is characterized by overactivity, restlessness, distractibility, and short attention span, especially in young children; the behavior usually diminishes in adolescence". The emphasis was on movement, and the assumption was that the child would grow out of it.
  • In 1972 the Canadian psychologist Virginia Douglas of McGill University, in an influential paper based on her presidential address to the Canadian Psychological Association, argued that the core problem was not excessive movement but deficits in sustained attention and impulse control, which were also the symptoms that responded best to stimulant drugs.
  • DSM-III (1980) followed Douglas and renamed the condition attention deficit disorder (ADD), with or without hyperactivity, with three separate symptom lists (inattention, impulsivity and hyperactivity), explicit numbers of symptoms, onset before age 7 and a duration of at least six months.
  • DSM-III-R (1987) renamed it attention-deficit hyperactivity disorder and merged the three lists into a single list of 14 symptoms, of which 8 were needed, with onset before 7; children who were inattentive without hyperactivity were moved to a vague residual category, "undifferentiated attention-deficit disorder".
  • ICD-10 (1992) kept the older European concept of hyperkinetic disorder, narrower than the American one (Section 3.7.4).
  • DSM-IV (1994) created the modern structure after a large field trial: two lists of nine symptoms, inattention and hyperactivity-impulsivity, with six of nine required in either or both; three subtypes (combined, predominantly inattentive and predominantly hyperactive-impulsive); some impairing symptoms before age 7; impairment in two or more settings; and the name "attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder", with the slash.
  • DSM-5 (2013) kept the two lists but replaced "subtypes" with "presentations" (Section 3.7.3), raised the age by which several symptoms must be present from 7 to 12, lowered the threshold to five symptoms for people aged 17 and over, added examples of how each symptom looks in adolescents and adults, dropped autism as an exclusion so that both can be diagnosed together (Section 3.6.2), and moved ADHD from the chapter of disorders usually first diagnosed in childhood to the new neurodevelopmental chapter (Section 1.8.2). DSM-5-TR (2022) left the criteria essentially unchanged, and ICD-11, in force from 2022, finally adopted the name attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (code 6A05).

Figure 3.10 sets these names in order. One myth from this history survives in conversation and deserves a name here: the belief that stimulants have a "paradoxical" calming effect only on hyperactive children, so that a child who calms down on a stimulant must have ADHD. It is false, as Book 2, Section 10.4.3 explains and Section 3.8.6 shows in detail.

Exam trap

Know the sequence of names. Still (1902): "defect of moral control", the usual starting point of ADHD's scientific history. Minimal brain damage gave way to minimal brain dysfunction (1960s) when no damage could be shown. DSM-II (1968): hyperkinetic reaction of childhood. DSM-III (1980): attention deficit disorder, with or without hyperactivity, after Virginia Douglas's 1972 emphasis on sustained attention. DSM-III-R (1987): ADHD as one list. DSM-IV (1994): two lists, three subtypes, onset before 7. DSM-5 (2013): presentations, before 12. Bradley's 1937 drug was Benzedrine, an amphetamine, not methylphenidate. And do not confuse Still with Kanner: Kanner (1943) described autism.

The two lists: the DSM-5-TR criteria

Vedant is 10 and in Class 5 at a private English-medium school in Lucknow. He lives in a joint family with his parents, Ritika, 34, a bank clerk, and Mehul, 37, who runs a mobile-phone shop, his sister, who is 4, and his paternal grandparents. The school has sent a note home, and his class teacher's list of complaints is long. Vedant cannot sit through a period: he is out of his seat to sharpen his pencil or to look out of the window. He runs in the corridors, talks constantly, blurts out answers before she has finished the question and pushes into the line. He loses pencils, rubbers and water bottles almost every week. He leaves his classwork half done, makes careless mistakes in mathematics although he knows the methods, and "daydreams" during dictation. At home, his mother says, he has been "never still since he could walk". He climbs on the furniture, interrupts the adults, cannot wait his turn at carrom or cricket and forgets what he was sent to fetch before he reaches the next room; his homework takes two hours, and only gets done with his mother sitting beside him. His grandparents are unworried: "Boys are like this; his father was the same." It turns out that Mehul, as a boy, was often punished at school for restlessness, and left his studies after Class 10.

Is Vedant a lively boy in a demanding school, or does he have ADHD? DSM-5-TR answers the question with five criteria, A to E. Each has a reason behind it, and each rules something out.

Criterion A: the symptoms. The first requirement is "a persistent pattern of inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity that interferes with functioning or development". The two halves of that phrase are the two symptom dimensions that factor-analytic research has found again and again in children's behaviour ratings, and DSM-5-TR lists nine symptoms for each. In the manual's description, inattention shows itself in behaviour as wandering off task, failing to follow through on instructions or finish work, difficulty sustaining focus and disorganisation, and it is "not attributable to defiance or lack of comprehension". By hyperactivity the manual means excessive motor activity, such as a child running about when it is not appropriate, or excessive fidgeting, tapping or talkativeness; in adults it may appear as extreme restlessness or as wearing others out with their activity. And impulsivity is acting hastily in the moment without forethought, sometimes in ways that risk harm (darting into the road without looking), and it may reflect a desire for immediate rewards or an inability to delay gratification; it shows itself as social intrusiveness and, in adults, as important decisions taken without thinking of the consequences.

The nine symptoms of inattention (criterion A1), in paraphrase, are that the person often:

  1. fails to give close attention to details or makes careless mistakes in schoolwork, at work or in other activities;
  2. has difficulty sustaining attention in tasks or in play, for example in lectures, conversations or long reading;
  3. does not seem to listen when spoken to directly, the mind seeming to be elsewhere even when nothing obvious is distracting;
  4. does not follow through on instructions and fails to finish schoolwork, chores or duties at work, starting tasks but quickly losing focus;
  5. has difficulty organising tasks and activities: managing tasks in sequence, keeping belongings in order, managing time and meeting deadlines;
  6. avoids, dislikes or is reluctant to take up tasks that need sustained mental effort, such as homework, or for adults preparing reports and filling in forms;
  7. loses things needed for tasks and activities: school materials, pencils, books, tools, wallets, keys, spectacles, mobile phones;
  8. is easily distracted by extraneous stimuli, which in older adolescents and adults may include unrelated thoughts; and
  9. is forgetful in daily activities: chores and errands, and for adults returning calls, paying bills and keeping appointments.

The nine symptoms of hyperactivity and impulsivity (criterion A2) are that the person often:

  1. fidgets with or taps the hands or feet, or squirms in the seat;
  2. leaves the seat when remaining seated is expected, in the classroom, the office or elsewhere;
  3. runs about or climbs where it is inappropriate (in adolescents or adults this may be limited to feeling restless);
  4. is unable to play or take part in leisure activities quietly;
  5. is "on the go", acting as if "driven by a motor", unable to be, or uncomfortable being, still for long, as in restaurants or meetings, so that others find the person restless or hard to keep up with;
  6. talks excessively;
  7. blurts out an answer before a question has been completed, completing other people's sentences or unable to wait for a turn in conversation;
  8. has difficulty waiting his or her turn, for example in a queue; and
  9. interrupts or intrudes on others, butting into conversations, games or activities, using other people's things without asking, or in adolescents and adults taking over what others are doing.

The second list mixes two kinds of symptom, and the papers test whether you can tell them apart. The first six items (fidgeting, leaving the seat, running and climbing, not playing quietly, being "on the go" and talking excessively) are the hyperactivity items: they are about too much movement and noise. The last three (blurting out answers, difficulty waiting one's turn and interrupting or intruding) are the impulsivity items: they are about acting before thinking and before others have finished. All three impulsivity items concern the social timing of action, which is why impulsive children so quickly irritate their classmates. Table 3.21 gives every symptom with what it looks like in a schoolchild and in an adult.

Table 3.21 The eighteen DSM-5-TR symptoms of ADHD, in paraphrase, with what each looks like in a school child and in an adult. Each symptom must be present "often".

Criterion A attaches four conditions to the lists, and each is there for a reason. The count. Six or more of the nine symptoms in either list (or both) are required. For older adolescents and adults, aged 17 and over, five are enough, because the symptoms are more subtle in adults and because many of the examples were written with children in mind; a stricter threshold would miss adults who are clearly impaired. The duration. The symptoms must have persisted for at least six months, which rules out the restlessness of a child going through a bad patch: a family upheaval, a new school, an illness. The developmental yardstick. They must be present "to a degree that is inconsistent with developmental level". A three-year-old who cannot sit through a twenty-minute story is ordinary; a ten-year-old who cannot sit through a forty-minute period, when his classmates can, is not. The comparison is always with other children of the same developmental level, which is why ADHD in a child with intellectual disability requires symptoms excessive for his mental age (Section 3.7.5). The exclusion of other reasons. Each list carries a note that the symptoms are not solely a manifestation of oppositional behaviour, defiance, hostility or a failure to understand tasks or instructions. A child who will not do his homework because he refuses to be told what to do, or because he does not understand it, is not showing inattention.

Criterion B: several symptoms before age 12. ADHD begins in childhood, and DSM-5-TR requires that several inattentive or hyperactive-impulsive symptoms were present before the age of 12. DSM-IV had required some impairing symptoms before 7. Why the change, and why is no earlier age fixed? The manual explains that the requirement conveys the importance of a substantial clinical presentation during childhood, while an earlier age is not specified because precise onset is so hard to establish in retrospect: adults' recall of their childhood symptoms is unreliable, so school reports, old report cards and the memories of parents are valuable. Two rules follow. ADHD cannot be diagnosed in someone with no symptoms at all before 12. And when ADHD-like symptoms first appear after the age of 13, they are more likely to be explained by another mental disorder, such as depression, or to represent the cognitive effects of substance use.

Criterion C: two or more settings. Several symptoms must be present in two or more settings: at home, at school or work, with friends or relatives, in other activities. The rule exists because a child who is restless only in one classroom may be responding to that classroom (a teacher he fears, a lesson he cannot follow, a seat at the back from which he cannot see the board), while ADHD is a characteristic of the child that goes with him wherever he goes. DSM-5-TR adds two practical points. Confirmation of symptoms across settings usually cannot be done accurately without consulting informants who have seen the person in those settings, such as parents, teachers, spouses or employers: this is one of the reasons multi-informant assessment is recommended (Section 3.1). And the symptoms vary with context even within one setting: they may be minimal or absent when the person is receiving frequent rewards for appropriate behaviour, is under close supervision, is in a new setting, is doing something especially interesting, has constant external stimulation such as an electronic screen, or is in a one-to-one situation, such as the clinician's office. A child who sits quietly through an hour's assessment, or plays a video game for three hours without moving, may still have ADHD. A calm child in the consulting room proves nothing.

Criterion D: impairment. There must be clear evidence that the symptoms interfere with, or reduce the quality of, social, academic or occupational functioning. Lively, inattentive and talkative people who are doing well do not have a disorder.

Criterion E: exclusions. The symptoms must not occur exclusively during the course of schizophrenia or another psychotic disorder, and must not be better explained by another mental disorder, such as a mood, anxiety, dissociative or personality disorder or substance intoxication or withdrawal. The differential diagnosis that this criterion demands is the subject of Section 3.7.5. Notice what is missing from the exclusions since DSM-5: autism spectrum disorder. A child who meets the criteria for both is now given both diagnoses (Section 3.6.2). Figure 3.11 puts the five criteria in the order in which a clinician checks them.

Now apply the criteria to Vedant. From his teacher's and parents' accounts he shows at least six inattention symptoms: careless mistakes in mathematics (A1a), drifting off into "daydreams" during dictation (A1b), classwork left half done (A1d), homework he cannot get through on his own, so that it takes two hours with his mother beside him (A1f, and arguably A1e), losing pencils, rubbers and water bottles (A1g) and forgetting what he was sent to fetch (A1i). He shows at least seven hyperactive-impulsive symptoms: leaving his seat (A2b), running in corridors and climbing on furniture (A2c), being "never still" (A2e), talking constantly (A2f), blurting out answers (A2g), pushing into the line and being unable to wait his turn at carrom or cricket (A2h) and interrupting the adults (A2i). The pattern has been present far longer than six months. His school reports, and the remarks of his Class 1 teacher, confirm that several symptoms were present before the age of 7, so criterion B is easily met. The symptoms are present at school and at home (criterion C). They clearly impair his schoolwork and his relationships with classmates (criterion D). On parent and teacher rating scales, of the kind described in the Assessment book, both informants rate him high on inattention and on hyperactivity-impulsivity. What remains is criterion E, the question of whether anything else explains the picture better, which is taken up in Section 3.7.5. Notice also what the history rules out on the way: his milestones and speech were normal, his hearing and vision have been tested and are normal, he has no tics, he sleeps well, and on screening he reads and spells at the level of his class.

Exam trap

"Which is NOT a hyperactivity/impulsivity problem?" Difficulty playing quietly, squirming and fidgeting, talking excessively, or "doesn't appear to listen when spoken to" (RML 2021). The answer is the last: not seeming to listen when spoken to directly is an inattention item (A1c). Squirming and fidgeting (A2a), difficulty playing quietly (A2d) and talking excessively (A2f) are all hyperactivity items. Learn the split: of the nine A2 items, the first six are hyperactivity and the last three (blurting out answers, difficulty waiting one's turn, interrupting or intruding) are impulsivity. "Talks excessively" is hyperactivity, not impulsivity, and "easily distracted" and "forgetful" are inattention.

Exam trap

"DSM-5-TR requires symptoms to be present before what age?" 12 (MET Mock 2; PYQ Mock 2). The options 5, 6, 7, 10 and 16 are distractors: 7 was the DSM-IV rule (and is ICD-10's research rule), and 6 is the age in ICD-10's clinical guidelines ("before age 6 years", Section 3.7.4). The wording matters: several symptoms before 12, not the full disorder and not impairment. Two more numbers ride with it: at least six months of symptoms, and the threshold of five symptoms (instead of six) from age 17. Symptoms in two or more settings, not one.

Presentations, severity and remission

Once the criteria are met, DSM-5-TR asks the clinician to say which kind of ADHD the person currently shows. There are three presentations, defined by which of the two lists has been met over the past six months:

  • combined presentation (code F90.2): both criterion A1 (inattention) and criterion A2 (hyperactivity-impulsivity) are met;
  • predominantly inattentive presentation (F90.0): A1 is met but A2 is not, the pattern once called attention deficit disorder without hyperactivity, or simply "ADD"; and
  • predominantly hyperactive/impulsive presentation (F90.1): A2 is met but A1 is not.

"Predominantly" is a precise word here. A child with the predominantly inattentive presentation may have four or five hyperactive-impulsive symptoms, just not six; he is not necessarily a calm child. Notice too that the codes run in an unexpected order, inattentive first and combined last, which examiners occasionally exploit.

Why "presentations" rather than "subtypes", as DSM-IV called them? A subtype implies a stable kind of disorder, the way type 1 and type 2 diabetes are different conditions. ADHD's subtypes turned out not to be stable. Benjamin Lahey and colleagues followed children diagnosed with ADHD in the preschool years through primary school and reported in 2005 that 37 percent of the children who began in the combined subtype, and half of those who began in the inattentive subtype, met criteria for a different subtype at least twice in the next six assessments; children who began in the hyperactive-impulsive subtype were even more likely to shift, mostly into the combined subtype. DSM-5-TR's account of development describes the same drift: in preschool the main manifestation is hyperactivity, inattention becomes more prominent in the primary school years, and in adolescence running and climbing give way to fidgetiness or an inner feeling of restlessness and impatience. Section 3.8.2 follows this course into adult life. The word "presentation" therefore describes the picture a person shows now, which may change, rather than a fixed type of disorder.

Three further specifiers complete the diagnosis. The specifier in partial remission is used when the full criteria were met in the past, fewer than the full criteria have been met for the past six months, and the symptoms still cause impairment: a common state in adults whose hyperactivity has faded but whose disorganisation has not. Current severity is graded in three steps. In mild ADHD, few if any symptoms beyond the number needed for the diagnosis are present, and they cause no more than minor impairment. In severe ADHD, many symptoms beyond those required, or several that are particularly severe, are present, or the symptoms cause marked impairment. Moderate is everything in between. Finally, two residual categories exist for people with clinically significant ADHD-type difficulties who do not meet the full criteria. The category other specified attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (F90.8) is used when the clinician wishes to record the reason, for example "with insufficient inattention symptoms" in a girl with five rather than six. The category unspecified attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (F90.9) is used when the clinician chooses not to give the reason, or when there is not enough information, as in an emergency department.

Vedant meets both lists: at least six inattention symptoms and at least seven hyperactive-impulsive symptoms, over far more than six months. His presentation is therefore combined. He has a little more than the minimum number of symptoms, and clear impairment at school and at home that is not yet marked, so his clinician grades the severity as moderate.

The children who are most often missed are the ones at the other end of the presentations. Anika, 11, is in Class 6 at a girls' school in Nagpur. Her teachers like her: she is polite, quiet and never disrupts a lesson. But her report card has fallen every year. In tests she skips questions without noticing, reads "less than" as "more than" and runs out of time; her notebooks are full of half-copied notes; she forgets which homework is due, leaves her tiffin on the bus, and, her mother says, "goes into her own world" in the middle of a conversation. At home she takes three hours over work her brother finishes in one, and she has begun to say she is "stupid". Nobody has ever suggested ADHD, because she does not look like Vedant. A careful assessment finds six inattention symptoms, two hyperactive-impulsive ones (she fidgets with her hair and talks a great deal with friends), a history of the same "dreaminess" in her earliest school reports, and no learning, mood or anxiety disorder to explain it: ADHD, predominantly inattentive presentation. DSM-5-TR notes that girls with ADHD are more likely than boys to show mainly inattentive features (Section 3.8.1 gives the figures), and quiet inattention disturbs nobody else, so it is referred late or not at all. Many such girls are first seen in adolescence or adulthood, after years of being called careless, lazy or not very bright, often with secondary anxiety and low self-esteem.

One more construct sits at the edge of the inattentive presentation. Some children who are dreamy and inattentive are also slow: they stare, seem mentally foggy or confused, move and think slowly and appear underactive and drowsy, which is the opposite of hyperactivity. For decades this pattern was called sluggish cognitive tempo. In a report published online in 2022 (in print in 2023), a work group of researchers, led by Stephen P. Becker, reviewed the evidence that it is a distinct set of symptoms, related to but separable from ADHD inattention, and agreed by consensus on a new name, cognitive disengagement syndrome. It is a research construct, not a DSM-5-TR diagnosis: a child with these features is diagnosed with ADHD only if he meets the ADHD criteria. ICD-10, as the next subsection shows, had refused the label "attention deficit disorder" in part because it would draw such "dreamy" children into the category.

Exam trap

DSM-5 speaks of presentations, not subtypes, because a child's presentation often changes with age (hyperactivity first, inattention later). The old "ADD" is now the predominantly inattentive presentation. The DSM-5-TR codes are F90.0 inattentive, F90.1 hyperactive/impulsive, F90.2 combined, which do not match ICD-10's meanings for the same codes (F90.0 disturbance of activity and attention, F90.1 hyperkinetic conduct disorder). Severity is mild, moderate or severe, judged by the number of symptoms beyond the threshold and the degree of impairment. "Sluggish cognitive tempo", renamed cognitive disengagement syndrome by a 2022 work-group report (printed 2023), is not a DSM diagnosis.

Hyperkinetic disorder: the ICD-10 view

For most of the period in which DSM was developing its broad concept of attention deficit, European psychiatry used a narrower one, and Indian clinics, which code with ICD-10, still meet it every day. ICD-10 (1992) places it in the block of "behavioural and emotional disorders with onset usually occurring in childhood and adolescence" (F90 to F98) under the name hyperkinetic disorder (F90). Its definition in the Clinical Descriptions and Diagnostic Guidelines is worth knowing almost word for word: the group of disorders is characterised by early onset; "a combination of overactive, poorly modulated behaviour with marked inattention and lack of persistent task involvement"; and "pervasiveness over situations and persistence over time" of these behaviours.

The guidelines then spell out the requirements. The cardinal features are impaired attention and overactivity, and both are necessary for the diagnosis, evident in more than one situation (home, classroom, clinic). Impaired attention is shown by breaking off tasks prematurely and leaving activities unfinished, changing frequently from one activity to another as interest is diverted, although, the manual notes, laboratory studies do not generally show an unusual degree of sensory or perceptual distractibility. Overactivity means excessive restlessness, especially where relative calm is required: running and jumping about, getting up from a seat, excessive talkativeness and noisiness, fidgeting and wriggling. Both must be judged against children of the same age and IQ. The behaviour problems should be of early onset, before age 6 years, and of long duration; in preschool children only extreme levels justify the diagnosis, because normal variation is so wide. The stricter Diagnostic Criteria for Research demand abnormal inattention, overactivity and impulsivity together, at home and at school, demonstrated by direct observation rather than only by report, beginning before the age of 7 and lasting at least six months.

ICD-10 also lists the associated features, which are "not sufficient for the diagnosis or even necessary, but help to sustain it": disinhibition in social relationships, with a lack of normal caution and reserve towards adults; recklessness in dangerous situations and proneness to accidents; and impulsive flouting of social rules, such as interrupting others, answering questions before they are finished and difficulty waiting turns, breaches of rules that are "unthinking (rather than deliberately defiant)". Hyperkinetic children are often unpopular with other children and may become isolated; cognitive impairment is common, specific delays in motor and language development are disproportionately frequent, and learning disorders and motor clumsiness occur with undue frequency (they are coded separately, under F80 to F89, not made part of the diagnosis). Secondary complications include dissocial behaviour and low self-esteem, and the disorder is several times more frequent in boys than in girls.

The subdivision is by conduct, not by symptom profile, because follow-up studies showed that the outcome in adolescence and adult life depends heavily on whether aggression and dissocial behaviour are present. The category disturbance of activity and attention (F90.0) is used when the criteria for hyperkinetic disorder are met but those for conduct disorder are not; it includes "attention deficit disorder or syndrome with hyperactivity" and "attention deficit hyperactivity disorder". The category hyperkinetic conduct disorder (F90.1) is used when the criteria for both hyperkinetic disorder and conduct disorder (F91) are met, one of the few combination categories left in ICD-10. F90.8 is "other hyperkinetic disorders", and F90.9, hyperkinetic disorder unspecified, is a residual category that includes "hyperkinetic reaction or syndrome of childhood or adolescence" not otherwise specified, which is where the old phrase "hyperkinetic syndrome" lives on. A child who is inattentive without being overactive does not meet F90 at all; ICD-10 places "attention deficit disorder without hyperactivity" in F98.8, among "other specified behavioural and emotional disorders" of childhood, alongside nail-biting and nose-picking. The manual explains why it avoided the label "attention deficit disorder": the term implies knowledge of psychological processes that is not yet available, and it would draw in "anxious, preoccupied, or 'dreamy' apathetic children whose problems are probably different".

Two exclusion rules make ICD-10 narrower still. Pervasive developmental disorders (autism) take precedence: a child with autism is not also given hyperkinetic disorder. And anxiety and mood disorders take precedence too: restlessness that is part of severe anxiety or agitated depression should not lead to a diagnosis of hyperkinetic disorder unless there is separate evidence for it. The result is a much narrower category than DSM's ADHD. Because it requires both inattention and overactivity, pervasiveness across situations and the absence of anxiety, mood and autistic disorders, ICD-10 hyperkinetic disorder corresponds roughly to a severe form of DSM's combined presentation, and studies that apply both systems to the same children find far fewer cases of hyperkinetic disorder than of ADHD. A child with DSM-5-TR's predominantly inattentive presentation has no ICD-10 hyperkinetic disorder at all.

ICD-11, in force since 2022, abandoned the separate European concept. Its category 6A05 attention deficit hyperactivity disorder follows the DSM model: a persistent pattern, lasting at least six months, of inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity, with significant symptoms before age 12, evident across multiple settings and directly harming functioning, recorded as predominantly inattentive (6A05.0), predominantly hyperactive-impulsive (6A05.1) or combined presentation (6A05.2), with other specified (6A05.Y) and unspecified (6A05.Z) categories. Table 3.22 sets the three systems side by side; the full code table for the chapter is Table 3.2.

Table 3.22 ADHD in DSM-5-TR, hyperkinetic disorder in ICD-10, and ADHD in ICD-11.
FeatureDSM-5-TR ADHDICD-10 hyperkinetic disorder (F90)ICD-11 ADHD (6A05)
Symptoms neededInattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity (six of nine; five from 17)Impaired attention and overactivity both required (research criteria: inattention, overactivity and impulsivity)Inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity
OnsetSeveral symptoms before 12Before 6 (clinical guidelines); before 7 (research criteria)Significant symptoms before 12
Settings and durationTwo or more settings; 6 monthsPervasive across situations, persistent; research criteria: home and school, directly observed, 6 monthsMultiple settings; 6 months
SubdivisionCombined, predominantly inattentive, predominantly hyperactive/impulsive presentationsF90.0 disturbance of activity and attention; F90.1 hyperkinetic conduct disorder6A05.0 inattentive, 6A05.1 hyperactive-impulsive, 6A05.2 combined
Inattention without overactivityPredominantly inattentive presentationNot F90: "attention deficit disorder without hyperactivity" is F98.86A05.0

Exam trap

"Hyperkinetic syndrome is characterized by the following except": overactivity, distractibility, short attention span or social withdrawal (IHBAS 2020). The answer is social withdrawal. Overactivity, distractibility (the ICD's "impaired attention" and frequent changes of activity) and a short attention span (lack of persistent task involvement) are the core of the syndrome, and impulsivity goes with them. Hyperkinetic children are the opposite of withdrawn: they are socially disinhibited, intrusive and lacking in normal reserve. ICD-10 does say they may become isolated, but that is rejection by other children, not withdrawal by the child. A child who withdraws from others points instead to depression, social anxiety disorder or autism. Even DSM-II kept the two apart: withdrawal had its own category, the "withdrawing reaction of childhood (or adolescence)" (308.1), listed straight after the hyperkinetic reaction (308.0). Note also that "hyperkinetic reaction" was DSM-II's term (1968), "hyperkinetic disorder" is ICD-10's (F90), and ICD-10's F90.1 is hyperkinetic conduct disorder, not the hyperactive presentation.

What else could it be? Differential diagnosis and co-occurring disorders

Inattention and restlessness are among the least specific signs in psychiatry. A child can fail to attend because he cannot hear, cannot see the board, cannot read the textbook, is frightened, is sad, is having seizures, is short of sleep or is simply six and bored. Criterion E and the developmental yardstick of criterion A exist to force the clinician to ask about each of these before settling on ADHD. DSM-5-TR's differential diagnosis is long; Table 3.23 condenses it, and the paragraphs that follow explain the distinctions most often confused.

Table 3.23 The look-alikes of ADHD: what each shares with it and what tells them apart.
ConditionShares with ADHDWhat tells it apartCan both be diagnosed?
Ordinary high activity in a young childEnergy, short attention spanAppropriate for age; settles when it matters; no impairmentNot a disorder
Oppositional defiant disorderRefuses tasks; conflict with adultsRefusal comes from negativity, hostility and defiance, not from difficulty sustaining effort or forgettingYes, very often
Intermittent explosive disorderImpulsive behaviourSerious aggression towards others; no attention problem; rare in childhoodYes
Stereotypic movement disorder; tic disordersConstant movementFixed, repetitive movements (rocking, self-biting) or tics, not generalised fidgetiness; may need prolonged observationYes
Specific learning disorderInattentive in classInattention mainly during the impaired skill (reading, writing, mathematics)Yes, often
Intellectual developmental disorderInattention and restlessness at schoolSymptoms only in academic work beyond the child's ability; ADHD needs symptoms excessive for mental ageYes
Autism spectrum disorderInattention, social problems, tantrumsSocial disengagement and indifference to facial and vocal cues; tantrums at a change of routine rather than from impulsivityYes, since DSM-5
Reactive attachment disorderSocial disinhibitionNot the full ADHD cluster; history of severe neglect; lack of enduring relationships (Section 7.4.5)Usually not needed
Anxiety disorders; post-traumatic stress disorderInattention, restlessnessInattention from worry, rumination or trauma symptoms, not from pursuit of novelty; a trauma historyYes
Depressive disordersPoor concentrationConcentration fails only during the depressive episodeYes
Bipolar disorderActivity, impulsivity, distractibilityEpisodic (a manic or hypomanic episode lasts at least 4 days), with elevated mood and grandiosity; rare before adolescence (Chapter 5)Yes
Disruptive mood dysregulation disorderIrritability, outburstsPervasive irritability and severe recurrent outbursts; impulsivity and inattention not essential (Section 5.3.2)Yes; most children with it also meet ADHD criteria
Substance use; medication effectsInattention, restlessnessBegan after the substance or drug (bronchodilators, isoniazid, antipsychotic-induced akathisia, thyroid replacement)Only with clear evidence of earlier ADHD
Personality disorders (adults)Disorganisation, intrusiveness, emotional dysregulationADHD lacks fear of abandonment, self-injury and extreme ambivalenceYes
Psychotic disorders; neurocognitive disordersInattentionSymptoms only during psychosis; or a decline from a previous level, usually in adulthoodNot for symptoms confined to psychosis
Medical mimicsInattention, "daydreaming", overactivityImpaired hearing or vision, absence seizures, hyperthyroidism, sleep-disordered breathing, lead exposure; found by examination and testsTreat the cause first

Oppositional defiant disorder (Chapter 11) is the commonest confusion and the commonest companion. Both children may refuse their homework. The child with oppositional defiant disorder refuses because he resists conforming to other people's demands; his behaviour is marked by negativity, hostility and defiance. The child with ADHD avoids the homework because sustaining mental effort is hard for him, because he forgot the instruction, or because something more interesting caught his attention. The distinction is complicated, DSM-5-TR notes, by the fact that some children with ADHD develop secondary oppositional attitudes towards tasks they always fail at, and come to devalue them.

Specific learning disorder (Section 3.9) produces a child who looks inattentive in class, through frustration, lack of interest or weak working memory and processing speed, but whose attention is much better when he is doing something that does not demand his weak skill. A boy with dyslexia who stares out of the window during the reading lesson and builds an elaborate Lego model for an hour at home is more likely to have a learning disorder than ADHD; a boy who cannot stay with the Lego either may have both. The reverse distinction, ADHD as a differential of specific learning disorder, is drawn in Section 3.9.6. Intellectual developmental disorder (Section 3.2) produces ADHD-like symptoms in children placed in classrooms whose demands exceed their ability, and the symptoms then disappear in non-academic tasks; ADHD is diagnosed alongside it only when the inattention or hyperactivity is excessive for the child's mental age. Autism (Sections 3.5 and 3.6) shares inattention, social difficulty and hard-to-manage behaviour with ADHD, but the social problems differ in kind. The child with ADHD wants to join in and is rejected because he is intrusive, cannot wait his turn and breaks the rules of the game; the autistic child is socially disengaged, isolated and indifferent to faces and tone of voice. Autistic tantrums typically follow an unexpected change of routine; the child with ADHD may have a tantrum during a major transition because of impulsivity and poor self-control.

Mood disorders are distinguished by time. In depression, poor concentration appears only during the depressive episode. In bipolar disorder, increased activity, impulsivity and distractibility are episodic, come with elevated mood and grandiosity, and last days or weeks; the mood swings of ADHD come and go within the same day, and a manic or hypomanic episode must last four days or more even in children. Bipolar disorder is rare before adolescence, even in children with severe irritability, whereas ADHD is common among irritable, angry children. Pervasive, severe irritability with frequent temper outbursts suggests disruptive mood dysregulation disorder (Section 5.3.2), and most children with that disorder also meet the criteria for ADHD, which is diagnosed separately. Anxiety also impairs attention, but the anxious child is inattentive because he is preoccupied with worry and rumination, while the child with ADHD is inattentive because he is drawn to whatever is new and stimulating. Post-traumatic stress disorder is easily missed: in children under 6 it often shows itself only as restlessness, irritability, inattention and poor concentration; parents may minimise the trauma and teachers may not know of it, so a careful history of traumatic exposure is part of every ADHD assessment. In adolescents and adults, symptoms that first appear after heavy use of cannabis, stimulants or alcohol may be the effects of the substance, and clear evidence of ADHD before the substance use, from informants or old records, is essential before ADHD is diagnosed.

Finally, there are the medical mimics, which every psychologist must think of because the cure lies outside psychology. Tejas, 8, is in Class 3 at a government school in Bhopal, in a class of more than fifty. His teacher has written to his parents that he "never listens", "stares into space" and leaves his work incomplete, and asks whether he needs medicine for hyperactivity. At home he is an ordinary, lively boy. In the clinic, during an hour of play and talk, the psychologist notices that every few minutes he stops in mid-sentence, stares blankly with his eyelids fluttering for a few seconds, and then carries on exactly where he left off, unaware that anything has happened. Asked to blow at a paper windmill and breathe fast for a few minutes, he has another such spell. He is referred to a paediatric neurologist, and an electroencephalogram (EEG) shows the generalised three-per-second spike-and-wave pattern of childhood absence epilepsy: brief seizures of absence, often dozens a day, that interrupt attention without any convulsion. The "daydreaming" stops with antiepileptic treatment. Other mimics are commoner still. A child who cannot hear the teacher because of a middle-ear problem, or cannot see the board from the back of a crowded classroom, will look inattentive and restless; hearing and vision should be checked in every child referred for ADHD, as they were for Vedant. An overactive thyroid causes restlessness, poor concentration and weight loss. Sleep-disordered breathing, including obstructive sleep apnoea from enlarged tonsils and adenoids, leaves children tired, inattentive and paradoxically overactive by day; DSM-5-TR notes that a quarter to a half of people with ADHD report sleep problems, so sleep should be asked about in every case, both as a mimic and as a companion. And exposure to lead, which DSM-5-TR lists among the environmental correlates of ADHD (Section 3.8.5), should be considered where a child may have been exposed.

Clinical link: five questions before accepting the label "hyperactive"

Indian clinicians are often asked to confirm a diagnosis that a school has already made. Five questions protect the child. In how many settings? Ask the parents, the teacher and, where possible, a tuition teacher or grandparent; if the problem exists only in one classroom, look at that classroom. Since when? Ask for the earliest school reports; a problem that began last year with a new school, a family upheaval or a bereavement is not ADHD. What does he do in one-to-one play? Watch him yourself, remembering that a calm hour in the clinic does not rule ADHD out, but that staring spells, tics, odd social behaviour or a struggle to read will point elsewhere. Can he see the board and hear the teacher? Test vision and hearing before anything else. What is the class like? Ask about the class size, the seating, the teaching style and the medium of instruction; a bright, active child of seven in a class of sixty, taught by rote in a language he does not speak at home, may be restless for reasons that no tablet will treat.

Diagnosing ADHD rarely ends the diagnostic process, because ADHD seldom comes alone. DSM-5-TR lists its companions. Oppositional defiant disorder co-occurs in about half of children with the combined presentation and about a quarter of those with the predominantly inattentive presentation. Conduct disorder co-occurs in about a quarter of children and adolescents with the combined presentation, depending on age and setting. Most children with disruptive mood dysregulation disorder meet the ADHD criteria, although only a small proportion of children with ADHD have DMDD. Among the neurodevelopmental disorders, ADHD co-occurs with specific learning disorder, autism, intellectual developmental disorder, language disorders, developmental coordination disorder and the tic disorders (Section 3.10). Anxiety disorders, major depressive disorder, obsessive-compulsive disorder and intermittent explosive disorder occur in a minority of people with ADHD, though more often than in the general population. Substance use disorders are more frequent in adults with ADHD than in others, but are present in only a minority of them (Section 3.8.2), and in adults antisocial and other personality disorders may co-occur. A quarter to a half of people with ADHD report sleep difficulties, and allergic and autoimmune disorders and epilepsy are more common than expected. Although ADHD is diagnosed more often in males, females with ADHD have higher rates of several co-occurring disorders, among them oppositional defiant disorder, autism, and personality and substance use disorders. The practical rule is that every child diagnosed with ADHD should be screened for learning difficulties, oppositional and conduct problems, anxiety, mood, tics and sleep problems, because the companion often decides the outcome.

Vedant's assessment can now be completed. His hearing and vision are normal, he has no tics, he sleeps well, and on screening he reads and spells at the level of his class, so there is no specific learning disorder to explain his inattention, and his milestones and speech were normal. He is not oppositional: he is not defiant or spiteful, though he gets upset when he is scolded. He shows no sign of an anxiety or mood disorder, and his symptoms began in early childhood and have been continuous. The diagnosis is attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, combined presentation, moderate. Two other facts in his history, his birth at 34 weeks weighing 1.9 kg and his father's childhood restlessness, matter for the question of why he has ADHD, which Section 3.8.5 takes up; how he is treated, and how his grandparents' fear that "the tablet is a drug like ganja" is answered, is told in Section 3.8.7.

Exam trap

ADHD is diagnosed alongside, not instead of, many of its look-alikes: oppositional defiant disorder (about half of children with the combined presentation), specific learning disorder, autism (since DSM-5) and disruptive mood dysregulation disorder. It is not diagnosed when the symptoms occur only during a psychotic disorder, and ICD-10 (unlike DSM-5-TR) does not give hyperkinetic disorder alongside autism. Bipolar mania is episodic (at least 4 days); ADHD is persistent. ODD is won't; ADHD is can't (or forgot). And ADHD is not a learning disability, although it often accompanies one (Section 3.9).

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  2. In Folstein and Rutter's 1977 twin study, concordance for autism was 36 percent in identical pairs and percent in fraternal pairs.
  3. The 1998 Lancet paper that claimed a link between the MMR vaccine and autism, fully retracted in 2010, was led by Andrew .

  5. Uta Frith's theory that autistic people focus on details at the expense of the whole is called weak coherence.

  8. DSM-5-TR requires several inattentive or hyperactive-impulsive symptoms to have been present before the age of .
