Answers: (1) 3, (2) 0, (3) Wakefield, (4) Down, (5) central, (6) irritability, (7) moral, (8) 12, (9) inattention, (10) conduct.

3.8 Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder II: Course, Causes and Drug TreatmentLower yield · not asked yet

Section 3.7 taught you how to recognise attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD): the eighteen symptoms, the age, duration and setting rules, the three presentations and the look-alikes. A diagnosis, though, is where a family's questions begin rather than end. Parents who have just heard the word ask four things, almost always in the same order. Is it common, or is our child unusual? Will he grow out of it? Did we cause it, and if not, what did? And is the tablet the doctor mentions safe? Those four questions organise this section. It looks first at who has ADHD and how the picture changes from the nursery to middle age, then at the genetic, brain, environmental and psychological explanations, and finally at the drugs: the stimulants, the non-stimulants, and the place of medicine beside the psychological treatments that Book 2 teaches.

Who has ADHD?

How common is ADHD? It sounds like the simplest question in this chapter, and it has one of the most slippery answers. DSM-5-TR reports that population surveys find ADHD in about 7.2 percent of children worldwide. In a class of forty, that is roughly three children. But the same paragraph of the manual adds that cross-national estimates range from 0.1 percent to 10.2 percent of children and adolescents, a hundredfold spread, and that the rate is higher still in special populations such as children in foster care and young people in correctional settings. For adults, a cross-national meta-analysis gives a figure of about 2.5 percent. (A meta-analysis, as the Research Methodology book explains, pools the results of many separate studies into one estimate.)

Why should one disorder be a hundred times commoner in one survey than in another? It is tempting to think that some countries simply have more restless children, but DSM-5-TR says plainly that the differences between regions appear to be due mainly to differences in method. Surveys use different diagnostic interviews. Some ask only whether the symptoms are present; others also require that the symptoms impair the child's life, and they define impairment in different ways. Some ask parents, some teachers, some both. And some count ICD-10's narrow hyperkinetic disorder, which demands inattention, overactivity and impulsivity together in several settings, rather than DSM's broader ADHD (Section 3.7.4 explains the difference). Drop the impairment rule from a survey and the rate climbs, because many lively children show six of the nine behaviours without coming to any harm; add it back and the rate falls. A prevalence figure, in other words, is partly a property of the ruler.

It is also partly a property of the society doing the measuring. What counts as normal boisterousness differs between cultures, between families and even between a parent and a teacher looking at the same child, and DSM-5-TR notes that informants' ratings are shaped by their own cultural background as well as the child's. The manual goes further than most diagnostic texts in naming bias. In the United States, it observes, African American and Latinx children are identified with ADHD less often than non-Latinx White children, possibly because their symptoms are mislabelled as oppositional or disruptive through explicit or implicit clinician bias, so that they receive a disruptive behaviour diagnosis instead; the higher rate among White children may partly reflect greater parental demand for a diagnosis. The general lesson travels well. When an Indian teacher calls one boy "hyperactive" and another "naughty", or a family hears "ADHD" where its grandparents would have said "a spirited child", the label depends on who is looking as well as on what the child does.

The sex ratio shows the same mixture of biology and perception. In the general population ADHD is about twice as common in boys as in girls (2 to 1), and about 1.6 times as common in men as in women. Girls are more likely than boys to show a mainly inattentive picture, and DSM-5-TR suggests that the sexes may differ in their genetic and cognitive liabilities. But the ratio in clinics has always been far more lopsided than the ratio in the population, for a reason you met in Section 3.7.3: a boy who climbs the classroom cupboard is referred, while a girl who gazes out of the window and loses her homework is called a dreamer. Notice also that the ratio narrows in adulthood. Part of the reason may be that adults refer themselves, and women seek help more readily than men do, so adult figures depend less on who is sent to the clinic by parents and teachers.

What about India? Estimates vary widely with the method, the age group and the setting (school, clinic or household), for exactly the reasons just described. The largest multi-site household survey, the INCLEN study of neurodevelopmental disorders described in Section 3.1.6, assessed 6- to 9-year-olds in five regions for ADHD as one of nine conditions and found at least one neurodevelopmental disorder in 13.6 percent of children of that age; for ADHD on its own, quote an Indian figure only together with its source, because single studies range widely.

The most instructive finding about prevalence, though, is not a number for any country. It comes from asking a strange question: does a child's birthday affect whether he has ADHD?

Key study: the relative-age effect (Morrow and colleagues, 2012)

Question. In the Canadian province of British Columbia, the cut-off date of birth for entering school is 31 December, so a child born in December is typically the youngest in his class and a child born in January the oldest, almost a year older. Does being the youngest in the class make a diagnosis of ADHD more likely? Method. Richard L. Morrow and his colleagues at the University of British Columbia used health records for 937,943 children who were 6 to 12 years old at some time between December 1997 and November 2008, and compared the chances of receiving a diagnosis of ADHD, and a prescription for an ADHD drug (methylphenidate, dextroamphetamine, mixed amphetamine salts or atomoxetine), among children born in December and children born in January. Result. Boys born in December were 30 percent more likely to be diagnosed with ADHD than boys born in January, and girls born in December 70 percent more likely than girls born in January. The December-born children were also more likely to be given medication: by 41 percent for boys and 77 percent for girls. Conclusion. A child's age relative to his classmates influences whether he is diagnosed and treated. The authors warned of the harms of overdiagnosis and overprescribing, including effects on sleep, appetite and growth. Criticism. A records study cannot say which individual children were wrongly diagnosed; it shows a pattern across hundreds of thousands. Similar relative-age effects have been reported in other countries with different cut-off dates, which makes it hard to explain the finding by anything about December babies themselves.

Why does a birthday matter? Go back to the wording of criterion A in Section 3.7.2: the symptoms must be present "to a degree that is inconsistent with developmental level". The comparison that rule intends is with children of the same age. But the people who first raise the alarm, teachers, compare a child with the others in the room, and in any class some of those others are nearly a year older. At six, a year is a sixth of a lifetime and a large slice of the development of self-control. Consider Reyansh, five years and ten months old, who has just started Class 1 at a school in Jaipur whose admission cut-off fell a few weeks after his birthday. Most of his classmates are already six and a half. Within a month his diary carries the remarks "cannot sit through the period", "fidgety", "does not finish copying". In the clinic, playing with blocks and talking about his cousin's wedding, Reyansh behaves like an ordinary, rather excited five-year-old. Measured against children of his own age, he is not unusual at all; measured against the oldest children in his class, he looks like a textbook case. The clinician's first job is to ask the child's date of birth, the class's age range and how he behaves with children of his own age, and very often to wait and watch rather than to diagnose.

The relative-age effect is not evidence that ADHD is a myth. It is evidence that the disorder sits at the end of a continuum, that the cut-point is drawn by people, and that people draw it in the wrong place when they use the wrong yardstick. You will meet the same theme again in Section 3.9, where a child's reading must be judged against age, schooling and the language of instruction before it is called a disorder.

Exam trap

DSM-5-TR gives about 7.2 percent of children worldwide (cross-national range 0.1 to 10.2 percent) and about 2.5 percent of adults; DSM-5 (2013) had given about 5 percent for children, so either figure may appear in a key, but 7.2 is the current one. The male to female ratio is about 2 to 1 in children and 1.6 to 1 in adults, and females more often have the predominantly inattentive presentation. Rates are higher in foster care and correctional settings. Regional differences are mainly due to method, not to real differences between countries. The youngest children in a class are more often diagnosed and medicated (the relative-age effect).

ADHD from the preschool years to adulthood

Does a hyperactive child grow out of it? For most of the twentieth century the answer in textbooks was yes: hyperactivity was a disorder of childhood that faded at puberty. The answer changed when researchers followed hyperactive children into adolescence and adulthood and found that many of them were still struggling, although they were no longer climbing on the furniture. DSM-5-TR now describes the course in a way that explains both the old belief and the new finding.

Many parents, the manual says, first notice excessive motor activity when the child is a toddler, but before about the age of 4 the symptoms are hard to tell apart from the very variable behaviour of normal young children. In the preschool years the main manifestation is hyperactivity. ADHD is most often identified in the primary school years, when inattention becomes more prominent and more impairing, because school is the first place where a child is asked, day after day, to sit still, listen, wait, organise his things and keep his mind on work he did not choose. The disorder is relatively stable through early adolescence, although some young people take a worse course and develop antisocial behaviour. In adolescence and adulthood overt hyperactivity, the running and climbing, becomes less obvious and may be confined to fidgeting or an inner feeling of jitteriness, restlessness or impatience. Inattention, poor planning and impulsivity persist, and in adults impulsivity may remain a problem even when the hyperactivity has died down. A substantial proportion of children with ADHD remain relatively impaired into adulthood.

The disorder, in other words, does not so much change as move to a different stage. A preschooler with ADHD is in trouble for not sitting through the story. A ten-year-old is in trouble for unfinished classwork and lost water bottles. A teenager is in trouble for homework left to the last night, for quarrels, and for risks taken on a motorbike. An adult is in trouble for missed deadlines, unpaid bills, lost jobs and forgotten promises. Figure 3.12 shows the shift schematically. The old belief that children outgrow ADHD came from watching the most visible symptom, motor overactivity, which really does decline; the newer view comes from watching the less visible ones.

How many children with ADHD still have it as adults? The honest answer is that it depends on what you count. In 2006 Stephen V. Faraone, Joseph Biederman and Eric Mick pooled the published follow-up studies that had control groups. When "persistent ADHD" meant still meeting the full diagnostic criteria, only about 15 percent of the children met them at the age of 25. When it also included people who no longer met full criteria but still had impairing symptoms, which DSM-IV called ADHD in partial remission, the figure rose to about 65 percent. Both numbers are correct; they answer different questions. The authors added a further caution: the criteria were written with children in mind (a 25-year-old rarely "climbs on furniture"), so part of the apparent decline may reflect criteria that do not fit adults rather than true recovery. That is precisely the problem DSM-5 addressed by lowering the threshold to five symptoms from the age of 17 and adding adult examples to the items (Section 3.7.2). Barlow and Durand summarise the position simply: about half of children with ADHD continue to have difficulties into adulthood.

What does adult life look like for those who do? The longest answer comes from Rachel G. Klein and her colleagues in New York, who had followed 207 boys diagnosed with hyperactivity at a mean age of 8 (all free of conduct disorder in childhood) and in 2012 reported on the 135 of them they could reassess as men, 33 years after they were first seen, at a mean age of 41, comparing them with 136 men who had not had the disorder. Most of the men with a history of ADHD, 84 percent, were working, but in jobs of significantly lower status than the comparison group. They had on average two and a half fewer years of education and were much less likely to hold higher degrees. They were more often divorced, and more often had substance use problems and antisocial personality disorder. Related reports from the same cohort found more risky driving, more sexually transmitted infections, more head injuries and more visits to emergency departments. Klein's men were not, on the whole, disasters; most had jobs and families. But the disorder had followed them, quietly taxing almost every area of their lives.

DSM-5-TR lists the same costs across the lifespan. At school, ADHD is associated with reduced performance and lower academic attainment; problems with schoolwork and neglect by peers go mainly with inattention, whereas outright rejection by peers and, to a lesser extent, accidental injury go mainly with hyperactivity and impulsivity. Effort that is inconsistent on long tasks is often read by adults as laziness, irresponsibility or refusal to cooperate, so the child is scolded for what he cannot easily help, and self-esteem suffers. Family relationships may be marked by discord and negative exchanges. In young adults there is poor job stability, and in adults poorer occupational performance, more unemployment and more conflict with other people. Children with ADHD are significantly more likely than their peers to develop conduct disorder in adolescence and antisocial personality disorder in adulthood (Chapter 11), and through that route substance use disorders and imprisonment; the risk of substance use disorder is raised especially when conduct disorder or antisocial personality disorder develops. People with ADHD are injured more often, are more likely to experience trauma and to develop post-traumatic stress, and, as drivers, have more traffic accidents and violations (Barlow and Durand note crashes, speeding tickets and suspended licences among teenagers with ADHD). ADHD is a risk factor for suicidal thoughts and behaviour in children, and in adults it is associated with a raised risk of suicide attempts, particularly when mood, conduct or substance use disorders are also present. There may also be more obesity and high blood pressure.

The starkest of these costs was measured in Denmark, where every resident has a personal number that links health, school and death records. Søren Dalsgaard and his colleagues followed 1.92 million Danes from their first birthday to 2013, of whom 32,061 had been diagnosed with ADHD, and published the results in The Lancet in 2015. The death rate was 5.85 per 10,000 person-years among those with ADHD and 2.21 among those without, and after allowing for age, sex, family psychiatric history and parental age, education and employment, the mortality rate ratio was 2.07: about double. Accidents were the most common cause of death. The excess was largest in people first diagnosed as adults (a ratio of 4.25), and although conduct disorder, oppositional defiant disorder and substance use disorder made it worse, ADHD remained associated with higher mortality (a ratio of 1.50) after people with those conditions were excluded, and more so in girls and women than in boys and men. Keep the absolute numbers in view when you explain this to a family: six deaths per 10,000 person-years is a small risk for any one person. The point of the study is that the impulsive dash across the road and the overtaking on a blind curve are not trivial, and that a disorder often dismissed as a schoolroom nuisance carries real danger.

Consider what this looks like in one adult. Zara, 29, a software tester in Bengaluru, comes to a psychiatrist after her third job in five years ends with the words "brilliant but unreliable". She is not restless to look at, though she says her mind "never switches off" and she cannot sit through a film without her phone. She misses deadlines, forgets bill payments until the electricity is cut, loses her keys several times a week, and has two fines for jumping traffic signals on her scooter. Her old school report cards, which her mother has kept, say "talkative, careless, does not complete her work" from Class 2 onwards, which matters, because criterion B of Section 3.7.2 requires several symptoms before the age of 12. Zara meets the criteria for ADHD, predominantly inattentive presentation. Her story is typical of women diagnosed late: a bright girl whose inattention was hidden by intelligence and by quiet behaviour, until the demands of adult life outran the scaffolding her family and school had provided without knowing it.

What makes the difference between Klein's men who did well and those who did not? The research gives no single number, but the pattern is consistent. DSM-5-TR states that family interaction patterns in early childhood are unlikely to cause ADHD but may influence its course and contribute to the later development of conduct problems. A family that stays warm and keeps rules clear and consistent, rather than sliding into the cycle of commands, defiance and harsh punishment that Book 2, Section 4.10.3 describes, protects the child from the secondary damage. So does a school that adapts its demands rather than repeating the same punishment, effective treatment, and the absence of conduct disorder and substance misuse. Above all there is fit, in the sense Book 2, Section 10.1.3 explains: the same energy and appetite for novelty that ruin a child's afternoons at a desk can serve an adult well in sales, in sports coaching, in emergency work or in running his own shop. The task of care is to get the child safely to the point where he can choose a life that suits the way he is built.

Exam trap

The symptom that most clearly declines with age is motor hyperactivity; inattention, poor planning and impulsivity persist, and in adolescents and adults hyperactivity may survive only as fidgeting or an inner restlessness. ADHD is hard to distinguish from normal behaviour before about age 4 and is most often identified in primary school. "Children outgrow ADHD" is false for many: about 15 percent still meet full criteria at 25 but about 65 percent if partial remission is counted (Faraone and colleagues, 2006). The raised mortality in ADHD (about double in the Danish cohort) is mainly from accidents.

Genes

If ADHD is not caused by birthdays or bad parenting, what does cause it? The single most powerful influence turns out to be the one that families most often suspect when they say "his father was exactly the same": genes. The three classic methods for separating genes from upbringing, family studies, twin studies and adoption studies, are explained in Chapter 1, Sections 1.4.2 to 1.4.4 (and in Book 1, Section 9.7), and all three point the same way for ADHD. The disorder runs in families: the parents, brothers and sisters of a child with ADHD are more likely than people in the general population to have it themselves. Identical (monozygotic) twins, who share all their genes, are far more often both affected than non-identical (dizygotic) twins, who share about half. And in adoption studies, children with ADHD resemble their biological relatives more than the families who raised them.

Putting the twin studies together, DSM-5-TR gives the heritability of ADHD as approximately 74 percent, the figure that Faraone and Henrik Larsson reported in their 2019 review of the genetics of ADHD. That is among the highest heritabilities of any psychiatric disorder, comparable to schizophrenia and bipolar disorder and well above depression and anxiety. Barlow and Durand add that the relatives of children with ADHD have more psychopathology of other kinds too, including conduct disorder, mood disorders, anxiety disorders and substance misuse, which suggests that some of the genetic liability is shared across disorders rather than unique to ADHD.

A heritability of 74 percent is easy to misread, so it is worth being precise about what it means. Heritability is a statistic about a population: the proportion of the differences between people in that population that can be traced to differences in their genes. It does not mean that 74 percent of any one child's ADHD comes from his genes and 26 percent from his upbringing, which is meaningless, and it does not mean that ADHD is fixed or untreatable. Short sight is highly heritable too, and a pair of spectacles corrects it completely. A highly heritable disorder can respond well to treatment, and the environment can change how a genetic risk shows itself. What the number does tell you is that blaming parents for causing ADHD is not only unkind but, on the evidence, largely wrong.

Which genes are involved? The first answer came from candidate gene studies, which pick a gene that might plausibly matter and test whether one version of it is commoner in people with the disorder. For ADHD the obvious candidates were genes of the dopamine system, because the drugs that help ADHD act on dopamine. Barlow and Durand summarise the strongest of these associations: the dopamine transporter gene (DAT1), the dopamine D4 receptor gene (DRD4) and the dopamine D5 receptor gene (DRD5). DAT1 attracted special interest because its product, the dopamine transporter, is the very protein that methylphenidate blocks. For DRD4, a 2001 meta-analysis by Faraone and colleagues found that one variant, the 7-repeat allele, is indeed associated with ADHD, but that the association is small. That is the pattern for every candidate gene so far: each variant raises the risk by only a tiny amount, and many people with the variant never develop ADHD.

The modern method is the genome-wide association study (GWAS), which scans hundreds of thousands of genetic markers across the whole genome in very large samples without guessing in advance where to look (Chapter 1 explains the method and the polygenic scores it produces). The first GWAS of ADHD to find variants that passed the strict genome-wide threshold of significance was published in 2019 by Ditte Demontis and an international consortium. Combining 20,183 people diagnosed with ADHD and 35,191 controls, it identified 12 independent loci. The associated regions were concentrated in parts of the genome that have changed little in evolution, in genes that tolerate loss of function poorly, and around regulatory sequences that are active in the brain, which is exactly how DSM-5-TR summarises the GWAS findings. The manual draws the conclusion in six words: there is no single gene for ADHD. Faraone and Larsson estimate that about a third of the heritability is carried by a polygenic component made of many common variants, each with a minute effect, and that rare copy number variants (stretches of DNA that are deleted or duplicated, also noted by Barlow and Durand) account for part of the rest.

Genes do not act in a vacuum, and ADHD research has produced one of the most quoted examples of a gene-environment interaction, a case in which the effect of a gene depends on the environment, or the effect of an environment depends on the gene. In 2003 Kahn, Khoury, Nichols and Lanphear reported that children carrying a particular DAT1 genotype showed more hyperactive and impulsive behaviour if their mothers had smoked during pregnancy; neither the genotype alone nor the smoking alone had the same effect. Later studies have looked at maternal stress, alcohol in pregnancy and marital discord in the same way. The message for a clinician is that genetic risk and environmental risk are not rival explanations; they work together.

A last genetic idea links the genes to the psychology. An endophenotype (Chapter 1, Section 1.4.8) is a measurable characteristic, between the gene and the full disorder, that is found in affected people and, in milder form, in their unaffected relatives. The leading candidate in ADHD is weak inhibitory control: the ability to stop a response once it has been started. It is measured with the stop-signal task, in which the person presses a key as fast as possible whenever a letter appears but must hold back whenever a tone sounds just after the letter; the measure is how long the person needs to cancel a response once the tone sounds (the stop-signal reaction time). Barlow and Durand report that poor inhibitory control on such tasks is found both in children with ADHD and in their unaffected brothers, sisters and parents, which is what you would expect if it were part of the inherited liability. Keep that finding in mind; it is the experimental root of the most influential psychological theory of ADHD, which you will meet in Section 3.8.5.

Exam trap

The heritability of ADHD is about 74 percent (DSM-5-TR; older sources say 70 to 80 percent). That does not mean ADHD is untreatable, or that 74 percent of one child's symptoms are genetic. There is no single ADHD gene: candidate genes in the dopamine system (DAT1, DRD4 with its 7-repeat allele, DRD5) have small effects, and the first genome-wide significant loci (12 of them) were reported only in 2019 (Demontis and colleagues). Weak inhibitory control in affected children and their unaffected relatives is the leading endophenotype.

The ADHD brain

Genes build brains, so the next question is what kind of brain the genes of ADHD build. The detailed neuroanatomy belongs to the Physiological book; this subsection gives only what you need to understand the disorder and its drugs.

The oldest biological idea is the catecholamine hypothesis: that ADHD involves underactivity of the two catecholamine neurotransmitters, dopamine and noradrenaline (called norepinephrine in American books), in the brain circuits that control attention and behaviour. Dopamine signals reward and helps the frontal lobes keep a goal in mind against distraction; noradrenaline tunes alertness and the signal-to-noise ratio of prefrontal neurons. The main support for the hypothesis is the action of the drugs that help: every effective ADHD medicine increases the activity of dopamine, noradrenaline or both, as Sections 3.8.6 and 3.8.7 explain. The dopamine genes of Section 3.8.3 point the same way.

But notice the logical trap in that support, which Chapter 1, Section 1.5.4 warns against: reasoning backwards from a treatment to a cause. Paracetamol relieves a headache, yet nobody thinks headaches are caused by a lack of paracetamol. The point has extra force here, because, as you will see in Section 3.8.6, stimulants sharpen attention in people without ADHD as well. That a drug which raises dopamine helps ADHD shows that dopamine is part of the machinery the drug can adjust. It does not prove that low dopamine is where the disorder began.

Which machinery? The circuits most often implicated are the frontostriatal circuits: loops that run from the prefrontal cortex, the brain's executive, down to the striatum (the caudate nucleus and putamen of the basal ganglia), through the thalamus and back to the cortex. The prefrontal cortex holds goals and rules in mind and stops actions that do not fit them; the striatum helps select which action to release and which to hold back, and its lower part, the nucleus accumbens, is a centre of reward and motivation. The cerebellum, long thought of as a purely motor structure, contributes to the timing of behaviour and is also implicated. A disorder of attention, impulse control and timing is exactly what you might predict from a weakness in these loops.

Is there direct evidence? Many small imaging studies reported smaller volumes of these structures in ADHD, and Barlow and Durand note that the overall brain volume of children with ADHD averages slightly less, by 3 to 4 percent, than that of other children. The most convincing evidence comes from pooling. In 2017 Martine Hoogman and the ENIGMA ADHD working group combined brain scans of 1,713 people with ADHD and 1,529 controls from 23 sites, aged 4 to 63. The volumes of the nucleus accumbens, amygdala, caudate, hippocampus and putamen, and the overall intracranial volume, were smaller in the ADHD group. But look at the size of the differences: the effect sizes were between about 0.1 and 0.2 of a standard deviation, which means the two groups overlapped almost completely. The differences were clearest in children and were not significant in adults, and they were not explained by stimulant medication.

That pattern, a difference in children that fades in adults, fits the most striking imaging finding of all, the discovery that the ADHD brain may be not so much different as late.

Key study: a delay in cortical maturation (Shaw and colleagues, 2007)

Question. Is the brain of a child with ADHD built to a different plan from other children's, or is it built to the same plan more slowly? Method. At the National Institute of Mental Health in Bethesda, Philip Shaw, Judith L. Rapoport and their colleagues scanned 223 children with ADHD and 223 typically developing children repeatedly as they grew up, collecting 824 magnetic resonance scans in all. At more than 40,000 points across the cerebral cortex they measured its thickness and plotted each point's growth curve. In childhood the cortex thickens, reaches a peak, and then thins in adolescence as unused connections are pruned, so the age at which each point reached its peak thickness could be used as an index of how mature it was. Result. In both groups the cortex matured in the same order, with the primary sensory areas peaking first and the higher association areas later. But in the children with ADHD the whole sequence was shifted. The median age by which half of the cortical points had reached peak thickness was 10.5 years in the ADHD group and 7.5 years in the controls, a delay of about three years. The delay was greatest in the prefrontal regions that control attention and the planning of movement. Conclusion. In ADHD the cortex follows the normal plan of development but follows it late: a delay, not a deviation. Criticism. These are group averages with wide overlap, so a scan cannot say whether a particular child has ADHD, and a delay on average does not mean that every child catches up; a substantial minority, as Section 3.8.2 showed, remain impaired as adults.

The idea of a maturational delay makes sense of several facts at once: that symptoms are measured against developmental level, that the youngest children in a class are diagnosed most (Section 3.8.1), that hyperactivity often fades with age, and that group differences in brain volume are smaller in adults. A different line of research looks not at the structure of the brain but at its rhythm. When you are not doing any task, a set of connected regions known as the default mode network becomes active; it supports mind-wandering, daydreaming and thinking about yourself. Normally it quietens when you start a task. Edmund Sonuga-Barke and F. Xavier Castellanos proposed in 2007 that in ADHD the slow rhythms of this resting network intrude into periods of active work, producing the periodic lapses that make a child's performance so variable: sharp one minute, lost the next.

Every one of these findings is a difference between group averages, and the distributions of the two groups overlap heavily. DSM-5-TR is explicit that no biological marker is diagnostic of ADHD. Raised slow-wave (theta) and reduced fast-wave (beta) activity on the electroencephalogram were once proposed as a marker, but a later review found no difference in theta or beta power between people with and without ADHD; and although some imaging studies show differences, the manual concludes that until problems of criteria, sample size, tasks and technique are resolved, no form of neuroimaging can be used to diagnose ADHD. A clinic that offers to diagnose ADHD from a brain scan or a "brain map" is offering something the science does not support. The diagnosis remains a clinical one, built from history, informants and observation, as Section 3.7 described.

Exam trap

The neurotransmitters of ADHD are dopamine and noradrenaline (the catecholamines), and the circuits are frontostriatal (prefrontal cortex with the caudate and putamen), with the cerebellum also implicated. Shaw and colleagues (2007) found a delay of about three years in cortical maturation (median peak 10.5 versus 7.5 years), most marked in the prefrontal cortex: a delay, not a deviation. There is no diagnostic biological marker: neither EEG theta/beta ratios nor brain imaging can diagnose ADHD. That stimulants help does not prove that ADHD is a dopamine deficiency.

Environment and psychological models

A heritability of 74 percent leaves a quarter of the variation in ADHD to the environment, and the environment that matters most is the earliest one. DSM-5-TR lists the environmental risk factors with unusual care, separating the ones it regards as established from the ones that are only correlations. It also notes temperamental traits that may predispose a child to ADHD without being specific to it: reduced behavioural inhibition and effortful control, negative emotionality and a high appetite for novelty.

The firmest environmental risk is being born too small or too soon. Very low birth weight and the degree of prematurity convey a greater risk of ADHD, and the relation is graded: the lower the birth weight, the greater the risk. A dose-response relation like this is one of the classic signs that an association may be causal, since it is hard to explain by chance. The next item on the list is prenatal exposure to tobacco smoke, which DSM-5-TR says is associated with ADHD even after allowing for the parents' psychiatric history and socioeconomic status. Here, though, a cleverer design has complicated the story. In 2014 Charlotte Skoglund and her colleagues used Swedish national registers to compare not only unrelated children but cousins and brothers and sisters born to the same mother, who had smoked during one pregnancy and not during another. Across the whole population, heavy smoking in pregnancy went with about two and a half times the risk of ADHD. Among cousins the association was smaller, and among siblings it disappeared. The authors concluded that the link between smoking in pregnancy and ADHD is due mainly to factors shared within families, most probably genes that make a mother more likely both to smoke and to pass on a liability to ADHD, rather than to the smoke itself. Barlow and Durand make a similar point: women who smoke in pregnancy also tend to be poorer and more stressed, so many studies confound smoking with everything that goes with it. None of this makes smoking in pregnancy safe; it harms the baby in many other ways. It means only that smoking is a weaker cause of ADHD than the raw figures suggest.

DSM-5-TR then lists a group of factors that "have been correlated with" later ADHD without, it says, any proof that the associations are causal: exposure to neurotoxins such as lead, infections such as encephalitis, and alcohol in the womb. It adds that a minority of cases may be related to reactions to aspects of diet, that visual and hearing impairments, metabolic abnormalities and nutritional deficiencies should be considered as possible influences on the symptoms, and that ADHD is commoner in people with epilepsy. Family interaction patterns, as Section 3.8.2 noted, are unlikely to cause ADHD but may shape its course.

Now apply the list to a real child. Vedant, the ten-year-old from Lucknow whose diagnosis was made in Section 3.7, was born at 34 weeks weighing 1.9 kilograms. That makes him both preterm and of low birth weight (under 2.5 kilograms), though not of very low birth weight (under 1.5 kilograms), the band DSM-5-TR singles out, with the risk rising as birth weight falls. His mother neither smoked nor drank in pregnancy. And his father, Mehul, was often punished at school for restlessness and left his studies after Class 10. The most likely account of Vedant's ADHD is therefore an inherited liability, which his grandparents recognised when they said "his father was the same", perhaps added to by a modest perinatal risk. Nothing in it points to the way Ritika and Mehul have brought him up.

One environmental cause is severe enough to produce an ADHD-like picture on its own. The English and Romanian Adoptees study, described in Section 3.3.6, followed children who had spent their earliest months or years in grossly depriving Romanian orphanages before being adopted into British families. In 2016 Mark Kennedy, Sonuga-Barke and their colleagues reported that adoptees who had spent more than six months in an institution were nearly four times as likely as the other adoptees to meet the criteria for ADHD at 15 (19 percent) and more than seven times as likely in young adulthood (29.3 percent), and that the disorder was as common in women as in men. The authors described it as a persistent, deprivation-specific variant of ADHD. Severe early neglect is rare, but the finding is a reminder that the clinician must always ask about a child's early care.

Just as important as knowing the real risks is knowing the false ones, because they are what Indian parents, like parents everywhere, will bring to the clinic. The most popular is sugar. Every parent who has watched a birthday party has seen children go wild after the cake, so the belief that sugar causes hyperactivity feels obvious. It was tested directly in double-blind studies, in which children drank either sugar or an artificial sweetener and neither they, their parents nor the researchers knew which. In 1995 Mark L. Wolraich and two colleagues combined 16 such reports, containing 23 studies, in a meta-analysis published in the Journal of the American Medical Association. Sugar did not affect children's behaviour or cognitive performance. The authors suggested that parents' strong belief comes from expectation and from association: children eat sugar at parties, and parties are exciting.

Food additives have a longer and more complicated history. In 1975 the American allergist Benjamin Feingold argued in his book Why Your Child Is Hyperactive that artificial colours, flavourings and preservatives caused hyperactivity, and recommended a diet free of them. Hundreds of thousands of families tried the Feingold diet, but controlled studies found little or no effect on ADHD symptoms. Then in 2007 Donna McCann and her colleagues at the University of Southampton ran a large randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial in the general population, not in children with ADHD: 153 three-year-olds and 144 eight- and nine-year-olds drank either fruit juice or juice containing sodium benzoate (a preservative) and one of two mixtures of artificial colours. On a combined measure of hyperactive behaviour rated by observers, teachers and parents, the additive drinks produced small but significant increases in hyperactivity. Small is the important word: the effects were a fraction of the difference between a child with ADHD and one without. The trial suggests that some additives nudge ordinary children's behaviour a little; it does not show that additives cause ADHD. Accordingly, the 2018 guideline of the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) in England advises clinicians not to recommend removing artificial colours and additives from the diet as a general treatment for ADHD, but to ask whether particular foods seem to affect a child's behaviour and, if a food diary supports a link, to refer the family to a dietitian.

The newest suspect is the screen. Children with more ADHD symptoms do spend more time on phones, games and television, but the studies are correlational, and the direction could easily run the other way: a child who cannot settle to a book may be drawn to the fastest-moving thing in the house. Until experiments settle it, screens are best treated as a matter of good habits rather than as a cause of ADHD.

Biological and environmental risks explain why a child develops ADHD. Psychological models try to explain what, in the mind, has gone wrong: why eighteen apparently different symptoms cluster together. The most influential answer came from Russell A. Barkley (born 1949), an American clinical psychologist whose parent training programme Book 2, Section 10.3.5 describes.

In 1997, in a long paper in the Psychological Bulletin titled "Behavioral inhibition, sustained attention, and executive functions: constructing a unifying theory of ADHD", Barkley proposed that the core deficit is not in attention at all but in behavioural inhibition: the capacity to hold back a response, to stop a response already under way, and to protect the pause from distraction. Inhibition matters, he argued, because it creates a delay between an event and the person's response to it, and in that delay four executive functions do their work. The first is non-verbal working memory: holding images of the past and the future in mind, which gives a person hindsight, forethought and a sense of time. The second is the internalisation of speech, or verbal working memory: the inner voice with which we instruct ourselves, follow rules and reflect. The third is the self-regulation of affect, motivation and arousal: calming oneself when angry and generating the motivation to persist at a dull task with no immediate reward. The fourth is reconstitution: taking behaviour apart and recombining it, the analysis and synthesis that underlie fluent speech, problem solving and creativity. Together these four put behaviour under the control of internal information (plans, rules, the future) instead of whatever is happening at the moment, and that is what gives a person motor control, fluency and goal-directed persistence (Figure 3.13). Barkley intended the model for the hyperactive-impulsive and combined presentations, and regarded the purely inattentive picture as possibly a different problem.

The model had an important practical implication, which Barkley put in a phrase that clinicians still quote: ADHD is a disorder of performance, of doing what one knows, rather than a disorder of knowledge, of knowing what to do. Vedant knows the methods for his mathematics sums, yet he makes careless mistakes; on Barkley's view, that is because he cannot hold back long enough to check. A child with ADHD can recite the classroom rules perfectly and break them a minute later. The help he needs, on this view, is less in teaching him more and more in supporting the point of performance: rules made visible on a card, time made visible on a clock, rewards made frequent and immediate, which is precisely what the behavioural treatments of Book 2, Sections 4.10.5 and 10.4.3 do. Barkley's own review found the strongest evidence for deficits in inhibition, working memory, the regulation of motivation and motor control, and he acknowledged that far more research was needed. The difficulty that emerged later was heterogeneity: many children with ADHD perform normally on executive function tests, so executive dysfunction cannot be the whole story.

That difficulty is what the dual pathway model of the British psychologist Edmund Sonuga-Barke (2002, elaborated in 2003) set out to solve. Sonuga-Barke proposed that there are two distinct routes into ADHD. In the first, ADHD results from executive dysregulation of thought and action caused by poor inhibitory control, tied to the branch of the dopamine system that projects to the prefrontal cortex and its circuits through the upper (dorsal) striatum; this is essentially Barkley's pathway. In the second, ADHD is a motivational style called delay aversion: waiting is unusually unpleasant to the child, because his "delay of reward gradient" is altered, so he escapes or avoids delay whenever he can. This route is tied to the reward circuits of the lower (ventral) striatum and the nucleus accumbens and to the branch of the dopamine system that serves them. Delay aversion shows itself most clearly on choice-delay tasks, in which a child chooses between a small reward now and a larger one after a wait: children with ADHD pick the small, immediate reward more often. And when waiting cannot be avoided, as in a long lesson, the delay-averse child creates stimulation to make the time pass, by fidgeting, chatting or attending to something else, so that some of his inattention and overactivity is a way of coping with boredom. A head-to-head comparison of the two accounts, on which Sonuga-Barke built the model, found that poor inhibition and delay aversion were independent characteristics, which could occur separately or together. One practical consequence is that a child on the delay-aversion pathway may do better with short tasks and immediate rewards than with drills in self-control.

A third model, the cognitive-energetic model of the Amsterdam neuropsychologist Joseph A. Sergeant (proposed for ADHD in 2000 and reappraised in 2005), places the problem partly in state regulation: the energetic resources of arousal, activation and effort that keep a person in the right state to perform. Sergeant argued that performance depends on three levels, the basic computational mechanisms of attention, these energetic state factors, and executive management above them, and that some of the apparent inhibition deficit in ADHD reflects difficulty in adjusting activation and effort to the demands of the task. It helps to explain why the performance of children with ADHD depends so much on the conditions of a task, such as its pace and its rewards, and why they tend to do worst when a task is slow and boring.

The three models are less rivals than different views of one elephant. Barkley describes the executive machinery, Sonuga-Barke adds the motivational engine, and Sergeant adds the fuel supply. Table 3.24 brings together everything this section and Section 3.8.3 have said about causes, with a column that is easy to forget: what each finding does not show.

Exam trap

In Barkley's 1997 model the primary deficit is behavioural inhibition, not attention; it impairs four executive functions (non-verbal working memory, internalisation of speech, self-regulation of affect, motivation and arousal, and reconstitution) and through them motor control. Sonuga-Barke's dual pathway model adds delay aversion (preferring a small immediate reward) as a second, motivational route. Sergeant's cognitive-energetic model stresses state regulation (arousal, activation, effort). Sugar does not cause hyperactivity; the Feingold additive-free diet has little support. The environmental risk DSM-5-TR treats as firmest is very low birth weight and prematurity, with risk rising as weight falls.

Drug treatment I: the stimulants

The drug treatment of ADHD began by accident in 1937, when the American psychiatrist Charles Bradley gave the amphetamine Benzedrine to children in a residential home in Rhode Island and found that many of them became calmer and better at their schoolwork; Book 2, Section 10.4.3 tells that story. The drug most often prescribed today came a few years later. In 1944 Leandro Panizzon, a chemist at the Swiss pharmaceutical company Ciba in Basel, synthesised methylphenidate. His wife Marguerite, known as Rita, took it to counter her low blood pressure, and the drug was named Ritalin after her. It was approved for medical use in the United States in 1955, and its use for hyperactive children grew from the 1960s onwards.

The stimulants used for ADHD today belong to two chemical families. The first is methylphenidate itself. The original short-acting tablets work for only a few hours, so a child taking them needs a second dose in the middle of the school day, which means a teacher or nurse handing out a controlled drug and a child taking a tablet in front of his classmates. Long-acting preparations were developed to solve that problem, including an osmotic-release tablet (sold as Concerta) that releases the drug gradually through the day from a single morning dose. The second family is the amphetamines: dextroamphetamine (dexamfetamine); mixed amphetamine salts, sold in the United States since 1996 as Adderall, which is a mixture of four amphetamine salts; and lisdexamfetamine (approved in the United States in 2007), a prodrug in which dextroamphetamine is chemically joined to the amino acid lysine, so that the capsule is inactive until enzymes in the red blood cells split off the lysine and release the active drug slowly. Be careful with one sentence in Barlow and Durand, which lists "methylphenidate (Ritalin, Adderall)": Adderall is not methylphenidate. Ritalin and Concerta are methylphenidate; Adderall, Dexedrine and Vyvanse are amphetamines.

How do they work? A dopamine or noradrenaline neuron releases its transmitter into the synapse and then pumps it back into itself through a transporter protein, the dopamine transporter or the noradrenaline transporter; this reuptake ends the signal. Methylphenidate blocks both transporters, so the transmitter stays in the synapse longer and acts more strongly, especially in the prefrontal cortex and the striatum, the frontostriatal circuits of Section 3.8.4. Positron emission tomography (PET) studies by Nora D. Volkow and her colleagues in 1998, in healthy adult volunteers, showed how complete the effect is: ordinary oral doses of methylphenidate occupy more than half of the brain's dopamine transporters, and the time the drug takes to reach its peak in the brain, about an hour, matches the time its effect on behaviour takes to reach its peak. The amphetamines also block reuptake, but they do something more: they enter the nerve terminal and make it release dopamine and noradrenaline, pushing the transmitter out through the transporter in the wrong direction. Both families, in the end, raise catecholamine signalling in the circuits that hold attention on a goal and hold back unwanted responses. (The Physiological book teaches the synapse in detail, and Chapter 10 of this book covers stimulant misuse and stimulant use disorder.)

For decades it was said that stimulants have a "paradoxical" calming effect on hyperactive children, opposite to their stimulating effect on everyone else, and that a calm response to a stimulant therefore proved that a child had ADHD. Both claims are wrong. Barlow and Durand state the evidence plainly: on the same low doses, children and adults with and without ADHD respond in the same way, with better focus on the task in front of them. The drug is not calming the child; it is improving the control systems that let him direct his own behaviour, and a child who can attend to his work stops wandering round the room. A good response to a stimulant tells you nothing about the diagnosis, because almost anyone's attention would sharpen.

How well do they work? Hundreds of controlled trials since Bradley's time have found that stimulants reduce the core symptoms of ADHD in most children, often within days of reaching the right dose, and many children who do not respond to one family respond to the other. The best comparison of the drugs is a 2018 network meta-analysis in The Lancet Psychiatry by Samuele Cortese, Andrea Cipriani and their colleagues, which combined 133 double-blind randomised trials (81 in children and adolescents, 51 in adults and one in both). A network meta-analysis allows drugs that were never tested against each other directly to be compared through their common comparison with placebo. Effects were expressed as standardised mean differences, the size of the improvement in standard deviation units, where 0.8 or more is conventionally a large effect. On clinicians' ratings of core symptoms after about 12 weeks, every drug beat placebo in children and adolescents: the amphetamines by 1.02, methylphenidate by 0.78 and the non-stimulant atomoxetine by 0.56. In adults the amphetamines (0.79) and methylphenidate (0.49) again beat placebo, as did bupropion and atomoxetine, while modafinil did not. The amphetamines had the largest effects but were also less well tolerated than placebo at every age. Weighing benefit against tolerability, the authors concluded that methylphenidate should be the preferred first choice for short-term treatment in children and adolescents, and the amphetamines in adults. They also noted a gap: there were not enough data to compare the drugs at 26 or 52 weeks.

Guidelines follow this evidence. The 2018 NICE guideline offers medication to a child of 5 or over only when ADHD still causes significant impairment after changes to the child's environment have been made and reviewed, and after the family has been given information about the disorder. It recommends methylphenidate, short or long acting, as the first-line drug for children aged 5 and over and young people; if a six-week trial at an adequate dose does not help enough, a switch to lisdexamfetamine; and for adults, lisdexamfetamine or methylphenidate first.

Just as important is what stimulants do not do. Barlow and Durand point out that some children do not respond, and that most of those who do respond improve in their ability to focus but show little gain in academic achievement or social skills: attention is the door to learning, but the drug does not walk the child through it. The effects also last only as long as the drug is taken; stop it, and the symptoms return. And the long-term evidence is weaker than the short-term evidence. In the MTA study (Section 3.8.7) the advantage of carefully managed medication had disappeared by three years, and in the follow-up to the age of about 25, reported by James M. Swanson and colleagues in 2017, young adults who had taken stimulants consistently for years did not have fewer symptoms than those who had used little medication, although they were somewhat shorter. That was a naturalistic comparison, not a randomised one, since the families had chosen whether to continue, but it is a reason to review the need for medication regularly rather than continuing it by default.

The side effects of the stimulants are common but usually mild and manageable. The most frequent are reduced appetite and weight loss (the lunch box comes home full), difficulty falling asleep, headache and stomach ache, and irritability or tearfulness, sometimes as a "rebound" in the late afternoon or evening as the dose wears off. Stimulants raise heart rate and blood pressure by small amounts. Barlow and Durand list insomnia, drowsiness and irritability among the unpleasant effects. Growth is the side effect parents worry about most, and here the MTA follow-ups give numbers. Children who had never taken a stimulant before the study and then took one during the follow-up grew, by the 36-month assessment, an average of 2.0 centimetres less in height and 2.7 kilograms less in weight than children who stayed unmedicated, with no sign of catching up by 36 months (Swanson and colleagues, 2007), and the 2017 adult follow-up found those who had used medication through childhood about 2.5 centimetres shorter as adults than those who had used little. Rarely, stimulants provoke psychotic or manic symptoms, especially at high doses. Tics may appear or worsen in some children, though most children with tics tolerate stimulants; Section 3.10.6 deals with that question.

These effects determine how a prescription is monitored. NICE advises a full assessment before starting, including a medical history with particular attention to the heart, and measurements of height, weight, pulse and blood pressure. It asks for a cardiology opinion first if there is, for example, congenital heart disease or past heart surgery, a sudden death in a first-degree relative under 40, breathlessness or fainting on exertion, palpitations, or chest pain that sounds cardiac. Once treatment begins, height is measured every six months and weight every three months in children aged 10 and under, and both are plotted on a growth chart, with pulse and blood pressure checked at review visits.

The final worry is misuse. The same stimulants are drugs of misuse, taken for elation and to fight fatigue, and Barlow and Durand note the widespread and mistaken belief among students that prescription stimulants are harmless "study drugs". People with ADHD are themselves at raised risk of later substance problems. Does treating a child with a stimulant make that risk worse, or better? In 2013 Kathryn Humphreys, Timothy Eng and Steve Lee combined 15 longitudinal studies with 2,565 participants, in which treatment came before the measurement of substance use, and found that children who had and had not been treated with stimulants had comparable outcomes for alcohol, nicotine, cannabis, cocaine and other drugs: stimulant treatment neither protected against nor increased the risk of later substance use disorders. The practical risk is diversion, the tablets being sold or given away, which is why the drugs are tightly controlled. In the United States methylphenidate and the amphetamines are Schedule II controlled substances. In India amphetamine, dexamphetamine and methylphenidate are all named in the list of psychotropic substances in the Schedule to the Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances (NDPS) Act, 1985 (Book 2, Section 1.10.10 teaches the Act), and methylphenidate, amphetamine and dexamphetamine are also Schedule X drugs under the Drugs and Cosmetics Rules, 1945, which can be sold only on a doctor's prescription, a copy of which the pharmacist must keep for two years.

Exam trap

Stimulants are not paradoxical: at low doses children and adults with and without ADHD respond the same way, with better focus, so a good response does not confirm the diagnosis. Adderall is an amphetamine (mixed amphetamine salts), not methylphenidate; Ritalin and Concerta are methylphenidate; lisdexamfetamine is an amphetamine prodrug. Methylphenidate blocks reuptake of dopamine and noradrenaline; amphetamines block reuptake and release them. Methylphenidate is the preferred first choice in children (Cortese and colleagues, 2018; NICE 2018). The commonest side effects are appetite loss and insomnia. Stimulant treatment in childhood does not raise the risk of later substance use disorder (Humphreys and colleagues, 2013).

Drug treatment II: the non-stimulants, and how drugs sit beside the psychological treatments

Why would a doctor choose anything other than a stimulant? Because some children do not respond to either family or cannot tolerate their side effects; because some have tics, anxiety or sleep problems that a stimulant may worsen; because a parent or older brother in the house misuses drugs and diversion is a real risk; and because some families will not accept a controlled drug. For these situations there are the non-stimulant medicines.

The first and best known is atomoxetine, sold as Strattera and approved in the United States in 2002. It is a selective noradrenaline reuptake inhibitor: it blocks the noradrenaline transporter but not the dopamine transporter. There is a neat twist in its pharmacology. In the prefrontal cortex there are few dopamine transporters, and dopamine there is cleared largely by the noradrenaline transporter, so atomoxetine raises dopamine as well as noradrenaline in the prefrontal cortex, where attention is controlled, without raising dopamine in the reward circuits of the striatum. That is why, as Barlow and Durand put it, it does not produce the same "highs" as the stimulants when taken in larger doses, and why it is not a controlled drug. Its drawbacks are slowness and a smaller effect. Its first effects usually take one to four weeks to appear and its full effect several weeks, so a family must persist through a month in which nothing much seems to happen; and in Cortese's network meta-analysis its effect in children (0.56) was smaller than either stimulant family's. Common side effects are abdominal pain, reduced appetite, nausea, tiredness and dizziness. Rarely it has caused liver problems, and it carries a warning about suicidal thinking in children and adolescents (a boxed warning added by the United States Food and Drug Administration in 2005), so mood is watched closely in the early weeks. Because it acts around the clock, it covers the evenings and early mornings that a stimulant may not.

The second group is the alpha-2 adrenergic agonists, clonidine and guanfacine. Both were first used to lower blood pressure (immediate-release clonidine as Catapres and guanfacine as Tenex), and extended-release forms are now approved in the United States for ADHD (clonidine as Kapvay and guanfacine as Intuniv), alone or added to a stimulant. They stimulate alpha-2 receptors for noradrenaline; in the prefrontal cortex, stimulation of the alpha-2A receptors, on which guanfacine acts more selectively, is thought to strengthen the signals of the networks that hold information in mind. Barlow and Durand note that clonidine and guanfacine reduce the core symptoms of hyperactivity and impulsivity. Clinically they are most useful when ADHD comes with tics or with marked difficulty falling asleep, both of which they may ease. Their side effects follow from their origin as blood pressure drugs: sleepiness and tiredness, low blood pressure, a slow pulse and dizziness. And they must never be stopped suddenly, because abrupt withdrawal, especially of clonidine, can cause a dangerous rebound rise in blood pressure; the dose is tapered down.

A few other drugs have a place further down the list. Tricyclic antidepressants such as imipramine, which act on the same noradrenaline system, were used for ADHD before atomoxetine existed (Barlow and Durand mention imipramine), and the antidepressant bupropion was better than placebo in adults in Cortese's analysis; both are now second- or third-line choices (antidepressant classes are taught in Chapter 5, Section 5.7.2). The newest addition is viloxazine, another noradrenaline reuptake inhibitor, which was marketed as an antidepressant in Europe from 1974 (never in the United States), withdrawn worldwide in 2002 for commercial reasons, and brought back in the United States as an ADHD treatment (Qelbree), approved for children and adolescents in 2021 and for adults in 2022. Like atomoxetine it is not a controlled substance.

Could genes one day tell the doctor which drug to choose? At present the choice is made by trial and error: one drug at a low dose, increased step by step, changed if it fails. Barlow and Durand describe the hope of psychopharmacogenetics, the study of how a person's genes influence his response to a drug, with an example: in a 2007 study by Guilherme Polanczyk and colleagues, children with ADHD who carried a particular variant of the gene for the alpha-2A adrenergic receptor (ADRA2A) responded especially well to methylphenidate, particularly in their inattention. No such test is yet used in routine practice. Table 3.25 brings the ADHD drugs together.

Table 3.25 The drugs used for ADHD.

Drugs are only half of ADHD treatment, and Book 2, Section 10.4.3 teaches the other half in full: behavioural parent training (Book 2, Sections 10.3.5 and 4.10.4), behavioural classroom management with its daily report cards (Section 4.10.5), and intensive peer-focused programmes such as the summer treatment programme. It also tells, as a Key study, the Multimodal Treatment Study of Children with ADHD (MTA, 1999), whose essentials you should carry here. Five hundred and seventy-nine children aged 7 to 9.9 with the combined type of ADHD were randomly assigned to four arms for 14 months: carefully managed medication, intensive behavioural treatment, the two combined, or ordinary community care. For the core symptoms, the two arms with carefully managed medication did better than intensive behavioural treatment and community care, and the combination was not significantly better than medication alone; the combination brought modest additional benefits in other areas, such as oppositional behaviour, anxiety, social skills, parent-child relations and reading, and achieved its results with somewhat lower doses. By 36 months the groups no longer differed. The guideline conclusions that follow are also Book 2's: behavioural parent training comes first for preschool children (NICE offers an ADHD-focused group parent-training programme as the first-line treatment under 5 and advises against medication under 5 without a second opinion from a specialist service), while for a school-age child with marked symptoms the usual recommendation is medication together with parent training and classroom measures.

A few other treatments deserve a sentence each, with their evidence. Organisational skills training, which teaches children to manage materials, time and homework, helped in a trial by Howard Abikoff and colleagues (2013) of 158 children in Classes 3 to 5: compared with a waiting list, it improved organisation, homework and academic performance, and 60 percent of the children in either active treatment (skills training, or a comparison programme that rewarded organised behaviour without teaching the skills) no longer met the study's criteria for organisational impairment, against 3 percent of the waiting-list group. Cognitive behavioural therapy adapted for adults with ADHD (planning, organising, reducing distractibility and challenging unhelpful thoughts) was tested by Steven Safren and colleagues (2010) in 86 adults who were already taking medication but still had symptoms; twelve sessions of CBT produced more improvement than twelve sessions of relaxation with educational support, with 53 percent against 23 percent responding on a clinician's global rating, and the gains lasted at the 12-month follow-up. Neurofeedback, in which a child learns to change his own brainwave patterns while watching a display, looked effective in a 2016 meta-analysis by Cortese and colleagues only on ratings by people who knew which children had been trained; when the raters were probably blind, or the comparison was sham feedback, the effect was not significant. Restriction diets were discussed in Section 3.8.5. The Book 2 sections give the methods; the point here is that only the behavioural treatments and medication have strong evidence for children, and CBT for adults.

Return, finally, to Vedant. His treatment began not with a prescription but with psychoeducation for the whole joint family, because his grandparents feared that "the tablet is a drug like ganja". His parents were enrolled in a parent-training programme, and his class teacher agreed to classroom measures (Book 2, Sections 10.3.5 and 4.10.5). After three months methylphenidate was started at a low dose and increased step by step. Two side effects appeared: his appetite at lunch fell, and he took longer to fall asleep. Both were managed by giving the dose after breakfast, so that he had eaten his breakfast before the drug took effect, and by checking his weight and height at each visit. His teacher then reported that he was finishing his classwork and that there were fewer clashes in the line.

Clinical link

How should a clinician answer a grandparent who says the tablet is "a drug like ganja"? Not by dismissing the fear, because it contains a fact: methylphenidate really is a controlled psychotropic substance under the NDPS Act, just as cannabis is controlled under the same Act, and it can be misused. The honest answer separates the drug from the way it is used. Taken by mouth, at a low dose, on a doctor's prescription, with the family keeping the strip and counting the tablets, it improves attention without producing a "high", and the best evidence is that treated children are no more likely than untreated ones to develop substance problems later. The law exists to stop the tablets reaching people who would misuse them, which is why the family should never share them and should keep them out of reach. Explaining this, and inviting the grandparents to the review visits, is often what decides whether a prescription is actually taken.

Exam trap

Atomoxetine is not a stimulant. It is a selective noradrenaline reuptake inhibitor (approved in the United States in 2002) with no "high" and no controlled status, but it takes weeks to work. Clonidine and guanfacine are alpha-2 adrenergic agonists, useful when tics or sleep problems coexist; never stop them suddenly. In the MTA study (579 children, 14 months, four arms) carefully managed medication beat intensive behavioural treatment for core symptoms, the combination added benefits in other areas, and by 36 months the groups no longer differed. For preschool children, parent training comes first.

3.9 Specific Learning Disorder: Dyslexia, Dysgraphia and DyscalculiaMedium yield · 3 Qs

Diya is nine and in Class 4 at an English-medium school in Pune. At home her family speaks Marathi. In the science quiz she is the first to put up her hand, and she can tell you, with gestures, how a seed becomes a plant. Ask her to read the same lesson aloud, though, and everything changes. She reads slowly, one word at a time, her finger pressed under each word; she guesses from the first letter and the picture, so that "horse" becomes "house" and "stopped" becomes "stamp"; she loses her place and reads the same line twice. Her spelling test comes back with "frend", "Wensday" and "becos". Her Marathi reading is slow too, though she makes fewer outright mistakes in it. Her class teacher's remark in the diary is "Diya is intelligent but does not try", and her mother has begun to wonder whether that is true. Her father says quietly that he too hated reading aloud in school.

Is Diya lazy, poorly taught, slow, or something else? This section answers that question. It traces the history of the idea that a bright child can have a specific difficulty with reading, writing or arithmetic, teaches the DSM-5-TR criteria and why the old rule based on IQ was dropped, describes the three kinds of difficulty and who has them, explains the theories of their cause, works through the conditions that can be mistaken for them, and ends with what helps, and what does not.

Bright children who cannot read: the history of an idea

The idea began not with children but with adults who had lost the ability to read. In 1877 the German physician Adolf Kussmaul (1822 to 1902), then professor of medicine at Strasbourg, described patients who after a stroke or other brain damage could still see, speak and think but could no longer read words, although their eyesight was sound. He called the condition word blindness (Wortblindheit). Ten years later, in 1887, Rudolf Berlin (1833 to 1897), an ophthalmologist in Stuttgart, published a monograph titled "A special kind of word blindness (dyslexia)" and gave the world the term dyslexia, from Greek roots meaning "difficulty with words". Like Kussmaul's, his cases were of reading lost after brain disease. Today such a loss in a previously literate adult is called acquired dyslexia, or alexia, and it is a neuropsychological syndrome taught in the Physiological book. Pinel and Barnes point out that it is relatively rare, whereas the developmental form, which becomes apparent while a child is learning to read, is common.

The developmental form was first described in a single case report. In 1895 a Glasgow eye surgeon, James Hinshelwood (1859 to 1919), published a paper in The Lancet on word blindness and visual memory in adults. It was read by W. Pringle Morgan, a general practitioner in Seaford, on the south coast of England, who had a patient who seemed to fit. On 7 November 1896 Morgan published "A case of congenital word blindness" in the British Medical Journal. The patient was Percy F., "a well-grown lad, aged 14", who, Morgan wrote, "has always been a bright and intelligent boy, quick at games, and in no way inferior to others of his age". His great difficulty had always been his inability to learn to read. He knew his letters but stumbled over common short words; he spelled his own name "Precy"; and yet he did sums well and could work algebra correctly. Morgan concluded that the problem was congenital, a defect present from birth, which he linked to the left angular gyrus, the region where the adult cases of word blindness had damage. In a single page he set out the essence of the modern concept: a specific failure to learn to read, in a child of normal intelligence and opportunity, unexplained by poor eyesight or lack of teaching.

Hinshelwood took up the theme and made it his own. In 1896 he published "A case of dyslexia: a peculiar form of word-blindness", and from 1900 a series of papers on "congenital word-blindness" in children, including a 1911 report of two cases that he described as hereditary. In 1917 he gathered his work into the monograph Congenital Word-Blindness. He defined the condition as a congenital defect in children with otherwise normal brains, making it difficult to learn to read, and he located it in visual memory for words and letters, a theory that Barlow and Durand classify with the early neurological explanations. He was wrong about the visual mechanism, as Section 3.9.5 shows, but right about almost everything else: that the problem is specific, that it can be inherited, and that such children can be taught by special methods.

In the United States the story continued with the neuropsychiatrist Samuel Torrey Orton (1879 to 1948), who from 1925 studied children who could not learn to read, called their difficulty "strephosymbolia" (twisted symbols), and blamed incomplete dominance of one cerebral hemisphere over the other; the dominance theory was later abandoned, but the teaching method he built with Anna Gillingham survives, and Book 2, Section 10.5.9 tells that part of the story. The name of the wider category came in 1963. At a conference in Chicago in April of that year, organised by parents of children who had been labelled "brain-injured", "perceptually handicapped" or simply backward, the American psychologist Samuel A. Kirk (1904 to 1996) proposed the term learning disabilities for children with specific difficulties in language, reading, writing or arithmetic who were neither intellectually disabled nor sensorily impaired. The parents adopted it, and their group became what is now the Learning Disabilities Association of America.

The law followed. The American Education for All Handicapped Children Act of 1975 (Public Law 94-142) required public schools to evaluate and educate children with disabilities, and defined a specific learning disability as a disorder in one or more of the basic psychological processes involved in understanding or using spoken or written language, which may show itself as an imperfect ability to listen, think, speak, read, write, spell or do mathematical calculations. The regulations issued under it at the end of 1977 told schools how to identify such a child: a team had to find a severe discrepancy between achievement and intellectual ability in one or more areas, from oral expression to mathematical reasoning, that was not primarily the result of a visual, hearing or motor handicap, mental retardation or several other excluded causes. That rule, the IQ-achievement discrepancy, shaped the diagnosis for more than three decades, and Section 3.9.2 explains why it was abandoned. A scientific version of the same idea came from the Isle of Wight studies in Britain, where Michael Rutter and William Yule argued in 1975 that "specific reading retardation", reading far below the level predicted from age and IQ, was a different condition from general reading backwardness in children of lower ability; later studies, as Frank Vellutino and his colleagues reviewed in 2000, failed to confirm the distinction reliably.

The psychiatric manuals took the concept in stages. DSM-III (1980) listed "specific developmental disorders", such as developmental reading disorder and developmental arithmetic disorder, and coded them on Axis II (the multiaxial system is described in Chapter 1, Section 1.7.5). DSM-IV (1994) and DSM-IV-TR (2000) renamed them learning disorders and listed three separate disorders, reading disorder, mathematics disorder and disorder of written expression, each defined by achievement substantially below that expected for the person's age, measured intelligence and education. DSM-5 (2013) merged the three into a single diagnosis, specific learning disorder, with specifiers for the domains affected, because, as Barlow and Durand explain, the three overlap so much that a clinician is better served by a broad view of the person's learning; DSM-5-TR (2022) kept that structure. ICD-10 calls the group specific developmental disorders of scholastic skills (F81, with specific reading disorder, specific spelling disorder, specific disorder of arithmetical skills and a mixed category), and ICD-11 calls it developmental learning disorder (6A03); Table 3.2 in Section 3.1 gives the codes side by side. In India, public awareness changed sharply in 2007 with the Hindi film Taare Zameen Par (Stars on the Earth), directed by Aamir Khan, in which an art teacher at a boarding school realises that an artistically gifted eight-year-old whose schoolwork is failing is dyslexic. Figure 3.14 sets out the milestones.

Exam trap

Kussmaul (1877) described "word blindness", and Berlin (1887) coined "dyslexia", both for reading lost after brain damage. Pringle Morgan (1896, British Medical Journal, Percy F., aged 14) published the first case of developmental or congenital word blindness. Hinshelwood, a Glasgow eye surgeon, wrote Congenital Word-Blindness (1917) and blamed visual memory. Orton coined "strephosymbolia" and blamed incomplete cerebral dominance. Kirk coined "learning disabilities" (1963). DSM-IV had three separate learning disorders; DSM-5 merged them into one specific learning disorder with specifiers.

The DSM-5-TR criteria, and why the IQ-discrepancy rule was dropped

DSM-5-TR defines specific learning disorder by four criteria, and like the ADHD criteria of Section 3.7 each exists to rule something in or something out.

Criterion A requires difficulties in learning and using academic skills, shown by at least one of six symptoms that have lasted for at least 6 months despite help aimed at those very difficulties. The six symptoms fall into three groups. Two concern reading.
