The first is inaccurate, or slow and effortful, word reading: reading single words aloud wrongly or haltingly, frequently guessing, struggling to sound words out, as Diya does when "stopped" becomes "stamp". The second is difficulty understanding what is read: a Class 7 boy may read a mathematics word problem aloud perfectly and still have no idea what it asks him to find. Two concern writing. The third symptom is difficulty with spelling, adding, leaving out or substituting vowels and consonants ("frend", "Wensday"), and in Devanagari, leaving out or confusing the matras, the vowel signs attached to consonants. The fourth is difficulty with written expression: many grammatical or punctuation errors within sentences, poorly organised paragraphs, and ideas that do not come across clearly, so that a page on "My favourite festival" is three long sentences that run into one another. Two concern mathematics. The fifth is difficulty mastering number sense, number facts or calculation: a poor grasp of numbers, their size and their relations, counting on fingers to add 7 and 5 long after classmates simply know the answer, and getting lost midway through a long division and switching methods. The sixth is difficulty with mathematical reasoning: knowing how to multiply but not seeing that "six packets of eight biscuits" calls for it.

The phrase "despite the provision of interventions that target those difficulties" is the most important in the criterion. A child who reads badly because nobody has taught her to read has a problem of teaching, not a disorder. DSM-5-TR defines persistence as restricted progress, no evidence that the child is catching up with classmates, for at least 6 months despite extra help at home or school; for example, difficulty reading single words that does not fully or rapidly improve with instruction in phonological skills or word identification strategies may indicate a specific learning disorder. A child's response to good teaching thus becomes part of the evidence for the diagnosis.

Criterion B requires that the affected skills be substantially and quantifiably below those expected for the person's chronological age, and that they cause significant interference with academic or occupational performance or with daily living. "Quantifiably" means measured: the deficit must be confirmed by individually administered, standardised achievement tests (the tests themselves belong to the Assessment book) together with a comprehensive clinical assessment. From the age of 17, a documented history of impairing learning difficulties may take the place of the standardised testing, because an adult's school record often tells the story better than a test taken years later.

How far below is "substantially" below? DSM-5-TR admits that academic skills are distributed along a continuum with no natural cut-point, so any threshold is to a large extent arbitrary. It suggests that scores at least 1.5 standard deviations below the population mean for age give the greatest diagnostic certainty. Work it out on the usual scale with a mean of 100 and a standard deviation of 15: 1.5 standard deviations is 22.5 points, so the threshold is a standard score of about 78 or less, which lies below the 7th percentile. On clinical judgement a more lenient threshold, about 1.0 standard deviation below the mean (a standard score of about 85), may be used when the learning difficulties are supported by converging evidence from the clinical assessment, the academic history, school reports or test scores. And because standardised tests do not exist in every language, the diagnosis may rest partly on clinical judgement of the measures that are available, a real problem in a country of many languages and scripts.

Criterion C requires that the learning difficulties begin during the school years, although they may not become fully obvious until the demands for the affected skills exceed the person's limited capacities, for example in timed tests, in reading or writing long and complex reports for a tight deadline, or under very heavy academic loads. A bright child may compensate for years, by memorising, by listening hard in class, by effort, until the volume of reading in Class 9 or in college overwhelms the strategy. DSM-5-TR notes that specific learning disorder can occur in intellectually gifted people, whose achievement may then be low only relative to their own ability or to their achievement in other areas, not to the population average.

Criterion D lists what must be ruled out: the difficulties must not be better accounted for by intellectual disabilities, uncorrected problems of vision or hearing, other mental or neurological disorders, psychosocial adversity, lack of proficiency in the language of instruction, or inadequate teaching. Each exclusion protects the meaning of the word "specific". The difficulty is specific because it is not part of a general slowness of learning (intellectual disability, Section 3.2), not the result of a child being unable to see the blackboard or hear the teacher, not the result of a disease of the brain, not the result of poverty, chronic absence or an unstable home, and not the result of being taught in a language the child does not yet command. A note to the criteria adds that all four are to be judged by a clinical synthesis of the person's developmental, medical, family and educational history, school reports and psychoeducational assessment; no single test score makes the diagnosis.

Where does intelligence come in? The difficulty must be "unexpected" in the sense that it occurs in a person of otherwise normal intellectual functioning, which DSM-5-TR says is generally estimated by an IQ score of greater than about 70, plus or minus 5 points for measurement error. The phrase unexpected academic underachievement is often cited, the manual says, as the defining characteristic of the disorder. If a person has intellectual disability, specific learning disorder can be diagnosed as well only when the learning difficulties are in excess of those usually associated with the intellectual disability. Notice that DSM-5-TR's floor is low: a child of IQ 75 can, in principle, have a specific learning disorder. Indian certification is stricter. Under the 2024 guidelines for certifying specific learning disability, described in Book 2, Section 9.9.8, the child must be at least 8 years old, must have an IQ of 85 or more, and must show attainment at least three standard deviations below his or her current class on the NIMHANS battery. A clinician can therefore diagnose specific learning disorder by DSM-5-TR in a child who would not receive an Indian disability certificate.

Why did DSM-5 drop the old requirement that achievement be far below what IQ predicts? Book 2, Section 10.5.9 teaches the educational argument in full. Because a gap between ability and achievement takes years to grow large enough to count, children were identified only in the middle school years, long after early help would have worked best, which critics called a "wait to fail" model; in 2004 the reauthorised Individuals with Disabilities Education Act in the United States allowed schools to use response to intervention instead, with tiers of increasingly intensive teaching. Barlow and Durand add that the old definition was often operationalised as a discrepancy of more than 2 standard deviations between achievement and IQ. There were two further problems, both diagnostic. First, discrepancy scores are unreliable: the difference between two imperfect test scores is less reliable than either score alone, so a child could qualify on Monday and fail to qualify on Friday. Second, and more fundamental, the discrepancy did not pick out a different kind of poor reader. Vellutino, Scanlon and Lyon reviewed the evidence in 2000 and concluded that the IQ-achievement discrepancy does not reliably distinguish poor readers from normal readers, whereas language-based measures do, and in their own study IQ did not distinguish the poor readers who responded readily to remedial teaching from those who were hard to remediate. Poor readers with high IQs and poor readers with lower IQs, in other words, have the same core problem with the sounds of words (Section 3.9.5) and respond to the same teaching. DSM-5's solution keeps what was right in the old idea, that the disorder is specific and not explained by low intelligence, and replaces the discrepancy with two things: achievement measured against age, and persistence despite targeted intervention.

Finally, DSM-5-TR grades current severity. In mild specific learning disorder, there are some difficulties in one or two academic domains, mild enough that the person may compensate or function well with appropriate accommodations or support, especially during the school years. In moderate disorder, the difficulties are marked, in one or more domains, so that the person is unlikely to become proficient without some periods of intensive, specialised teaching during the school years, and some accommodations or support for at least part of the day may be needed. In severe disorder, the difficulties affect several domains and the person is unlikely to learn the skills without ongoing, intensive, individualised and specialised teaching for most of the school years; even with a range of accommodations he may not be able to complete all activities efficiently.

Exam trap

The DSM-5 criteria for specific learning disorder include roughly average intellectual functioning with persistent difficulties in academic skills (at least 6 months, despite targeted intervention, well below age expectation on standardised tests). The distractors each point to another disorder: "IQ below 50" means at least moderate intellectual disability (Section 3.2), which by criterion D excludes the diagnosis unless the learning difficulty is in excess of the disability; "only attention deficit" describes ADHD (Section 3.7); "motor coordination deficit only" describes developmental coordination disorder (Section 3.10.1). The 1.5 standard deviation guide equals a standard score of about 78 (below the 7th percentile). The IQ-achievement discrepancy is no longer required.

Read option (a) carefully. "Average intellectual functioning" is the usual picture and the best of the four options, but strictly DSM-5-TR asks only for intellectual functioning in the normal range, an IQ above about 70 (plus or minus 5), so a child of IQ 75 can still be diagnosed. The figure of 85 or more belongs to the Indian certification guidelines of 2024 (Book 2, Section 9.9.8), not to DSM-5-TR; do not carry it into a question about the DSM criteria.

Three kinds of difficulty: reading, writing and mathematics

Specific learning disorder is one diagnosis, but it is never recorded bare. DSM-5-TR asks the clinician to specify every academic domain that is impaired and, within each, every subskill, and because of the ICD coding rules each impaired domain is coded separately. There are three domains, each with its own code and its own list of subskills, set out in Table 3.26. A child with problems in both reading and mathematics therefore receives two codes, for example F81.0 specific learning disorder with impairment in reading, with impairment in reading rate or fluency and in reading comprehension, and F81.2 specific learning disorder with impairment in mathematics, with impairment in accurate or fluent calculation.

Table 3.26 The specifiers of specific learning disorder in DSM-5-TR.
Specifier (code)Subskills to recordCommon name
With impairment in reading (F81.0)Word reading accuracy; reading rate or fluency; reading comprehensionDyslexia (DSM-5-TR's permitted alternative term for the word-recognition, decoding and spelling pattern)
With impairment in written expression (F81.81)Spelling accuracy; grammar and punctuation accuracy; clarity or organisation of written expressionOften called dysgraphia (not a DSM-5-TR term)
With impairment in mathematics (F81.2)Number sense; memorisation of arithmetic facts; accurate or fluent calculation; accurate mathematical reasoningDyscalculia (DSM-5-TR's permitted alternative term)

Two of the familiar names are written into DSM-5-TR itself, as notes under the specifiers. Dyslexia, the manual says, is an alternative term for a pattern of learning difficulties characterised by problems with accurate or fluent word recognition, poor decoding and poor spelling. Decoding means turning letters into sounds and sounds into a word: seeing "s-t-o-p-p-e-d" and arriving at "stopped". Notice that this pattern straddles two domains, since poor spelling is a subskill of written expression, and that the manual adds a warning: if a clinician uses the word dyslexia, any additional difficulties, such as poor reading comprehension or weak mathematical reasoning, must also be specified. Difficulty in learning to map letters onto the sounds of one's language is, DSM-5-TR notes, one of the commonest forms of specific learning disorder. Dyscalculia is the alternative term for a pattern of difficulties in processing numerical information, learning arithmetic facts and performing accurate or fluent calculations, and here too any further difficulty, such as in mathematical reasoning, should be specified.

The third name, dysgraphia, does not appear in DSM-5-TR at all, and that is why it causes confusion. It is used in two senses. In the first, it means a learning difficulty in written expression, poor spelling, grammar, punctuation and organisation of written language, which DSM-5-TR records as specific learning disorder with impairment in written expression. In the second, common in Indian notes and school reports, it simply means poor handwriting: letters badly formed, uneven in size, slow to produce and hard to read. If the handwriting problem lies in the motor act of forming letters, it belongs to developmental coordination disorder, which DSM-5-TR describes as often affecting the legibility and speed of written output, and which the manual distinguishes from specific learning disorder by its emphasis on the motor component (Section 3.10.1 teaches it). A child can have both: clumsy, laborious handwriting and, separately, difficulty spelling words and organising sentences. The question to ask is whether the problem disappears when the child types or dictates. If his typed or dictated composition is fluent and well organised, the difficulty was in his hand; if it is still disorganised and misspelled, it is in his language.

What does dyslexia look like as a child grows? DSM-5-TR describes a sequence that is worth knowing in detail, because parents and teachers notice the early signs long before anyone thinks of a disorder. Before school starts, the child may show little interest in games with the sounds of language, such as rhyming and repeating, may have trouble learning nursery rhymes, may keep mispronouncing words, and may have trouble remembering the names of letters, numbers or the days of the week. Diya's mother remembers that at five Diya could not learn the alphabet song or the rhymes her cousins chanted, and kept forgetting the names of letters she had learned the day before. At the stage when children first learn letters, the future dyslexic child may be unable to break a spoken word into its parts (to hear "cowboy" as "cow" and "boy"), to tell which words rhyme, or to say which of three words starts with the same sound as "cat".

In the early primary classes the hallmark, especially in English, is marked difficulty in learning which letters go with which sounds: the child cannot read common short words, cannot recognise common irregular words such as "said" and "two", and makes errors that show the sound-letter link has not formed. By Classes 4 to 6, as with Diya now, the child may mispronounce or skip parts of long words, confuse words that sound alike, read the first part of a word correctly and then guess wildly, spell very poorly, take far longer than classmates over homework and tests, and express fear of reading aloud or refuse to do it. By adolescence the child may have mastered decoding, so that he reads words correctly, but reading remains slow and effortful; comprehension and written expression become the main problems, along with persistent spelling errors. Picture a 20-year-old commerce student in Delhi who reads every page of his textbook twice to understand it, avoids reading for pleasure entirely, writes answers full of spelling mistakes, and runs out of time in every examination although he knows the material. Adults with specific learning disorder, DSM-5-TR notes, often avoid activities that demand reading or arithmetic and use alternative routes to print, such as text-to-speech software, audiobooks and video.

A word about a famous myth. Many people believe that the defining sign of dyslexia is writing or reading letters backwards, b for d, "was" for "saw". Every beginning reader reverses letters, because in everyday life an object is the same object whichever way it faces, and children must learn that b and d are not. Reversals fade with reading experience in typical children and persist longer in children who read less, so they are better seen as a sign of slow reading development than as its cause. Orton's "strephosymbolia" was built on reversals, and critics pointed out that mirror reading and reversals are not the salient feature of specific reading disability. As Section 3.9.5 shows, the core problem is with the sounds of language, not with seeing.

Dyscalculia looks different. Krish, eleven, in Class 6 in Chennai, reads well above his class level and writes vivid stories. But he still counts on his fingers to add 8 and 6, cannot remember the multiplication tables however often he recites them, takes a long time to say whether 47 or 74 is the larger number, cannot estimate whether an answer is roughly right, gets lost halfway through a long division, and dreads being sent to the shop because he cannot work out the change. His problem is not reading the word problems but the numbers themselves: their size, their order and their relations. DSM-5-TR notes that some people have a circumscribed difficulty that lasts throughout life, such as an inability to grasp the basic sense of number, for example to judge which of two numbers or two sets of dots is larger.

How a learning disorder shows itself also depends on the writing system a child is learning, and this matters greatly in India. DSM-5-TR explains that in English the observable hallmark of a reading difficulty is inaccurate and slow reading of single words, because English spelling is deep or opaque: the same letters stand for different sounds in different words (think of "cough", "though", "through" and "bough"), so a child cannot simply sound words out. In alphabetic languages with a more direct mapping between sounds and letters, such as Spanish and German, and in non-alphabetic writing systems such as Chinese and Japanese, the hallmark is slow but accurate reading. Barlow and Durand describe a study by Eraldo Paulesu and colleagues in 2001 that compared dyslexic adults who read English, French or Italian. The Italian readers, whose spelling is very regular, did better on reading tests, yet brain imaging showed the same reduced activity in the left temporal lobe in all three groups while they read. The biology of dyslexia appears to be the same everywhere; what differs is how hard the writing system makes it to hide.

Most Indian scripts, Devanagari for Hindi and Marathi and the scripts of Kannada, Tamil, Telugu, Malayalam, Bengali and others, are not alphabets in the English sense but alphasyllabaries: each written unit, the akshara, stands for a syllable, a consonant with its vowel sign (matra) or a conjunct of consonants, and the mapping between akshara and sound is far more regular than in English. On DSM-5-TR's principle you would expect dyslexia in these scripts to show more often as slow, laboured reading than as frequent errors, which is what Diya's Marathi reading suggests; the large number of akshara and conjunct forms means, though, that learning them all can take several years even for typical readers. Assessment tools developed for Indian languages are described in the Assessment book.

Language raises a second, practical question, because so many Indian children are taught in English while speaking another language at home. Criterion D excludes difficulties better explained by a lack of proficiency in the language of instruction, and DSM-5-TR asks that for a child learning in a second language the assessment consider whether the reading problem comes from limited proficiency in that language or from a specific learning disorder. The manual lists warning signs that favour a learning disorder: a family history of learning disorder or of language delay in the home language, learning difficulties and weak phonological memory in the second language, and failure to catch up with peers. It insists on assessing the child's proficiency in the first language as well as in the second, and it adds a reassuring fact: children who speak a language at home that differs in its sounds from the language of instruction are not more likely than their peers to have phonological deficits. In practice, a child who reads her home language fluently and struggles only with English, after a year or two in an English-medium school, more probably needs more English than a diagnosis; a child like Diya, slow in both scripts and with a father who hated reading, more probably has a specific learning disorder.

Exam trap

A common characteristic of dyslexia is difficulty with reading and decoding words (inaccurate or slow word recognition and poor spelling). The distractors each name another disorder: difficulty understanding spoken language is language disorder (Section 3.4.2); difficulty with motor coordination is developmental coordination disorder (Section 3.10.1); difficulty concentrating during lectures suggests ADHD (Section 3.7). DSM-5-TR permits the terms dyslexia and dyscalculia but not "dysgraphia"; poor handwriting from motor causes belongs to developmental coordination disorder. Letter reversals are not the core of dyslexia.

Who has it, and what becomes of it

DSM-5-TR gives the prevalence of specific learning disorder across the domains of reading, writing and mathematics as 5 to 15 percent of school-age children, a figure drawn from studies in Brazil, Northern Ireland and the United States; the prevalence in adults, it says, is unknown. The width of that range has the same explanation as the range for ADHD: there is no natural cut-point, so the rate depends on where the threshold is drawn (1.0 or 1.5 standard deviations), which tests are used and in which language. Other sources divide the figure up. Pinel and Barnes put developmental dyslexia at 5 to 12 percent of English-speaking children, depending on the criteria, and Barlow and Durand estimate that reading difficulties, the commonest of the learning disorders, occur in some form in 4 to 10 percent of the general population and mathematics disorder in about 1 percent, with little information on disorders of written expression. For India, school-based estimates vary widely with the tests and languages used; the multi-site INCLEN study (Section 3.1.6) found learning disorders among the commoner neurodevelopmental conditions in 6- to 9-year-olds across its five regions, and no single Indian number should be quoted without its source.

Specific learning disorder is more common in boys than in girls, with ratios from about 2 to 1 to 3 to 1, and DSM-5-TR states that the difference cannot be attributed to ascertainment bias, to differences in definition or measurement, or to language, ethnic background or socioeconomic status. (Barlow and Durand cite research suggesting that boys and girls may be equally affected, so you may meet both claims; the DSM-5-TR statement is the current authoritative one.) Pinel and Barnes give the same two-to-three-fold excess of boys for developmental dyslexia.

The course follows the demands of school. Onset, recognition and diagnosis usually occur during the primary school years, when children are required to learn to read, spell, write and do arithmetic, although precursors such as language delay, difficulty with rhyming or counting, and difficulty with the fine motor skills needed for writing commonly appear before formal schooling begins. Some children show the disorder first in their behaviour, through reluctance to engage in learning or oppositional behaviour, and a teacher may see a "naughty" child before she sees a struggling reader. Specific learning disorder is lifelong, but its course and expression vary with the demands of the environment, the range and severity of the person's difficulties, his other abilities, any co-occurring disorders, and the support and teaching available. Problems with reading fluency and comprehension, spelling, written expression and everyday numeracy usually persist into adulthood, though the particular array of difficulties may shift with age, and many adults compensate, by avoidance, by sheer effort or by technology. As DSM-5-TR puts it, because the disorder typically persists into adulthood, reassessment is rarely necessary once it has been diagnosed.

Several early signs predict how a child will fare. Marked inattentive, internalising or externalising behaviour in the preschool years predicts later difficulties in reading and mathematics, though not necessarily a learning disorder, and a poor response to effective teaching. Language impairment in the preschool years is strongly associated with later reading problems, and delays in speech or language or weak phonological awareness, working memory or rapid naming may predict later specific learning disorder in reading and in written expression. Co-occurring ADHD predicts worse mental health outcomes than specific learning disorder alone. On the other side, systematic, intensive, individualised teaching using evidence-based methods may improve the difficulties in some people or help others develop compensatory strategies, softening the otherwise poor outcomes.

What are those outcomes? DSM-5-TR lists them bluntly: lower academic attainment, higher rates of dropping out of high school, lower rates of further education, high levels of psychological distress and poorer overall mental health, higher rates of unemployment and underemployment, and lower incomes. Children with poor reading and mathematics skills report more sadness and loneliness as they move up through the primary classes, and episodes of severe anxiety or anxiety disorders, including bodily complaints and panic attacks, are common across the lifespan. The manual also reports two studies on suicide. In American adolescents aged 15 in public schools, poor reading ability was associated with suicidal thoughts and behaviour even after allowing for sociodemographic and psychiatric factors. In a population-based study of Canadian adults, the lifetime rate of suicide attempts was higher among those with a specific learning disorder than among those without, even after adjustment for childhood adversity, mental illness, substance use and sociodemographic factors; within the learning disorder group, having witnessed chronic domestic violence between parents and ever having had major depressive disorder were associated with a further rise in risk. Dropping out of school and depressive symptoms raise the risk of poor mental health, while high levels of social and emotional support predict better outcomes.

The human side of these statistics is captured in a quotation that Barlow and Durand reproduce from an adult who had never received special help at school: "I faked my way through school because I was very bright." He described low self-esteem lasting into college and judging himself by his failures, and then a change: once he could use a computer for his writing, his poor handwriting and written expression no longer defined him. Barlow and Durand conclude that the negative outcomes in adulthood can be lessened by the right supports, such as a positive relationship with caring adults and accommodations in further education and at work. For Diya, the most important consequence of a diagnosis may simply be that her teacher stops writing "does not try".

Specific learning disorder rarely travels alone. The three domains commonly co-occur with one another, so that a child with dyslexia often has difficulty with mathematics too, and the disorder co-occurs with ADHD, communication disorders, developmental coordination disorder and autism spectrum disorder, as well as with anxiety and depressive disorders. DSM-5-TR notes that the co-occurrence of specific learning disorder and ADHD is more frequent than would be expected by chance. These comorbid conditions do not rule the diagnosis out, but they make testing harder, because each can independently interfere with learning; that is the subject of Section 3.9.6.

Exam trap

Prevalence: 5 to 15 percent of school-age children (DSM-5-TR); adult prevalence unknown. Males outnumber females about 2 to 1 to 3 to 1, and DSM-5-TR says this is not due to referral bias. Diagnosis usually occurs in primary school, but preschool precursors (late talking, trouble with rhymes and letter names) are common. The disorder is lifelong; it is linked to dropping out, unemployment, lower income, anxiety and a raised risk of suicidal behaviour. Co-occurrence with ADHD is more frequent than chance.

Why some children cannot learn to read: causes and theories

DSM-5-TR opens its account of the disorder with a statement of cause: specific learning disorder is a neurodevelopmental disorder with a biological origin, an interaction of genetic, epigenetic and environmental factors that affects the brain's ability to perceive or process verbal or non-verbal information efficiently and accurately. Talking and walking emerge as the brain matures, but reading, spelling and arithmetic are cultural inventions only a few thousand years old that must be taught explicitly, and the disorder is a failure of the brain to take up that teaching normally.

Start, as Hinshelwood's hereditary cases suggested a century ago, with families. DSM-5-TR reports that specific learning disorder aggregates in families, especially when it affects reading, mathematics and spelling. The relative risk of a learning disorder in reading is about 4 to 8 times higher, and in mathematics about 5 to 10 times higher, in the first-degree relatives of affected people than in the relatives of unaffected people, although the figures depend on whether the parents' difficulties are measured by tests or by self-report. Twin studies give high heritability, above 0.6, for both reading ability and reading disability, in alphabetic and non-alphabetic languages alike, and for most other learning abilities and disabilities. The genetic influences on different learning skills are highly correlated, which suggests that the genes involved in one difficulty are largely the same genes involved in another, and explains why reading and mathematics problems so often travel together. Barlow and Durand note that genes on chromosomes 1, 2, 3, 6, 11, 12, 15 and 18 have all been repeatedly linked to reading difficulties, so here, as with ADHD, there is no single gene. And the environment works with the genes. Both a family history of dyslexia and the parents' own literacy predict reading problems in the children, and Barlow and Durand cite evidence that the home reading habits of a family can significantly affect outcomes, so that reading aloud to a child at risk may lessen the impact of the genetic influence. Diya's father's dislike of reading aloud is therefore two clues at once.

The environmental risks DSM-5-TR lists are mostly early and physical: low socioeconomic status; prenatal or early-life exposure to air pollution, nicotine, flame-retardant chemicals (polybrominated diphenyl ethers and polychlorinated biphenyls), lead or manganese; and preterm birth or very low birth weight. One genetic condition carries an especially high risk: in neurofibromatosis type 1, up to 75 percent of people have a learning disorder.

What is different in the brain? Pinel and Barnes, in their chapter on language, make a methodological point first. A brain that has done far less reading than its peers will differ from theirs because of the reading it has not done, so a difference in a dyslexic brain may be a consequence of the disorder rather than its cause. One solution is to compare dyslexic children with younger, "ability-matched" children who read at the same level, since the two groups have had the same amount of reading practice. In one such study (Hoeft and colleagues, 2007), dyslexic children still showed reduced activity in the left parietal cortex and at the left occipitotemporal junction, where the occipital lobe meets the temporal lobe. Barlow and Durand summarise the wider imaging literature: three areas of the left hemisphere appear to be involved in dyslexia, Broca's area in the frontal lobe (articulation and word analysis), a left parietotemporal area (word analysis, linking letters to sounds) and a left occipitotemporal area (recognising the visual form of words). That last region overlaps the visual word form area, a part of the left fusiform gyrus that Laurent Cohen, Stanislas Dehaene and their colleagues in Paris characterised in 2000 as specialised for recognising strings of letters, the region that lets a skilled reader see a familiar word whole and instantly. In dyslexic readers the left-hemisphere reading network, the temporoparietal route for sounding words out and the occipitotemporal route for fluent recognition, is underactive. For mathematics, a different region, around the intraparietal sulcus of the parietal lobe, appears critical for the sense of number and is implicated in dyscalculia; for written expression, Barlow and Durand report, no specific brain deficit has yet been identified.

Can teaching change that brain? In 2003 Elise Temple, John D. E. Gabrieli and their colleagues, among them Paula Tallal, scanned 20 dyslexic children aged 8 to 12 with functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) while they did a phonological task, before and after a remediation programme focused on auditory processing and oral language. The children's oral language and reading improved, and so did their brains: activity increased in the left temporoparietal cortex and the left inferior frontal gyrus, coming closer to the pattern of normal readers, and the children whose left temporoparietal activity rose most were those whose oral language improved most. Activity also rose in some right-hemisphere regions, which looked like compensation. The finding matters for parents as much as for scientists: good teaching is not a trick for working round a fixed defect; it changes the way the brain processes print (Book 2, Section 10.5.9 makes the same point).

But what exactly is the reading network failing to do? Here one theory dominates. The phonological deficit theory holds that most people with dyslexia have a specific difficulty in representing and manipulating the sound units of speech, the phonemes, and therefore difficulty learning how letters map onto them. Pinel and Barnes report "fairly widespread agreement" that dyslexia results most commonly from a specific disturbance of phonological processing, the representation and comprehension of speech sounds, while noting that some researchers still argue for other mechanisms. Vellutino, Fletcher, Margaret J. Snowling and Scanlon, reviewing four decades of research in 2004, reached the same conclusion: in most cases the cause of specific reading disability is a deficiency in phonological skills, not in visual, semantic or syntactic processing, and supposed deficits in general learning abilities or in low-level sensory processing have weak validity as causes. Think about what reading an alphabetic script demands. To read "cat" you must know that the spoken word is made of three sounds, /k/, /a/ and /t/, and that each letter stands for one of them. If your mental representations of those sounds are fuzzy, the letters have nothing firm to attach to, and every new word must be learned by brute memory.

The phonological deficit shows itself in three kinds of task, all of which can be given to a child who cannot yet read. The first is phonological awareness, the ability to hear and manipulate the sound structure of words: judging whether two words rhyme, clapping the syllables of "ba-na-na", saying which word starts with a different sound, saying "stop" without the /s/. The second is rapid automatised naming: naming a page of familiar colours, objects, letters or digits as fast as possible, which measures how quickly a person can retrieve the spoken labels for visual symbols. The third is verbal short-term memory: repeating a string of digits or a made-up word such as "blonterstaping". Children with dyslexia are typically weak on all three. The theory that phonological awareness causes reading success, rather than simply accompanying it, rests above all on one classic study.

Key study: categorising sounds and learning to read (Bradley and Bryant, 1983)

Question. Children who read well are good at hearing rhymes and sounds in words, but which causes which? Perhaps sound awareness helps children learn to read, or perhaps learning to read makes children aware of sounds. Method. At the University of Oxford, Lynette Bradley and Peter Bryant tested 118 four-year-olds and 285 five-year-olds who could not yet read on a sound categorisation task: the child heard three or four words and picked the odd one out, the word that did not share a sound with the others (as "hat" is the odd one out in "pin, win, hat, tin"). They followed the children for several years and measured their reading and spelling. Then they took a group of children who had been poor at sound categorisation and gave them 40 individual training sessions spread over two years. One group was trained to categorise words by their sounds; a second had the same training and also used plastic letters to see how the shared sounds were spelled; a third, a control group, was trained to sort the same pictures by meaning (a cat and a dog are both animals); and a fourth had no training. Result. The children's sound categorisation before they went to school predicted their reading and spelling years later, even when intelligence and memory were taken into account. In the training study, the children who were trained to categorise sounds and were also shown with plastic letters how the shared sounds were spelled did best, clearly ahead of the controls; training in sounds alone helped less. Conclusion. The ability to categorise sounds is a cause of success in learning to read, not merely a consequence, and training it works best when the sounds are connected to the letters that spell them. Criticism. The training groups were small, and the study did not involve children diagnosed with dyslexia; but its combination of a longitudinal prediction with an experimental test became the model for later research, and its practical message, phonological awareness taught together with letters, is the basis of modern phonics teaching.

Two refinements followed. Maryanne Wolf and Patricia Bowers proposed in 1999 the double-deficit hypothesis: that weak phonological awareness and slow naming speed (on rapid automatised naming) are separable sources of reading failure, so that some children have one deficit, some the other, and those with both, the "double deficit", are the most severely impaired; later tests have given it mixed support. And a decisive test of the phonological theory against its rivals came from a multiple case study by Franck Ramus, Uta Frith and colleagues in London, published in 2003. They gave 16 dyslexic university students and 16 controls a full battery of phonological, auditory, visual and motor tests and then looked at each dyslexic person individually. All 16 had a phonological deficit; 10 also had an auditory deficit, 4 a motor deficit and only 2 a visual (magnocellular) deficit. Five had a phonological deficit and nothing else, showing that a phonological deficit is sufficient on its own to cause a literacy problem. Auditory problems, when present, made the phonological deficit and the literacy problem worse, but neither they nor the motor and visual problems appeared to be the cause.

The rival theories are worth knowing, both for who proposed them and because each has inspired treatments that parents will be offered. The magnocellular theory, associated with the Oxford physiologist John Stein (with Vincent Walsh, 1997), starts from dyslexic readers' frequent complaint that small letters seem to blur and move. It proposes a weakness in the magnocellular part of the visual system, which processes fast-changing information and guides visual attention, and Barlow and Durand illustrate it with a scan in which a brain area that normally responds to moving dots (V5/MT) failed to switch on in dyslexic adults. The cerebellar deficit hypothesis of Roderick I. Nicolson and Angela J. Fawcett (developed with Paul Dean in 2001) proposes that many dyslexic children have a general difficulty in making skills automatic, which depends on the cerebellum, and that this explains why their problems, such as balance and motor skill, are not confined to reading. The rapid auditory processing theory of the American neuroscientist Paula Tallal (born 1947), set out in a 1980 paper on auditory temporal perception, phonics and reading disabilities, holds that some children cannot perceive rapid changes in sound, such as the brief transitions that distinguish /ba/ from /da/, and therefore build poor phoneme representations. Tallal co-founded in 1996 a company producing a computer program, Fast ForWord, that slows and exaggerates speech sounds to train this skill; but a systematic review and meta-analysis of six controlled studies by Strong, Torgerson, Torgerson and Hulme in 2011 found no significant effect of Fast ForWord on any reading or language outcome compared with either active or untreated control groups. Each of these deficits is real in some dyslexic people, and Pinel and Barnes note that attentional, auditory, visual and sensorimotor deficits have all been proposed; but many dyslexic people do not have them, and when they do the deficits do not account for all aspects of the disorder. Table 3.27 compares the theories.

Table 3.27 Theories of dyslexia (and one of dyscalculia).
Theory (main names)Core claimEvidence forEvidence against
Visual memory (Hinshelwood, 1917); incomplete cerebral dominance (Orton, 1920s)Faulty visual memory for words; reversals from mixed dominanceHistorical case seriesReversals are normal in beginners; the problem is linguistic; abandoned
Phonological deficit (mainstream)Poor representation and manipulation of speech sounds, so letter-sound mapping failsWeak phonological awareness, naming and verbal memory; early sound skills predict reading; training with letters helps (Bradley and Bryant, 1983); present in all 16 cases (Ramus, 2003)Does not by itself explain the sensory and motor problems some dyslexic people have
Double deficit (Wolf and Bowers, 1999)Phonological and naming-speed deficits are separate; both together are worstNaming speed predicts reading beyond phonologyMixed support for the subtypes
Magnocellular visual (Stein)Weak fast-processing visual pathway; letters blur and moveSome dyslexic readers show motion-processing deficitsFound in only a minority (2 of 16 in Ramus, 2003)
Cerebellar or automaticity (Nicolson and Fawcett)Skills fail to become automaticBalance and motor weaknesses in some childrenMotor problems, when present, rarely cerebellar (Ramus, 2003)
Rapid auditory processing (Tallal, 1980)Cannot perceive rapid sound changes, so phonemes are fuzzyAuditory deficits in many dyslexic peopleDeficits not specific to rapid sounds or speech; Fast ForWord ineffective (2011 review)
Number sense (Butterworth) for dyscalculiaCore deficit in grasping sets and their numerositySlow subitising and dot comparison; intraparietal sulcus differencesWorking memory and language also contribute in some children

Mathematics has its own leading theory. The British cognitive neuropsychologist Brian Butterworth (born 1944) argued in The Mathematical Brain (1999) that human numerical ability rests on an inherited, specialised mechanism, a "number module", that extracts the numerosity of a set (how many things there are) and represents it abstractly. In 2011, with Sashank Varma and Diana Laurillard, he described developmental dyscalculia in Science as a core deficit in understanding sets and their numerosities, the foundation of all elementary school mathematics. A closely related idea, associated with Dehaene's account of the "number sense", is the approximate number system, the capacity, present in infants and other animals, to judge quantities roughly and to order them along a mental number line, whose imprecision has been proposed as a cause of dyscalculia. One early sign is weak subitising, the ability to see at a glance, without counting, how many objects there are in a small set (up to about four). Children with dyscalculia can subitise fewer objects and take longer even when they are right, and they are slow on tasks as simple as saying which of two sets of dots is larger, the very task DSM-5-TR uses to describe the circumscribed form of the disorder. For Krish, the finger-counting, the slowness with 47 and 74 and the inability to estimate are not failures of effort or memory but signs that the sense of quantity on which arithmetic is built is weak.

Exam trap

The mainstream explanation of dyslexia is the phonological deficit theory: difficulty representing and manipulating speech sounds, shown in phonological awareness, rapid automatised naming and verbal short-term memory. Match the minority theories to their names: magnocellular visual, John Stein; cerebellar or automaticity deficit, Nicolson and Fawcett; rapid auditory processing, Paula Tallal (Fast ForWord); double deficit (phonology plus naming speed), Wolf and Bowers; number sense in dyscalculia, Brian Butterworth. Dyslexia involves the left temporoparietal and occipitotemporal (visual word form) regions; dyscalculia the intraparietal sulcus. Bradley and Bryant (1983) showed that sound categorisation causes reading success.

Not every poor reader has a learning disorder: differential diagnosis

Every year schools refer children to clinics with the same complaint, "poor in studies", and only some of them have a specific learning disorder. The others are failing for other reasons, and some of those reasons are easily mended. A sensible clinician works from the simplest explanations to the most complex, and the word "specific" in the name of the disorder tells you what has to be excluded.

Begin outside the child. DSM-5-TR distinguishes specific learning disorder from normal variations in academic attainment that are due to external factors: lack of educational opportunity, consistently poor teaching, or learning in a second language. The difference is that in specific learning disorder the difficulties persist even though the child has had adequate opportunity, the same teaching as classmates who are learning normally, and competence in the language of instruction. In India this exclusion carries real weight. A child who has changed schools three times, who has missed months of school through illness or migration, who sits at the back of a class of sixty, or who moved from a Hindi-medium to an English-medium school last year may read badly for reasons that have nothing to do with his brain. The first questions are therefore about attendance, class size, changes of school and medium, and whether his classmates under the same teacher are learning.

Then check the senses. Uncorrected problems of vision or hearing are excluded by criterion D, and DSM-5-TR separates learning difficulties caused by neurological or sensory disorders (a childhood stroke, a head injury, hearing or vision impairment) by the presence of abnormal findings on examination. A child who cannot see the blackboard or hear the teacher from the third row will fall behind in everything that depends on them. An eye test and a hearing test cost little and come first. The manual also separates specific learning disorder from the learning problems of a neurocognitive disorder: in specific learning disorder the difficulties are present from the developmental period, even if they become obvious only when demands rise, and there is no marked decline from a former level. A child whose reading was fine until last year and is now deteriorating needs a neurological assessment, not remedial teaching.

Then consider general ability. In intellectual developmental disorder (Section 3.2) the learning difficulties are global, affecting every subject, and come with deficits in adaptive functioning; specific learning disorder occurs in the presence of normal intellectual functioning, which DSM-5-TR puts at an IQ of at least about 70, plus or minus 5. Between the two lies the child whom Indian schools call a slow learner, a child with borderline intellectual functioning (IQ roughly 71 to 84, Section 3.2.4) who is behind in most subjects by a similar amount. The key is the profile: a slow learner is evenly slow; a child with a specific learning disorder, like Diya, is strong in some things (oral science, reasoning, conversation) and weak in a narrow band of skills.

Then the other neurodevelopmental disorders, which are the commonest source of confusion because they so often co-occur with specific learning disorder. ADHD is the most important. DSM-5-TR explains the difference precisely: the poor school performance of ADHD may not reflect a specific difficulty in learning academic skills but rather a difficulty in performing them. A child with ADHD can decode words perfectly well when he is attending; he makes errors because he skips words, loses his place and gives up on long passages, and his performance is uneven across all subjects and from day to day. A child with dyslexia reads the same words wrongly whether he is attending or not. The two often occur together, more often than chance would predict, and if criteria for both are met both diagnoses are given (Section 3.7.5 makes the same distinction from the ADHD side). Developmental coordination disorder causes handwriting problems that are motor in origin (Sections 3.9.3 and 3.10.1). Language disorder (Section 3.4.2) is a problem with spoken language, understanding or producing words and sentences, which often precedes and accompanies reading difficulties but is diagnosed on oral language; a child can have both. And autism spectrum disorder can produce uneven school performance for reasons of social communication and restricted interests rather than a specific academic deficit.

Two conditions often grouped with the learning difficulties in schools are not DSM-5-TR diagnoses at all. Auditory processing disorder (APD), also called central auditory processing disorder, describes difficulty in recognising and interpreting sounds, especially speech sounds, in a person whose ears and peripheral hearing are normal: trouble following speech against background noise, following spoken instructions with several steps, or telling similar sounds apart. It is diagnosed by audiologists with special behavioural tests of auditory processing, and professional bodies such as the American Speech-Language-Hearing Association (in a 2005 technical report) and the British Society of Audiology (in a 2018 position statement) have published definitions and guidance. It is controversial, because a child may do badly on an auditory test for reasons other than poor auditory perception, such as inattention, the demands of the task or limited language ability; some experts therefore insist that the diagnosis requires a problem specific to hearing, and the overlap of APD with language disorder, dyslexia and ADHD is much debated. DSM-5-TR does not list it. The second is non-verbal learning disability, a proposed pattern of weak visuospatial processing, poor motor coordination and difficulty reading social cues alongside good or even advanced verbal skills and rote memory. The idea of "non-verbal disabilities" was described in 1967 and discussed further by Helmer Myklebust in 1975, and the Canadian neuropsychologist Byron P. Rourke tried to give it consistent criteria and a brain model; a review of the literature found the proposed criteria inconsistent, and it is not a DSM diagnosis. Its features overlap with developmental coordination disorder, social communication disorder and autism.

Finally, remember emotion and psychosis. Anxiety, depression, bullying and family conflict can all make a child stop learning, and a child who has failed for years with a learning disorder often becomes anxious or depressed as well, so the two must be disentangled. DSM-5-TR also separates specific learning disorder from the cognitive difficulties of schizophrenia and other psychotic disorders, which show a decline, often rapid, from a previous level; if both conditions are present, both can be diagnosed. Table 3.28 summarises the differential.

Table 3.28 Telling specific learning disorder from its look-alikes.
ConditionWhat the child showsHow it differs from specific learning disorder
Normal variation; poor teaching, absence, change of mediumLow attainmentExplained by lack of opportunity or language; improves with ordinary good teaching (criterion D)
Uncorrected vision or hearing problemFalls behind in what depends on the board or the teacher's voiceAbnormal eye or hearing test; improves with correction
Intellectual developmental disorder (Section 3.2)Behind in all subjects; adaptive deficitsGlobal, not specific; IQ below about 70
Slow learner, borderline intellectual functioning (Section 3.2.4)Evenly behind in most subjectsEven profile, not a narrow deficit
Neurocognitive disorder; brain injuryDecline from a former level; neurological signsLoss of skills rather than failure to acquire them
ADHD (Section 3.7)Careless, uneven work in every subjectDifficulty performing, not learning, the skill; often co-occurs, and both may be diagnosed
Developmental coordination disorder (Section 3.10.1)Slow, illegible handwritingMotor problem; typed or dictated work is fine
Language disorder (Section 3.4.2)Limited spoken vocabulary and grammar, poor understanding of speechOral language impaired; often co-occurs with reading problems
Auditory processing disorderCannot follow speech in noise or long oral instructions, despite normal hearingDiagnosed by audiologists; not in DSM-5-TR; overlaps with language disorder and ADHD
Non-verbal learning disabilityWeak visuospatial, motor and social skills; good verbal skillsProposed pattern (Rourke), not a DSM diagnosis
Anxiety, depression, psychosisStops learning; in psychosis, a declineEmotional or psychotic cause; may follow a learning disorder

Run Diya through the table. Her vision and hearing were tested and are normal. She has attended the same school since kindergarten, and most of her classmates, taught by the same teachers, read fluently. Her intelligence on assessment is in the average range and her oral reasoning is a strength, so she is neither intellectually disabled nor a slow learner. She is attentive and eager in the science quiz and in conversation, so her errors are not the careless slips of ADHD; she misreads the same words whether or not she is concentrating. Her spoken Marathi and English are fluent, and her handwriting, though slow, is legible. Her reading is slow in Marathi as well as in English, and six months of extra reading help at school have made little difference. On standardised achievement testing her word reading, reading fluency and spelling are well below the level expected for her age. She has specific learning disorder with impairment in reading (word reading accuracy and reading rate or fluency) and with impairment in written expression (spelling accuracy): the pattern DSM-5-TR permits you to call dyslexia.

Exam trap

Asked which is not typically considered a learning difficulty, choose ADHD. ADHD harms learning and often co-occurs with a learning disorder, but it is a disorder of attention and self-control, and in DSM-5-TR it is a separate neurodevelopmental disorder; its school problems reflect difficulty performing academic skills, not learning them. Dyslexia (reading), dysgraphia (writing) and dyscalculia (mathematics) are learning difficulties, and auditory processing disorder is grouped with them in educational usage, although DSM-5-TR does not list it. Remember too that the slow learner (borderline intellectual functioning) has an even profile, not a specific deficit.

Help at school and in the clinic

Specific learning disorder is treated by teaching, and Book 2, Section 10.5.9 explains the methods in full: remediation, which teaches the weak skill directly, and accommodation, which changes the conditions of learning and examination so that the disability does not hide what the child knows; phonological awareness training and systematic phonics followed by fluency and comprehension work; Direct Instruction; the multisensory Orton-Gillingham and Fernald methods, widely used but with little rigorous research behind them; and response to intervention. The essence can be put in a sentence. What works is teaching that is explicit, systematic, intensive and early: sounds and letters taught directly and in a planned order, practised daily in small groups or one to one, with progress checked every few weeks, beginning as soon as a child starts to lag rather than after he has failed for years. That is exactly what Bradley and Bryant's training study and Temple's imaging study would predict. In India the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016 directs that examinations be adapted for students with disabilities, naming extra time, a scribe and exemption from second and third language courses, and school boards grant such concessions to students with a certificate of specific learning disability, with details that differ from board to board (Book 2, Sections 10.5.9 and 9.9.8). In the classroom, a child like Krish can be allowed a multiplication chart or a calculator for problems whose point is reasoning rather than recall, and Diya can be given oral tests, reduced copying and audiobooks while her reading is remediated.

What about medicine? Here the answer is short, and it matters, because Indian parents are often offered tablets and syrups for "weak memory" and "poor concentration". There is no drug for specific learning disorder. Barlow and Durand state that biological treatment is typically restricted to those who also have ADHD, for whom a stimulant may help (Section 3.8.6). Even then, be clear about what the stimulant does: it helps a child with co-occurring ADHD to attend to the lesson, to stay in his seat and to finish the page, but it does not teach him to decode. A child with both dyslexia and ADHD who is given methylphenidate becomes a better-behaved poor reader unless he also receives reading teaching, which fits Barlow and Durand's finding that most children who respond to stimulants improve in attention but show little gain in academic skills.

Parents will also meet a market of unproven remedies, and the clinician's job is to recognise them and advise against them gently but clearly. The first is coloured overlays and tinted lenses. In the 1980s the educational psychologist Helen Irlen proposed a condition she called scotopic sensitivity syndrome, now usually called Irlen syndrome, in which reading difficulty was said to stem from a light-based visual processing problem that coloured overlays or lenses could correct. A systematic review of the research on coloured overlays and lenses by Griffiths and colleagues in 2016 examined 51 published items and found that most studies had a high or uncertain risk of bias, that the studies at lower risk of bias gave less support for any benefit, and that the improvements reported were generally small and similar to those produced by a placebo. The second is vision therapy and eye exercises. In 2009 a joint statement by the American Academy of Pediatrics (its Section on Ophthalmology and Council on Children with Disabilities), the American Academy of Ophthalmology, the American Association for Pediatric Ophthalmology and Strabismus and the American Association of Certified Orthoptists stated that most experts regard dyslexia as a language-based disorder, that vision problems are not the cause of primary dyslexia or learning disabilities, that scientific evidence does not support the efficacy of eye exercises, behavioural vision therapy or special tinted filters or lenses for improving long-term educational performance, and that such approaches should not be recommended. A child with a learning difficulty should have his eyes tested, because a correctable problem of vision should be corrected, but correcting it does not treat dyslexia. The third is the computer training programmes based on the rapid auditory processing theory, which, as Section 3.9.5 showed, have not improved reading in controlled trials.

The fourth is the shelf of "brain tonics": memory syrups, herbal extracts, multivitamins and nootropic drugs such as piracetam, often sold to parents of children who are doing badly at school. Pinel and Barnes, reviewing the evidence on so-called smart drugs, conclude that no purported nootropic has been convincingly shown to improve memory; the research is mostly in animals or in elderly people with memory problems, it tends to be of low quality, and the few positive findings are hard to replicate or tiny. No guideline recommends any drug or supplement for dyslexia, dysgraphia or dyscalculia. The real risk of these remedies is not the bottle itself but the months of effective teaching lost while a family waits for it to work. The most useful thing a clinician can give Diya's parents is a clear explanation: that her difficulty is real, specific and common; that it has nothing to do with laziness or intelligence; that it responds to the right teaching; and that her teacher's remark, "does not try", is the one thing in her diary that needs to change.

Exam trap

Specific learning disorder is treated by educational intervention (remediation plus accommodation); there is no medication for the learning disorder itself. Stimulants are used only for co-occurring ADHD, and they improve attention, not decoding. Coloured overlays and tinted lenses (Irlen), eye exercises and behavioural vision therapy are not supported by evidence (2009 joint statement of the American paediatric and ophthalmology bodies), because dyslexia is a language-based, not a visual, disorder. The RPwD Act 2016 names extra time, a scribe and exemption from second and third languages as examination accommodations.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. DSM-5-TR estimates that ADHD occurs in about percent of children worldwide and about 2.5 percent of adults.
  2. Children who are the youngest in their school class are more often diagnosed with and treated for ADHD, a finding called the effect.
  3. The heritability of ADHD given in DSM-5-TR is approximately percent.
  4. Shaw and colleagues (2007) found that the cortex of children with ADHD reaches peak thickness about three years late, most markedly in the cortex.
  5. In Barkley's 1997 model the primary deficit in ADHD is in , which impairs four executive functions.
  6. Atomoxetine is not a stimulant; it is a selective reuptake inhibitor.
  7. Adderall is a mixture of salts, not a form of methylphenidate.
  8. The term "dyslexia" was coined in 1887 by the Stuttgart ophthalmologist .
  9. For the greatest diagnostic certainty, DSM-5-TR suggests achievement scores at least standard deviations below the mean, a standard score of about 78.
  10. The mainstream explanation of dyslexia, supported by Bradley and Bryant's 1983 study, is the deficit theory.

Answers: (1) 7.2, (2) relative-age, (3) 74, (4) prefrontal, (5) behavioural inhibition, (6) noradrenaline (norepinephrine), (7) amphetamine, (8) Rudolf Berlin, (9) 1.5, (10) phonological.

3.10 Motor Disorders: Developmental Coordination, Stereotypies, Tics and Tourette's DisorderMedium yield · 4 Qs

Sit at the back of a Class 3 room in any Indian school for one morning and you may see the whole of this section. One boy cannot cut along a line with his scissors, drops the ball every time it is thrown to him at break, and writes so slowly and so laboriously that he is still on his second sentence when the others have finished the page. A small girl near the front flaps both hands rapidly beside her face whenever the teacher brings out the picture cards, and stops the moment her name is called. And a boy by the window blinks hard, twice, three times, wrinkles his nose, gives a little sniff and jerks one shoulder, until the teacher, who has watched this all term, tells him for the fourth time this week to stop making faces.

All three children have a problem with movement, and in all three it began in the developmental period. That is why DSM-5-TR places their conditions in its neurodevelopmental chapter, under the heading motor disorders. What goes wrong differs. In developmental coordination disorder the child has too little motor skill: coordinated movements are learned late, carried out slowly and performed badly. In stereotypic movement disorder the child has too much repetition: rhythmic, apparently purposeless movements such as rocking, hand flapping or head banging. In the tic disorders, of which Tourette's disorder is the best known, the child makes movements and sounds that are sudden, brief and unwanted, and that often arrive with a strange urge to make them. DSM-IV-TR had scattered these conditions across three headings of its childhood section (motor skills disorder, the tic disorders, and stereotypic movement disorder among the "other disorders of infancy, childhood, or adolescence"); DSM-5 brought them together, and Table 3.1 shows where each one went. This section takes the clumsy child first, then the child who rocks and flaps, and then gives four subsections to tics, because tics and Tourette's disorder are what the papers ask about most.

The clumsy child: developmental coordination disorder

Kabir is eight and in Class 3 at a school in Bengaluru. His mother brings him to a child guidance clinic because his class teacher has written in his diary, for the third time this term, that his handwriting is "untidy and incomplete". The history comes out slowly. Kabir sat and walked a little later than his older sister. He could not pedal a tricycle until well after his friends, and at eight he still cannot button his school shirt without help, so his mother buys him shoes with Velcro straps instead of laces. At meals his rice and dal end up around his plate rather than on it. In cricket at break he is always sent to the deep field, and he still drops almost every catch; the other boys say he runs "like a puppet". Yet Kabir reads well beyond his age, does sums in his head faster than anyone in his class, and is full of ideas. He has also become an expert at avoiding the things he finds hard: he "forgets" his compass box, offers to keep score instead of playing, and takes so long to change for physical education that the lesson is half over. Lately he has started to say that he is "useless at everything". The paediatrician finds normal muscle strength, tone and reflexes, normal vision and no sign of cerebral palsy. On a standardised test of motor skills he scores below the fifth centile for his age in manual dexterity and in ball skills.

Children like Kabir were called clumsy long before anyone thought of them as having a disorder. In 1962 the British neurologist John Walton (1922 to 2016) and two colleagues described a group of them in the journal Brain under the title "Clumsy children: developmental apraxia and agnosia", and over the next thirty years the labels multiplied: developmental dyspraxia, clumsy child syndrome, perceptuo-motor dysfunction, "physically awkward" children and minimal brain dysfunction, among others. DSM-III-R (1987) introduced the name developmental coordination disorder (DCD), and in 1994 an international consensus meeting of researchers in London, Ontario, adopted it as the common term so that doctors, therapists and teachers would at last be talking about the same children. ICD-10 calls the condition specific developmental disorder of motor function (F82) and ICD-11 calls it developmental motor coordination disorder (6A04). DSM-5-TR itself lists childhood dyspraxia, specific developmental disorder of motor function and clumsy child syndrome as other names. In Britain the everyday word is still dyspraxia, and it is the word you are most likely to meet in an exam option, although the European Academy of Childhood Disability's international recommendations of 2019 advise against it. In Sweden and the other Nordic countries, the child psychiatrist Christopher Gillberg described a related concept, DAMP, deficits in attention, motor control and perception, which combined the features of ADHD and DCD in one diagnosis; it is now seldom used outside a few Scandinavian centres.

DSM-5-TR's criteria (code F82) have four parts, and each rules something out.

  • Criterion A: skill well below what is expected. The acquisition and execution of coordinated motor skills is substantially below what would be expected for the child's chronological age and opportunity for learning and using the skill. The difficulty shows in two ways: as clumsiness (dropping things, bumping into furniture and people) and as slowness and inaccuracy in motor tasks such as catching a ball, using scissors or cutlery, handwriting, riding a bicycle or taking part in sport. The words about opportunity matter: a child who has never been given scissors or a bicycle cannot be judged on them.
  • Criterion B: it matters in daily life. The motor deficit significantly and persistently interferes with the activities of daily living expected at that age (self-care such as dressing and eating), with productivity at school, with prevocational and vocational activities, and with leisure and play. A low score on a motor test is therefore not, by itself, a diagnosis; many slightly awkward children manage their lives perfectly well.
  • Criterion C: onset in the early developmental period. The child never acquired these skills normally. A child or adult who loses skills he once had, after a head injury, a stroke or an illness, has an acquired disorder, which is a different thing entirely (Table 3.29).
  • Criterion D: not better explained by something else. The difficulties are not better explained by intellectual developmental disorder or by visual impairment, and they are not attributable to a neurological condition affecting movement, such as cerebral palsy, muscular dystrophy or a degenerative disorder. This is why every assessment for DCD must include an examination of vision and a neurological examination. Intellectual disability does not rule DCD out, but if both are present the motor difficulties must be in excess of those expected for the child's mental age; DSM-5-TR sets no IQ cut-off and no discrepancy rule.

How is the diagnosis actually made? DSM-5-TR describes it as a clinical synthesis of four kinds of information: the developmental and medical history, the physical examination, a report from the school (or, for an adult, the workplace), and an individual assessment with psychometrically sound and culturally appropriate standardised tests. The most widely used test is the Movement Assessment Battery for Children, now in its second edition (Movement ABC-2), which measures manual dexterity, aiming and catching, and balance (Table 3.4 lists it with the other tools). The international recommendations of 2019 suggest the 16th centile of its total score as the cut-off and regard a score at or below the 5th centile, as Kabir's was, as unequivocal evidence of a motor problem, provided the other criteria are met. DCD is usually not diagnosed before the age of 5, for three reasons that DSM-5-TR gives: children acquire motor skills at very different ages, so the normal range is wide; measurements in early childhood are unstable and some slow children simply catch up; and other causes of motor delay may not yet have shown themselves. The 2019 recommendations allow a diagnosis below 5 only when the impairment is severe and two assessments at least three months apart agree.

The disorder has no formal subtypes, but some children are impaired mainly in gross motor skills (running, balance, ball games) and others mainly in fine motor skills (buttoning, using cutlery, handwriting). Some show extra, normally suppressed movements, such as small choreiform (dance-like) movements of the outstretched hands or mirror movements, in which the resting hand copies what the active hand is doing. These "overflow" movements are called neurological soft signs: signs of an immature nervous system rather than of a definite lesion, and their value in diagnosis is still unclear. Handwriting deserves special mention because it is where DCD and specific learning disorder meet. Children with DCD often write slowly and illegibly, and their schoolwork suffers. DSM-5-TR separates the two by asking what is wrong with the writing: in DCD the problem is the motor act itself (forming the letters, keeping them on the line, writing at speed), whereas in specific learning disorder with impairment in written expression the problem lies in spelling, grammar, punctuation and the organisation of ideas (Section 3.9.3). Kabir's sentences are well spelled and well constructed; they are simply slow, cramped and painful to produce.

Indian context

DSM-5-TR warns that "activities of daily living" differ between cultures, and that motor development itself runs faster or slower than United States norms depending on how much independent movement caregivers expect and allow. Two Indian examples make the point. Many families eat with their hands, so a child's skill with a knife and fork, one of DSM-5-TR's examples, says nothing unless he has been taught to use them. And a child from a crowded urban home with no open space, or from severe poverty, may never have had a bicycle or a ball to practise with. The phrase "opportunity for skill learning and use" in criterion A is there for exactly these children: lack of practice is not a disorder. DSM-5-TR adds that the higher rates reported in some low- and middle-income countries may partly reflect the effect of disadvantage on motor development.

How common is the disorder? DSM-5-TR gives 5 to 8 percent of children aged 5 to 11 across countries, and 7 to 8 percent in Canada, Sweden and Taiwan; in the United Kingdom about 1.8 percent of 7-year-olds have severe DCD and a further 3 percent probable DCD. Boys are affected more often than girls, by between 2 to 1 and 7 to 1. The onset is in early childhood. Delayed motor milestones (sitting, crawling, walking) may be the first sign, although many children with DCD reach these milestones on time; more often the problem is noticed when the child tries to hold a spoon, button clothes or play ball games, and in middle childhood when puzzles, models, ball games and handwriting expose it. The course is variable but stable over a year or two, and in an estimated 50 to 70 percent of children the coordination problems continue through adolescence. Adults with DCD have continuing difficulty learning new complex motor skills such as driving or using tools, and slow handwriting can hold them back at work.

DCD rarely travels alone. DSM-5-TR lists as common companions the communication disorders, specific learning disorder (especially of reading and writing), autism spectrum disorder, disruptive and emotional problems, joint hypermobility syndrome and, most often of all, ADHD, with about 50 percent co-occurrence. That overlap raises a practical question in the clinic. Children with ADHD fall over, bump into things and knock over cups because they are distracted and impulsive, not because they cannot coordinate; children with autism may do badly in ball games because they have no interest in them. Careful observation across contexts tells these apart, and when the criteria for both disorders are truly met, both diagnoses are given. Children who have ADHD and DCD together are more impaired than children with ADHD alone. The consequences of DCD reach well beyond motor skill: DSM-5-TR lists reduced participation in team games and sport, poor self-esteem, emotional and behavioural problems, weaker academic achievement, poor physical fitness, low physical activity, obesity and a poorer quality of life. Kabir's "I am useless at everything" is typical, and it is often the secondary unhappiness, not the clumsiness, that brings a family to the clinic.

What causes it? DSM-5-TR names three environmental risk factors: prematurity, low birth weight and prenatal exposure to alcohol. On the biological side it points to impairments in visual-motor skill, including visual-motor perception and spatial mentalising (holding and turning spatial information over in the mind), and to possible cerebellar dysfunction, which would explain why the problems grow as movements become more complex and need rapid adjustment. One influential account is the internal modelling deficit hypothesis. When you reach for a cup of chai, your brain does not wait for your eyes to report where your hand has gone; it predicts the result of the movement in advance from an internal model of your body and corrects the movement on the fly. The hypothesis holds that children with DCD form these predictions poorly and must depend on slow visual feedback, so their movements are slow, jerky and inaccurate, especially when speed or precision is needed. The 2019 international review concluded that the hypothesis holds reasonably well across many kinds of movement, but the precise neural basis of DCD remains unclear. Because DCD so often co-occurs with ADHD, specific learning disorder and autism, shared genetic influences have been proposed, although twin studies find consistent co-occurrence only in severe cases.

Treatment is not a medicine. The 2019 recommendations, based on the trial evidence, favour activity-oriented approaches, also called task-oriented approaches: the child practises the very skills he needs, in meaningful settings, usually with an occupational therapist or a physiotherapist. Three are best studied. Task-specific training teaches the skill itself, step by step, with plenty of practice. Neuromotor task training, developed in the Netherlands, analyses where in a task the child's movement breaks down and trains that component within the task, and it helps both gross and fine motor problems. The Cognitive Orientation to daily Occupational Performance (CO-OP) approach asks the child to choose his own goals (riding a bicycle, tying laces, writing faster) and teaches him a general problem-solving strategy, "Goal, Plan, Do, Check", with which he discovers for himself the plan that works. Methods aimed at supposed underlying processes rather than at real tasks, such as sensory integration therapy and kinaesthetic training, have not shown convincing benefit and are not recommended.
