Methylphenidate is not a treatment for DCD, though in children who also have ADHD it can improve some aspects of motor performance when it is combined with other help (Section 3.8.6 teaches the drug). At school, simple accommodations go a long way: a keyboard instead of handwriting, extra time in examinations, fewer copying tasks, sports in which he can succeed. Kabir began weekly sessions with an occupational therapist working on his three goals (buttoning his shirt, catching a tennis ball and writing a sentence in under a minute), and his teacher stopped marking the neatness of his handwriting.

That leaves the confusion the papers exploit. "Dyspraxia" sounds like "apraxia", and both sound like the other neuropsychological "a-" words, agnosia and anomia. The difference is not the prefix. Dyspraxia (DCD) is developmental: the child never acquired coordinated movement normally. Apraxia, agnosia and anomia are acquired: an adult (or child) who once had the ability loses it after damage to the brain, usually a stroke, a tumour, a head injury or a dementia. The German neurologist Hugo Liepmann (1863 to 1925) gave the classic account of apraxia in 1900, from his study of a 48-year-old imperial civil servant, a Regierungsrat, who could no longer carry out many purposeful movements on request although he was not paralysed, could feel normally and understood perfectly well what he was asked to do. Liepmann distinguished ideomotor apraxia (the idea of the act is intact but cannot be turned into the right movement, so the patient cannot show how to wave goodbye or use a comb when asked), ideational apraxia (the plan or concept of the act is lost, so the patient muddles the sequence of a multi-step task or uses a toothbrush as if it were a comb) and limb-kinetic apraxia (a loss of fine, precise finger movements). The term agnosia, for a failure to recognise objects, faces or sounds despite intact sensation, was coined by Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) in 1891, in his monograph On Aphasia, written years before psychoanalysis. And anomia is an acquired difficulty in finding the names of things, the core of anomic aphasia (the aphasias as signs are described in Section 2.4.6). Table 3.29 adds two more look-alikes that DCD's criterion D excludes, ataxia and cerebral palsy. The acquired syndromes themselves, and the brain damage behind them, are taught in the Physiological book.

Table 3.29 Dyspraxia (developmental coordination disorder) and the conditions it is confused with.
ConditionWhat is wrongDevelopmental or acquiredTypical example
Dyspraxia (developmental coordination disorder)Coordinated motor skills learned late and performed slowly and inaccuratelyDevelopmental; strength, sensation and neurological examination normalAn 8-year-old who drops catches, cannot button his shirt and writes slowly
Apraxia (Liepmann, 1900)Loss of learned, purposeful movements despite intact strength, sensation and comprehensionAcquired, usually after left parietal or frontal damageAfter a stroke, a man cannot show how to use a comb when asked
Agnosia (Freud, 1891)Failure to recognise objects, faces or sounds despite intact sensationAcquiredA woman sees a key clearly but cannot say what it is until she touches it
AnomiaDifficulty naming objects and finding words; the core of anomic aphasiaAcquired"It's the thing you write with..." for a pen
AtaxiaIncoordination from damage to the cerebellum or to the sensory (position sense) pathwaysAcquired or inherited; abnormal neurological signsWide-based, unsteady gait after alcohol damage to the cerebellum
Cerebral palsyNon-progressive disorder of movement and posture from injury to the developing brainEarly brain injury; abnormal tone and reflexes; excluded by DCD criterion DA child born very prematurely with stiff legs and scissoring gait
Exam trap

"A neurological disorder characterised by impairments in motor performance and coordination" (Amity 2023) is dyspraxia, that is, developmental coordination disorder: a developmental failure to acquire coordinated movement. Apraxia is the acquired loss of skilled, purposeful movement (Liepmann, 1900) in a person who once had it, agnosia is an acquired failure of recognition (Freud's term, 1891) and anomia an acquired failure of naming. The question turns on "developmental versus acquired" and on "action versus recognition versus naming", not on the prefix. Two smaller traps: DCD is not diagnosed if cerebral palsy, muscular dystrophy or visual impairment explains the clumsiness; and handwriting difficulty from a motor cause points to DCD, whereas poor spelling, grammar and organisation in writing points to specific learning disorder.

Movements without a purpose: stereotypic movement disorder

Ira is five and goes to a nursery school in Chennai. Whenever something exciting happens (her favourite cartoon begins, the birthday cake arrives, she is waiting for her turn on the swing) she stands still, holds both arms bent at the elbow and flaps her hands rapidly beside her shoulders for ten or twenty seconds, often with her mouth stretched open. It has happened many times a day since before her second birthday. If her mother calls her name or touches her arm she stops at once, smiles and carries on with what she was doing. Ira talks fluently, looks at people when she speaks to them, plays elaborate games of pretend with her cousins and has a best friend at school. Her parents have read about autism on the internet and are frightened. The developmental paediatrician who examines her finds no sign of autism, intellectual disability or any neurological disorder, and tells them that what they are seeing is a stereotypy: a repetitive, rhythmic, patterned movement that serves no obvious purpose.

Compare a second child. A 9-year-old boy with severe intellectual disability, who lives in a residential home, bangs his forehead against the wall or the floor many times a day, hard enough to have raised a thick callus, and bites the back of his hand until it bleeds. Staff have had to fit him with a padded helmet and must watch him almost constantly. Both children show stereotypies. Only one of them clearly has a disorder, and the reasons why are written into the criteria.

DSM-5-TR's stereotypic movement disorder (code F98.4) is defined by four criteria. Criterion A is repetitive, seemingly driven and apparently purposeless motor behaviour, with examples such as hand shaking or waving, body rocking, head banging, self-biting and hitting one's own body. Two phrases in it are carefully chosen. "Seemingly driven" means that the child appears compelled to make the movement, although, unlike a compulsion, there is no obsession behind it. "Apparently purposeless" admits that the movement may in fact serve the child in some way: DSM-5-TR notes that stereotypies may, for example, reduce anxiety in response to stress, and many children plainly enjoy them. Criterion B requires that the behaviour interferes with social, academic or other activities, and adds that it may result in self-injury. Criterion C requires onset in the early developmental period. Criterion D excludes repetitive behaviour caused by the physiological effects of a substance or by a neurological condition, and behaviour better explained by another neurodevelopmental or mental disorder, DSM-5-TR's examples being trichotillomania (hair-pulling disorder) and obsessive-compulsive disorder.

Three specifiers complete the diagnosis. The first is with self-injurious behaviour (or behaviour that would cause injury if preventive measures were not used) or without self-injurious behaviour. The second is associated with a known genetic or other medical condition, neurodevelopmental disorder or environmental factor, with examples such as Lesch-Nyhan syndrome, intellectual developmental disorder and intrauterine alcohol exposure; the clinician then records, for example, "stereotypic movement disorder associated with Lesch-Nyhan syndrome" and codes the associated condition separately. The third is current severity, which is defined not by how often the movements occur but by what it takes to keep the child safe:

  • Mild: the symptoms are easily suppressed by a sensory stimulus or by distraction.
  • Moderate: the symptoms require explicit protective measures and behavioural modification.
  • Severe: continuous monitoring and protective measures are needed to prevent serious injury.

Apply the criteria to the two children. Ira's flapping is real and repetitive, but it does not interfere with her play, her friendships or her learning, it causes her no distress and it stops the moment she is distracted. DSM-5-TR says plainly that in such typically developing children, whose daily routine is rarely affected, the diagnosis is not appropriate; if teasing at school or the disruption of lessons ever made the movements a real problem, she would qualify, and the severity would be mild. The boy in the residential home meets every criterion, and his diagnosis is recorded as stereotypic movement disorder, with self-injurious behaviour, associated with severe intellectual developmental disorder, severe.

What do stereotypies look like? DSM-5-TR stresses that each child has his or her own individually patterned, "signature" behaviour. Non-injurious stereotypies include body rocking, bilateral hand flapping or rotating, flicking or fluttering the fingers in front of the face, arm waving and head nodding, often with mouth stretching alongside the arm movements; several may be combined, as in a child who cocks his head, rocks his body and waves a piece of string in front of his face all at once. Self-injurious stereotypies include head banging, face slapping, eye poking and biting of the hands, lips or other body parts. Eye poking is particularly worrying and is more common in children with visual impairment; the rocking, eye pressing and hand flapping of some blind children were once called "blindisms". The injuries range from mild bruising or reddening of the skin to lacerations, the loss of fingers and, from repeated head banging, retinal detachment. A movement may last a few seconds or several minutes, and may occur many times a day or only every few weeks; it appears when the child is engrossed in something else, excited, stressed, tired or bored. Some children restrain themselves, sitting on their hands, wrapping their arms in their clothes or seeking out a protective device.

Simple stereotypies such as rocking are very common in ordinary young children: DSM-5-TR quotes 5 to 19 percent in British and American samples. Complex stereotypies like Ira's are much rarer, at about 3 to 4 percent. In people with intellectual disability in high-income countries, between 4 and 16 percent show stereotypy and self-injury, the risk rising with the severity of the disability, and among people with intellectual disability living in residential facilities 10 to 15 percent may have stereotypic movement disorder with self-injury. Stereotypies typically begin within the first three years of life. Of the children who develop complex motor stereotypies, about 80 percent show them before 24 months, 12 percent between 24 and 35 months and 8 percent at 36 months or later. In most typically developing children the movements become less frequent and less severe with time, whereas in people with intellectual disability self-injurious stereotypies may persist for years, changing in form. DSM-5-TR adds a practical warning: a stereotypy in a child of one to three may be the first visible sign of an undetected neurodevelopmental problem, so it deserves a proper developmental assessment.

Why do some children develop them? DSM-5-TR lists several kinds of risk. In the environment, social isolation encourages self-stimulation that may progress to stereotypies with self-injury, and an environment with too little stimulation raises the risk; this is one reason stereotypies were so common among children reared in understimulating institutions (Section 3.3.6 describes the Romanian orphanages). Stress and fear increase the frequency of the movements. Genetically, the disorder seems somewhat heritable, since a family history of motor stereotypies is common. In the brain, children with stereotypies have been found to have a significantly reduced volume of the putamen, a part of the basal ganglia, which points to the cortico-striatal circuits for habitual behaviour (running from the premotor cortex to the back of the putamen) as the likely site. Lower cognitive functioning carries a greater risk and a poorer response to treatment, and stereotypies are most frequent in moderate, severe and profound intellectual disability. Some genetic syndromes have their own characteristic stereotypies, a behavioural phenotype: in Lesch-Nyhan syndrome, dystonic movements together with biting of the fingers and lips severe enough to mutilate them unless the child is restrained; in Rett syndrome and Cornelia de Lange syndrome, self-injury that grows out of hand-to-mouth stereotypies; fragile X and Smith-Magenis syndromes also show them (the syndromes as causes of intellectual disability are taught in Section 3.3). Finally, a stereotypy can be a response to pain. DSM-5-TR names middle ear infection, dental problems and gastro-oesophageal reflux.

Clinical link

A child who cannot speak cannot tell you that his ear hurts. When a non-verbal child with autism or severe intellectual disability suddenly starts banging the side of his head, or a long-standing stereotypy suddenly worsens, the first step is not a behaviour plan but a medical examination: ears, teeth, constipation, reflux, a fracture, an infection. Behavioural clinicians begin with a functional analysis (Book 2, Section 2.8.2), and "it relieves pain" is one of the functions it must consider.

Stereotypies have to be told apart from several other repetitive movements, and DSM-5-TR's differential diagnosis is worth learning point by point. Normal development comes first: simple stereotypies are common in infancy, and rocking during the transition between sleep and waking usually resolves with age. Autism spectrum disorder includes stereotyped movements in its criterion B (Section 3.5.4), and a stereotypy may be its presenting sign, so every child referred for repetitive movements needs to be examined for the social and communication deficits of autism, which are absent in stereotypic movement disorder. When autism is present, stereotypic movement disorder is diagnosed as well only when there is self-injury or when the stereotypies are severe enough to become a focus of treatment in their own right. Tics are the commonest confusion, and DSM-5-TR lists the differences: stereotypies begin earlier (usually before 3, against a mean age at onset of 4 to 6 for tics); they are fixed and consistent in pattern, whereas tics change their form over time; they involve the arms, hands or whole body, whereas tics most often involve the eyes, face, head and shoulders; they are more rhythmic and last longer, whereas tics are brief, rapid, random and fluctuating; they are ego-syntonic (children enjoy them), whereas tics are usually ego-dystonic; and they lack the premonitory urge that precedes many tics. A caution about distraction: stereotypies usually stop when the child is distracted, for example by hearing his name called or being touched, but DSM-5-TR also notes that tics, too, are reduced by distraction, so distraction is a weaker clue than age, rhythm and the urge.

Obsessive-compulsive disorder differs in having obsessions: the compulsion is performed in response to an intrusive thought or a rigid rule, whereas a stereotypy has no thought behind it (Section 6.5). Trichotillomania and excoriation disorder are body-focused repetitive behaviours that may look driven but are not purposeless (they are aimed at hair or skin), are not usually rhythmic, and begin around puberty or later rather than in early childhood (Section 6.6.5). Other neurological and medical conditions must be excluded by history and examination: habits, mannerisms, paroxysmal dyskinesias, benign hereditary chorea, myoclonus, dystonia and chorea; tardive dyskinesia is recognised by a history of long-term antipsychotic treatment and its characteristic movements of the mouth and face (Section 4.10.4). Repetitive skin picking or scratching during amphetamine intoxication is a substance-induced obsessive-compulsive and related disorder, not stereotypic movement disorder. And functional (conversion) stereotypies are suggested by a sudden onset, distractibility, a pattern that keeps changing with unexplained improvement or worsening, and other symptoms of functional neurological symptom disorder (Section 7.8). The descriptive meaning of stereotypy, set beside mannerisms, tics, compulsions and perseveration, is taught in Section 2.9.6, whose Table 2.25 and sorting tree (Figure 2.14) sort the repeated acts; here they become a diagnosis. Children with chronic motor stereotypies often have co-occurring ADHD, motor coordination problems, tics or Tourette's disorder, and anxiety.

Treatment depends on severity. For a typically developing child like Ira, explanation and reassurance are usually all that is needed, together with the knowledge that the movements tend to fade. When stereotypies interfere with learning or friendships, behavioural methods are used: awareness training and a competing response, as in habit reversal (Book 2, Section 4.9.1), and differential reinforcement of other behaviour, in which the child is rewarded for intervals without the stereotypy (Book 2, Section 4.6.3). For children with intellectual disability or autism, enriching an understimulating environment, teaching other ways to obtain sensory stimulation or to communicate needs, and treating any painful condition come first. Self-injury that endangers the child calls for protective equipment such as a helmet for head banging or padded splints, applied in a way that does not simply add restraint to deprivation. Medicines are a last resort, used only for severe self-injury when other measures have failed: antipsychotics such as risperidone are the drugs most often used (Section 3.2.7 summarises their evidence for challenging behaviour in intellectual disability), and naltrexone, which blocks opioid receptors, has been tried on the theory that self-injury releases the body's own opioids, with mixed results.

Exam trap

Stereotypic movement disorder means repetitive, seemingly driven, apparently purposeless movement (rocking, hand flapping, head banging, self-biting) with onset in the early developmental period, specified with or without self-injurious behaviour. Three confusions recur. A stereotypy is rhythmic, fixed, often enjoyed, begins before 3 and has no premonitory urge; a tic is sudden, brief, non-rhythmic, begins at 4 to 6, changes over time and is often preceded by an urge. A compulsion answers an obsession. And in a child with autism, the stereotypies are part of the autism; stereotypic movement disorder is added only when there is self-injury or the movements are severe enough to need treatment themselves. Severity is graded by the protective measures needed (mild, easily suppressed by distraction; moderate, protective measures and behaviour modification; severe, continuous monitoring), not by IQ or frequency.

What a tic is, and what it is not

Yash is twelve and in Class 7 in Pune. His parents have brought a notebook to the clinic in which they have written down, over six years, every new thing he has done. At 6 it was blinking: hard, deliberate-looking blinks, several in a row, so frequent that they took him to an eye doctor, who found nothing wrong. The blinking faded over a few months and was replaced by nose wrinkling and a grimace that pulled one corner of his mouth sideways. At 8 came a sniff and a throat clear, and he was treated for months for an allergy that never seemed to get better. At 9 he began to shrug his right shoulder and jerk his head to the left, and at 10 a sharp, high-pitched "eek" appeared, followed later by a grunt like a short bark. The worst year was the year he turned eleven, in which his head jerked so often that his neck ached. His grandmother, convinced that someone had cast an evil eye on him, tied a black thread round his wrist. His class teacher sent him out of the room for "mimicking" her, because when she cleared her throat he cleared his straight afterwards. And yet the notebook also shows patterns. The movements are worst after school, when he is tired, when relatives visit and during an exciting cricket match on television; they almost vanish when he practises the tabla or builds models, and he hardly ticced at all during a week-long holiday at his uncle's farm. When the psychologist asks what happens just before a head jerk, Yash thinks for a while and says: "It's like a sneeze that has to come. There's a pressure in my neck, and it goes away when I do it. I can hold it in class for a bit, but then it comes out double at break."

Yash has tics, and almost everything a clinician needs to know about tics is in that notebook. DSM-5-TR defines a tic as "a sudden, rapid, recurrent, nonrhythmic motor movement or vocalization". Each word excludes something. Sudden and rapid separate a tic from the slow, sustained postures of dystonia and from the continuous restlessness of akathisia. Recurrent means the same movement comes back again and again, which separates a tic from a single startle or a one-off gesture. Nonrhythmic separates it from tremor, which beats regularly like a metronome, and from the rhythmic rocking and flapping of stereotypies. And motor movement or vocalization tells you that a tic can be heard as well as seen. Older definitions, including DSM-IV-TR's, also called tics "stereotyped"; DSM-5 removed that word from the definition in its criteria (the introduction to the DSM-5-TR chapter still uses it), which helps keep tics apart from stereotypies. DSM-5-TR adds that some motor tics are slower twisting or tightening movements, so speed is typical rather than absolute, and that although tics can involve almost any muscle group or sound, certain tics, eye blinking and throat clearing above all, are common to almost everyone who has them. A person's tics change over time, as Yash's did, but at any one time the current repertoire recurs in a characteristic way.

Tics are classified along two lines, motor or vocal, and simple or complex, which gives the four kinds in Table 3.30. A simple motor tic involves a limited group of muscles and is brief: an eye blink, a facial grimace, a shoulder shrug, a jerk of the head, an extension of an arm or leg. A simple vocal tic is a meaningless sound, often produced by contraction of the diaphragm or of the muscles of the throat: throat clearing, sniffing, chirping, barking, grunting, coughing. A complex motor tic lasts longer and often combines simple tics (turning the head while shrugging the shoulder, for example), and it can look purposeful: touching objects or people, jumping, squatting, twirling. Complex motor tics include echopraxia, the imitation of someone else's movements, and copropraxia, the making of obscene or taboo gestures. A complex vocal tic has linguistic meaning, a word or part of a word: palilalia is repeating one's own sounds or words, echolalia is repeating the last word or phrase someone else has said, and coprolalia is uttering socially unacceptable words, including obscenities and ethnic, racial or religious slurs. DSM-5-TR makes a point about coprolalia that clinicians find useful: it comes out as an abrupt, sharp bark or grunt and lacks the prosody, the natural melody and emphasis, of the same words spoken in anger. The words are the person's, but the delivery is the tic's. Echolalia, palilalia and the other disorders of speech as signs are described in Section 2.4.6, and tics among the other abnormal movements in Section 2.9.6.

Table 3.30 The four kinds of tic, with examples.
KindMotor ticsVocal tics
Simple: brief, few muscles, no meaningEye blinking, grimacing, nose wrinkling, shoulder shrugging, head jerking, extending a limbThroat clearing, sniffing, coughing, grunting, chirping, barking, squeaking
Complex: longer, combined, may look purposeful or have meaningTouching, tapping, jumping, squatting, twirling, combined head and shoulder movements; echopraxia (copying others' movements); copropraxia (obscene gestures)Words or parts of words; palilalia (repeating one's own words); echolalia (repeating others' words); coprolalia (obscene or taboo words, said with an abrupt bark)

The most important thing about a tic is its strange relation to the will. Most people with tics, including young children, describe a premonitory urge: a localised, uncomfortable bodily sensation before the tic (an itch, pressure, tension, a tingling or a feeling that something is "not right" in the part of the body that is about to move), together with an urge to move. Yash's "pressure in my neck" is a textbook description, though DSM-5-TR notes that many people find these feelings hard to put into words. The tic relieves the sensation, and a feeling of relief and reduced tension follows. Sometimes the person feels he must repeat a tic, or perform it in a particular way, until it feels "just right". Because the urge can be resisted for a while, tics are not completely involuntary: DSM-5-TR says that tics are generally experienced as involuntary but that some can be voluntarily suppressed for varying lengths of time. Holding them back takes effort and attention, which is why children who spend a lesson suppressing their tics often learn very little in it. In a survey of 170 patients with different movement disorders at a Toronto clinic, published in 1991, the neurologist Anthony E. Lang found that most people with tics described them as at least partly intentional, something they did in response to the urge, whereas people with other movement disorders described their movements as entirely involuntary. For this reason tics are often called semi-voluntary, or even "unvoluntary", a word coined for acts that are neither fully willed nor fully automatic. The behavioural model of the urge, in which each tic is negatively reinforced by the relief it brings, is taught with the treatment it led to in Book 2, Section 4.9.2.

Tics are also strongly influenced by their surroundings. DSM-5-TR states that tics are increased by anxiety, excitement and exhaustion and are better during calm, focused activities; many people tic less when a task demands focused attention and motor control, which is why Yash ticced least at the tabla, and stressful or exciting events, such as an examination, make them worse. Talking about tics can trigger them (as every clinician who asks about a child's tics soon sees). And seeing a gesture or hearing a sound may be followed by a similar tic, the echo phenomenon behind Yash's throat clearing, which others may wrongly take as deliberate mockery. DSM-5-TR singles out this misunderstanding as a particular problem with authority figures who know little about tics: teachers, supervisors and the police. Finally, tics wax and wane: they come and go in bouts over days and weeks, vary in severity over months, and change their location and form over the years, so that a child who blinked at 6 may shrug at 9 and grunt at 10. A tic that disappears is often replaced by another.

Remember

A tic is sudden, rapid, recurrent and non-rhythmic, motor or vocal, simple or complex. It is usually preceded by a premonitory urge, can be suppressed for a while (so it is semi-voluntary rather than fully involuntary), is followed by relief, waxes and wanes and changes its form over time, gets worse with anxiety, excitement and fatigue and better during calm, focused activity. Consciousness is never affected.

Many other things can make a body move suddenly or repeatedly, and Table 3.31 sets them out. Four of them appear as options in exam questions. A seizure is the result of an abnormal, excessive and synchronised electrical discharge of neurons in the brain. A focal motor seizure can produce jerking of one limb, and an absence seizure, with its brief stare and fluttering eyelids, is sometimes mistaken for blinking tics; but seizures cannot be held back by an effort of will, are not preceded by an urge that the movement relieves, often alter or abolish awareness, may be followed by confusion or sleepiness, and show abnormal activity on an electroencephalogram (EEG). Dissociation is not a movement at all but a disruption of the normally integrated functions of consciousness, memory, identity, emotion and perception (Chapter 7); the functional seizures sometimes produced in dissociative states (Section 7.8.5) involve a change in responsiveness that tics never do. The tremor of Parkinson's disease is rhythmic, a regular back-and-forth oscillation that is most obvious at rest (the "pill-rolling" movement of thumb against fingers), and it comes with slowness of movement and muscular rigidity in an older adult (Chapter 10). The answer to "sudden, rapid, purposeless involuntary movements" is the tic precisely because it is sudden rather than rhythmic, brief rather than continuous, and occurs with consciousness intact.

The neurologist's list adds several more, which DSM-5-TR uses in its differential diagnosis. The movements of chorea (from the Greek for dance) are rapid, random, continual, irregular and unpredictable; they flow from one part of the body to another, usually on both sides and involving the face, trunk and limbs, and they worsen during attempted voluntary action; chorea is seen in Sydenham's chorea, the neurological form of rheumatic fever, and in Huntington's disease. A jerk of myoclonus is sudden, shock-like and one-directional, faster than a tic, not suppressible, with no premonitory urge, and it may occur during sleep; hiccups and the jerk that sometimes wakes you as you fall asleep are harmless forms of it. In dystonia the simultaneous, sustained contraction of opposing muscles twists part of the body into an abnormal posture, often triggered by attempts at voluntary movement and absent during sleep; the acute dystonia caused by antipsychotic drugs is described in Section 4.10.4, together with akathisia, the inner restlessness and urge to keep moving that the same drugs can cause, which is continuous and is felt as restlessness rather than as brief, discrete jerks. Paroxysmal dyskinesias are episodes of dystonic or choreic movement, usually brought on by sudden voluntary movement or exertion. Then there are the psychiatric look-alikes. A stereotypy (Section 3.10.2) is rhythmic, fixed and begins earlier. A compulsion is aimed at preventing or reducing anxiety and usually answers an obsession, such as a fear of contamination (Section 6.5); the line can blur, because a person with tics may feel he must touch both shoulders equally, or tic a certain number of times, until it feels "just right", and DSM-5-TR concedes that complex tics and compulsions can be hard to tell apart, especially since they so often occur in the same person. The body-focused repetitive behaviours, persistent hair pulling, skin picking and nail biting, are more goal-directed and complex than tics.

Table 3.31 Is it a tic? Tics and the movements that resemble them.
MovementWhat it looks likeUrge and suppressionOther clues
TicSudden, rapid, non-rhythmic movement or sound; changes form over timeUsually a premonitory urge; briefly suppressible; relief afterOnset 4 to 6; worse with stress and fatigue; awareness intact
SeizureJerking, stiffening, staring; from an abnormal electrical dischargeNo urge; cannot be held backAltered awareness, confusion afterwards, EEG change
DissociationA disruption of consciousness, memory or identity, not a movement as suchNot applicableChanged responsiveness; Chapter 7
Parkinson's tremorRhythmic oscillation, most marked at rest ("pill-rolling")No urge; not suppressibleOlder adult; slowness and rigidity; Chapter 10
ChoreaFlowing, random, continual, irregular movements of face, trunk and limbsNo urge; not suppressibleSydenham's chorea, Huntington's disease
MyoclonusShock-like, one-directional jerk, faster than a ticNo urge; not suppressibleMay occur in sleep; hiccups are a normal form
DystoniaSustained twisting posture from opposing muscles contracting togetherNo urgeTriggered by voluntary movement; absent in sleep; antipsychotics (4.10.4)
AkathisiaContinuous restlessness, pacing, shifting from foot to footFelt as inner restlessnessFollows starting an antipsychotic (4.10.4)
StereotypyRhythmic, fixed, prolonged flapping, rocking or head bangingNo urge; often enjoyed; stops with distractionOnset before 3; arms, hands or whole body (3.10.2)
CompulsionPurposeful-looking act such as washing, checking or orderingDriven by an obsession or a rule; relieves anxietyEgo-dystonic thoughts behind it (6.5)
Functional tic-like behaviourComplex, often rhythmic arm movements and vocalisations; "tic attacks"Urge and suppressibility often absentSudden onset in adolescence or adulthood, mostly in females (7.8)

The last row of Table 3.31 describes something that took movement-disorder clinics by surprise. Riya, a 16-year-old in Delhi, was in her room for most of the second year of the pandemic, studying online for her board examinations and spending her evenings watching short videos on her phone, including many made by young people with Tourette's disorder. Over a single weekend she developed violent, rhythmic throwing movements of her arms, hitting herself in the chest, and loud shouted words, some of them obscene, sometimes in "attacks" lasting more than an hour. She had never had a tic before. From 2020 and 2021 neurologists in several countries reported a sudden rise in referrals of patients like Riya. In 2021 Tamara Pringsheim and her colleagues described rapid-onset functional tic-like behaviours in young people, mostly girls and young women, during the COVID-19 pandemic: compared with patients with Tourette's disorder, they were older at onset (in one small series about 15 years against about 10), had more complex arm and hand movements, more complex vocalisations and more coprolalia, and were more often depressed. In Germany Kirsten R. Müller-Vahl and her colleagues (2022) traced a cluster to a single popular YouTube creator and called it a new form of mass sociogenic illness spread through social media. The features that distinguish these presentations from tics are the late, abrupt onset; movements that often involve the arms, legs and trunk rather than mainly the face, head and shoulders; frequent absence of a premonitory urge and of suppressibility; coprolalia far more often than in Tourette's disorder; and poor response to anti-tic drugs. DSM-5-TR asks that abnormal movements suggestive of tics that first appear outside the usual age range should be evaluated for other movement disorders, functional tic-like movements and vocalisations among them, and notes that "tic attacks" lasting from fifteen minutes to several hours suggest a functional disorder. Such presentations are diagnosed and managed as functional neurological symptom disorder (Section 7.8), not as Tourette's disorder, and anti-tic drugs are not the answer.

Exam trap

"Sudden, rapid, purposeless involuntary movement in the body" (Amity 2023) is a tic. Seizures are abnormal electrical discharges in the brain, cannot be suppressed and often alter awareness; dissociation is a disruption of consciousness, memory or identity, not a movement disorder; Parkinson's disease produces a rhythmic resting tremor with slowness and rigidity in older adults. Two finer points. Tics are better described as semi-voluntary than as fully involuntary, because the premonitory urge can be resisted for a while; if an option says tics "cannot be suppressed at all", it is wrong. And DSM-5 deleted the word "stereotyped" from the definition of a tic in its criteria, so an option describing a rhythmic, stereotyped movement beginning before age 3 points to a stereotypy, not a tic.

The tic disorders and their rules

Having tics is not the same as having a tic disorder, and having a tic disorder is not the same as having Tourette's disorder. DSM-5-TR recognises five tic disorders: Tourette's disorder, persistent (chronic) motor or vocal tic disorder, provisional tic disorder, and the other specified and unspecified tic disorders. All of them are built from the same four questions. What kinds of tic are there, motor, vocal or both (criterion A)? How long has it been since the first tic (criterion B)? How old was the person when the tics began (criterion C)? And is there a known cause, a substance or another medical condition (criterion D)? A fifth rule (criterion E) arranges the disorders in a hierarchy.

Tourette's disorder (F95.2) is the most demanding. Its criterion A requires that both multiple motor and one or more vocal tics have been present at some time during the illness, although not necessarily concurrently. "Multiple" means at least two different motor tics; one vocal tic is enough. "Not necessarily concurrently" means the child need not show both kinds at the same time: Yash's blinking at 6 and his sniffing at 8 both count, even though the blinking had stopped by the time the sniffing began. Criterion B requires that the tics may wax and wane in frequency but have persisted for more than 1 year since the first tic. Criterion C requires onset before the age of 18. Criterion D requires that the disturbance is not attributable to the physiological effects of a substance (DSM-5-TR's example is cocaine) or to another medical condition (its examples are Huntington's disease and postviral encephalitis). Yash meets all four: several motor tics and two vocal tics, six years since the first blink, onset at 6, and no drug or illness to explain them.

The second disorder, persistent (chronic) motor or vocal tic disorder (F95.1), has the same duration, age and exclusion rules, but its criterion A is the opposite of Tourette's: single or multiple motor or vocal tics have been present, but not both. The clinician specifies which: with motor tics only, or with vocal tics only (these are the only tic disorders that need such a specifier). Its criterion E adds that the criteria for Tourette's disorder have never been met. A 14-year-old boy who has blinked and shrugged for three years but has never made a vocal tic has persistent motor tic disorder; a girl who has cleared her throat and sniffed for two years with no motor tic at all has persistent vocal tic disorder.

The third, provisional tic disorder (F95.0), requires single or multiple motor and/or vocal tics that have been present for less than 1 year since the first tic began, with onset before 18 and no substance or medical cause, and its criterion E requires that the criteria for Tourette's disorder or for persistent tic disorder have never been met. It is the commonest tic disorder, because most childhood tics are short-lived. Prisha, aged 7, began blinking hard and making a small grunting noise four months ago, during the weeks after her family moved house; she has provisional tic disorder. If her tics fade by the summer, as they well may, that is the end of the story. If they are still present, on and off, a year after the first blink, her diagnosis will change: because she has had a vocal tic (the grunt) as well as motor tics, it will become Tourette's disorder, provided that by then she has shown at least two different motor tics. The diagnosis is called provisional because, at the time it is made, no one can know which way the tics will go.

Two residual categories remain. Other specified tic disorder (F95.8) is used when tic-like symptoms cause distress or impairment but do not meet the full criteria for any tic disorder and the clinician wants to say why, DSM-5-TR's example being "with onset after age 18 years"; it is also the diagnosis when the history, examination or laboratory results give strong evidence of a plausible, proximal and probable cause, so that criterion D is not met. Unspecified tic disorder (F95.9) is used when the clinician chooses not to give the reason, or there is not enough information to be more specific. Notice a gap that the rules leave: a person with a single motor tic and a vocal tic for more than a year fits neither Tourette's disorder (which needs multiple motor tics) nor persistent tic disorder (which forbids having both kinds), and so receives one of these residual diagnoses.

Why are the rules written this way? The 1-year rule in criterion B, DSM-5-TR explains, ensures that the people given a diagnosis of Tourette's disorder or persistent tic disorder have persistent symptoms, not a passing phase of the kind most children with tics go through. Because tics wax and wane, some people have tic-free intervals of weeks or months, but these do not reset the clock: anyone whose tics began more than a year ago is considered to have persistent symptoms, whatever the gaps. The age rule in criterion C reflects the natural history of tics. They typically begin before puberty, at an average of 4 to 6 years, and the rate of new cases falls in the later teens; a first tic in adulthood is exceedingly rare and is often linked to illicit substances (excessive cocaine use, for example), to an insult to the central nervous system, or to a functional neurological disorder. DSM-5-TR adds a caution, however: it is not unusual for a teenager or adult to come for a first assessment and, when carefully questioned, to describe milder tics going back to childhood, even with tic-free periods of months or years. The hierarchy of criterion E means that a diagnosis, once earned, is never lost by improvement. A man who met the criteria for Tourette's disorder at 12 and has only a single motor tic at 30 does not "drop down" to persistent motor tic disorder: having previously met the criteria for Tourette's disorder rules out the lower diagnosis, and a past persistent tic disorder rules out provisional tic disorder in the same way. Figure 3.15 turns all of this into a decision tree.

The rules have changed more than most students realise, and old textbooks and question banks still carry the earlier versions. Tourette's disorder entered the DSM for the first time in DSM-III (1980), which required an age at onset between 2 and 15 years. DSM-III-R (1987) widened this to onset before 21, and DSM-IV (1994) set the present limit of before 18. DSM-IV also added a requirement that the tics cause marked distress or significant impairment, which critics pointed out would exclude the many people who live well with their tics; DSM-IV-TR (2000) removed it. DSM-IV-TR still required that during the year of tics there had never been a tic-free period of more than 3 consecutive months, a rule DSM-5 (2013) dropped, which is why tic-free gaps no longer reset the clock. DSM-IV-TR's short-lived category was transient tic disorder, defined as tics occurring many times a day, nearly every day, for at least 4 weeks but for no longer than 12 consecutive months. DSM-5 renamed it provisional tic disorder and abolished the 4-week minimum, on the argument that a tic disorder can only be called "transient" in retrospect, once it has gone. And, as Section 3.10.3 noted, DSM-5 deleted "stereotyped" from the definition of a tic. "Chronic motor or vocal tic disorder" became "persistent (chronic) motor or vocal tic disorder", with the old name kept in brackets.

The ICD has its own history. ICD-10 (F95) has three named categories: F95.0 transient tic disorder, whose tics do not persist for longer than 12 months; F95.1 chronic motor or vocal tic disorder, with motor or vocal tics, but not both, lasting more than a year; and F95.2 combined vocal and multiple motor tic disorder, with the alternative name de la Tourette's syndrome, in which there are or have been multiple motor tics and one or more vocal tics, though not necessarily at the same time, with onset almost always in childhood or adolescence. ICD-11 made a bigger change. It classifies the primary tics or tic disorders (8A05.0) in its chapter on diseases of the nervous system, among the movement disorders, rather than in the mental disorders chapter, although Tourette syndrome and the two chronic tic disorders are also cross-listed among the neurodevelopmental disorders, with which they begin and so often co-occur. Its categories are Tourette syndrome (8A05.00), in which both motor and phonic tics have been present for at least a year, though not necessarily at the same time or consistently, with onset during the developmental period; chronic motor tic disorder (8A05.01); chronic phonic tic disorder (8A05.02); and transient motor tics (8A05.03), with other specified and unspecified categories, and a separate code for secondary tics (8A05.1), such as tics that follow an infection or tics associated with another developmental disorder. ICD-11 says "phonic" where DSM-5-TR says "vocal", and it splits the persistent disorder into two named disorders where DSM-5-TR uses one name with a specifier. Table 3.32 sets the three systems side by side, using the codes of Table 3.2.

Exam trap

Tourette's disorder (DSM-5-TR) requires multiple motor tics and one or more vocal tics, present at some time but not necessarily concurrently, persisting for more than 1 year since the first tic, with onset before 18, not caused by a substance or medical condition (MET Mock 4). Each wrong option in that question is a different disorder or a myth. Coprolalia is not required; it occurs in a minority (Section 3.10.5). Vocal tics only for more than a month is not Tourette's: vocal tics alone are a provisional tic disorder if present for less than a year and a persistent vocal tic disorder if present for more than a year. Only motor tics for 6 months is a provisional tic disorder (and would become persistent motor tic disorder after a year). Note also the wording of the keyed option, "at least 1 year": the DSM-5-TR criterion says more than 1 year since the first tic, but the introduction to the same DSM-5-TR chapter and ICD-11 both say at least 1 year, so treat the two wordings as the same rule and mark the keyed option.

Exam trap

The duration for Tourette's disorder and for persistent tic disorder is 1 year, not 1 month, 3 months or 6 months (MET Mock 3). Each distractor has a history: 1 month echoes DSM-IV-TR's 4-week minimum for transient tic disorder, which DSM-5 abolished; 3 months echoes DSM-IV-TR's rule that no tic-free period could last more than 3 consecutive months, also dropped in DSM-5; and 6 months is the duration for ADHD (Section 3.7) and for many other DSM disorders, not for tics. The onset limit is before 18 in DSM-5-TR (it was 2 to 15 in DSM-III and before 21 in DSM-III-R). Older names to recognise: transient tic disorder became provisional tic disorder, and chronic motor or vocal tic disorder became persistent (chronic) motor or vocal tic disorder.

Tourette's disorder: history, course and causes

The first clear description of the disorder came from the same physician you met at the opening of this chapter. In 1825 Jean Marc Gaspard Itard, the doctor who had tried to educate Victor of Aveyron, published a paper on involuntary functions of the organs of movement, grasping and voice, in which he described a French noblewoman known in the literature as the Marquise de Dampierre. From the age of seven she had suffered from involuntary movements together with sudden cries, and later from outbursts of obscene words that she could not control, in a woman of the Parisian aristocracy. According to the later account of her case, she went on ticcing and swearing until her death in her eighties.

Sixty years later her case reappeared in Paris. Georges Gilles de la Tourette (1857 to 1904) was a young neurologist at the Salpêtrière hospital, a pupil and assistant of Jean-Martin Charcot (1825 to 1893), the most famous neurologist in France, whose work on hysteria opens Chapter 7. In 1884 Charcot asked him to study disorders of movement, and in 1885 he published, in the Archives de Neurologie, a study of nine patients with what he called a nervous affliction characterised by motor incoordination accompanied by echolalia and coprolalia. The first of his nine cases was Itard's marquise. He described the features that still define the disorder: an onset in childhood, multiple motor tics, inarticulate cries and, in some patients, echolalia and coprolalia, with a lifelong course. Charcot, as was his habit with his students, attached his pupil's name to the condition, calling it la maladie des tics de Gilles de la Tourette. Gilles de la Tourette's own life was eventful: in 1893 a former patient who believed she had been hypnotised against her will shot him in the back of the head (some accounts say the neck), and he recovered; he later developed what was probably neurosyphilis and died in 1904 in a psychiatric hospital near Lausanne.

For the next seventy years, tics were mostly treated as a psychological problem. Psychoanalysts read them as the expression of unconscious conflict; the Hungarian analyst Sándor Ferenczi (1873 to 1933) published an influential paper, "Psycho-analytical observations on tic", in 1921, and until the early 1970s psychoanalysis was the preferred treatment. Parents were often blamed, and children were often thought to be doing it on purpose. The turning point was pharmacological. In 1961 a French psychiatrist, Seignot, reported that haloperidol, a new antipsychotic drug that blocks dopamine receptors, reduced tics. In 1965 the New York psychiatrist Arthur K. Shapiro treated a patient with haloperidol, and in 1968 he and his wife Elaine Shapiro published "Treatment of Gilles de la Tourette's syndrome with haloperidol" in the British Journal of Psychiatry. A disorder that responded to a dopamine-blocking drug, the Shapiros argued, was a disorder of the brain, not of upbringing. In 1972 families of patients, working with the Shapiros, founded the Tourette Syndrome Association in the United States (renamed the Tourette Association of America in 2015), and in 1980 DSM-III included Tourette's disorder for the first time. The disorder that had been a curiosity of nineteenth-century Paris had become a neurodevelopmental condition.

How does Tourette's disorder unfold over a lifetime? DSM-5-TR gives the course in a few sentences that are worth knowing almost word for word. The first tics typically appear between the ages of 4 and 6, and eye blinking is highly characteristic as the first symptom. The tics wax and wane, spread and change, and vocal tics usually appear some time after the first motor tics, as Yash's did. Severity peaks between 10 and 12 years and declines during adolescence. Many adults have much milder symptoms, but a minority have tics that remain severe or worsen in adulthood. Yash's notebook, from the first blink at 6 to the worst year at 11, follows this course closely. Figure 3.16 draws it as a schematic curve, and the study on which it rests is described in the Key study box.

Key study: the course of tics over the first two decades (Leckman and colleagues, 1998)

Question. What happens to the tics of children with Tourette's disorder as they grow up? Clinic samples give a gloomy picture, because the people who keep coming back are those who stay ill. Method. James F. Leckman and his colleagues at the Yale Child Study Center recontacted a birth-year cohort of 42 patients with Tourette's disorder first seen in their clinic, and obtained year-by-year information on the onset and severity of tics up to the age of 18 for 36 of them. Result. The mean age at onset of tics was 5.6 years. Tics were at their worst at a mean age of 10, and in almost every case the worst period was followed by a steady decline. By 18, nearly half of the cohort was virtually tic-free. About a fifth (8 patients, 22 percent) had reached a severe level of tics at their worst. Conclusion. For most children with Tourette's disorder the tics follow a predictable course, rising through the primary school years, peaking around the age of 10 and declining through adolescence, and parents can be told that improvement is the usual outcome. Criticism. The sample was small and drawn from one specialist clinic, and part of the severity history depended on recall and on clinic records; later studies have confirmed the general shape but found that a minority of adults continue to have troublesome tics.

How common are tics and Tourette's disorder? The answer depends on whether you count passing tics, diagnosed cases or cases found by careful surveys. Tics themselves are common: Barlow and Durand note that up to 20 percent of children show some tics at some point while growing up, and DSM-5-TR says that tics are common in childhood but transient in most cases. A national survey in the United States estimated the prevalence of clinically identified tic disorders at 3 per 1,000, with fewer identified cases among African American and Latinx children, possibly because of differences in access to care, and the prevalence of Tourette's disorder in Canadian school-age children is estimated at 3 to 9 per 1,000. Pinel and Barnes put Tourette's disorder at 0.3 to 1 percent of the population worldwide, and European guidelines give the same range. Males are affected two to four times as often as females, and in an international database of 3,500 patients compiled by Roger D. Freeman and his colleagues (2000) the ratio was 4.3 to 1; there are no sex differences in the kinds of tic, the age at onset or the course, although women with persistent tics may be more prone to anxiety and depression. Tics have been found in children on every continent, and their clinical features, course and causes do not seem to vary with ethnic or cultural background, although background shapes how families understand tics and whether they seek help.

And coprolalia, the symptom everyone associates with Tourette's disorder? It is the exception, not the rule. In a study of 597 consecutive new patients at 15 clinics in seven countries, Freeman and his colleagues (2009) found that about one in five males and one in seven females had ever had coprolalia, and fewer still copropraxia; coprolalia usually began at about 11, some five years after the first tics. Textbooks give figures between about 10 and 20 percent depending on the sample, and all agree on the conclusion: most people with Tourette's disorder never swear involuntarily, and coprolalia is not required for the diagnosis.

Tourette's disorder rarely comes alone, and its companions often matter more than the tics. DSM-5-TR notes that the risk shifts with age. Children before puberty with tic disorders are more likely to have ADHD, obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD) and separation anxiety disorder; teenagers and adults are more vulnerable to mood disorders, anxiety disorders and substance use disorders. ADHD, disruptive behaviour and OCD and related disorders are particularly common, and autism spectrum disorder, specific learning disorder and other movement disorders, including stereotypic movement disorder and Sydenham's chorea, also occur. In Freeman's database of 3,500 patients only about 12 percent had no reported co-occurring condition, ADHD being the commonest; problems with anger control, sleep, coprolalia and self-injury reached high levels mainly in those with co-occurring conditions. The obsessive-compulsive symptoms of people with tics tend to begin earlier and to centre on symmetry and exactness, on "just right" feelings, and on forbidden or taboo thoughts of an aggressive, sexual or religious kind, rather than on contamination; DSM-5-TR gives OCD a tic-related specifier for exactly this group (Section 6.5.5). Yash is restless, impulsive and disorganised at school, and he has to touch both shoulders equally before he can settle to his homework: he will need assessment for ADHD and for OCD as well as for his tics. The most serious companion is suicidality. A Swedish case-cohort study covering 1969 to 2013 found that people with Tourette's disorder or persistent tic disorder had a substantially raised risk of suicide attempts (odds ratio 3.86) and of death by suicide (odds ratio 4.39) compared with matched controls, and the risk, though smaller, remained substantial even after allowing for their other psychiatric conditions; tics persisting beyond young adulthood and a previous suicide attempt were the strongest predictors.

Clinical link

Pinel and Barnes call Tourette's disorder "a disorder of onlookers": much of the suffering comes from how other people react. Many children with mild or moderate tics are not distressed by them at all, and some are hardly aware of them; DSM-5-TR notes that a co-occurring condition such as ADHD or OCD often impairs functioning more than the tics themselves. When tics do cause harm, it is usually through teasing, bullying, punishment at school, conflict at home and the exhausting effort of suppressing them in class. Rarely, forceful tics cause injury, to the eye from hitting the face, or to the discs of the neck from violent head jerks. Understanding families, teachers and friends make an enormous difference: the American goalkeeper Tim Howard, who has Tourette's disorder, kept goal for Manchester United, Everton and the United States national team.

What causes Tourette's disorder? Genes come first. Tics run strongly in families, and DSM-5-TR estimates the heritability of tic disorders at 70 to 85 percent, with no difference between males and females in familial risk or heritability. The classic twin study, by Price, Kidd, Cohen, Pauls and Leckman at Yale in 1985, examined 43 same-sex twin pairs in which at least one twin had Tourette's disorder: the identical twin of an affected child also had Tourette's disorder in 53 percent of pairs, against 8 percent for non-identical twins, and the identical twin had some kind of tic in 77 percent, against 23 percent. The largest family study, by David Mataix-Cols and his colleagues in 2015, used the Swedish national patient register to identify 4,826 people diagnosed with Tourette's disorder or chronic tic disorder between 1969 and 2009: the full sibling of an affected person was about 18 times more likely to be diagnosed than someone from an unaffected family (odds ratio 17.68), and the heritability was estimated at 0.77. No single gene explains the disorder. DSM-5-TR describes rare genetic variants found in some families and many common variants whose combined effect rises with the severity of the tics, which suggests that tic disorders lie on a continuous developmental spectrum rather than falling into separate boxes. Chronic tic disorders also share genetic variance with OCD, ADHD and autism, which helps explain why they so often occur together.

The environment plays a part early in development. DSM-5-TR lists advanced paternal age and a series of pre- and perinatal adversities: impaired fetal growth, maternal fever during labour, maternal smoking, severe maternal psychological stress, preterm birth, breech presentation and caesarean delivery. People with tic disorders are also at increased risk of autoimmune disorders such as Hashimoto's thyroiditis, and the immune system and inflammation of the brain seem to play a part in at least some of them, most clearly in Sydenham's chorea. The proposal that a streptococcal throat infection can trigger the sudden onset of tics or OCD through an autoimmune attack on the basal ganglia, known as PANDAS, is described with its controversy in Section 6.7.7.

Where in the brain? Most researchers locate the disorder in the loops that run from the cerebral cortex to the basal ganglia (the striatum, made up of the caudate and putamen, and the globus pallidus), on to the thalamus and back to the cortex, the cortico-striato-thalamo-cortical circuits whose role in OCD is described in Section 6.7.6. These loops help select which movements and habits are released and which are held back, and a failure to inhibit unwanted motor programmes would produce just the kind of sudden, fragmentary movements that tics are. Because these circuits depend heavily on dopamine signalling, the success of dopamine-blocking drugs in reducing tics, from Seignot and the Shapiros onwards, has long been taken as evidence for a dopamine hypothesis, and Pinel and Barnes present it in that way. Treat the argument with the usual caution: a drug that relieves a symptom does not prove that the symptom is caused by the system the drug acts on, any more than the relief of a headache by aspirin proves that headaches are caused by a lack of aspirin.

Exam trap

Tourette's disorder was described by Georges Gilles de la Tourette in 1885 (nine patients, at the Salpêtrière), and named after him by his teacher Charcot; the first case, the Marquise de Dampierre, had been reported by Itard in 1825. Haloperidol's success (Seignot 1961; the Shapiros 1968) moved the disorder from psychoanalysis to neurology. Know the course figures: first tics at 4 to 6 (often eye blinking), peak at 10 to 12, decline in adolescence; males 2 to 4 times more often than females. Coprolalia is a minority symptom (roughly 10 to 20 percent) and is not required. "Pure" Tourette's without any co-occurring condition is the exception (about 12 percent in Freeman's database), with ADHD and OCD the commonest companions. Heritability is high, about 70 to 85 percent (0.77 in the Swedish register study).

Treating tics

The first question in treating tics is whether to treat them at all. Most tics are mild, many children are not troubled by them, and the natural history (Figure 3.16) is towards improvement in adolescence. For a large number of families the most useful treatment is therefore an explanation: what tics are, that they are not deliberate, that they are not a sign of madness, brain damage or an evil eye, that they will wax and wane, that telling a child to stop does not help and often makes them worse, and that the outlook is usually good. Pinel and Barnes describe the usual order of care as education first (of the patient, the family, friends and teachers), then treatment of the associated emotional problems such as anxiety and depression, and only then the tics themselves. The American Academy of Neurology's guideline of 2019, led by Tamara Pringsheim, agrees that watchful waiting is an acceptable approach for people whose tics cause no functional impairment. Tics deserve active treatment when they cause pain or injury (a neck that aches from head jerks), when they cause social or academic harm (teasing, exclusion, punishment, inability to concentrate in class), or when they cause the person real distress. And because a co-occurring condition often impairs more than the tics do (Section 3.10.5), the ADHD or the OCD is treated first when it is the bigger problem.

For Yash, treatment began with a long conversation with his parents and grandmother, and a letter to his school. His teacher learned that the throat clearing that had followed hers was an echo phenomenon, not mockery; he was allowed to leave the classroom for a few minutes when the tics built up, and to sit his examinations with extra time in a separate room. The class was given a short talk on tics, after which the teasing largely stopped. Simple accommodations of this kind (a pass to step out, a seat near the door, extra time and a separate room for examinations, no punishment for tics, and educating classmates) are often worth more than any medicine.

When the tics themselves need treatment, the first choice today is behavioural. Habit reversal training, in which the person learns to notice the premonitory urge and to perform a competing response that is physically incompatible with the tic until the urge passes, and the Comprehensive Behavioral Intervention for Tics (CBIT), which adds function-based interventions, relaxation and psychoeducation to habit reversal, are taught in full in Book 2, Sections 4.9.1 and 4.9.2, together with the trial by John Piacentini and his colleagues (2010) in which about 52.5 percent of children given CBIT were much improved against 18.5 percent given supportive therapy. Here the conclusion is what matters: the 2019 guideline found that CBIT reduces tic severity with an effect similar in size to that of medication and without its side effects, and recommended that it be considered as an initial treatment, with exposure and response prevention for tics as an acceptable alternative when CBIT is not available; European guidelines make the same recommendation. Suppressing tics in this way does not produce a rebound burst afterwards, as was once feared (Book 2, Section 4.9.2). Yash had eight sessions of CBIT aimed first at his head jerk, using a gentle tensing of the neck muscles as his competing response.

Medicines are used when tics are severe, when behavioural treatment is unavailable, has not worked or cannot be managed by the child, or when the tics are causing injury. Four groups matter, and Table 3.33 summarises them.

The alpha-2 adrenergic agonists, clonidine and guanfacine, were developed to lower blood pressure. They stimulate alpha-2 receptors and reduce the release of noradrenaline, and they are often tried first for mild to moderate tics, especially when the child also has ADHD, because they can help both conditions. Their effect on tics is modest: the 2019 review judged clonidine probably, and guanfacine possibly, more effective than placebo, with the benefit larger in people who also have ADHD. The commonest side effect is sedation; they can also lower blood pressure and slow the heart, so these must be monitored, and they must be stopped gradually, because sudden withdrawal can cause rebound high blood pressure.

The antipsychotics are the most effective drugs for tics, the class whose success first moved the disorder into neurology. Haloperidol and pimozide, which block dopamine D2 receptors, and aripiprazole, which is a partial agonist at those receptors, are the three drugs approved in the United States for Tourette's disorder (pimozide in 1984, aripiprazole in 2014); risperidone is also widely used. The 2019 review judged haloperidol, risperidone and aripiprazole probably more effective than placebo, and pimozide possibly so. Pinel and Barnes put the reduction in tics with antipsychotics at about 70 percent, but note that many patients or their families refuse or abandon them because of their adverse effects. These are the familiar effects of the class (Section 4.10 teaches their pharmacology in detail): sedation and fatigue; weight gain and metabolic changes; extrapyramidal effects such as acute dystonia, drug-induced parkinsonism and akathisia; tardive dyskinesia with long use; and a rise in prolactin, which is least with aripiprazole. Pimozide carries a particular risk of prolonging the QT interval of the heart's electrical cycle, which can lead to dangerous rhythm disturbances, so the guideline requires an electrocardiogram (ECG) and measurement of the QT interval before and after it is started. Because aripiprazole has a more favourable profile of adverse effects than the older drugs, the European guidelines of 2021 name it as their preferred medicine for tics in both children and adults, with tiapride (a dopamine-blocking drug used mainly in Europe), risperidone and the alpha-2 agonists as other options.

Key study: "Witty Ticcy Ray" (Oliver Sacks, 1981)

The neurologist Oliver Sacks (1933 to 2015) described in 1981, in the London Review of Books, and later in his book The Man Who Mistook His Wife for a Hat (1985), a young man he called Ray, who had had severe Tourette's disorder since early childhood. Ray's tics had cost him jobs and relationships, but they were also woven into his personality: he was a wild, inventive jazz drummer, famous for sudden improvisations that grew out of his tics, and a brilliant, unpredictable table-tennis player. When Sacks gave him haloperidol the tics almost vanished, and so, Ray found, did his spark; his drumming became slow and deliberate, and he felt he had lost part of himself. After much discussion they reached a compromise. Ray took haloperidol on weekdays, when he needed to be steady at work, and stopped it at weekends, when he became once more "witty ticcy Ray" and let fly on the drums. What it shows. A case history, not an experiment, but it makes a point that trials do not: the goal of treating tics is a better life, not a lower tic count, and the person with the tics has the decisive voice in weighing benefit against side effects.

Several other treatments are used in particular situations. Topiramate, an anticonvulsant, was judged possibly more effective than placebo and may be tried for mild but troublesome tics when other drugs fail or are not tolerated. Tetrabenazine works differently from the antipsychotics: by blocking the vesicular monoamine transporter (VMAT2) it depletes nerve terminals of dopamine instead of blocking its receptors. It is used off-label, without support from randomised trials in tics, and it can cause drowsiness, depression and parkinsonism. Injection of botulinum toxin into the muscles involved, which blocks the release of acetylcholine at the junction between nerve and muscle and so weakens the muscle, is probably effective in adolescents and adults for a single troublesome, localised simple motor tic (a forceful neck or eye tic, for example) or for severely disabling vocal tics; its effect lasts 12 to 16 weeks, so injections must be repeated, and it can cause weakness and, when used for vocal tics, a soft voice. For the small number of adults with severe tics that have not responded to behavioural treatment and to several classes of medicine, deep brain stimulation is an option: electrodes are implanted in deep brain structures and stimulated continuously. Vandewalle and colleagues reported the first case, using stimulation of the thalamus, in 1999; the 2019 review found stimulation of the anteromedial globus pallidus probably effective and the evidence for thalamic targets insufficient. It is reserved for adults with severe, treatment-refractory and especially self-injurious tics, after assessment by a multidisciplinary team that includes a mental health professional.

Finally, the question that worries parents of children who have both tics and ADHD: can a stimulant make tics worse? For decades the answer seemed to be yes. Stimulants were observed to bring out or worsen tics in some children, and methylphenidate's product information long listed tics, or a family history of Tourette's disorder, among the reasons not to prescribe it. The question was tested directly by the Tourette's Syndrome Study Group in a trial published in 2002, in which 136 children aged 7 to 14 with ADHD and a chronic tic disorder were randomly assigned to methylphenidate, clonidine, both drugs, or placebo. The combination worked best for ADHD. The proportion of children whose tics worsened was no higher with methylphenidate than with placebo, and tic severity actually fell in all the groups that received active treatment, most in the combination group; the authors concluded that the old advice to avoid methylphenidate in children with tics was not supported. The 2019 review went further and judged that methylphenidate probably reduces tic severity in children with tics and ADHD, and that atomoxetine does not worsen tics. Tics are therefore no longer an absolute bar to stimulant treatment. The practical rule is to start with a low dose and to watch the tics; the occasional child whose tics clearly worsen can be switched to atomoxetine or an alpha-2 agonist. The ADHD drugs themselves are taught in Sections 3.8.6 and 3.8.7. Yash's assessment confirmed ADHD, combined presentation, and he was started on methylphenidate while his tics were recorded week by week; they did not worsen, his homework began to get finished, and a year later, at 13, his head jerk had faded and only an occasional sniff remained.

Exam trap

The first-line treatment for troublesome tics is behavioural: habit reversal training or CBIT (Book 2, Sections 4.9.1 and 4.9.2). The most effective drugs are the antipsychotics; the three approved in the United States for Tourette's disorder are haloperidol, pimozide and aripiprazole (aripiprazole in 2014), and pimozide needs an ECG because it prolongs the QT interval. Clonidine and guanfacine are milder and are often chosen when ADHD is also present. A tic is treated with habit reversal; a compulsion with exposure and response prevention (Book 2, Section 4.9.2). And stimulants are not absolutely contraindicated in children with tics: in the 2002 Tourette's Syndrome Study Group trial, methylphenidate did not worsen tics more than placebo did.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  3. In stereotypic movement disorder, symptoms that require continuous monitoring and protective measures to prevent serious injury are graded .
  4. Stereotypies usually begin before the age of 3, whereas tics begin at a mean age of years.
  5. The localised, uncomfortable sensation that precedes a tic and is relieved by it is the urge.
