Answers: (1) dyspraxia, (2) apraxia, (3) severe, (4) 4 to 6, (5) premonitory, (6) palilalia, (7) 18, (8) provisional, (9) 1885, (10) aripiprazole.

The case of Victor of Aveyron, revisited

Go back to the boy who came out of the forest near Saint-Sernin-sur-Rance in January 1800, and put the questions of this chapter to him one at a time. Was he intellectually disabled, as Pinel judged? DSM-5-TR would ask for three things: a deficit in intellectual functions confirmed by individualised, standardised testing, deficits in adaptive functioning in the conceptual, social and practical domains, and onset in the developmental period (Section 3.2.2). Nobody could test Victor; the scale of Binet and Simon lay more than a century ahead, in 1905 (Section 3.1.2). And even a valid score would not have settled it, because a child who has been neglected or never taught may perform at a low level that improves, sometimes dramatically, with care and teaching (Section 3.2.6). Was he a normal child stunted by isolation, as Itard believed? The English and Romanian Adoptees study (Section 3.3.6) gave the answer Itard lacked: severe early deprivation can produce intellectual impairment that is largely reversible in a loving family, the earlier the better, but children deprived for longer kept a quasi-autistic pattern, disinhibited social behaviour and inattention into adult life. Or was he autistic, as Uta Frith suggested? Read the opening again with criterion B in mind. He rocked for long periods, a stereotyped movement (B1); he seemed hardly to feel the cold and ignored a pistol shot, yet turned at once to the crack of a walnut, the hypo- and hyperreactivity to sensory input that DSM-5 added as B4 (Section 3.5.4); and he paid little attention to people (criterion A, Section 3.5.3). But almost every feature of autism, taken alone, occurs in some other condition, rocking among them as a result of neglect (Section 3.5.7), and criterion C asks about the early developmental period, which nobody saw.

That is why the question stays open, as Section 3.5.1 concludes. What Victor's case lacked was not a clever examiner but the tools this chapter describes: a developmental history from the pregnancy onwards, several informants who knew the child in different settings (Section 3.1.4), and individually administered, standardised measures of intelligence and adaptive behaviour (Section 3.2.2). What it did not lack was consequence. Itard's patient, daily teaching passed to his pupil Édouard Seguin, who turned it into the first systematic method for educating children with intellectual disability and was among the founders, in 1876, of the association that became the AAIDD (Section 3.2.1); it was Seguin, not Pinel, who showed that such children could be taught. And Itard returned to this chapter by another door: in 1825 he described the Marquise de Dampierre, whose movements, cries and obscene words made her the first of Gilles de la Tourette's nine patients in 1885 (Section 3.10.5). The doctor who could not teach a wild boy to speak left his mark on two of this chapter's disorders: the education of children with intellectual disability and the first description of Tourette's disorder.

Chapter summary

3.1 Disorders of a Developing Brain: Defining and Classifying the Neurodevelopmental Disorders. The neurodevelopmental disorders begin in the developmental period, often before school, and impair personal, social, academic or occupational functioning. They are dimensional, show delay or deviance, and frequently co-occur. They are not the neurodevelopmental model of schizophrenia (Weinberger; Murray and Lewis, 1987) or the neurocognitive disorders, and not every childhood disorder belongs: ADHD does, while OCD, generalised anxiety disorder and borderline personality disorder do not. The categories grew from DSM-I's mental deficiency to DSM-5's neurodevelopmental chapter; ICD-10 spreads them over F70 to F79, F80 to F89 and F90 to F98. Because behaviour varies across contexts, children are assessed through several informants and a developmental history. The INCLEN survey (Arora, 2018) found a disorder in 9.2 percent of Indian children aged 2 to under 6 and 13.6 percent aged 6 to 9; the RPwD Act 2016 governs certification.

3.2 Intellectual Developmental Disorder: Definition, Criteria and Severity. Esquirol (1838) called idiocy a condition, not a disease, and the boundary has since moved: Heber (1959) added adaptive behaviour and drew the line one standard deviation below the mean, Grossman (1973) two. DSM-5-TR requires deficits in intellectual functions confirmed by clinical assessment and individualised, standardised testing (about two standard deviations below the mean, 65 to 75 with the margin), deficits in adaptive functioning in the conceptual, social and practical domains, and onset in the developmental period. ICD-10 and DSM-IV-TR graded severity by IQ (mild 50 to 69, "educable"; moderate 35 to 49, "trainable", with partial independence; severe 20 to 34; profound below 20); DSM-5 grades it by adaptive functioning. Global developmental delay is for children under 5, and borderline intellectual functioning is not a disorder. Prevalence is about 1 percent. No medicine improves intellect; in Tyrer's 2008 trial placebo matched antipsychotics.

3.3 The Causes of Intellectual Disability: Genes, Pregnancy, Birth and Childhood. Causes are sorted by when they act and, after Zigler (1967), into organic and cultural-familial groups, a division Reichenberg confirmed in 2016. Down syndrome (Down, 1866), usually trisomy 21 from nondisjunction, was traced to an extra chromosome by Gautier, Lejeune and Turpin in 1959 and brings early Alzheimer's disease. Fragile X syndrome, an expanded CGG repeat in FMR1, is the commonest inherited cause; Prader-Willi and Angelman syndromes show genomic imprinting. In phenylketonuria (Følling, 1934) missing phenylalanine hydroxylase leaves phenylalanine unconverted to tyrosine; Bickel's diet and Guthrie's newborn test prevent the damage. Fetal alcohol syndrome (Jones and Smith, 1973) joins a small head, smooth philtrum, thin upper lip and short palpebral fissures. TORCH infections, iodine deficiency, birth asphyxia, meningitis, lead and severe deprivation, measured in the English and Romanian Adoptees study, complete the list.

3.4 Communication Disorders: Language, Speech, Fluency and Social Communication. Speech is the line, language the code and communication its social use; each communication disorder is a fault in one layer. Language disorder is a developmental deficit in vocabulary, sentence structure and discourse, receptive problems carrying the poorer outlook; Landau-Kleffner syndrome (1957) is language lost, not undeveloped. Speech sound disorder affects producing speech sounds. Childhood-onset fluency disorder, stuttering, begins by age 6 in 80 to 90 percent of cases; Wendell Johnson's diagnosogenic theory, tested in Mary Tudor's "Monster Study" (1939), blamed the listener, but stuttering is now seen as a disorder of speech timing and control with a strong genetic contribution. Social (pragmatic) communication disorder, new in DSM-5, is told from autism by the absence of restricted, repetitive behaviour. When a child does not speak, ask where the barrier lies (inherent mutism, selective mutism, autism, learning disorder), and test hearing first.

3.5 Autism Spectrum Disorder I: From Kanner to the Spectrum, and the Diagnosis. Bleuler coined autism in 1911 for a symptom of schizophrenia; Kanner described early infantile autism in eleven children in 1943, Asperger "autistic psychopathy" in 1944, and Sukhareva six similar boys in 1925. Wing and Gould's triad of impairments (1979) made autism a spectrum, while Bettelheim's refrigerator mother was discredited by Rimland, twin studies and studies of parents. DSM-III named infantile autism (1980), and DSM-5 merged autistic disorder, Asperger's disorder, childhood disintegrative disorder (Heller, 1908) and PDD-NOS into one spectrum, leaving out Rett syndrome (Rett, 1966; MECP2, 1999). Criterion A needs all three deficits in social communication and social interaction across multiple contexts, criterion B two of four restricted, repetitive patterns, sensory reactivity among them; onset is early, and an IQ below 70 is not a criterion. Severity levels 1 to 3 are rated per domain.

3.6 Autism Spectrum Disorder II: Who, Why and What Helps. Recorded prevalence rose to about 1 in 31 American eight-year-olds (2022), mainly through broader criteria, diagnostic substitution (Shattuck, 2006) and recording changes (Hansen, 2015); the well-ascertained sex ratio is about 3 to 1. Intellectual disability and ADHD (28 percent; Lai, 2019) are the commonest companions, and language development is compromised more often in autism than in ADHD or specific learning disorder. Heritability is about 80 percent (Bai, 2019), after Folstein and Rutter's twins (1977); older parents and valproate add risk, while Wakefield's 1998 MMR claim was fraudulent and retracted, and Hviid's 657,461 Danish children showed no link. Theories include theory of mind (the Sally-Anne task of Baron-Cohen, Leslie and Frith, 1985), poor encoding of social cues, weak central coherence (Frith, 1989), executive dysfunction and the extreme male brain. Behavioural and educational treatment comes first; risperidone and aripiprazole treat severe irritability.

3.7 Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder I: History, Symptoms and Diagnosis. Crichton described a disease of attention in 1798, Hoffmann drew Fidgety Philip in 1845, and Still (1902) a "defect of moral control". Encephalitis lethargica, Bradley's Benzedrine, minimal brain damage and minimal brain dysfunction (1966) followed; DSM-II named the hyperkinetic reaction of childhood, Douglas (1972) moved the emphasis to attention, and DSM-III created attention deficit disorder. DSM-5-TR requires six or more of nine inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity symptoms (five from age 17) for at least 6 months, several before age 12 (7 in DSM-IV), in two or more settings, with impairment. "Does not seem to listen when spoken to directly" is inattention; talking excessively is hyperactivity. The presentations are combined, predominantly inattentive and predominantly hyperactive/impulsive. ICD-10's hyperkinetic disorder (F90) needs both impaired attention and overactivity, and social withdrawal is not a feature. Oppositional defiant disorder, learning disorder and medical mimics are the main look-alikes.

3.8 Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder II: Course, Causes and Drug Treatment. DSM-5-TR puts ADHD at about 7.2 percent of children and 2.5 percent of adults, twice as common in boys, and the youngest in a class are diagnosed most (Morrow, 2012). Hyperactivity fades but inattention often persists: about 15 percent meet full criteria at 25, about 65 percent counting partial remission (Faraone, 2006), and mortality is about doubled (Dalsgaard, 2015). Heritability is about 74 percent; the catecholamine hypothesis points to dopamine and noradrenaline in frontostriatal circuits, and Shaw (2007) found cortical maturation about three years late. Low birth weight and prematurity are firm risks; sugar is not. Barkley's behavioural inhibition model (1997), Sonuga-Barke's dual pathway and Sergeant's cognitive-energetic model explain the psychology. Methylphenidate (Panizzon, 1944) and the amphetamines block dopamine and noradrenaline reuptake, methylphenidate first in children (Cortese, 2018); atomoxetine, clonidine and guanfacine are the non-stimulants.

3.9 Specific Learning Disorder: Dyslexia, Dysgraphia and Dyscalculia. Kussmaul (1877) named word blindness and Berlin (1887) dyslexia, both acquired; Hinshelwood and Pringle Morgan (1896) described the congenital form, Orton blamed incomplete cerebral dominance, and Kirk coined "learning disabilities" in 1963. DSM-5 merged the learning disorders into one diagnosis: difficulties lasting at least 6 months despite targeted intervention, skills well below age expectation, onset in the school years, and no better explanation by intellectual disability, sensory problems or inadequate teaching. Intelligence is in the normal range; the IQ-achievement discrepancy gave way to response to intervention. Specifiers name impairment in reading (dyslexia), written expression and mathematics (dyscalculia). Prevalence is 5 to 15 percent of schoolchildren. The phonological deficit theory, supported by Bradley and Bryant (1983) and Ramus (2003), explains most dyslexia. ADHD is not a learning disorder, and auditory processing disorder joins them only in educational usage. Treatment is teaching.

3.10 Motor Disorders: Developmental Coordination, Stereotypies, Tics and Tourette's Disorder. Developmental coordination disorder, or dyspraxia, is a developmental failure to acquire coordinated movement, in 5 to 8 percent of children, often with ADHD; it differs from Liepmann's acquired apraxia (1900), Freud's agnosia (1891) and anomia. Stereotypic movement disorder is driven, purposeless repetitive movement. A tic is a sudden, rapid, recurrent, non-rhythmic movement or vocalisation, usually preceded by a premonitory urge and briefly suppressible, unlike a seizure, dissociation or Parkinson's tremor. Tourette's disorder needs multiple motor and one or more vocal tics, not necessarily together, for more than 1 year, with onset before 18; provisional tic disorder lasts under a year. Itard described the first case (1825), Gilles de la Tourette nine patients (1885). Tics peak around 10 (Leckman, 1998), coprolalia is a minority symptom, and OCD and ADHD are common companions. Habit reversal comes first, then alpha-2 agonists and antipsychotics.

From the past papers

  1. Language is a barrier to communication for (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Inherently mute person. (b) Selective mutism. (c) Individual with ASD. (d) Learning disorder.

    Answer: (a) Inherently mute person. For a person who is inherently mute the spoken-language channel itself is blocked, so another channel (sign, writing, augmentative and alternative communication) is needed (Section 3.4.6). In selective mutism language is intact and anxiety blocks its use in some settings; in autism the difficulty lies in the social use of communication; in a learning disorder spoken communication is usually unaffected. One printed explanation ends with a stray "C", which is not the key.

  2. A key differentiation between global developmental delay and intellectual disability is (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Age of assessment (under 5 years for GDD, 5 years or over for ID). (b) Presence of social deficits. (c) Language delay alone. (d) Motor impairment.

    Answer: (a) Age of assessment (under 5 years for GDD, 5 years or over for ID). Global developmental delay is reserved for children under 5 whose severity cannot yet be reliably assessed; the age stands for testability (Section 3.2.4). Social deficits, language delay and motor impairment all occur in both conditions, so none of them separates the two.

  3. When diagnosing developmental disorders, multi-information assessment is recommended because (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) parents tend to over report problems. (b) teachers provide no relevant information. (c) symptoms may vary across contexts. (d) only clinical observation is not sufficient.

    Answer: (c) symptoms may vary across contexts. A child behaves differently at home, at school and in the clinic, so each informant sees a valid but partial picture, which is why ADHD needs symptoms in two or more settings (Section 3.1.4). Parents may under-report as easily as over-report, teachers are often the best judges of attention and learning, and the limits of clinic observation are a consequence of the real reason, not the reason itself.

  4. According to DSM-5, which of the following is required for a diagnosis of Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD)? (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Delayed speech alone (b) Persistent deficits in social communication and interaction across multiple contexts (c) Intellectual disability only (d) Hyperactivity and impulsivity

    Answer: (b) Persistent deficits in social communication and interaction across multiple contexts. Criterion A requires persistent deficits in social communication and social interaction across multiple contexts, in all three of its areas, and criterion B adds restricted, repetitive behaviour (Section 3.5.3). Delayed speech alone is neither required nor sufficient, intellectual disability is a specifier, and hyperactivity and impulsivity belong to ADHD.

  5. The DSM-5 criteria for specific learning disorder include (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Average intellectual functioning with persistent difficulties in academic skills (b) IQ below 50 (c) Only attention deficit (d) Motor coordination deficit only

    Answer: (a) Average intellectual functioning with persistent difficulties in academic skills. Specific learning disorder is a persistent difficulty in academic skills, lasting at least 6 months despite targeted help, in a person of otherwise normal intellectual functioning (Section 3.9.2). An IQ below 50 means at least moderate intellectual disability, attention deficit alone describes ADHD, and a motor coordination deficit alone describes developmental coordination disorder. Strictly, DSM-5-TR asks only for an IQ above about 70 (plus or minus 5), so "average" is the best option rather than an exact rule.

  6. Language development is compromised most commonly in (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Attention Deficit-Hyperactivity Disorder. (b) Autism spectrum disorder. (c) Specific learning disability. (d) Childhood onset dissociation.

    Answer: (b) Autism spectrum disorder. Delayed or absent language is the commonest first concern in autism, it has its own specifier, and language disorder is one of its most frequent companions (Section 3.6.2). Some keys mark specific learning disability, but that disorder impairs written language and number, not the development of spoken language; the language delays of ADHD are not specific to it; and "childhood onset dissociation" is not a diagnostic category.

  7. In Phenylketonuria (PKU), the body fails to convert phenylalanine into which substance due to a deficiency of phenylalanine hydroxylase? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Urea (b) Tyrosine (c) Tryptophan (d) Histidine

    Answer: (b) Tyrosine. Phenylalanine hydroxylase converts phenylalanine into tyrosine, the first step towards L-dopa, dopamine and noradrenaline; in PKU, an autosomal recessive disorder described by Følling in 1934, phenylalanine builds up instead (Section 3.3.4). Urea is the end product of the body's disposal of nitrogen, tryptophan is the precursor of serotonin and histidine of histamine.

  8. Research evidence suggests that people with autism spectrum development show deficits in: (RML 2020)

    (a) Anger management (b) Encoding social cues (c) Deception (d) Memory retrieval

    Answer: (b) Encoding social cues. Eye-tracking and face-processing studies, such as Klin's 2002 study in which autistic viewers looked far less at the eyes, show that the first step of Crick and Dodge's social information-processing model, encoding the cues, is impaired (Section 3.6.7). Difficulty deceiving is a consequence of the theory of mind difficulty, not the deficit the research names; anger problems are not a core deficit, and memory retrieval is usually intact.

  9. The cardinal DSM-IV-TR diagnostic criterion for Mental Retardation is based primarily on an IQ score: (RML 2021)

    (a) Seven standard deviations below the mean. (b) One standard deviations below the mean. (c) Two standard deviations below the mean. (d) Four standard deviations below the mean

    Answer: (c) Two standard deviations below the mean. Two standard deviations below the mean is about 70 on a test with mean 100 and SD 15, and DSM-5-TR keeps the idea with a margin of about 5 points, giving 65 to 75 (Section 3.2.2). One SD (about 85) was Heber's old 1959 line, four SD (about 40) marks the boundary between moderate and severe disability in ICD-11, and seven SD would be a score no test can produce.

  10. Within attention deficit/hyperactivity disorder, which of the following is NOT classified as a problem relating to hyperactivity/impulsivity? (RML 2021)

    (a) Experiences difficulties in playing quietly. (b) Rather than sitting still, squirms in seat and fidgets. (c) Talks excessively. (d) Doesn't appear to listen when being told something or spoken to

    Answer: (d) Doesn't appear to listen when being told something or spoken to. Not seeming to listen when spoken to directly is inattention item A1c (Section 3.7.2). Difficulty playing quietly, squirming and fidgeting, and talking excessively are all hyperactivity items; note that talking excessively counts as hyperactivity, not impulsivity.

  11. Maya's baby was born with a smaller than average head size, facial malformations, and an intellectual disability. It is likely that Maya _____ during her early pregnancy. (RML 2022)

    (a) drank too much caffeine (b) drank alcohol (c) smoked marijuana (d) ate fish with mercury in it

    Answer: (b) drank alcohol. A small head, a characteristic face (smooth philtrum, thin upper lip, short palpebral fissures) and intellectual disability make up fetal alcohol syndrome, named by Jones and Smith in 1973 (Section 3.3.5). Caffeine and cannabis produce no such malformation syndrome, and methylmercury from contaminated fish damages the fetal brain, as at Minamata, but does not produce the FAS face.

  12. In which of the following situations would a diagnosis of "global developmental delay" be inappropriate? (RML 2022)

    (a) The patient is a child who is too young to fully manifest specific symptoms or to complete requisite assessments. (b) The patient, a 7-year-old boy, has a full-scale IQ of 65 and severe impairment in adaptive functioning. (c) The patient's scores on psychometric tests suggest intellectual disability, but there is insufficient information about the patient's adaptive functional skills. (d) The patient's impaired adaptive functioning suggests intellectual developmental disorder, but there is insufficient information about the level of cognitive impairment measured by standardized instruments.

    Answer: (b) The patient, a 7-year-old boy, has a full-scale IQ of 65 and severe impairment in adaptive functioning. The 7-year-old is over 5, has been validly tested, scores inside the 65 to 75 zone and has established adaptive deficits, so he meets criteria A, B and C and the diagnosis is intellectual developmental disorder (Section 3.2.4). Each of the other options describes an incomplete picture in a young child (too young to test, adaptive skills not yet established, or cognitive level not yet measured), which is exactly what the provisional label is for.

  13. Which of the following best describes a common characteristic of dyslexia? (RML 2025)

    (a) Difficulty understanding spoken language. (b) Difficulty with reading and decoding words. (c) Difficulty with motor coordination. (d) Difficulty concentrating during lectures.

    Answer: (b) Difficulty with reading and decoding words. Dyslexia, the pattern DSM-5-TR names under impairment in reading, is inaccurate or slow word recognition, poor decoding and poor spelling, rooted in most cases in a phonological deficit (Sections 3.9.3 and 3.9.5). Difficulty understanding spoken language is language disorder, difficulty with motor coordination is developmental coordination disorder, and difficulty concentrating during lectures suggests ADHD.

  14. Which is NOT typically considered a learning difficulty? (RML 2025)

    (a) Dysgraphia (b) ADHD (c) Dyslexia (d) Auditory processing disorder

    Answer: (b) ADHD. ADHD harms learning and often co-occurs with a learning disorder, but it is a separate neurodevelopmental disorder of attention and self-control (Section 3.9.6). Dyslexia, dysgraphia and dyscalculia are learning difficulties, and auditory processing disorder is grouped with them in educational usage, although DSM-5-TR does not list it.

  15. Hyperkinetic syndrome is characterized by the following except: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Social Withdrawal (b) Over-activity (c) Distractibility (d) Short attention-span

    Answer: (a) Social Withdrawal. ICD-10's hyperkinetic disorder (F90) requires impaired attention and overactivity, shown by distractibility and a short attention span, and hyperkinetic children are socially disinhibited and intrusive, not withdrawn (Section 3.7.4). They may become isolated because other children reject them, but a child who withdraws points instead to depression, social anxiety disorder or autism.

  16. Infantile Autism was first described by: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Leo Kanner (b) Cameron (c) Goldstein (d) Jasper

    Answer: (a) Leo Kanner. Leo Kanner described early infantile autism in eleven children in 1943 (Section 3.5.1). Norman Cameron is known for over-inclusive thinking, Kurt Goldstein for concrete thinking, and "Jasper" stands for Karl Jaspers, the founder of descriptive psychopathology.

  17. Which disorder is classified as a neurodevelopmental disorder? (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) OCD (b) ADHD (c) Borderline personality disorder (d) Generalized Anxiety Disorder

    Answer: (b) ADHD. ADHD sits in DSM-5-TR's neurodevelopmental chapter with intellectual disability, the communication disorders, autism, specific learning disorder and the motor disorders (Section 3.1.1). OCD belongs to the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, generalised anxiety disorder to the anxiety disorders and borderline personality disorder to the personality disorders.

  18. Which is NOT a core symptom to diagnose as autism in children as per DSM-5? (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Communication skills (b) Unusual repetitive behavior (c) Social reciprocity (d) I.Q. is below 70

    Answer: (d) I.Q. is below 70. Intellectual impairment is a specifier ("with or without accompanying intellectual impairment"), and many autistic people have average or high intelligence (Section 3.5.5). Communication and social reciprocity (criterion A) and unusual repetitive behaviour (criterion B) are the core.

  19. A condition characterised by the occurrence of sudden, rapid, purposeless involuntary movement in the body: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Dissociation (b) Tics (c) Parkinson's Disease (d) Seizures

    Answer: (b) Tics. A tic is a sudden, rapid, recurrent, non-rhythmic movement or vocalisation, usually preceded by a premonitory urge (Section 3.10.3). Seizures are abnormal electrical discharges that cannot be suppressed and often alter awareness, dissociation is a disruption of consciousness, memory or identity rather than a movement, and Parkinson's disease gives a rhythmic resting tremor with slowness and rigidity. Strictly, tics are semi-voluntary rather than fully involuntary.

  20. A neurological disorder characterised by impairments in motor performance and coordination is known as: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Dyspraxia (b) Apraxia (c) Agnosia (d) Anomia

    Answer: (a) Dyspraxia. Dyspraxia, that is, developmental coordination disorder, is a developmental failure to acquire coordinated movement (Section 3.10.1). Apraxia is the acquired loss of skilled, purposeful movement (Liepmann, 1900), agnosia an acquired failure of recognition (Freud's term, 1891) and anomia an acquired failure of naming.

  21. Individuals who are moderately retarded: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Can achieve partial independence in activities of daily living (b) Can become self-supporting adults with appropriate training (c) Are generally institutionalised (d) Are usually diagnosed in infancy

    Answer: (a) Can achieve partial independence in activities of daily living. Moderate disability (the old "trainable" level, IQ 35 to 49) allows partial independence in daily living with supervised or supported work (Section 3.2.3). Becoming self-supporting with training describes the mild ("educable") level; most people at every level now live with their families or in the community; and diagnosis in infancy fits the severe and profound levels.

  22. Infantile autism was first described by: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Hans Asperger (b) Leo Kanner (c) Eugen Bleuler (d) Kurt Schneider

    Answer: (b) Leo Kanner. Kanner's 1943 paper "Autistic disturbances of affective contact" first described the condition (Section 3.5.1). Bleuler only coined the word, in 1911, for a symptom of schizophrenia; Asperger described the verbally fluent form a year after Kanner, in 1944; and Schneider is known for the first-rank symptoms of schizophrenia.

  23. A child exhibits no eye contact, delayed language, and repetitive movements. These are characteristic of: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) ADHD (b) Conduct disorder (c) Autism spectrum disorder (d) Specific learning disorder

    Answer: (c) Autism spectrum disorder. Absent eye contact and delayed language reflect the social communication deficit, and repetitive movements the restricted, repetitive behaviour: the two domains of autism (Section 3.5.4). ADHD is inattention and hyperactivity-impulsivity, conduct disorder a repetitive pattern of violating others' rights and major social rules, and specific learning disorder a difficulty with reading, writing or mathematics; none of them requires both domains.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Autism spectrum disorder (DSM-5) requires persistent deficits in social communication and social interaction across multiple contexts (criterion A, all three areas) plus restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour, interests or activities (criterion B, two of four); an IQ below 70, delayed speech alone and hyperactivity are not required. asked 3 times
  2. Leo Kanner first described early infantile autism in 1943 ("Autistic disturbances of affective contact"); Eugen Bleuler coined the word autism in 1911 for a symptom of schizophrenia, and Hans Asperger described the verbally fluent form in 1944. asked 2 times
  3. Global developmental delay is diagnosed only under the age of 5, when severity cannot be reliably assessed; a 7-year-old with an FSIQ of 65 and severe adaptive impairment receives intellectual developmental disorder, not global developmental delay. asked 2 times
  4. The intellectual criterion of intellectual disability is an IQ about two standard deviations below the mean (about 70, or 65 to 75 with the measurement margin), and no score diagnoses on its own: deficits in adaptive functioning are required. asked 2 times
  5. DSM-5-TR ADHD: several symptoms present before age 12 (7 in DSM-IV), for at least 6 months, in two or more settings; six of nine symptoms in either list, five from age 17. asked 2 times
  6. Tourette's disorder: multiple motor and one or more vocal tics, not necessarily at the same time, for more than 1 year since the first tic, with onset before 18; coprolalia occurs in a minority and is not required. asked 2 times
  7. Multi-informant assessment is recommended because a child's symptoms vary across contexts (home, school, clinic). asked once
  8. ADHD is a neurodevelopmental disorder; OCD, generalised anxiety disorder and borderline personality disorder belong to other chapters of DSM-5-TR. asked once
  9. Language itself is the barrier to communication for the inherently mute person; in selective mutism language is intact and anxiety blocks speech in some settings. asked once
  10. Of ADHD, autism, specific learning disorder and dissociation, language development is most commonly compromised in autism spectrum disorder. asked once
  11. Autistic people show deficits in encoding social cues: they look less at eyes and faces (Klin, 2002), the first step of Crick and Dodge's social information-processing model. asked once
  12. In phenylketonuria (Følling, 1934; autosomal recessive) a deficiency of phenylalanine hydroxylase stops phenylalanine being converted into tyrosine; newborn screening and a low-phenylalanine diet prevent the intellectual disability. asked once
  13. A small head, smooth philtrum, thin upper lip, short palpebral fissures and intellectual disability mean fetal alcohol syndrome from drinking in pregnancy (Jones and Smith, 1973). asked once
  14. Moderate intellectual disability (IQ 35 to 49, "trainable") allows partial independence in activities of daily living; the mild level ("educable") can become self-supporting with training. asked once
  15. "Does not seem to listen when spoken to directly" is an inattention symptom of ADHD; fidgeting, difficulty playing quietly and talking excessively are hyperactivity symptoms. asked once
  16. ICD-10 hyperkinetic disorder (F90) is overactivity with impaired attention (distractibility, short attention span) and impulsivity; social withdrawal is not a feature. asked once
  17. DSM-5 specific learning disorder: persistent difficulty in academic skills (at least 6 months despite targeted intervention, well below age expectation) in a person of normal-range, usually average, intelligence; the IQ-achievement discrepancy is no longer required. asked once
  18. Dyslexia is difficulty with accurate or fluent word reading and decoding, with poor spelling, caused in most cases by a phonological deficit. asked once
  19. ADHD is not a learning disability; dyslexia, dysgraphia and dyscalculia are learning difficulties, and auditory processing disorder is grouped with them in educational usage. asked once
  20. A tic is a sudden, rapid, recurrent, non-rhythmic motor movement or vocalisation, preceded by a premonitory urge; it is not a seizure, dissociation or the tremor of Parkinson's disease. asked once
  21. Dyspraxia (developmental coordination disorder) is a developmental impairment of motor performance and coordination; apraxia (Liepmann, 1900), agnosia (Freud, 1891) and anomia are acquired. asked once
  22. The commonest co-occurring conditions in autism are intellectual disability and ADHD (ADHD in about 28 percent, Lai and colleagues, 2019). asked once
